- **Single escape VC** — valid but requires adaptive routing (fails N7) and Duato proof formalization (fails N6).
- **Timeout-based** — valid but requires timeout hardware and breaks closed-form proofs (fails N6).

**This is a constraint-driven verdict, not a proof of universal superiority.** Adaptive routing is a real technique used in production NoCs; it is not selected here because the workload and hardware budget do not demand it.

---

# 

---
# Flit Layout and Multi-Flit Packet Structure

## 1. What a Flit Is

A **flit** (flow control digit) is the smallest unit of data that the network moves in one clock cycle. The router buffers and forwards flits, not packets. A **packet** is a coherence message. A packet is made of one or more flits.

## 2. Flit Width

**64 bits.**

Why 64:

| Candidate | Fit with 64-byte cache line | BRAM alignment | Router crossbar |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 32 bits | 16 body flits | Good | Narrow |
| **64 bits** | **8 body flits** | **Good** | **Wide but manageable** |
| 128 bits | 4 body flits | Awkward | Very wide, high area |

64 bits gives 8 body flits per 64-byte line. Clean power of 2. Fits BRAM widths (36-bit BRAMs are doubled for 64-bit). Router crossbar is 64 bits wide — manageable on the XC7Z020.

## 3. Flit Types

There are two flit types:

| Type | Purpose | Payload |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Header flit** | Carries routing and control info | Address, message type, VC ID |
| **Body flit** | Carries data | 8 bytes of cache line or DMA payload |

A single-flit packet is a header flit with the `tail` bit set. A multi-flit packet has one header and one or more body flits. The last flit (header or body) has the `tail` bit set.

## 4. Header Flit Layout (64 bits)

```
   Bit:  63    56 55    48 47  44 43 42 41 40 39 38 37    32 31              0
         ┌────────┬────────┬──────┬─────┬──┬──┬──┬──┬───────┬─────────────────┐
         │dest[7:0]│src[7:0]│msg[3:0]│vc[1:0]│H │T │W │E │reserved│   addr[31:0]  │
         │  8 b   │  8 b   │  4 b  │ 2 b  │1 │1 │1 │1 │  6 b   │     32 b      │
         └────────┴────────┴──────┴─────┴──┴──┴──┴──┴───────┴─────────────────┘
```

### 4.1 Field Descriptions

| Field | Bits | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `dest` | [63:56] | Destination tile ID (8 bits, supports up to 256 tiles) |
| `src` | [55:48] | Source tile ID (8 bits) |
| `msg` | [47:44] | Message type (GETS, GETM, DATA, etc.) |
| `vc` | [43:42] | Virtual channel ID (0–3) |
| `H` | [41] | Head bit — 1 for the first flit of a packet |
| `T` | [40] | Tail bit — 1 for the last flit of a packet |
| `W` | [39] | Write enable — 1 for store requests and memory writes |
| `E` | [38] | Exclusive indication — 1 to grant exclusive ownership on data reply |
| `reserved` | [37:32] | Reserved for future use (6 bits) |
| `addr` | [31:0] | Byte address of the cache line |

### 4.2 Why 8-Bit Tile IDs

The 4×4 mesh needs 4 bits (16 tiles). The parameterised design supports up to 8×8 (64 tiles) with 6 bits. 8 bits gives headroom for 16×16 (256 tiles) without redesigning the flit.

The cost is 4 extra bits per flit in the header. For a 64-bit flit, that is acceptable.

### 4.3 Why 4-Bit Message Type

There are 14 messages (see §6). 4 bits holds 16 values. This leaves 2 codes free for future use.

### 4.4 Why 2-Bit VC ID

Four virtual channels are used (request, forward, response, invalidation). 2 bits holds 4 values exactly. No headroom, but 4 VCs is the design decision.

If a fifth VC is ever needed, the VC field must widen to 3 bits, which requires re-laying the flit. This is a known trade-off.

### 4.5 Why Separate H and T Bits

The `head` and `tail` bits allow four cases:

| H | T | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 0 | First flit of a multi-flit packet |
| 0 | 0 | Middle flit |
| 0 | 1 | Last flit of a multi-flit packet |
| 1 | 1 | Single-flit packet (both head and tail) |

This encoding is used by AXI-Stream, TileLink, and other on-chip protocols.

### 4.6 Why the Write Enable Bit

The `W` bit lets a memory request carry a write indication. When the L2 bank sends a memory request to `mem_tap`, the `W` bit tells the memory whether to read or write.

Without this bit, the memory would need a separate signal or a separate message type for writes. Putting it in the flit keeps the memory interface simple.

### 4.7 Why the Exclusive Bit

The `E` bit lets the directory tell the requester whether a cache line is being granted in the E state or the S state. This is what enables the silent E→M upgrade.

When the L2 bank sends `DATA` to a requester, it sets `E=1` if the directory state is `DIR_E`. If the requester sees `E=1`, it installs the line as E. Otherwise as S.

## 5. Body Flit Layout (64 bits)

```
   Bit:  63                                                          0
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                   data[63:0]                                │
         │                    64 bits                                  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

A body flit carries 8 bytes of data. It has no header fields. The router routes body flits using the path established by the header flit (wormhole switching).

The only non-payload bits in a body flit are the `tail` bit. Some designs put the tail bit in the header fields of the body flit, but this design keeps body flits pure payload and encodes the tail in the packet length.

**Correction:** This design does need a tail indicator in the body flit. The `tail` bit lives in bit 63 of the body flit, and bits 62:0 hold data. This is a compromise:

```
   Bit:  63    62                                                      0
         ┌─────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ T   │                    data[62:0]                            │
         │ 1 b │                     63 bits                             │
         └─────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

With 63 bits of data per body flit, a 64-byte (512-bit) cache line needs 9 body flits (512/63 = 8.13, rounded up). This is worse than the 8 body flits we expected.

**Alternative:** Keep body flits 64 bits of pure data and use the header flit's reserved bits to encode the packet length. The router counts flits as they pass and knows when the packet ends.

```
   Header reserved bits [37:32] = packet length (flit count)
```

This is cleaner. Body flits are pure payload. The router tracks the count.

## 6. Message Type Encoding (4 bits)

| Code | Message | Direction | Payload |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0000 | GETS | L1 → dir | Address only |
| 0001 | GETM | L1 → dir | Address only |
| 0010 | PUTS | L1 → dir | Address + data (write-back) |
| 0011 | PUTM | L1 → dir | Address + data (write-back) |
| 0100 | UNBLOCK | L1 → dir | Address only |
| 0101 | INV | dir → L1 | Address only |
| 0110 | INVACK | L1 → dir | Address only |
| 0111 | DATA | either | Address + data |
| 1000 | FWD_GETS | dir → owner | Address only |
| 1001 | FWD_GETM | dir → owner | Address only |
| 1010 | DATAFWD | owner → requester | Address + data |
| 1011 | DMA_READ | DMA → dir | Address + length |
| 1100 | DMA_WRITE | DMA → dir | Address + data |
| 1101 | DMA_ACK | dir → DMA | Address only |
| 1110 | reserved | — | — |
| 1111 | reserved | — | — |

## 7. Packet Sizes by Message Type

| Message | Header | Body | Total flits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| GETS | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| GETM | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| PUTS | 1 | 8 | 9 |
| PUTM | 1 | 8 | 9 |
| UNBLOCK | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| INV | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| INVACK | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| DATA | 1 | 8 | 9 |
| FWD_GETS | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| FWD_GETM | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| DATAFWD | 1 | 8 | 9 |
| DMA_READ | 1 | 0 | 1 |
| DMA_WRITE | 1 | up to 16 | 2–17 |
| DMA_ACK | 1 | 0 | 1 |

Control messages (GETS, INV, INVACK, UNBLOCK, FWD_GETS, FWD_GETM) are single-flit. Data-bearing messages (PUTS, PUTM, DATA, DATAFWD) are 9 flits. DMA transfers are variable up to 17 flits.

## 8. Multi-Flit Packet Structure

A multi-flit packet has a header, zero or more body flits, and a tail. The tail is the last flit of the packet. The head is the first.

### 8.1 Example: DATA Packet (9 flits)

```
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  FLIT 0 — HEADER                                                  │
   │  H=1, T=0                                                         │
   │  dest, src, msg=DATA, vc=2 (response), W=0, E=?                   │
   │  addr = 0x00001040                                                │
   │  reserved[5:0] = packet length = 9                                │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  FLIT 1 — BODY                                                    │
   │  H=0, T=0                                                         │
   │  data[63:0] = bytes 0–7 of cache line                             │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  FLIT 2 — BODY                                                    │
   │  H=0, T=0                                                         │
   │  data[63:0] = bytes 8–15 of cache line                            │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              ...
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  FLIT 8 — BODY (TAIL)                                             │
   │  H=0, T=1                                                         │
   │  data[63:0] = bytes 56–63 of cache line                           │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### 8.2 Example: Single-Flit GETS

```
   ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  FLIT 0 — HEADER (also TAIL)                                      │
   │  H=1, T=1                                                         │
   │  dest, src, msg=GETS, vc=0 (request), W=0, E=0                    │
   │  addr = 0x00001040                                                │
   │  reserved[5:0] = packet length = 1                                │
   └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

One flit. Head and tail both set.

## 9. How the Router Handles Multi-Flit Packets

### 9.1 Wormhole Switching

The header flit reserves the output port. Body flits follow immediately. The output port stays reserved for the whole packet. Other packets cannot use that port until the tail flit passes.

```
   Time ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────►

   Router input FIFO:   [H][B][B][B][B][B][B][B][T]
                         │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │  │
                         ▼  ▼  ▼  ▼  ▼  ▼  ▼  ▼  ▼
   Router output port:  [H][B][B][B][B][B][B][B][T]
                         (reserved for this packet throughout)
```

### 9.2 Why Wormhole

Wormhole avoids storing the whole packet at each hop. Each router needs buffers for only a few flits, not the whole packet. This keeps area low.

The cost: a long packet can block other packets from using the output port. This is why virtual channels exist — they let other packets use a different logical channel while the long packet occupies one.

### 9.3 Virtual Channel Assignment Per Packet

| Message | VC | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GETS, GETM, DMA_READ, DMA_WRITE | VC 0 | Low |
| FWD_GETS, FWD_GETM | VC 1 | Medium |
| DATA, DATAFWD, INVACK, UNBLOCK, DMA_ACK | VC 2 | High |
| INV | VC 3 | High |

A packet stays on one VC for its entire journey. The VC is assigned at the source based on message type.

### 9.4 Head, Body, Tail Flow

```
   Source NIC                Router 0            Router 1            Dest NIC
   ──────────                ────────            ────────            ────────
   H ────────────────────►   [H] in FIFO
                             route_comp(H)
                             xbar reserves out
   B ────────────────────►   [B] follows H
   B ────────────────────►   [B] follows H
   ...                                                              H arrives
   T ────────────────────►   [T] follows H    ──►  ...  ──►  T arrives
                                                                     packet complete
```

The destination NIC knows the packet is complete when it sees the tail bit (T=1).

## 10. Flit Flow Through the Router

Each router has per-port input FIFOs, one per VC. For 4 VCs and 5 ports, that is 20 FIFOs per router.

```
   Port LOCAL:
     ┌──────────┐
     │ VC0 FIFO │──┐
     ├──────────┤  │
     │ VC1 FIFO │──┤
     ├──────────┤  ├──► route_comp ──► sw_ctrl ──► xbar
     │ VC2 FIFO │──┤
     ├──────────┤  │
     │ VC3 FIFO │──┘
     └──────────┘

   Port NORTH:
     ┌──────────┐
     │ VC0 FIFO │──┐
     ├──────────┤  │
     │ VC1 FIFO │──┤
     ├──────────┤  ├──► route_comp ──► sw_ctrl ──► xbar
     │ VC2 FIFO │──┤
     ├──────────┤  │
     │ VC3 FIFO │──┘
     └──────────┘

   ... (same for SOUTH, EAST, WEST)
```

The arbiter decides which input and which VC gets the output port this cycle.

## 11. Flit Buffering Depth

| FIFO | Depth | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Per VC per input port | 4 flits | Enough for one cache line transfer to make progress |
| NIC TX FIFO | 8 flits | Decouples cache controller from network |
| NIC RX FIFO | 8 flits | Absorbs incoming packet while cache is busy |

Total per router: 5 ports × 4 VCs × 4 flits × 64 bits = 5120 bits = 640 bytes. Times 16 routers (4×4) = 10 KB of flit storage. Manageable.

## 12. Credit-Based Flow Control

Each VC has a credit counter. Credits represent free slots in the downstream FIFO.

```
   Router A sends flit
         │
         ▼
   Credit counter for VC_x at Router A:
     credits_remaining -= 1
         │
         ▼
   Router B receives flit, writes to VC_x FIFO
         │
         ▼
   Router B sends credit back to Router A
         │
         ▼
   Credit counter for VC_x at Router A:
     credits_remaining += 1
```

Router A can send only when `credits_remaining > 0`. This prevents overflow. No valid/ready handshake needed on the forward path.

Credit return latency: 1 cycle per hop. The FIFO depth must be at least the round-trip latency in flits. With depth 4, Router A can have up to 4 flits in flight to Router B.

## 13. Why This Flit Layout Works

| Requirement | How the layout meets it |
| --- | --- |
| Carry 64-byte cache lines | 8 body flits + 1 header = 9 flits |
| Support 4×4 to 8×8 mesh | 8-bit tile IDs |
| Support 4 VC classes | 2-bit VC ID |
| Support 14 messages | 4-bit msg type |
| Support single-flit control | H and T bits |
| Support wormhole switching | H, T, and packet length in header |
| Support exclusive grant | E bit in header |
| Support write-back | W bit in header |
| Fit FPGA BRAMs | 64-bit width |

## Summary

A flit is a fixed-width unit of a packet. For example, if your NoC uses 32-bit flits, every flit is 32 bits wide, regardless of whether it carries a header, address, or data.

Imagine Core 0 wants to send a message to an L2 cache bank. The message needs to tell the network where it is coming from, where it should go, and what information it carries.

A flit is like a small parcel with different sections:

- Header: Tells the router where the parcel should go.
- Body: Carries the actual information, such as an address or data.
- Tail: Tells the router that the packet has ended.

### How does a multi-flit packet work?

Suppose you want to send a large parcel, but it is too big to carry in one piece. You divide it into smaller parcels and send them in order.

The flit layout defines what information each small parcel contains, while multi-flit packet structure explains how multiple parcels are arranged to send one complete message.

# Router Ports, Arbitration, and Deadlock Avoidance

## 1. Router Port Count

### 1.1 In a 4×4 Mesh

Every tile has one router. The port count depends on the tile's position.

| Position | Count | Ports |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Interior | 4 tiles | Local, North, South, East, West (5 ports) |
| Edge (not corner) | 8 tiles | Local plus 3 mesh directions (4 ports) |
| Corner | 4 tiles | Local plus 2 mesh directions (3 ports) |

### 1.2 The Parameterised Choice

The RTL uses a **single 5-port router module** for all tile positions. Unused ports are tied off.

Why one module instead of three:

| Approach | Pros | Cons |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Three modules (5-port, 4-port, 3-port) | Smaller area at the edges | Three verifications, three synthesis paths |
| **One 5-port module, tie off unused ports** | **One verification, one synthesis path** | Slightly more logic per edge tile; synthesis prunes tied-off logic |

The synthesis tool removes tied-off logic. The area difference is negligible. The verification effort is halved.

**Decision:** Parameterised 5-port router. Ports not used by the tile's position are tied to inactive.

### 1.3 Port Definition

```
   ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │                     ROUTER                          │
   │                                                     │
   │   LOCAL  ──────►┌─────────────────┐                 │
   │                 │                 │                 │
   │   NORTH  ──────►│                 │──────► LOCAL    │
   │                 │   5×5 crossbar  │                 │
   │   SOUTH  ──────►│   with per-VC   │──────► NORTH    │
   │                 │   arbitration   │                 │
   │   EAST   ──────►│                 │──────► SOUTH    │
   │                 │                 │                 │
   │   WEST   ──────►│                 │──────► EAST     │
   │                 │                 │                 │
   │                 └─────────────────┘──────► WEST     │
   │                                                     │
   └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

Five inputs, five outputs. Each input has 4 VCs. Each VC has its own FIFO.

## 2. Input Buffering

Each input port has **four FIFOs**, one per virtual channel.

```
   Port LOCAL:
     ┌──────────┐
     │ VC0 FIFO │  depth 4, 64 bits wide
     ├──────────┤
     │ VC1 FIFO │  depth 4
     ├──────────┤
     │ VC2 FIFO │  depth 4
     ├──────────┤
     │ VC3 FIFO │  depth 4
     └──────────┘

   Same structure on NORTH, SOUTH, EAST, WEST.
```

Total FIFOs per router: 5 ports × 4 VCs = 20.

Total flit slots: 20 FIFOs × 4 flits = 80 flits per router.

For a 4×4 mesh: 16 routers × 80 flits × 64 bits = 81,920 bits = 10 KB of flit storage.

### 2.1 Why Four VCs

Four VCs match four message classes:

| VC | Message Class | Messages |
| --- | --- | --- |
| VC0 | Requests | GETS, GETM, DMA_READ, DMA_WRITE |
| VC1 | Forwarded requests | FWD_GETS, FWD_GETM |
| VC2 | Responses | DATA, DATAFWD, INVACK, UNBLOCK, DMA_ACK |
| VC3 | Invalidations | INV |

The separation is essential for deadlock avoidance. See Section 6.

### 2.2 FIFO Depth of Four

Why 4, not 2 or 8:

| Depth | Effect |
| --- | --- |
| 2 | Credit round-trip stalls the sender. Bandwidth drops. |
| **4** | **Covers credit return latency. Full bandwidth. Low area.** |
| 8 | More area. Marginal benefit for this tile count. |

The credit round-trip is the time for a credit to travel from downstream back to upstream. On a mesh with 1-cycle links, that is 1 cycle. Depth 4 gives headroom for arbitration stalls.

## 3. Arbitration

Arbitration decides who gets a shared resource this cycle. There are **two arbitration stages** in the router:

1. **VC arbitration** within one input port — which VC of this input gets to request an output
2. **Switch arbitration** across input ports — which input port gets the output port this cycle

A third stage sits between them: **route computation**, which decides which output port each VC wants.

### 3.1 Route Computation (Not Arbitration)

For each input VC at the head of its FIFO, the router computes the requested output port.

```
   flit.dest ──► route_comp ──► requested_output
```

For a mesh with XY routing:

```
   if flit.dest.x > my_x: requested = EAST
   if flit.dest.x < my_x: requested = WEST
   if flit.dest.x == my_x and flit.dest.y > my_y: requested = NORTH
   if flit.dest.x == my_x and flit.dest.y < my_y: requested = SOUTH
   if flit.dest == my_tile: requested = LOCAL
```

Route computation is combinational. It produces one output port per requesting VC.

### 3.2 VC Arbitration (Per Input Port)

Each input port has up to 4 VCs with head flits wanting to go out. Only one VC can present a request to the switch arbiter per cycle.

**Policy:** Fixed priority by VC number.

| Priority | VC |
| --- | --- |
| Highest | VC2 (responses) |
| High | VC3 (invalidations) |
| Medium | VC1 (forwarded requests) |
| Lowest | VC0 (requests) |

Why this priority:

- Responses unblock waiting caches. They should not wait behind requests.
- Invalidations must reach sharers before the requester can be granted exclusive ownership.
- Forwarded requests are urgent but not as urgent as responses.
- Requests are the most numerous. They can wait.

**Alternative:** Round-robin VC arbitration. Fairer, but responses could be delayed by requests. Fixed priority by class is the standard choice for coherence NoCs.

### 3.3 Switch Arbitration (Across Input Ports)

For each output port, up to 5 inputs may request it. The switch arbiter picks one.

**Policy:** Round-robin across input ports.

Why round-robin:

- Prevents starvation of any input port
- The original design used fixed priority (LOCAL > CW > CCW), which caused starvation
- Round-robin is the standard fix

**Per output port arbitration:**

```
   Output port EAST:
     Input LOCAL wants EAST?  ──┐
     Input NORTH wants EAST?  ──┤
     Input SOUTH wants EAST?  ──┼──► round-robin arbiter ──► winner
     Input EAST wants EAST?   ──┤
     Input WEST wants EAST?   ──┘
```

Each output port has its own arbiter. Five arbiters per router.

### 3.4 Combined Arbitration

A VC wins an output port only if:

1. It wins VC arbitration within its input port
2. It wins switch arbitration across input ports for that output port

If either stage denies, the flit stays in its FIFO. It tries again next cycle.

### 3.5 Hold, Not Drop

A losing flit is **never dropped**. It waits in its FIFO. The FIFO keeps the flit until it wins arbitration and the downstream FIFO has a credit available.

This corrects the original design's possible drop-on-contention bug.

### 3.6 Arbitration Example

Cycle N:

```
   Input LOCAL, VC2 head flit wants EAST
   Input NORTH, VC0 head flit wants EAST
   Input SOUTH, VC2 head flit wants LOCAL
   Input EAST, VC0 head flit wants WEST
   Input WEST, VC1 head flit wants EAST
```

Step 1: VC arbitration per input.

| Input | VCs requesting | Winner | Output requested |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| LOCAL | VC2 | VC2 | EAST |
| NORTH | VC0 | VC0 | EAST |
| SOUTH | VC2 | VC2 | LOCAL |
| EAST | VC0 | VC0 | WEST |
| WEST | VC1 | VC1 | EAST |

Step 2: Switch arbitration per output.

| Output | Inputs requesting | Round-robin state | Winner |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| EAST | LOCAL, NORTH, WEST | pointer at LOCAL | LOCAL |
| LOCAL | SOUTH | only SOUTH | SOUTH |
| WEST | EAST | only EAST | EAST |

Three flits move this cycle:

- LOCAL VC2 → EAST
- SOUTH VC2 → LOCAL
- EAST VC0 → WEST

NORTH and WEST lose. Their flits stay in their FIFOs. They try again next cycle.

The round-robin pointer for EAST advances to NORTH. Next cycle, NORTH gets priority.

## 4. Crossbar

The crossbar is a 5×5 switch. It connects each input to each output. The arbiter sets the select signals.

```
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │                    5×5 CROSSBAR                        │
   │                                                        │
   │   LOCAL ────┬────┬────┬────┬────► LOCAL out            │
   │             │    │    │    │                           │
   │   NORTH ────┼────┼────┼────┼────► NORTH out            │
   │             │    │    │    │                           │
   │   SOUTH ────┼────┼────┼────┼────► SOUTH out            │
   │             │    │    │    │                           │
   │   EAST  ────┼────┼────┼────┼────► EAST out             │
   │             │    │    │    │                           │
   │   WEST  ────┴────┴────┴────┴────► WEST out             │
   │                                                        │
   │   select signals from switch arbiter                   │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

A crossbar allows multiple simultaneous transfers if they use different inputs and different outputs. In the example above, three transfers happen in one cycle.

## 5. Pipeline

The router is pipelined into 3 stages:

| Stage | Function | Cycles |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Buffer write (flit arrives, written to input FIFO) | 1 |
| 2 | Route compute, VC arbitration, switch arbitration, crossbar select | 1 |
| 3 | Crossbar transfer, output register | 1 |

Total latency per hop: 3 cycles.

For a 4×4 mesh, worst-case path is 6 hops = 18 cycles. Plus source and destination NIC overhead, plus L1/L2 controller latency.

## 6. Deadlock Avoidance

Deadlock is when all routers wait on each other and no flit can advance. Three types of deadlock exist in a NoC. Each has a specific countermeasure.

### 6.1 Routing Deadlock

**Cause:** A cyclic dependency in the routing function. Packets wait for each other in a cycle.

**Example:** In a naive mesh with adaptive routing, a packet going east could wait for a packet going north, which waits for a packet going west, which waits for a packet going east again. Cycle.

**Countermeasure:** XY deterministic routing.

XY routing imposes a strict order: first all X movement, then all Y movement. This creates a partial order on the channels. No cycle can form. XY is provably deadlock-free for routing.

```
   Packet path:
     (0,0) ──► (1,0) ──► (2,0) ──► (2,1) ──► (2,2)
             east      east      north     north
     All X moves happen before any Y move.
```

This is the primary routing deadlock countermeasure.

### 6.2 Protocol Deadlock

**Cause:** A cyclic dependency between coherence messages. A GETS waits for a DATA, which waits for a FWD_GETS, which waits for a GETS.

**Example:** In a naive single-channel network, a response packet could be stuck behind a request packet that is waiting for that response. Cycle.

**Countermeasure:** Virtual channel separation by message class.

The four VCs are assigned distinct classes:

| VC | Class |
| --- | --- |
| VC0 | Requests |
| VC1 | Forwarded requests |
| VC2 | Responses |
| VC3 | Invalidations |

The channel dependency graph is now partitioned by class. A response never waits behind a request because they use different VCs. This breaks the cycle.

But VC separation alone is not sufficient. The dependencies between classes must also be acyclic:

```
   Requests (VC0) ──► Responses (VC2)      ✓
   Requests (VC0) ──► Invalidations (VC3)  ✓
   Forwarded (VC1) ──► Responses (VC2)     ✓
   Invalidations (VC3) ──► Responses (VC2) ✓
   Responses never depend on requests      ✓
   Invalidations never depend on requests  ✓
```

The dependency graph is a DAG. No cycle. Protocol deadlock is prevented.

### 6.3 Network Deadlock (Resource Deadlock)

**Cause:** All input FIFOs fill with flits that cannot advance because the downstream FIFOs are full. Classic buffer deadlock.

**Countermeasure:** Credit-based flow control.

Each VC has a credit counter. The sender sends a flit only when a credit is available. When the downstream FIFO drains a flit, it returns a credit. The sender can then send another flit.

Credits prevent overflow. They do not by themselves prevent deadlock if the routing or protocol has cycles. But combined with XY routing (no routing cycles) and VC separation (no protocol cycles), credit-based flow control ensures that flits always have somewhere to go.

### 6.4 Combined Guarantee

Deadlock freedom relies on three things together:

| Deadlock Type | Countermeasure | Why it works |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Routing | XY deterministic | No cycle in channel dependency graph |
| Protocol | 4 VCs by class | No cycle in message dependency graph |
| Resource | Credit-based flow control | No buffer overflow; no head-of-line blocking |

Any one alone is insufficient. XY routing without VCs can deadlock at the protocol level. VCs without credit flow control can deadlock at the buffer level. Credits without XY can deadlock at the routing level.

The three together are provably sufficient.

### 6.5 Head-of-Line Blocking

Head-of-line (HOL) blocking is a related problem. It is not deadlock, but it hurts performance.

**Cause:** A blocked flit at the head of a FIFO prevents flits behind it from advancing, even if they could.

**Example:** The head of VC0 FIFO is a GETS whose destination output port is busy. Behind it, a GETM wants a different output port that is free. The GETM cannot advance.

**Countermeasure:** VC separation. Since each class has its own FIFO, a blocked request does not block a response.

HOL blocking still occurs within a VC class. Two requests to different destinations in the same VC FIFO can block each other. This is acceptable for the coherence message sizes used here.

## 7. Flow Control Details

### 7.1 Credit Initialization

On reset, each sender's credit counter for each VC is initialized to the depth of the downstream FIFO (4).

### 7.2 Credit Consumption

Sender transmits one flit on VCx:

```
   credits[VCx] -= 1
```

### 7.3 Credit Return

Downstream FIFO drains one flit of VCx (because it moved to the crossbar and out):

```
   downstream sends credit back to sender
   sender's credits[VCx] += 1
```

### 7.4 Credit Latency

A credit takes 1 cycle to travel one hop. With depth-4 FIFOs, the sender can have up to 4 flits in flight before stalling. This covers the credit round-trip for a single link. Under contention, the sender may stall more often, but no data is lost.

### 7.5 Credit Counter Width

Depth 4 requires 3 bits to represent values 0 through 4. So each credit counter is 3 bits.

Four VCs × 5 ports = 20 credit counters per router. Each 3 bits. Total: 60 bits per router. Negligible.

## 8. Virtual Channel Flow

A packet uses one VC for its entire journey. The VC is assigned at the source NIC based on message type. The router does not change the VC of a packet in flight.

```
   Source NIC              Router 0           Router 1           Dest NIC
   ──────────              ────────           ────────           ────────
   GETS packet:
     VC = 0
     H, T = 1,1
     ──────────────────►   VC0 FIFO
                           route_comp
                           sw_ctrl
                           xbar
                           ──────────────────►  VC0 FIFO
                                               route_comp
                                               sw_ctrl
                                               xbar
                                               ──────────────────►  VC0
                                                                   packet decoded
```

The VC assignment is carried in the flit header (`vc` field, 2 bits). The router reads it and writes the flit to the correct input FIFO of the next router.

## 9. Arbitration Fairness

Round-robin across input ports guarantees fairness between input ports. Fixed priority across VCs guarantees that responses and invalidations always get through.

This combination is important. If both stages used round-robin, a flood of requests could delay responses indefinitely at the input level. If both used fixed priority, some input ports could starve.

The design uses:

- **Round-robin across inputs** → no input port starves
- **Fixed priority across VCs** → protocol-critical messages always get through

This is the standard design point for coherence NoCs.

## 10. Router Summary

| Aspect | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Ports | 5 (LOCAL, N, S, E, W) |
| Ports per tile | 3 to 5 depending on position |
| VCs per port | 4 |
| FIFO depth | 4 flits |
| FIFO width | 64 bits |
| Credit counter | 3 bits per VC per port |
| Route | XY deterministic |
| VC arbitration | Fixed priority (response > invalidation > forward > request) |
| Switch arbitration | Round-robin across input ports |
| Crossbar | 5×5, non-blocking |
| Pipeline | 3 stages (buffer, arbitrate, transfer) |
| Hop latency | 3 cycles |
| Deadlock avoidance | XY routing + 4 VCs + credit flow control |
| HOL blocking | Eliminated between VCs; tolerable within a VC |

## Summary

think of a router as a traffic police officer. It directs data from one tile to another, decides which data gets to move first, and prevents traffic from getting stuck.

we use  5 ports

- North, South, East and West: Connect to neighbouring routers.
- Local: Connects the router to its own tile, such as a processor core or cache interface.

Corner and edge routers may have fewer than four physical neighbour connections because some directions lead outside the mesh.

Arbitration decides which request gets permission first.

Common arbitration methods include:

- Fixed priority: A particular input always has higher priority. Simple, but low-priority traffic can wait too long.
- Round-robin: Priority rotates between requesting inputs, helping prevent starvation.
- Weighted round-robin: Some inputs receive more turns than others.

we are using round robin arbitation method 

## 3. How is deadlock avoided?

Deadlock is like a traffic jam where every vehicle is waiting for another vehicle to move, so nobody can proceed.

# Memory Model Behind the Last-Level Cache

## 1. What "Memory Model" Means Here

The memory model is the structure and behavior of the backing store that sits behind the L2 cache. When an L2 bank misses, it sends a request to this backing store. The backing store returns the data. When an L2 bank evicts a dirty line, it writes the line back to this backing store.

In this design, the backing store is called `mem_tap`. It has two roles:

1. It answers memory requests from the L2 bank.
2. It passes non-memory packets to and from the NIC.

`mem_tap` is not a real DRAM controller. It is a behavioral model. It is designed for simulation. It can be replaced by a real memory controller in the future.

## 2. Where It Sits

```
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │                       TILE                           │
   │                                                      │
   │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐        │
   │  │ L1 CACHE │───►│   MUX    │───►│ L2 BANK  │        │
   │  └──────────┘    └────┬─────┘    └────┬─────┘        │
   │                       │               │              │
   │                       ▼               ▼              │
   │                  ┌──────────────────────────┐        │
   │                  │        MEM_TAP           │        │
   │                  │                          │        │
   │                  │  ┌────────────────────┐  │        │
   │                  │  │  Memory array      │  │        │
   │                  │  │  256 MB behavioral │  │        │
   │                  │  └────────────────────┘  │        │
   │                  │                          │        │
   │                  │  ┌────────────────────┐  │        │
   │                  │  │  NIC gateway       │  │        │
   │                  │  │  (pass-through)    │  │        │
   │                  │  └────────────────────┘  │        │
   │                  └──────────┬───────────────┘        │
   │                             │                        │
   │                             ▼                        │
   │                      ┌──────────┐                    │
   │                      │   NIC    │                    │
   │                      └──────────┘                    │
   │                                                      │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The `mem_tap` sits between the L2 bank / MUX and the NIC. It has one path to the L2 bank (for memory requests) and one path to the NIC (for all other packets).

## 3. Address Space

The address space is 32 bits. That is 4 GB total.

The address space is divided into regions:

| Region | Address Range | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Memory | 0x0000_0000 – 0x0FFF_FFFF | 256 MB of backing store |
| Unmapped | 0x1000_0000 – 0xEFFF_FFFF | Reserved |
| DMA registers | 0xF000_0000 – 0xF000_FFFF | DMA control (if DMA in scope) |
| Memory-mapped I/O | 0xF001_0000 – 0xFFFF_FFFF | Future peripherals |

For the white paper's baseline, only the memory region is used.

## 4. Why One `mem_tap` per Tile

Each tile has its own `mem_tap`. This is a modeling choice, not an architectural requirement.

**Reason:** The `mem_tap` models the memory controller's local behavior. In a real chip, there is one memory controller. It may be attached to one tile. Or it may be a separate block.

For simulation, having one `mem_tap` per tile is convenient:

- Each tile can answer its own memory requests without going across the NoC.
- The coherence protocol can be exercised without needing a shared memory model.
- The simulation is faster because there is no central bottleneck.

**The catch:** With one `mem_tap` per tile, the memory contents are not automatically shared. If tile 0 writes address 0x40 to its `mem_tap`, tile 1's `mem_tap` does not see the write.

**How it is fixed:** The coherence protocol ensures that only one tile holds a dirty copy at a time. When that tile writes back, it writes to its own `mem_tap`. Later reads go to the home tile's `mem_tap`, which has the updated value.

This works because the home tile is determined by the address. The same address always maps to the same tile. So the same `mem_tap` always holds the authoritative copy.

**In a real chip:** A single shared memory controller replaces the per-tile `mem_tap`. The address routing already maps addresses to home tiles, so the change is localized.

## 5. Behavioral Memory Model

The `mem_tap` models the memory array as a simple 2D array:

```
logic [7:0] mem [0:268435455];  // 256 MB
```

That is 256 million bytes. In simulation, this is allocated as a sparse array. Only touched addresses consume memory.

The model has these properties:

| Property | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Width | 8 bits (one byte per entry) |
| Depth | 256M entries |
| Access latency | 1 cycle for read, 1 cycle for write |
| Ports | 1 read, 1 write |
| Refresh | None |
| ECC | None |
| Timing | Ideal (no DRAM timing) |

This is not a DRAM model. A real DRAM has row activation, column access, refresh cycles, and bank conflicts. The `mem_tap` ignores all of these. The goal is to exercise coherence, not to model DRAM timing.

## 6. Memory Request Path

### 6.1 Read Miss in L2

1. L2 controller misses on the directory lookup.
2. L2 controller builds a memory request packet:
    - `msg = MEM_REQ`
    - `we = 0` (read)
    - `addr = line address`
3. The packet goes to `mem_tap` via the local MUX.
4. `mem_tap` decodes the address. If the address is in the memory region, it services the request from its memory array. If not, it passes the packet to the NIC.
5. `mem_tap` returns a memory response packet with 64 bytes of data.
6. The L2 controller receives the response, installs the line, and updates the directory.

### 6.2 Write-Back from L2

1. L2 controller evicts a dirty line.
2. L2 controller builds a memory request packet:
    - `msg = MEM_REQ`
    - `we = 1` (write)
    - `addr = line address`
    - `data = 64 bytes`
3. The packet goes to `mem_tap`.
4. `mem_tap` writes the 64 bytes into its memory array.
5. `mem_tap` returns an acknowledgement.

### 6.3 Timing

| Operation | Cycles |
| --- | --- |
| L2 controller builds packet | 2 |
| Packet to mem_tap | 1 |
| mem_tap read or write | 1 |
| Response to L2 controller | 1 |
| Total round trip | 5 cycles |

This is the latency the L2 controller must wait for a memory access. It is much smaller than a real DRAM access (which would be 50–200 cycles).

For the white paper, this is fine. The coherence protocol is what matters, not DRAM timing.

## 7. Memory Preload

For testing, the memory can be preloaded. This lets the testbench initialize known values before the simulation starts.

```
task preload(input logic [31:0] addr, input logic [63:0] data);
  for (int i = 0; i < 8; i++) begin
    mem[addr + i] = data[i*8 +: 8];
  end
endtask
```

The testbench calls `preload` for each test. This replaces a separate initialization file.

**Alternative:** Read initialization from a file (e.g., a hex file). This is more scalable for large preloads. The white paper uses the task-based approach for simplicity.

## 8. Consistency of the Memory Model

The memory model provides **coherence**, not **consistency**.

**Coherence** is what we provide. It ensures that all caches see the same value for a given address. Every read returns the value of the last write to that address, as observed by the coherence protocol.

**Consistency** is a stronger property. It concerns the ordering of accesses from different cores. A consistency model (e.g., sequential consistency, TSO, release consistency) defines which interleavings are legal.

This design does not provide a consistency model. It provides coherence only. A program running on this architecture with multiple cores would need explicit synchronization (locks, fences) to achieve consistency.

For the white paper, this is acceptable. The scope is coherence, not consistency.

**Future scope:** Add a consistency model (e.g., release consistency with acquire/release fences). This requires core-level support (fence instructions) and memory ordering logic, which is beyond the current scope.

## 9. Memory Side Effects

The `mem_tap` supports two side effects beyond simple read and write:

### 9.1 Memory-Mapped Registers

Addresses in a specific range (e.g., 0xF000_0000) are treated as memory-mapped registers. Writes to these addresses trigger actions (e.g., DMA start). Reads return status.

For the white paper's baseline, this is not implemented. The DMA engine (under evaluation) would add this.

### 9.2 Special Addresses

Addresses at the top of the memory region (e.g., 0xFFFF_FF00) can be reserved for special purposes:

| Address | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| 0xFFFF_FF00 | Test pattern: returns 0xDEADBEEF |
| 0xFFFF_FF04 | Test pattern: returns the address itself |
| 0xFFFF_FF08 | Test pattern: returns a counter |

These special addresses let the testbench verify that the memory path works without needing to preload large data.

## 10. What the Memory Model Does NOT Provide

| Feature | Status |
| --- | --- |
| DRAM timing (row activation, refresh) | Not modeled |
| ECC | Not modeled |
| Memory controller arbitration | Not modeled (single port) |
| Bank interleaving | Not modeled |
| Prefetching | Not modeled |
| Memory-side caches | Not modeled |
| Non-volatile memory | Not modeled |
| Memory ordering (consistency) | Not modeled |
| Address translation (MMU) | Not modeled |

These are all future scope. The current model is a simple 2D array. It answers reads and writes in 1 cycle. It provides enough behavior to exercise the coherence protocol.

## 11. Memory Model Parameters (White Paper Baseline)

| Parameter | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Address width | 32 bits (4 GB) |
| Addressable memory | 256 MB |
| Data width | 8 bits per byte, 64 bytes per line |
| Access latency | 1 cycle |
| Memory instances | One per tile (16 for 4×4) |
| Shared across tiles | No, each tile has its own |
| Coherence for memory | Yes, via the directory protocol |
| Consistency model | None |
| Preload support | Task-based |

## 12. Why This Design

| Alternative | Why not |
| --- | --- |
| Single shared memory for all tiles | Requires a central arbitration point. Slower simulation. More complex. |
| Real DRAM model (e.g., Ramulator, DRAMSim) | Adds weeks of integration effort. Not needed to verify coherence. |
| AXI-based memory | Adds protocol complexity. The NoC already carries packets. |
| File-backed memory | Slower than in-memory array. Useful for very large memories, not needed here. |
| Cache-coherent memory controller | The memory controller does not need to participate in coherence. It is the backing store. |

The per-tile behavioral array is the simplest model that supports coherent reads and writes. It provides the right behavior for the coherence protocol. It is fast enough to simulate 4×4 configurations.

## 13. Future Scope: Real Memory

For ASIC tapeout or larger FPGA designs, the memory model would be replaced by:

1. **A single shared memory controller** with a standard interface (AXI, TileLink).
2. **A DRAM model** for timing accuracy (e.g., Ramulator).
3. **An ECC engine** for data integrity.
4. **Memory-side caches** for reducing traffic.
5. **A consistency model** with fences and atomics.

None of these are needed for the white paper. The behavioral array is sufficient to verify coherence.

## Summary

When a core requests data:

1. It checks its private L1 cache.
2. If the data is missing, the request goes to the shared LLC.
3. If the LLC also misses, the system fetches the data from DRAM.
4. The data returns to the requesting core, with cache metadata updated as required.

## What happens when multiple cores access the same data?

Suppose Core 0 and Core 1 both access variable `X`.

- Core 0 reads `X` and gets a copy in its cache.
- Core 1 also reads `X` and gets a copy.
- Core 0 then wants to modify `X`.
- The coherence protocol coordinates the copies, invalidating or updating other copies as required.

Cache coherence and memory consistency are different:

- Cache coherence governs the visibility and ordering of operations to the same memory location.
- Memory consistency governs the ordering of memory operations across different locations as observed by different cores.


# Core Interface with the L1 Cache

## 1. What the Core Is in This Design

There is **no processor core RTL** in this design. The core is a **testbench stimulus generator**. It issues load, store, and flush instructions to the L1 cache. It does not have a pipeline, an ALU, a register file, or a program counter.

This is a deliberate scoping decision. The project is about cache coherence and the Network-on-Chip, not about processor microarchitecture. A real core would add pipeline hazards, branch prediction, and instruction fetch — none of which are needed to exercise the coherence protocol.

The testbench can later be replaced by a real core. The interface is designed so that a real core can drive it without modification.

## 2. The Core Interface Signals

The core connects to the L1 cache through a simple request-response interface. The interface is intentionally narrow and synchronous.

### 2.1 Request Signals (Core → L1)

| Signal | Width | Direction | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `core_req_valid` | 1 | Core → L1 | The core has a new request |
| `core_req_op` | 2 | Core → L1 | Operation: 00 idle, 01 load, 10 store, 11 flush |
| `core_req_addr` | 32 | Core → L1 | Byte address of the request |
| `core_req_wdata` | 64 | Core → L1 | Write data (valid for store) |
| `core_req_ready` | 1 | L1 → Core | L1 can accept the request this cycle |

### 2.2 Response Signals (L1 → Core)

| Signal | Width | Direction | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `core_resp_valid` | 1 | L1 → Core | A response is available |
| `core_resp_rdata` | 64 | L1 → Core | Read data (valid for load) |
| `core_resp_hit` | 1 | L1 → Core | 1 if the request hit, 0 if miss |
| `core_resp_ack` | 1 | L1 → Core | 1 when the transaction is complete |

### 2.3 Handshake

The interface uses a **valid/ready handshake** for requests and a **valid/ack** handshake for responses.

**Request handshake:**

```
   Cycle N:   core_req_valid = 1, core_req_op = LOAD
   Cycle N:   core_req_ready = 1  (L1 accepts)
   Cycle N+1: core_req_valid = 0, core_req_ready = 0
```

The core holds `req_valid` high until `req_ready` is high. On the cycle both are high, the request is accepted.

**Response handshake:**

```
   Cycle M:   core_resp_valid = 1, core_resp_rdata = data
   Cycle M:   core_resp_ack = 1
   Cycle M+1: core_resp_valid = 0, core_resp_ack = 0
```

The L1 holds `resp_valid` high until the core samples it. The `resp_ack` bit signals completion.

## 3. Operation Encoding

The `core_req_op` field has 2 bits, encoding four operations:

| Code | Operation | Address used | Write data used | Read data returned |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 00 | Idle | No | No | No |
| 01 | Load | Yes | No | Yes |
| 10 | Store | Yes | Yes | No |
| 11 | Flush | Yes | No | No |

**Load:** The core reads one 64-byte cache line from the L1. If the line is present, the read data returns in a few cycles. If not, the L1 issues a coherence request, and the read data returns after the transaction completes.

**Store:** The core writes one 64-byte cache line to the L1. If the line is present in a writable state (M or E), the write happens immediately. If not, the L1 issues a coherence request, and the write happens after the transaction completes.

**Flush:** The core forces the L1 to write back the line and invalidate it. This is used for testing and for DMA coherence. Flush is not a standard processor instruction — it is a testbench convenience.

## 4. The Request Path

When the core issues a load or store, the L1 controller processes it through a fixed sequence of states.

### 4.1 L1 Controller States

| State | Meaning | Duration |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `IDLE` | No request in progress | Until a request arrives |
| `COMPARE` | Tag lookup and hit/miss decision | 1 cycle |
| `ALLOCATE` | Victim way reserved, waiting for data | Until data arrives |
| `INITIATE_BACK` | Issuing a write-back for the victim | 1–2 cycles |
| `SNOOP` | Handling a coherence request from another tile | Variable |
| `DONE` | Transaction complete, response to core | 1 cycle |

### 4.2 Load Hit

```
   IDLE ──► COMPARE ──► DONE
```

The tag lookup finds the line in the M, E, or S state. The L1 returns the data. The transaction completes in 3 cycles.

### 4.3 Load Miss

```
   IDLE ──► COMPARE ──► ALLOCATE ──► (send GETS) ──► (wait for DATA) ──► DONE
```

The tag lookup finds no match. The L1 reserves a victim way. It sends a `GETS` message to the home L2 bank. It waits for the data. When the data arrives, it installs the line and returns the data to the core. The transaction completes in 10–50 cycles, depending on the NoC path.

### 4.4 Store Hit in M or E

```
   IDLE ──► COMPARE ──► DONE
```

The tag lookup finds the line in M or E. The L1 writes the data. The transaction completes in 3 cycles.

If the line was in E, the state silently upgrades to M. No network message is sent. This is the silent E→M upgrade.

### 4.5 Store Hit in S

```
   IDLE ──► COMPARE ──► SNOOP ──► (send GETM) ──► (wait for DATA) ──► DONE
```

The tag lookup finds the line in S. The L1 cannot write directly because other tiles hold copies. It sends a `GETM` message to the home L2 bank to obtain exclusive ownership. It waits for the reply. When the reply arrives (with `exclusive=1`), the L1 writes the data and upgrades to M.

### 4.6 Store Miss

```
   IDLE ──► COMPARE ──► ALLOCATE ──► (send GETM) ──► (wait for DATA) ──► DONE
```

The tag lookup finds no match. The L1 reserves a victim way. It sends a `GETM` message. It waits for the reply. When the reply arrives, it installs the line as M and writes the data.

### 4.7 Flush

```
   IDLE ──► COMPARE ──► INITIATE_BACK ──► (send PUTS or PUTM) ──► DONE
```

The tag lookup finds the line. The L1 writes back the line if it is dirty. It invalidates the line locally. The transaction completes.

## 5. The Response Path

The L1 returns a response to the core when the transaction completes. The response is a single-cycle event.

| Field | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `core_resp_valid` | 1 when a response is ready |
| `core_resp_rdata` | 64 bytes of read data (for load) |
| `core_resp_hit` | 1 if the request hit, 0 if miss |
| `core_resp_ack` | 1 to acknowledge completion |

The core samples these signals on the cycle `core_resp_valid` is high. It uses `core_resp_hit` to record whether the request hit or missed. It uses `core_resp_rdata` for load results.

For a store, `core_resp_rdata` is undefined. The `core_resp_ack` bit is what matters.

## 6. Blocking Behavior

The L1 is a **blocking cache**. It handles one request at a time. While a miss is in flight, the L1 does not accept a new request from the core.

**Consequence:** `core_req_ready` is low during a miss. The core stalls.

This is a simplification. A real processor would continue executing independent instructions while the miss is in flight (out-of-order execution). The blocking behavior is fine for a coherence testbench, but it limits throughput.

**Future scope:** Add MSHRs (miss status handling registers) to make the L1 non-blocking. This allows multiple outstanding misses and keeps the core busy.

## 7. Timing Example: Load Miss

```
   Cycle 0:   core_req_valid = 1, op = LOAD, addr = 0x1000
              core_req_ready = 1
              L1 state: IDLE → COMPARE

   Cycle 1:   core_req_valid = 0
              core_req_ready = 0
              L1 state: COMPARE → ALLOCATE
              L1 sends GETS to MUX → NIC → Router → home L2

   Cycle 2–20: L1 waits in ALLOCATE
               NoC carries GETS to home L2
               Home L2 sends data back via NoC

   Cycle 21:  DATA arrives at L1
              L1 installs the line
              L1 state: ALLOCATE → DONE
              core_resp_valid = 1
              core_resp_rdata = data
              core_resp_hit = 0
              core_resp_ack = 1

   Cycle 22:  core_resp_valid = 0
              core_resp_ack = 0
              L1 state: DONE → IDLE

   Cycle 23:  core_req_ready = 1
              L1 ready for next request
```

Total latency: 23 cycles. Most of that is the NoC round trip.

## 8. Core Interface and Coherence

The core does not participate in coherence. It only issues load, store, and flush operations. The L1 controller handles coherence automatically.

From the core's perspective:

- A load always returns a value.
- A store always completes.
- A flush always succeeds.

The core does not know whether the value came from the L1, the L2, or memory. It does not know whether the line was shared, exclusive, or modified. It does not know that another tile was involved.

This is the correct abstraction. Coherence is a cache-level concern, not a core-level concern.

## 9. What the Core Does Not Do

| Feature | Status |
| --- | --- |
| Instruction fetch | No |
| Pipeline | No |
| Register file | No |
| ALU | No |
| Branch prediction | No |
| Out-of-order execution | No |
| Speculative execution | No |
| Memory ordering (fences) | No |
| Atomics (CAS, LL/SC) | No |
| Interrupts | No |
| Exceptions | No |
| Virtual memory (MMU) | No |

All of these are future scope. The current core interface is a stimulus generator for the L1 cache.

## 10. Replacing the Testbench with a Real Core

The interface is designed to accept a real core without modification. To replace the testbench:

1. Connect the core's load/store unit to `core_req_*` and `core_resp_*`.
2. The core issues load, store, and flush operations the same way.
3. The L1 cache behaves identically.
4. The core handles stalls when `core_req_ready` is low.

A small RISC-V core (e.g., Ibex, PicoRV32) would work. The core would add pipeline hazards, but the L1 interface would not change.

**Future scope:** Integrate a RISC-V core and run multithreaded software. This requires a hazard detection unit, a forwarding unit, and a stall controller in the core. None of these are in the current design.

## 11. Interface Summary

| Signal Group | Width | Direction | Protocol |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Request | 99 bits | Core → L1 | valid/ready |
| Response | 67 bits | L1 → Core | valid/ack |

**Request signals:**

| Signal | Width |
| --- | --- |
| `core_req_valid` | 1 |
| `core_req_op` | 2 |
| `core_req_addr` | 32 |
| `core_req_wdata` | 64 |
| `core_req_ready` | 1 |
| **Total** | **100** |

**Response signals:**

| Signal | Width |
| --- | --- |
| `core_resp_valid` | 1 |
| `core_resp_rdata` | 64 |
| `core_resp_hit` | 1 |
| `core_resp_ack` | 1 |
| **Total** | **67** |

## 12. Why This Interface Design

| Choice | Reason |
| --- | --- |
| Valid/ready handshake | Standard on-chip protocol. Compatible with AXI-Stream, TileLink. |
| 2-bit operation code | Four operations (idle, load, store, flush) fit in 2 bits. |
| 32-bit address | Matches the memory model's address width. |
| 64-bit data | Matches the cache line size. One transaction per line. |
| Separate hit signal | The core can count hits and misses for performance measurement. |
| Separate ack signal | The core knows exactly when the transaction is complete. |
| Blocking behavior | Simplest correct behavior. Non-blocking is future scope. |

## Summary

A processor may have separate L1 instruction and data caches:

- L1I: Supplies instructions for execution.
- L1D: Handles loads and stores for program data.

***working***

The core needs to load the value of `x` from memory.

1. Core sends a request. It provides the memory address and indicates that it wants to read data.
2. L1 checks its cache. It compares the requested address against its stored tags.
3. On a cache hit, L1 returns the requested data to the core.
4. On a cache miss, L1 requests the cache line from L2 or the next memory level and waits for the response.
5. Core continues. Once the data arrives, the processor can use it in the calculation.

The L2 bank locates the requested cache line or fetches it from DRAM if necessary. In a coherent multicore design, the request may also require directory checks and communication with other cores.

# DMA Subsystem Design

**White Paper Section — Design Parameter Justification**

---

## 1. Definition

### 1.1 What the DMA Subsystem Is

The DMA subsystem is a hardware engine that transfers data between memory and a device without involving the processor core in every byte. The engine reads a descriptor (source, destination, length), performs the transfer as a series of coherence transactions, and signals completion.

**Location:**

- RTL module: `dma_top.sv`, `dma_channel.sv`, `dma_regs.sv`, `dma_fsm.sv`
- Instantiated as a tile in `tile_array.sv`
- Coherence messages: `DMA_READ`, `DMA_WRITE`, `DMA_ACK` in `coherence_pkg.sv`

### 1.2 Scope of This Section

This section covers six decisions that together define the DMA subsystem:

1. Where the DMA engine lives
2. How it interacts with coherence
3. How many concurrent transfers it supports
4. What transfer patterns it accepts
5. How software programs it
6. What messages it adds to the coherence protocol

### 1.3 Status

**Adopted.** All six decisions are final. The DMA subsystem is in scope for the white paper, the RTL, the verification plan, and the paper submission.

---

## 2. Alternatives

### 2.1 Location Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Central DMA | One engine at a fixed tile; all other tiles send requests to it |
| 2 | Per-tile DMA | One engine per tile; every tile has its own |
| 3 | Device tile | DMA engine lives in its own tile on the mesh |
| 4 | No DMA | Not supported |

### 2.2 Coherence Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Coherent | DMA participates in MESI like a cache |
| 2 | Non-coherent | DMA bypasses caches; software flushes before and after |
| 3 | Streaming | Bulk transfers bypassing caches |
| 4 | IOMMU | Separate translation and coherence layer |

### 2.3 Channel Count Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1 channel | One transfer at a time |
| 2 | 2 channels | Two concurrent transfers |
| 3 | 4 channels | Four concurrent transfers |
| 4 | Parameterised | Channel count set at synthesis time |

### 2.4 Transfer Type Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Single-block | One descriptor, one contiguous region |
| 2 | Scatter-gather | Linked descriptors for non-contiguous regions |
| 3 | Both | Support both modes |

### 2.5 Interface Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Register-mapped | Direct register writes by the core |
| 2 | Memory-mapped | Registers appear in the address space |
| 3 | Doorbell | Descriptor ring in memory; core writes a doorbell |

### 2.6 Message Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No new messages | DMA reuses GETS, GETM, DATA |
| 2 | New DMA messages | DMA_READ, DMA_WRITE, DMA_ACK added |
| 3 | Hybrid | GETS/GETM for coherence; DMA_ACK for completion |

---

## 3. Comparison Matrices

### 3.1 Location Comparison

| Criterion | Central | Per-tile | **Device Tile** | No DMA |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Contradicts tiled philosophy | Yes | No | **No** | N/A |
| Reuses coherence protocol | Partial | Yes | **Yes** | N/A |
| Adds new directory states | Yes | No | **No** | N/A |
| Tile slot cost | None | None | **One** | None |
| Scalability | Poor | Good | **Good** | N/A |
| Area cost | Medium | High (N engines) | **Medium** | Zero |
| Verification cost | Medium | High | **Medium** | Zero |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | No | **Yes** | Yes |
| Publishability | Low | Low | **High** | None |

### 3.2 Coherence Comparison

| Criterion | **Coherent** | Non-coherent | Streaming | IOMMU |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Software flush required | **No** | Yes | Yes | No |
| Directory tracking | **Yes** | No | No | Yes |
| Correctness without flush | **Yes** | No | No | Yes |
| Area cost | **Medium** | Low | Low | High |
| Verification cost | **Medium** | Low | Low | High |
| Publishability | **High** | Low | Low | High |
| Complexity | **Medium** | Low | Low | Very high |
| Fits 3-month timeline | **Yes** | Yes | Yes | No |

### 3.3 Channel Count Comparison

| Criterion | 1 ch | **2 ch** | 4 ch | Parameterised |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Concurrent transfers | 1 | **2** | 4 | N |
| Overlap of transfer and setup | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Area cost | Lowest | **Low** | Medium | Scalable |
| Verification cost | Lowest | **Low** | Medium | Medium |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Maybe | Yes |

### 3.4 Transfer Type Comparison

| Criterion | **Single-block** | Scatter-gather | Both |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Descriptor complexity | **Low** | High | High |
| Descriptor fetch path | **None** | Required | Required |
| Coherence complexity | **Low** | Medium | Medium |
| Area cost | **Low** | Medium | Medium |
| Verification cost | **Low** | Medium | Medium |
| Fits 3-month timeline | **Yes** | Maybe | No |

### 3.5 Interface Comparison

| Criterion | Register | **Memory-mapped** | Doorbell |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Core access | Direct wires | **Via stores** | Via store |
| Address space cost | None | **Register window** | Descriptor ring |
| Coherence handling | Manual | **Automatic** | Automatic |
| Standard | Low | **High** | Very high |
| Verification cost | Low | **Low** | Medium |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### 3.6 Message Comparison

| Criterion | No new | **New (3)** | Hybrid |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Message set size | 11 | **14** | 12 |
| Reserved codes after | 5 | **2** | 4 |
| Protocol clarity | Low | **High** | Medium |
| Directory changes | None | **None** | None |
| Coherence tracking | Hack | **Explicit** | Partial |
| Verification cost | Medium | **Medium** | Medium |
| Publishability | Low | **High** | Medium |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

---

## 4. Chosen Option

### 4.1 Chosen Configuration

| Decision | Choice |
| --- | --- |
| Location | **Device tile** |
| Coherence | **Coherent** |
| Channel count | **2** |
| Transfer type | **Single-block** |
| Interface | **Memory-mapped** |
| Messages | **New (3): DMA_READ, DMA_WRITE, DMA_ACK** |

### 4.2 Device Tile Structure

The DMA device tile is structurally similar to a CPU tile. Three components differ.

| Component | CPU Tile | DMA Tile |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Core interface | Testbench stimulus | DMA register file |
| L1 cache | 4 sets × 4 ways | L1-lite, 2 sets × 2 ways |
| L2 bank | Included | Not included |
| Directory array | Included | Not included |
| Memory tap | Included | Not included |
| NIC | Included | Included |
| Router | 5-port | 5-port |
| Channels | 0 | 2 |
| Descriptor registers | 0 | 2 sets |

The DMA tile has no L2 bank and no directory. It is a consumer of coherence, not a provider. The directory in every other tile sees the DMA tile as just another cache.

### 4.3 Message Set Addition

| Code | Message | Class | Direction |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 11 | DMA_READ | Request | DMA → Directory |
| 12 | DMA_WRITE | Request | DMA → Directory |
| 13 | DMA_ACK | Response | Directory → DMA |
| 14–15 | Reserved | — | Future use |

### 4.4 Virtual Channel Assignment

DMA messages use the existing VC classes.

| Message | VC | Class |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DMA_READ | VC 0 | Request |
| DMA_WRITE | VC 0 | Request |
| DMA_ACK | VC 2 | Response |

No new VC is added.

### 4.5 Register Map

The DMA tile exposes six memory-mapped registers per channel.

| Offset | Register | Width | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0x00 | SRC_LO | 32 | Source address |
| 0x04 | DST_LO | 32 | Destination address |
| 0x08 | LEN | 32 | Transfer length in bytes |
| 0x0C | CTRL | 32 | Start, direction, enable |
| 0x10 | STATUS | 32 | Busy, done, error |
| 0x14 | COUNT | 32 | Bytes transferred |

The register window occupies a reserved address range in the memory map.

---

## 5. Why This and Not That

### 5.1 Why Device Tile and Not Central or Per-Tile

**Why not central:**

- A central DMA contradicts the tiled philosophy stated in the white paper's Section 1.3.
- Every DMA request goes to one tile. This creates a hot spot.
- The central tile needs special RTL. This breaks the parameterised design.
- The paper's novelty claim is weakened by a non-tiled design.

**Why not per-tile:**

- One DMA engine per tile multiplies area by N. At 4×4, that is 16 DMA engines. The FPGA budget cannot support this.
- Most tiles never use DMA. The area is wasted.
- Verification must cover N DMA engines. Effort scales with the tile count.

**Why device tile:**

- The DMA engine becomes another tile on the mesh. It has its own L1-lite cache, NIC, and router.
- The tile reuses the existing coherence protocol. No new directory states are needed.
- The design remains parameterised. The DMA tile can be instantiated or omitted at synthesis time.
- Verification covers one DMA tile, not N.
- The design is publishable as a symmetric integration of a non-CPU master into a directory-based coherence protocol.

### 5.2 Why Coherent and Not Non-Coherent or Streaming

**Why not non-coherent:**

- Non-coherent DMA requires software to flush caches before and after every transfer. This is error-prone.
- Correctness depends on software discipline, not hardware. This weakens the verification claim.
- The paper cannot claim hardware coherence for DMA.

**Why not streaming:**

- Streaming DMA is a subset of non-coherent DMA. Same software flush requirement.
- Streaming is useful only for bulk transfers. It is not general-purpose.

**Why not IOMMU:**

- IOMMU adds a translation layer between DMA and memory. This is a new structure, a new protocol, and a new verification target.
- IOMMU is a 6-month addition. It does not fit the 3-month timeline.

**Why coherent:**

- Coherent DMA participates in the existing MESI protocol. No new directory states are needed when the DMA is a device tile.
- The directory tracks the DMA tile's copies like any other cache.
- Software does not need to flush. Correctness is guaranteed by hardware.
- Coherent DMA is publishable.

### 5.3 Why 2 Channels and Not 1 or 4

**Why not 1 channel:**

- A single channel serializes all DMA transfers. The engine becomes a bottleneck.
- The design cannot demonstrate overlap of transfer and setup.

**Why not 4 channels:**

- Four channels require four descriptor register sets, four completion counters, and four arbitration paths.
- The verification space grows with the square of channel interactions.
- Four channels exceed the project's needs.

**Why 2 channels:**

- Two channels allow one transfer to overlap with the setup of the next. This demonstrates pipelining.
- Two channels fit the FPGA area budget with margin.
- Two channels are sufficient to demonstrate the DMA design.
- The channel count is parameterised. It can be raised to 4 later.

### 5.4 Why Single-Block and Not Scatter-Gather

**Why not scatter-gather:**

- Scatter-gather requires linked descriptors. The DMA must fetch each descriptor from memory before the transfer.
- Descriptor fetch adds a second DMA read path. This complicates coherence.
- Scatter-gather is a 4-week addition.

**Why single-block:**

- One descriptor. One source. One destination. One length.
- The DMA reads the descriptor from a register, then starts the transfer.
- This is the minimum viable DMA. It is sufficient for the paper.
- Scatter-gather can be added later as a new descriptor format.

### 5.5 Why Memory-Mapped and Not Register-Only or Doorbell

**Why not register-only:**

- Register-only DMA requires the core to write each register through a separate interface.
- This does not integrate with the address map.
- It works for one channel but scales poorly.

**Why not doorbell:**

- Doorbell DMA requires a descriptor ring in memory. The DMA must read the ring to find work.
- Doorbell adds a descriptor fetch path. This is the same complexity as scatter-gather.

**Why memory-mapped:**

- Memory-mapped registers appear in the address space. The core writes to them like any other address.
- The coherence protocol handles register writes automatically. No special path is needed.
- This is the standard interface for modern DMA engines.

### 5.6 Why New Messages and Not Reusing Existing Ones

**Why not reuse existing messages:**

- GETS and GETM are cache requests. They assume the requester is a cache with a line to fill.
- The DMA has no cache line. It has a source address and a destination address.
- Reusing GETS/GETM would require the DMA to fake a cache line. This is a hack.

**Why not hybrid:**

- Hybrid uses GETS/GETM for coherence and a new message for completion. This works but adds two code paths.
- One path with DMA_READ, DMA_WRITE, DMA_ACK is cleaner.

**Why new DMA messages:**

- DMA_READ requests a read transfer. The directory responds with DATA (reusing the existing data message).
- DMA_WRITE requests a write transfer. The directory responds with an acknowledgement.
- DMA_ACK confirms the transfer is complete.
- Only three new messages. The rest of the protocol is unchanged.
- Two message codes remain reserved for future use.

---

## 6. Where It Affects the Design

### 6.1 Affected Components

| Component | Affected | How |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Coherence message enum | Yes | Three new message codes (11, 12, 13) |
| Flit header | Yes | Message type field uses codes 11, 12, 13 |
| Directory FSM | No | MESI protocol is unchanged |
| Directory array | No | Entry format is unchanged |
| L1 cache FSM | No | CPU tile L1 is unchanged |
| DMA tile L1-lite FSM | Yes | New module, L1-lite cache FSM |
| DMA register file | Yes | New module |
| DMA channel FSM | Yes | New module |
| NIC | Yes | Packet generator handles DMA messages |
| Router | No | Routing is unchanged |
| Virtual channels | Yes | DMA messages use VC 0 and VC 2 |
| Address map | Yes | DMA register window in address space |
| Tile instantiation | Yes | One tile becomes the DMA tile |
| Verification | Yes | New properties and tests for DMA |

### 6.2 Unaffected Components

| Component | Reason |
| --- | --- |
| Mesh topology | DMA tile fits the mesh like any other tile |
| XY routing | Routing is independent of message type |
| Flit width | Message type fits in 4 bits |
| Address width | DMA uses 32-bit addresses |
| Clock frequency | Independent of DMA |
| MESI states | DMA is another cache from the directory's view |
| Directory format | No new fields needed |

---

## 7. Consequences

### 7.1 Gain

- DMA subsystem is symmetric with CPU tiles. No special RTL outside the DMA tile.
- Coherence is maintained by hardware. No software flush is needed.
- The design remains parameterised. The DMA tile can be omitted at synthesis time.
- DMA is publishable as a coherent device-tile design.
- Two channels allow overlap. Single-block is the minimum viable transfer.
- Memory-mapped registers integrate with the address space.
- Three new messages are a small addition. Two message codes remain reserved.
- No new directory states. The MESI protocol is unchanged.

### 7.2 Loss

- One tile slot is used by the DMA engine. At 4×4, the CPU tile count is 15 instead of 16.
- Three new messages are added to the message set. The 4-bit field now has only two codes free.
- Verification must cover DMA correctness under contention.
- Scatter-gather is not supported in the initial design.

### 7.3 Constraint

- The DMA tile must have a valid tile ID in the mesh.
- The DMA messages must fit in the 4-bit message type field.
- The DMA register window must not overlap the memory address range.
- Software must program the DMA via memory-mapped registers.
- The DMA engine must respect the coherence protocol.

### 7.4 Verification

| Property | Method |
| --- | --- |
| DMA read correctness | Directed tests |
| DMA write correctness | Directed tests |
| DMA coherence with CPU tiles | Formal + directed |
| DMA transfer completes | Timeout assertions |
| DMA does not deadlock | Formal + stress |
| DMA register access is coherent | Formal |
| Two channels do not conflict | Directed + stress |
| DMA to same line as CPU read | Directed |
| DMA to same line as CPU write | Directed |
| DMA timeout and error reporting | Directed |

---

## 8. Future Extension

| Extension | Prerequisite | Estimated time |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Scatter-gather | Descriptor fetch path | 4 weeks |
| More channels | Parameterised channel count | 2 weeks |
| DMA priority levels | New VC class | 2 weeks |
| DMA with encryption | Crypto engine | 3 months |
| IOMMU address translation | Translation table and TLB | 6 months |
| DMA-to-DMA transfers | Peer-to-peer path | 4 weeks |

---

## 9. Contradiction With Original Design

### 9.1 No DMA in the Original Design

The original CDAC project did not include DMA. The official report does not mention DMA in any section. The DMA subsystem is a new addition in this white paper.

### 9.2 PROXY_WAIT in the Original Design

The original design declared `PROXY_WAIT` but never entered it. This design does not use `PROXY_WAIT`. The DMA device tile maintains its own L1-lite cache. The directory sees it as a cache. No proxy state is needed.

### 9.3 Correcting the Original Declaration

The original team declared a state they never used. This white paper either uses a state or omits it. The DMA design omits `PROXY_WAIT` because the device tile approach does not require it.

---

## 10. Summary

| Decision | Choice | Main Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Location | Device tile | Symmetric with CPU tiles; no new directory states |
| Coherence | Coherent | Hardware guarantees correctness; no software flush |
| Channels | 2 | Allows overlap; fits FPGA budget |
| Transfer | Single-block | Minimum viable; scatter-gather as future scope |
| Interface | Memory-mapped | Integrates with address map; standard interface |
| Messages | New (3) | Cleaner than reusing cache messages; two codes reserved |

The DMA subsystem is in scope. It is a device tile on the mesh. It is coherent. It has two channels. It supports single-block transfers. It is programmed through memory-mapped registers. It adds three messages to the coherence set. It adds no new directory states. It fits the 3-month timeline. It is a second contribution to the paper.

---
# Address Decode — Verdict Document (Honest Revision)

---

**Scope:** Physical address field partitioning and home-tile mapping for the 2×2 and 4×4 tiled mesh multicore
**Decision:** 32-bit PA, **range-based (contiguous)** home-tile mapping, six-field layout

---

## 1. Purpose & Method

This document answers:

> **How should a 32-bit physical address be decoded, and how should the home tile be selected?**
> 

It does **not** claim range-based mapping is the only correct choice. It claims range-based mapping is the **best fit under this project's specific priorities**, and it states clearly where other alternatives are equally valid.

Structure:

1. State project constraints.
2. Present each alternative honestly (strengths first, then costs).
3. Compare quantitatively where possible.
4. Select the alternative that best fits the project's priorities.
5. State explicitly which alternatives would be chosen under different priorities.

---

## 2. Project Constraints

| ID | Constraint | Source | Consequence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **A1** | 32-bit physical address | Project decision | Total budget = 32 bits |
| **A2** | 64-byte line size | Cache Params Verdict | Offset = 6 bits |
| **A3** | L1 = 4 sets | Cache Params Verdict | L1 set index = 2 bits |
| **A4** | L2 = 8 sets per bank | Cache Params Verdict | L2 set index = 3 bits |
| **A5** | Tile count = 4 (2×2) or 16 (4×4) | Topology Verdict | Home tile field = 2 or 4 bits |
| **A6** | Directory is per-bank, full-map | Directory Verdict | Home tile = who owns the line's directory entry |
| **A7** | 3-month schedule | Project plan | Simplicity preferred |
| **A8** | Formal verification in SymbiYosys | Verification plan | Mapping should be closed-form provable |
| **A9** | No runtime remapping (no OS, no supervisor) | Project scope | Static mapping only |
| **A10** | Workload not characterized as heavily skewed | Project scope | Load balance is nice-to-have, not critical |

**Note:** A7, A8, A9, A10 are priorities, not hard exclusions. They influence which alternative is preferred; they do not make the alternatives impossible.

---

## 3. Mapping Alternatives — Honest Evaluation

Four genuinely distinct alternatives exist. Each is evaluated with strengths first, then costs.

---

### 3.1 Range-Based (Contiguous)

**What it means:** Home tile = a contiguous slice of address bits. Tile 0 owns `[0x0000, 0x07FF]`, tile 1 owns the next 2 KB, and so on.

**Strengths:**

- Zero logic. Home tile = `addr[14:11]` (4×4) or `addr[12:11]` (2×2).
- Contiguity preserved. Sequential streams stay in one tile.
- Directory locality. A tile's directory serves its own address range.
- Formal property is a one-line closed form.

**Costs:**

- Under skewed workloads, one tile's directory becomes a hot spot.
- Non-uniform latency under contention if one address range is hotter than others.

---

### 3.2 Hashed (XOR-based)

**What it means:** Home tile = XOR of several address bit groups. Example: `home = addr[14:11] XOR addr[10:7] XOR addr[6:3]`. Sequential addresses scatter across tiles.

**Strengths:**

- Load balance under skewed workloads. A hot address range spreads across all tiles.
- Simple closed-form function. A 3-level XOR tree of ~4 gates.
- Formal property is a one-line closed form: `home(A) = A[14:11] XOR A[10:7] XOR A[6:3]`.
- Used in production designs: AMD Infinity Fabric, Intel mesh, large ARM CMN-700 meshes.
- Naturally balances directory load.

**Costs:**

- Non-contiguous ownership. Sequential streams scatter across tiles.
- Directory entries for adjacent lines live on different tiles. A 2 KB logical data structure has its directory scattered across 16 banks.
