- L1 miss traffic increases under streaming loads because the home tile of the next line is not the same as the last.
- Debug waveform does not show tile ID directly.
- ~4 gates more than range-based (negligible).
- Needs a hash function decision (which bits to XOR). Simple choice works, but a choice must be made.

**Does it fail any constraint?** **No.** Hashed satisfies A1–A10. It is provably correct, provable in formal, and simple.

---

### 3.3 Low-Order Interleaved

**What it means:** Home tile = bits just above the offset. Example: `home = addr[11:8]`. Adjacent cache lines go to different tiles.

**Strengths:**

- Excellent balance for strided streams that touch every cache line.
- Used in memory channel interleaving (DRAM banks), where it works very well.
- Sequential large reads balance across all tiles.

**Costs:**

- Interleave granularity = 64 B (one cache line). This is far finer than the natural directory span.
- Directory entries for adjacent lines always live on different tiles. No locality at all.
- Coherence traffic explodes: producer-consumer on adjacent lines crosses the NoC every access.
- Low-order bits overlap with L2 set index. The set index uses the same address bits; using them again for home tile creates a conflict in the L1 tag structure.
- Not used in any cache-coherent tiled design I am aware of. This is a **memory controller** technique, not a coherence technique.

**Does it fail a constraint?** **Yes — A4 in practice.** The interleaved bits would overlap with the L2 set index, forcing either a wider L2 set index or a tag restructure. Also granularity of 64 B is too fine for directory locality (A6). This is the only alternative with a genuine conflict.

---

### 3.4 Table-Based (Indirection)

**What it means:** A small SRAM stores the home tile ID per address block. The address indexes the table; the table output is the tile ID. Mapping is programmable.

**Strengths:**

- Fully programmable. OS or firmware can remap tile ownership.
- Used in OS-managed NUCA and some research designs.
- Can encode complex mappings (non-uniform blocks, exclusions).

**Costs:**

- Storage cost. A per-2KB-block table over 32-bit PA needs ~2²¹ entries — impossible. Must be two-level like page tables.
- Latency cost. Every L1 miss pays a table lookup (1–3 cycles).
- Verification cost. Mapping is stateful; formal proofs must reason about all possible table contents.
- Requires a supervisor or firmware to program the table. This project has neither.
- Coupled to address translation. If translation is out of scope, so is this.

**Does it fail a constraint?** **Yes — A7 and A9.** Design and verification of a table far exceeds schedule; runtime remapping is excluded by A9.

---

## 4. Honest Comparison Matrix

| Criterion | Range | Hashed | Low-order | Table |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Meets A1 (32-bit PA) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| Meets A2 (64 B line) | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ (overlap) | ✅ |
| Meets A3 (L1 4 sets) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| Meets A4 (L2 8 sets) | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ (overlap) | ✅ |
| Meets A5 (4/16 tiles) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| Meets A6 (dir per-bank) | ✅ | ✅ (weaker) | ❌ | ❌ |
| Meets A7 (3-month schedule) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| Meets A8 (formal) | ✅ | ✅ | ⚠ | ❌ |
| Meets A9 (no remap) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| Meets A10 (no skew expected) | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ |
| **Feasible for this project?** | **Yes** | **Yes** | **No** | **No** |
| **Logic cost (gates)** | **0** | **~4** | ~2 | lookup + table |
| **Streaming locality** | **High** | **Medium** | Low | High |
| **Skew load balance** | Medium | **High** | High | High |
| **Debug visibility** | **Trivial** | Medium | Medium | Complex |
| **Directory locality** | **High** | Low | Very low | High |
| **Production precedent** | Small-to-medium tiles | Large meshes | Memory controllers | Research |

**Two feasible candidates: range-based and hashed.**

---

## 5. Honest Proof — Why Range-Based Wins for This Project

### 5.1 What can be proven rigorously

**Claim 1:** Under range-based mapping, `home(A) = A[14:11]` (4×4). This is a direct bit slice; 0 gates; provable in 1 line of SVA.

**Claim 2:** Under hashed mapping, `home(A) = A[14:11] XOR A[10:7] XOR A[6:3]`. This is a 3-level XOR tree; ~4 gates; also provable in 1 line of SVA.

**Claim 3:** Both functions satisfy the determinism invariant: for every address, the home tile is a single-valued, closed-form function of the address.

**Claim 4:** Both functions satisfy the bit-budget arithmetic (32 bits total).

**What is NOT provable:** "One is objectively better than the other." Performance depends on workload. Under uniform random access, both produce nearly identical latency. Under streaming access, range-based wins because consecutive lines stay local. Under skewed access, hashed wins because load spreads.

### 5.2 Bit budget (identical for both)

| Field | 4×4 | 2×2 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Offset | 6 | 6 |
| L1 set | 2 | 2 |
| L2 set | 3 | 3 |
| Home tile | 4 | 2 |
| Tag | 17 | 19 |
| **Total** | **32** | **32** |

The bit layout is **independent of the mapping choice**. Range and hashed both use 4 home-tile bits in 4×4.

### 5.3 The actual selection argument

Under this project's priorities:

| Priority | Weight | Range | Hashed |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Simplicity (A7) | High | **0 gates** | 4 gates |
| Formal proof (A8) | Medium | One-line | One-line |
| Directory locality (A6) | Medium | **High** | Low |
| Streaming performance | Medium | **High** | Medium |
| Skew performance | Low (A10) | Medium | **High** |
| Debug visibility | Low | **Trivial** | Medium |

**Under uniform and streaming workloads (this project's target), range-based is equal or better.** Under skewed workloads, hashed is better — but A10 says skewed workloads are not the target.

**Honest conclusion:** Range-based wins because **the workload does not require hash's benefit**, and it costs 4 fewer gates and preserves streaming locality. Hashed is a legitimate alternative that would win if the workload were expected to be skewed.

---

## 6. Why the Alternatives Are Good — But Not For This Project

| Alternative | What it does better | Why that advantage does not apply |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Hashed** | Load balance under skewed workloads; production-proven at large scale | A10 excludes heavily skewed workloads. At 4–16 tiles with uniform or streaming access, range-based performs equal or better. Hashed is a valid choice — it just isn't needed here. |
| **Low-order interleaved** | Excellent for memory-channel bandwidth | A2 (64 B line) makes the interleave granularity too fine for cache coherence. A4 (L2 set index) conflicts with the interleaved bits. This is a memory-controller technique, not a coherence technique. |
| **Table-based** | Fully programmable; matches OS page tables | A7 (schedule) and A9 (no remap) exclude it. No supervisor exists in scope. |

**The honest reason for choosing range-based:** it is the simplest option that satisfies all constraints, and the workload does not demand hashed's specific advantage. Hashed is not rejected — it is **not needed**.

---

## 7. Final Bit Layout

### 4×4 (16 tiles)

```
 31                    15 14    11 10   8 7  6 5          0
┌────────────────────────┬─────────┬──────┬────┬────────────┐
│         Tag            │ Home    │ L2   │ L1 │  Offset    │
│        (17 bits)       │ tile(4) │ set  │ set│  (6 bits)  │
└────────────────────────┴─────────┴──────┴────┴────────────┘
```

### 2×2 (4 tiles)

```
 31                    13 12  11 10   8 7  6 5          0
┌────────────────────────┬───────┬──────┬────┬────────────┐
│         Tag            │ Home  │ L2   │ L1 │  Offset    │
│        (19 bits)       │ (2)   │ (3)  │(2) │  (6 bits)  │
└────────────────────────┴───────┴──────┴────┴────────────┘
```

### Cache Tag Widths

| Cache | 4×4 tag | 2×2 tag | Stored bits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | 24 | 24 | address[31:8] |
| L2 | 21 | 21 | address[31:11] |

---

## 8. What Would Change the Verdict

| If this changes… | Then consider switching to… |
| --- | --- |
| Workload expected to be heavily skewed | Hashed |
| Tile count grows beyond 64 | Hashed |
| NUCA becomes in scope | Hashed or low-order interleaved |
| Address translation added | Table-based (reuse page table walker) |
| Runtime remapping required | Table-based |
| Schedule extends and workload analysis available | Hashed (with tuning) |

None of these are planned.

---

## 9. Confidence Statement

You can state this with confidence:

> "For this project, range-based home-tile mapping is selected because it is the simplest option that satisfies every stated constraint, and the workload does not require hash's specific advantage. Hashed mapping is a legitimate alternative that would be preferred if the workload were expected to be skewed or the tile count were much larger. Under this project's target workloads (uniform and streaming), range-based and hashed perform equally; range-based costs 4 fewer gates and preserves directory locality. Low-order interleaving conflicts with the L2 set index and is a memory-controller technique, not a coherence technique. Table-based mapping is excluded by schedule and the absence of any runtime remapping infrastructure. The choice is a trade-off, not a proof that range-based is universally best."
> 

---

## 10. Final Decision

**Selected: Range-based (contiguous) home-tile mapping.**

**Selected because:**

- Simplest option that satisfies all constraints (A7).
- Deterministic, closed-form, formally provable in 1 line (A8).
- Preserves directory locality for streaming and uniform workloads (A6).
- Zero logic gates.

**Not selected, and honest reasons:**

- **Hashed** — viable and correct, but its advantage (load balance under skew) is not needed for this workload (A10). If workload skew is later characterized as significant, hashed should be reconsidered.
- **Low-order interleaved** — conflicts with L2 set index (A4) and uses line-granularity interleaving, too fine for coherence (A2).
- **Table-based** — needs a supervisor and schedule for verification that does not exist (A7, A9).

**This is a workload-driven verdict, not a proof of universal superiority.** Hashed and range-based are both correct; range-based is chosen for this project's specific target patterns. [ **It states the actual reason for the choice: simplicity + workload fit. ]**

---

# FPGA & ASIC Platforms — Verdict Document

---

## 1. Purpose & Method

This document answers:

> **Which FPGA board and ASIC PDK does this project target, at what tile count, and is the design feasible for tape-out?**
> 

It presents all available ASIC alternatives honestly, evaluates each with strengths and costs, and selects the option that best fits this project. The FPGA board is fixed by laboratory availability, so no board-level comparison is presented.

---

## 2. Project Constraints

| ID | Constraint | Consequence |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **P1** | FPGA board must be the one available in the college laboratory | Only the Digilent ZedBoard (XC7Z020) is available |
| **P2** | ASIC PDK must be open-source | Commercial PDKs requiring NDAs are excluded |
| **P3** | ASIC flow must be open-source | Vendor-locked EDA tools are excluded |
| **P4** | Fabrication cost must be accessible for an academic project | Only low-cost MPW shuttle programs are viable |
| **P5** | Design must fit the available FPGA board | Tile count depends on actual resource fit |
| **P6** | 4×4 tile count in simulation | Simulation has no resource constraints |
| **P7** | No proprietary EDA dependency for verification | Open-source tools (Yosys, SymbiYosys) preferred |

---

## 3. FPGA Platform — Digilent ZedBoard (XC7Z020)

### 3.1 Board Specifications

The ZedBoard is the FPGA development board available in the college laboratory. It is based on the Xilinx Zynq-7000 XC7Z020 SoC, which combines a dual-core ARM Cortex-A9 processing system with Artix-7 FPGA programmable logic.

| Resource | Amount |
| --- | --- |
| Logic cells | 85,000 |
| Look-up tables (LUTs) | 53,200 |
| Flip-flops (FFs) | 106,400 |
| Block RAM (36 Kb each) | 140 blocks (4.9 Mb total) |
| DSP48E1 slices | 220 |
| Clock management tiles (CMTs) | 4 |

The on-board programmable logic clock is 100 MHz from a SiT9102 oscillator.

### 3.2 Limitations of the XC7Z020

| Limitation | Detail | Impact on This Project |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **BRAM capacity ceiling** | Published NoC work on the same board required 192 BRAM cells for a 2×2 NoC, exceeding the 140 available | The 2×2 design may not fit if L1, L2, and directory arrays are all BRAM-mapped. Mitigation: LUTRAM for some cache arrays or reduced associativity. |
| **3×3 exceeds resources** | Published implementations confirm 3×3 NoC exceeds available resources on the XC7Z020 | 3×3 is not feasible. 2×2 is the practical maximum. |
| **Routing congestion at scale** | Physical multi-core clusters on Zynq-7020 show scaling is limited by routing congestion, not logic capacity | Place-and-route may fail or produce poor timing even if 2×2 fits logically. |
| **LUT utilization pressure** | Z-7020 designs reach ~52% LUT utilization at moderate complexity | Debug cores (ILA, VIO) consume additional LUTs, reducing available margin. |
| **Single PL voltage domain** | The entire programmable logic region shares one voltage domain | No per-tile power gating or DVFS is possible. |
| **Fixed 100 MHz PL clock** | The on-board PL oscillator is 100 MHz | Frequency cannot be increased without adding an external clock source. |

### 3.3 Approximate Resource Consumption — 2×2 Design

The following estimates are based on comparable NoC and coherence designs. **These are approximate values only.** Actual synthesis results will be produced during timing closure and area analysis.

| Block (per tile) | LUTs (approx.) | FFs (approx.) | BRAMs (approx.) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache (4 sets × 4 ways × 64 B, tags, FSM, MSHR) | ~1,200 | ~1,000 | ~2 |
| L2 slice (8 sets × 4 ways × 64 B, tags, FSM) | ~1,800 | ~1,500 | ~4 |
| Directory (32 lines × entry width, FSM) | ~400 | ~300 | ~1 |
| Router (3-port, 4 VC, wormhole, credit-based) | ~2,500 | ~2,000 | ~4 |
| NIC (TX/RX FIFOs, encode/decode) | ~1,500 | ~1,200 | ~2 |
| Local MUX, glue, clock/reset | ~600 | ~500 | 0 |
| **Per tile total** | **~8,000** | **~6,500** | **~13** |
| **× 4 tiles (2×2)** | **~32,000** | **~26,000** | **~52** |

| Resource | Available | Estimated | Utilization | Risk |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| LUTs | 53,200 | ~32,000 | ~60% | Moderate — debug cores may push to 70–75% |
| FFs | 106,400 | ~26,000 | ~24% | Low |
| BRAMs | 140 | ~52 | ~37% | Low if caches use BRAM efficiently; but published NoC work needed 192 BRAMs for 2×2 |

The BRAM estimate is the highest-risk item. Published NoC implementations on the same board required 192 BRAM cells for a 2×2 NoC, exceeding the 140 available. If our design's caches map entirely to BRAM, it may not fit. Mitigation options include LUTRAM for some cache arrays, reduced associativity, or directory storage sharing.

The 3×3 configuration exceeds available resources, confirmed by published implementations. The 4×4 configuration is estimated at ~128,000 LUTs and is simulation-only.

---

## 4. ASIC Platform — Open-Source PDK Comparison

### 4.1 Available Open-Source PDKs

Three open-source, fabricable PDKs are available for academic use. Each has been used in Open MPW shuttle runs.

| Parameter | SkyWater SKY130 | GlobalFoundries GF180MCU | IHP SG13G2 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Node** | 130 nm CMOS | 180 nm CMOS | 130 nm BiCMOS |
| **Core voltage** | 1.8 V | 3.3 V | 1.2 V |
| **Primary target** | Digital, mixed-signal | Mixed-signal, power management | RF, analog, high-speed |
| **PDK license** | Apache 2.0 | Apache 2.0 | Open (IHP) |
| **Digital flow** | Mature (OpenLane, Yosys) | Mature (LibreLane, OpenROAD) | Growing |
| **Community** | Largest | Growing | Smaller |
| **Fabrication route** | Efabless Open MPW / Tiny Tapeout | wafer.space MPW | IHP Low-Cost MPW |
| **Fabrication cost** | ~$70–$150/tile (Tiny Tapeout); ~$7,000 (ChipIgnite full shuttle) | ~$4,500 for 1,000 dies (~$4.50/die) | ~€2,800/mm² (SG13G2) |
| **Area per slot** | ~10 mm² (Caravel) / 160×100 µm (TT) | ~20 mm² | Area-dependent |
| **SRAM options** | 3 predefined sizes (8×1024, 32×256, 32×512) | Custom SRAM compiler | Custom SRAM compiler |
| **Digital tapeout evidence** | Strong (multiple academic tapeouts) | Limited | Limited |

### 4.2 SKY130 — Strengths and Limitations

**Strengths:**

- Most mature open-source digital flow; OpenLane is the standard RTL-to-GDSII toolchain.
- Largest community and reference designs.
- Lowest-cost prototyping entry: $50–$150 per Tiny Tapeout tile.
- Demonstrated tapeout success: a 2×2 NoC cluster on Sky130 achieved 100 MHz timing closure (setup slack +1.3 ns, hold slack +0.13 ns), zero DRC/LVS violations, and 100% netlist equivalence.
- Predefined SRAM macros reduce design effort.

**Limitations:**

- Only three predefined SRAM configurations. Custom cache sizing requires interleaving or designing memory from scratch.
- Timing closure at higher frequencies is challenging: one CNN accelerator achieved ~192 MHz maximum.
- Routing congestion can render large mesh designs unroutable without careful floorplanning.
- PDK maturity issues reported in some tool flows.

### 4.3 GF180MCU — Strengths and Limitations

**Strengths:**

- Larger area per slot (~20 mm² vs ~10 mm²) — allows larger designs.
- Custom SRAM compiler available — no predefined SRAM limitation.
- Higher voltage (3.3 V) — better noise margin.

**Limitations:**

- Less mature digital flow than Sky130.
- Larger node (180 nm vs 130 nm) — lower density.
- Less documented for digital tapeouts.
- Fabrication cost structure requires ordering full wafers (~$4,500 for 1,000 dies).

### 4.4 SG13G2 — Strengths and Limitations

**Strengths:**

- BiCMOS process with SiGe HBT transistors (350 GHz transition frequency) — excellent for RF/analog.
- Open PDK.

**Limitations:**

- BiCMOS complexity provides no benefit to a digital NoC design.
- Less mature digital flow support.
- Smaller community.
- Higher cost per area (~€2,800/mm²).

### 4.5 PDK Selection

| Criterion | Weight | SKY130 | GF180MCU | SG13G2 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Digital flow maturity | High | Best | Good | Fair |
| Community/references | High | Largest | Growing | Small |
| Fabrication cost (lowest entry) | High | **~$70–$150/tile** | ~$4,500 for 1,000 dies | ~€2,800/mm² |
| SRAM flexibility | Medium | Limited (3 sizes) | **Custom compiler** | Custom |
| Digital tapeout evidence | Medium | **Strong** | Limited | Limited |
| Area per slot | Medium | ~10 mm² | ~20 mm² | Variable |

**Verdict:** SKY130 is selected because it has the most mature digital flow, the largest community, the lowest-cost prototyping entry, and documented digital tapeout success. GF180MCU is a valid alternative with more SRAM flexibility and larger area per slot, but its digital flow is less tested. SG13G2 is optimized for RF/analog and provides no advantage for this project.

---

## 5. Sub-Decision — ASIC Tile Count & Tape-Out Feasibility

### 5.1 Published Tape-Out Evidence

A 2×2 NoC cluster on SkyWater 130 nm has been successfully implemented and verified tapeout-ready:

| Metric | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Core area | 1,712,237 μm² (~1.71 mm²) |
| Timing | 100 MHz, setup slack +1.3 ns, hold slack +0.13 ns |
| DRC violations | 0 |
| LVS mismatches | 0 |
| Max routing congestion | 82% on Metal3 |
| Total power | 378 mW |
| Standard cell utilization | 78% |

This demonstrates that a 2×2 NoC on Sky130 at 100 MHz is tapeout-feasible with current open-source tools.

### 5.2 4×4 Feasibility Estimate

| Parameter | 2×2 (demonstrated) | 4×4 (estimated) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Core area | ~1.71 mm² | ~6.8 mm² (4× scale) |
| Caravel slot area | ~7.8 mm² | ~7.8 mm² |
| Fits Caravel slot? | Yes | ⚠ Marginal — ~87% utilization |
| Timing at 100 MHz | Closed | Unknown — congestion risk |
| DRC/LVS | Clean | Unknown — needs verification |
| Tapeout demonstrated? | **Yes** | **No** |

4×4 on Sky130 is **plausible but unproven**. The estimated core area (~6.8 mm²) is within the Caravel slot (~7.8 mm²), but routing congestion at that utilization is a significant risk. No published 4×4 NoC tapeout on Sky130 exists to confirm feasibility.

### 5.3 Tape-Out Cost Comparison

| Route | PDK | Cost | What You Get |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Tiny Tapeout** | SKY130 | $70–$150 per tile | 160×100 µm² tile; ~1,000 gates — too small for a full NoC tile |
| **Efabless Open MPW** | SKY130 | Free (Google-sponsored runs) to ~$7,000 | ~10 mm² Caravel slot; can hold 2×2 NoC |
| **wafer.space** | GF180MCU | ~$4,500 for 1,000 dies | 1,000 chips; ~20 mm² per slot |
| **IHP MPW** | SG13G2 | ~€2,800/mm² | Area-dependent pricing |
| **Commercial** | TSMC/GF | $1M+ mask set | Not accessible |

Tiny Tapeout is not viable for this project. A Tiny Tapeout tile is 160×100 µm² with approximately 1,000 standard cells — far too small for a full NoC tile with cache, directory, router, and NIC. The Efabless Caravel slot (~10 mm²) is required.

### 5.4 Tape-Out Verdict

| Question | Answer |
| --- | --- |
| Can 2×2 be taped out on Sky130? | **Yes — demonstrated** |
| Can 4×4 be taped out on Sky130? | **Likely, but unproven** — area fits Caravel slot, but congestion risk is high |
| Is fabrication cost accessible? | **Yes** — Efabless Open MPW free runs or ~$7,000 for full shuttle |
| Is tapeout achievable with this project's resources? | **Yes for 2×2; uncertain for 4×4** |

**Conclusion:** The project can achieve tapeout-ready status for a 2×2 design on Sky130. A 4×4 tapeout is technically feasible from an area standpoint but carries significant congestion and timing closure risk. The safest path to actual fabrication is 2×2 on Sky130 via Efabless Open MPW.

---

## 6. Summary Comparison Matrix

| Criterion | ZedBoard (2×2) | ZedBoard (3×3) | ZedBoard (4×4) | Sky130 (2×2) | Sky130 (4×4) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| LUT fit | ✅ 60% | ❌ 135% | ❌ 240% | N/A | N/A |
| BRAM fit | ⚠ Marginal (192 needed vs 140) | ❌ | ❌ | N/A | N/A |
| Timing at 100 MHz | Likely | Unlikely | Impossible | Demonstrated | Risky |
| Tapeout proven | N/A | N/A | N/A | ✅ | ❌ Unproven |
| Cost | $300 board | Same | Same | Free–$7,000 | Free–$7,000 |

---

## 7. Why Alternatives Are Valid — But Not Selected

| Alternative | What it does better | Why it is not selected |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **GF180MCU** | Custom SRAM compiler; larger area per slot (~20 mm²) | Less mature digital flow; fabrication requires full wafer order (~$4,500) |
| **SG13G2** | SiGe HBT for RF/analog (350 GHz) | RF capability irrelevant to digital NoC; higher cost; smaller community |
| **Tiny Tapeout** | Lowest cost ($70–$150) | Tile too small (160×100 µm²; ~1,000 gates) for a full NoC tile |

---

## 8. Confidence Statement

For this project, the FPGA platform is the Digilent ZedBoard (XC7Z020) — the board available in the college laboratory — at 2×2 tile count. The 2×2 design fits LUTs with approximately 60% utilization, but BRAM is the binding constraint: published NoC work on the same board required 192 BRAM cells for a 2×2 NoC, exceeding the 140 available. Mitigation options include LUTRAM for cache arrays or reduced associativity. The 3×3 configuration exceeds available resources, confirmed by published implementations. The 4×4 configuration is simulation-only.

The ASIC platform is SkyWater SKY130 via OpenLane at 2×2 tile count. SKY130 is selected because it has the most mature open-source digital flow, the largest community, the lowest-cost prototyping entry, and demonstrated tapeout success for a 2×2 NoC (1.71 mm² core area, 100 MHz timing closure, zero DRC/LVS violations). GF180MCU is a valid alternative with more SRAM flexibility and larger area per slot, but its digital flow is less tested. SG13G2 is optimized for RF/analog and provides no advantage for this project. A 4×4 tapeout is technically plausible from an area standpoint (~6.8 mm² estimated vs ~7.8 mm² Caravel slot) but carries significant congestion risk and has not been demonstrated in published work.

---

## 9. Final Decision

| Selection | Value | Rationale |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **FPGA board** | Digilent ZedBoard (XC7Z020) | Only board available in lab (P1) |
| **FPGA tile count** | **2×2** | Maximum that fits; BRAM-bound |
| **FPGA mitigation** | LUTRAM for cache arrays or reduced associativity | BRAM insufficient (192 needed vs 140) |
| **ASIC PDK** | SkyWater SKY130 | Most mature digital flow; lowest cost |
| **ASIC flow** | OpenLane | Standard open-source RTL-to-GDSII |
| **ASIC tile count** | **2×2** | Demonstrated tapeout; 4×4 unproven |
| **ASIC fabrication** | Optional via Efabless Open MPW | Free–$7,000 for Caravel slot |
| **Simulation tile count** | **4×4** | Full-scale verification; no constraints |

---

# **What is the synthesis flow for FPGA and ASIC**

The synthesis flow defines how RTL becomes a bitstream (FPGA) or a GDS layout (ASIC). Vivado is chosen for FPGA because it provides full SystemVerilog support, debug cores (VIO, ILA), and the fastest path to hardware. Yosys and OpenLane are chosen for ASIC because they are open source and target the Sky130 PDK. The RTL source is identical for both flows. The differences appear only in elaboration, synthesis, place-and-route, and signoff. Simulation runs at three levels: RTL for full functional coverage, post-synthesis gate for synthesis sanity, and formal equivalence for RTL-to-netlist proof. The `ifdef SYNTHESIS` macro removes simulation-only constructs from the netlist. Lint runs on every commit. CDC runs after synthesis. The FPGA flow runs first to establish functional correctness on hardware. The ASIC flow runs second as future scope.

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies the synthesis flow for two targets:

1. **FPGA** — AMD Xilinx Zynq-7000 XC7Z020 on the ZedBoard, using Vivado.
2. **ASIC** — Sky130 PDK, using Yosys and OpenLane, as future scope.

The RTL source is the same for both targets. The tools differ. This finding also specifies RTL vs gate-level simulation, `ifdef SYNTHESIS` usage, lint checks, and CDC checks.

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **RTL** | Register Transfer Level. The source form of the design, written in SystemVerilog. Describes behavior in terms of registers and combinational logic. |
| **Gate level** | A netlist of logic gates. Produced by synthesis. No longer describes behavior, only structure. |
| **Synthesis** | The process of converting RTL into a gate-level netlist for a specific technology. |
| **Elaboration** | The first step of synthesis. The tool reads RTL and builds an internal representation. |
| **Bitstream** | The binary file that configures an FPGA. Produced by Vivado after place and route. |
| **GDS** | The layout file for an ASIC. Produced by Magic after routing and DRC. |
| **XDC** | Xilinx Design Constraints. A TCL-based file that defines the clock, I/O pins, and timing constraints for Vivado. |
| **SDC** | Synopsys Design Constraints. The same idea as XDC but for ASIC tools. |
| **Lint** | A static check for stylistic and structural problems in RTL. Not a syntax check. |
| **CDC** | Clock Domain Crossing. A signal that passes from one clock domain to another without synchronisation. |
| **WNS** | Worst Negative Slack. The most negative setup slack across all timing paths. Must be ≥ 0. |
| **WHS** | Worst Hold Slack. The most negative hold slack. Must be ≥ 0. |

| **WPWS** | Worst Pulse Width Slack. The most negative pulse-width slack. Must be ≥ 0. |
| --- | --- |
| **STA** | Static Timing Analysis. The tool that computes WNS, WHS, and WPWS. |
| **SVA** | SystemVerilog Assertions. Properties that check behavior during simulation or formal analysis. |
| **DRC** | Design Rule Check. Checks that the layout obeys the foundry rules. |
| **LVS** | Layout Versus Schematic. Checks that the layout matches the netlist. |
| **SDF** | Standard Delay Format. A file that carries gate delays for post-route simulation. |

# **3. Parameters**

## **3.1 Target Flow Order**

### **3.1.1 Definition**

The target flow order defines which synthesis flow runs first and which runs second.

**Unit:** sequence of flows.

**Location:** `scripts/` directory. `vivado_synth.tcl` for FPGA. `openlane_config.tcl` for ASIC.

### **3.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | FPGA only | Vivado flow only. No ASIC. |
| 2 | ASIC only | Yosys and OpenLane only. No FPGA. |
| 3 | FPGA first, ASIC later | Vivado first. OpenLane after FPGA results are stable. |
| 4 | Parallel FPGA and ASIC | Both flows run concurrently. |

### **3.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **FPGA only** | **ASIC only** | **FPGA first** | **Parallel** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Time to first result | Fast | Slow | **Fast** | Slow |
| Hardware debug | Yes | No | **Yes** | Partial |
| Silicon verification | No | Yes | **Later** | Later |
| Resource risk | Low | Medium | **Low** | Medium |
| Cost | Board only | Tapeout | **Board + tapeout** | Board + tapeout |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | No | **Yes** | No |
| Publishable | Weak | Strong | **Strong** | Strong |

**3.1.4**  

**Chosen : FPGA first, ASIC later**

The FPGA flow runs first. It establishes functional correctness on hardware. It uses Vivado. The ASIC flow runs second, as future scope. It uses Yosys and OpenLane. Both flows use the same RTL.

**3.1.5**

**Why not FPGA only:**

- No silicon path
- Weak publication case
- Does not demonstrate scaling to ASIC

**Why not ASIC only:**

- No hardware debug
- Tapeout is slow and expensive
- Does not fit the 3-month timeline

**Why not parallel:**

- Two flows running at once split team effort
- The ASIC flow depends on stable RTL

**Why FPGA first:**

- Fast turnaround
- Vivado has full SystemVerilog support
- VIO and ILA cores give hardware debug
- Establishes functional correctness before ASIC work begins
- ASIC flow becomes future scope

### **3.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Must synthesise on both flows |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Must synthesise on both flows |
| Directory array | Yes | Must infer BRAM on both flows |
| Message set | Yes | Encoded identically on both flows |
| Router | Yes | Must synthesise on both flows |
| NIC | Yes | Must synthesise on both flows |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Must synthesise on both flows |
| Address map | Yes | Independent of flow |
| Verification | Yes | RTL simulation validates both flows |

### **3.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Fast hardware validation
- Full SystemVerilog support
- Debug cores available

**Loss:**

- Two tool chains to maintain
- Two constraint formats

**Constraint:**

- RTL must be portable across Vivado and Yosy
- No vendor-specific primitives in the core RTL

**Verification:**

- FPGA bitstream runs on ZedBoard
- ASIC netlist passes DRC and LVS

### **3.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ASIC tapeout | Sky130 PDK, pad ring | 3 months |
| Multi-board FPGA | Larger device | 1 month |

### **3.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flow order | FPGA first, ASIC later | FPGA establishes correctness |

## **3.2 FPGA Flow Driver**

### **3.2.1 Definition**

The FPGA flow driver is the script or command sequence that runs the Vivado flow from RTL to bitstream.

**Unit:** driver type.

**Location:** `scripts/Makefile` and `scripts/vivado_synth.tcl`.

### **3.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Manual GUI flow | Use Vivado GUI for each step. |
| 2 | TCL batch flow | Run all steps from a TCL script. |
| 3 | Makefile flow | Wrap TCL calls in a Makefile. |
| 4 | CI-driven flow | GitHub Actions runs the flow on every push. |

### **3.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Manual GUI** | **TCL batch** | **Makefile** | **CI-driven** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Reproducibility | Low | High | **High** | Very high |
| Setup effort | Low | Medium | **Medium** | High |
| Debug visibility | High | Medium | **Medium** | Low |
| Team-friendly | No | Yes | **Yes** | Partial |
| CI-compatible | No | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### **3.2.4**

**Chosen: Makefile-wrapped TCL batch flow.**

A TCL script contains all Vivado commands. A Makefile wraps the calls so the flow runs with a single command. The Makefile has targets for synthesis, implementation, bitstream, and timing reports.

### **3.2.5**

**Why not manual GUI:**

- Not reproducible
- Not scriptable
- Does not fit a team workflow

**Why not TCL batch only:**

- A Makefile makes the targets explicit
- A Makefile integrates with CI

**Why not CI-driven only:**

- The team needs to run the flow locally during development
- CI is used for regression, not for daily work

**Why Makefile-wrapped TCL:**

- Reproducible
- Scriptable
- Team-friendly
- CI-compatible

### **3.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Synthesised via the driver |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Synthesised via the driver |
| Directory array | Yes | Inferred BRAM via the driver |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Synthesised via the driver |
| NIC | Yes | Synthesised via the driver |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Synthesised via the driver |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Bitstream validated on board |

### **3.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Reproducible flow
- Team-friendly
- CI-compatible

**Loss:**

- TCL script maintenance

**Constraint:**

- All Vivado commands must be in the script
- No manual steps allowed

**Verification:**

- Bitstream programs on ZedBoard
- Timing report signed off

### **3.2.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CI-driven regression | GitHub Actions runner | 1 week |
| Multi-board variants | Board-specific XDC | 2 weeks |

### **3.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| FPGA flow driver | Makefile-wrapped TCL | Reproducible, scriptable |

---

## **3.3 FPGA Flow Stages**

### **3.3.1 Definition**

The FPGA flow stages are the sequence of Vivado steps from RTL to bitstream.

**Unit:** stages.

**Location:** `scripts/vivado_synth.tcl`.

### **3.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | RTL to bitstream in one pass | No intermediate checkpoints. |
| 2 | RTL to netlist, then netlist to bitstream | Two-stage flow. |
| 3 | RTL to netlist, then checkpoint, then bitstream | Three-stage flow with checkpoints. |
| 4 | Full flow with CDC and timing gates | Adds CDC and timing signoff at each stage. |

### **3.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **One pass** | **Two-stage** | **Three-stage** | **Full gates** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Reproducibility | Low | Medium | **High** | High |
| Debug visibility | Low | Medium | **High** | High |
| Setup effort | Low | Medium | **Medium** | High |
| Iteration speed | Slow | Medium | **Fast** | Fast |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### **3.3.4**

**Chosen: Three-stage flow with checkpoints.**

The flow has three stages:

1. **Synthesis.** RTL to gate-level netlist.
2. **Implementation.** Netlist to placed-and-routed design.
3. **Bitstream.** Placed-and-routed design to `.bit` file.

Each stage saves a checkpoint. If a later stage fails, the earlier checkpoint is reloaded.

### **3.3.5**

**Why not one pass:**

- Any failure requires a full rerun
- Debug visibility is poor

**Why not two-stage:**

- Synthesis and implementation are logically separate
- Checkpoints help with iteration

**Why not full gates:**

- Adds complexity without benefit at this scale
- The three-stage flow is sufficient

**Why three-stage:**

- Clear separation
- Checkpoint saves time on iteration
- Debug visibility at each stage
- Fits the timeline

### **3.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Stage 1 output |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Stage 1 output |
| Directory array | Yes | Stage 1 output |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Stage 2 output |
| NIC | Yes | Stage 2 output |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Stage 2 output |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Bitstream is stage 3 output |

### **3.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Fast iteration
- Debug visibility

**Loss:**

- Three checkpoints to manage

**Constraint:**

- Checkpoints must be versioned

**Verification:**

- Each stage produces a report

### **3.3.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Incremental implementation | Vivado incremental flow | 1 week |
| Design checkpoint diffs | Scripting | 1 week |

### **3.3.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flow stages | Three-stage with checkpoints | Clear separation, fast iteration |

---

## **3.4 ASIC Flow Driver**

### **3.4.1 Definition**

The ASIC flow driver is the configuration and script set that runs the Yosys and OpenLane flow from RTL to GDS.

**Unit:** driver type.

**Location:** `scripts/openlane_config.tcl`.

### **3.4.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Yosys only | Synthesise but do not place or route. |
| 2 | Yosys + OpenROAD | Add placement and routing. |
| 3 | Yosys + OpenLane | Full flow with DRC and LVS. |
| 4 | Commercial ASIC flow | Use Synopsys or Cadence. |

### **3.4.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Yosys only** | **Yosys + OpenROAD** | **Yosys + OpenLane** | **Commercial** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Full flow | No | Partial | **Yes** | Yes |
| DRC and LVS | No | No | **Yes** | Yes |
| Open source | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | No |
| Setup effort | Low | Medium | **Medium** | High |
| Cost | Zero | Zero | **Zero** | High |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | No |

### **3.4.4**

**Chosen: Yosys + OpenLane.**

The ASIC flow uses Yosys for synthesis and OpenLane for floorplan, placement, clock tree synthesis, routing, static timing, DRC, and LVS. The output is a GDS file.

### **3.4.5**

**Why not Yosys only:**

- No physical layout
- No DRC or LVS
- Not a complete ASIC flow

**Why not Yosys + OpenROAD only:**

- OpenROAD provides physical layout but not the full OpenLane integration
- DRC and LVS would need separate setup

**Why not commercial:**

- License cost
- Does not fit the timeline
- Not required for this project

**Why Yosys + OpenLane:**

- Open source
- Complete flow
- Sky130 PDK supported
- Zero cost
- Fits the timeline

### **3.4.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Synthesised by Yosys |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Synthesised by Yosys |
| Directory array | Yes | Mapped to Sky130 SRAM macros |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Synthesised by Yosys |
| NIC | Yes | Synthesised by Yosys |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Synthesised by Yosys |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | GDS checked by DRC and LVS |

### **3.4.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Complete ASIC flow
- Open source
- Zero cost
- Sky130 supported

**Loss:**

- Sky130 cells are large
- 2×2 mesh may not fit

**Constraint:**

- Yosys has limited SystemVerilog package support
- Packages may need to be flattened

**Verification:**

- GDS is DRC clean
- Netlist matches schematic (LVS clean)

### **3.4.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Tapeout | Pad ring, power grid | 3 months |
| Multi-project wafer | Foundry schedule | 6 months |

### **3.4.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ASIC flow | Yosys + OpenLane | Open source, complete, Sky130 |

---

## **3.5 ASIC Flow Stages**

### **3.5.1 Definition**

The ASIC flow stages are the sequence of OpenLane steps from RTL to GDS.

**Unit:** stages.

**Location:** `scripts/openlane_config.tcl`.

### **3.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Synthesis only | Stop after Yosys. |
| 2 | Synthesis and placement | Add OpenROAD placement. |
| 3 | Full flow with routing | Add routing, STA, DRC, LVS. |
| 4 | Full flow with power grid | Add power grid and pad ring. |

### **3.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Synth only** | **Synth + place** | **Full flow** | **Full + power** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Physical layout | No | Partial | **Yes** | Yes |
| Timing analysis | No | Partial | **Yes** | Yes |
| DRC and LVS | No | No | **Yes** | Yes |
| Tapeout ready | No | No | **Partial** | Yes |
| Setup effort | Low | Medium | **High** | Very high |

### **3.5.4 Chosen Option**

**Chosen: Full flow with routing.**

The flow includes:

1. Synthesis (Yosys)
2. Floorplan (OpenROAD)
3. Placement (OpenROAD)
4. Clock tree synthesis (TritonCTS)
5. Routing (FastRoute, TritonRoute)
6. Static timing analysis (OpenSTA)
7. DRC (Magic)
8. LVS (Netgen)
9. GDS generation (Magic)

Power grid and pad ring are future scope.

### **3.5.5**

**Why not synthesis only:**

- No physical layout
- No timing analysis
- Not a complete flow

**Why not synthesis and placement:**

- No routing
- No DRC
- Not a complete flow

**Why not full with power grid:**

- Power grid is needed for tapeout, not for a first-pass study
- Pad ring is a separate design task
- Not required for the project's scope

**Why full flow with routing:**

- Complete physical design
- Timing verified
- DRC and LVS clean
- GDS ready for future pad ring and power grid

### **3.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Placed and routed |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Placed and routed |
| Directory array | Yes | Placed and routed |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Placed and routed |
| NIC | Yes | Placed and routed |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Placed and routed |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | GDS signed off |

### **3.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Complete physical design
- Timing verified
- DRC and LVS clean

**Loss:**

- Long runtime
- Complex setup

**Constraint:**

- Area may exceed test-chip limits
- Reduced configuration may be needed

**Verification:**

- STA report signed off
- DRC clean
- LVS clean

### **3.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Power grid | OpenROAD power grid script | 1 month |
| Pad ring | Pad frame design | 2 months |
| Tapeout | Foundry schedule | 6 months |

### **3.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ASIC flow depth | Full flow with routing | Complete physical design |

---

## **3.6 RTL vs Gate-Level Flow**

### **3.6.1 Definition**

The RTL vs gate-level flow defines which simulation runs at which stage of the design flow.

**Unit:** simulation level.

**Location:** `scripts/run_rtl_sim.tcl` and `scripts/run_gate_sim.tcl`.

### **3.6.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | RTL only | Simulate RTL only. Skip gate-level. |
| 2 | RTL + post-synthesis gate | Simulate RTL, then post-synthesis netlist. |
| 3 | RTL + post-synthesis + post-route | Add post-route netlist simulation. |
| 4 | RTL + gate + formal equivalence | Replace gate simulation with formal equivalence. |

### **3.6.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **RTL only** | **RTL + PS** | **RTL + PS + PR** | **RTL + gate + formal** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Detects RTL bugs | Yes | Yes | Yes | **Yes** |
| Detects synthesis bugs | No | Yes | Yes | **Yes** |
| Detects timing bugs | No | No | Yes | **No** |
| Simulation time | Fast | Slow | Very slow | **Fast** |
| Setup effort | Low | Medium | High | **High** |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | Partial | **Yes** |

### **3.6.4**

**Chosen: RTL + post-synthesis gate + formal equivalence.**

The primary verification runs at RTL. After synthesis, formal equivalence checking compares the gate-level netlist against the RTL. Post-synthesis gate-level simulation runs a subset of directed tests. Post-route simulation is not run.

### **3.6.5**

**Why not RTL only:**

- Synthesis bugs would go undetected
- The original design was never gate-level checked

**Why not RTL + post-synthesis + post-route:**

- Post-route simulation is too slow for the timeline
- Formal equivalence catches the same functional bugs
- Timing bugs are caught by static timing analysis

**Why not formal equivalence only:**

- A small set of gate-level directed tests provides a sanity check

**Why RTL + post-synthesis + formal:**

- RTL verification catches functional bugs
- Formal equivalence catches synthesis bugs
- Gate-level directed tests confirm the flow
- Fits the timeline

### **3.6.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| Directory array | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| Message set | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| Router | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| NIC | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| Address map | Yes | Simulated at RTL and gate level |
| Verification | Yes | This is the verification activity |

### **3.6.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Functional bugs caught at RTL
- Synthesis bugs caught by equivalence
- Gate-level sanity check

**Loss:**

- Two simulation environments
- Formal equivalence setup time

**Constraint:**

- Gate-level simulation runs a subset of tests
- Post-route simulation is not run

**Verification:**

- RTL: all directed tests + UVM random
- Gate-level: subset of directed tests
- Formal: equivalence between RTL and netlist

### **3.6.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Post-route simulation | Faster host | 2 weeks |
| Power-aware simulation | Power model | 1 month |
| SDF back-annotation | SDF file from tool | 1 week |

### **3.6.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Simulation levels | RTL + gate + formal | Catches all bug classes |
| Post-route simulation | No | Too slow for timeline |
| Equivalence | Formal | Fast and complete |

---

## **3.7 `ifdef SYNTHESIS` Usage**

### **3.7.1 Definition**

`ifdef SYNTHESIS` is a preprocessor directive that selects which code is compiled for synthesis and which is compiled for simulation.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** Throughout the RTL. Common locations: `cache_controller.sv`, `l2_cache_controller.sv`, `NIC.sv`, `Router.sv`, `tile.sv`.

### **3.7.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No `ifdef` | Simulation and synthesis code are identical. |
| 2 | `ifdef SYNTHESIS` for debug only | Debug signals removed for synthesis. |
| 3 | `ifdef SYNTHESIS` for debug and initial blocks | Also removes initial blocks. |
| 4 | `ifdef SYNTHESIS` for debug, initial, and assertions | Also gates SVA. |

### **3.7.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No ifdef** | **Debug only** | **Debug + initial** | **Debug + initial + SVA** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Synthesis warning risk | High | Low | Low | **Low** |
| Debug signal visibility | Full | Full in sim | Full in sim | **Full in sim** |
| Simulation constructs in netlist | Yes | No | No | **No** |
| SVA in netlist | Yes | Yes | Yes | **No** |
| RTL readability | High | Medium | Medium | **Low** |
| Verification effort | Low | Low | Low | **Medium** |

### **3.7.4**

**Chosen: `ifdef SYNTHESIS` for debug, initial, and assertions.**

The design uses `ifdef SYNTHESIS` in three places:

1. **Debug signals.** Signals used only by `$display` and `$monitor` are wrapped.
2. **Initial blocks.** Testbench initial blocks and memory preload tasks are wrapped.
3. **SVA assertions.** Formal and simulation assertions are wrapped.

When `SYNTHESIS` is defined, the wrapped code is removed. When `SYNTHESIS` is not defined, the wrapped code is compiled for simulation.

### **3.7.5**

**Why not no ifdef:**

- Simulation-only constructs appear in the netlist
- Synthesis tools warn or error on `$display`, `initial`, and SVA
- The original design mixed simulation and synthesis code, which caused compile issues

**Why not debug only:**

- Initial blocks also need to be wrapped
- Memory preload tasks are simulation-only

**Why not debug + initial:**

- SVA is simulation-only in most flows
- Formal tools handle SVA separately

**Why debug + initial + SVA:**

- Clean synthesis netlist
- Clean simulation build
- Assertions active only when needed
- Fits both Vivado and Yosys

### **3.7.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Debug signals wrapped |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Debug signals, SVA wrapped |
| Directory array | Yes | Debug signals wrapped |
| Message set | No | No simulation-only code |
| Router | Yes | Debug signals wrapped |
| NIC | Yes | Debug signals wrapped |
| Virtual channels | No | No simulation-only code |
| Address map | No | No simulation-only code |
| Verification | Yes | SVA only compiled when not synthesising |

### **3.7.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Clean synthesis netlist
- No simulation-only code in hardware
- SVA active only during verification

**Loss:**

- Two compile paths
- RTL is slightly less readable

**Constraint:**

- Every simulation-only construct must be wrapped
- The `SYNTHESIS` macro must be defined for synthesis and undefined for simulation

**Verification:**

- Simulate with `SYNTHESIS` undefined
- Synthesise with `SYNTHESIS` defined
- Confirm both builds match in function

### **3.7.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ifdef FORMAL` for formal-only code | Formal setup | 1 week |
| `ifdef FPGA` for FPGA-specific code | FPGA flow | 1 week |

### **3.7.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ifdef SYNTHESIS` usage | Debug + initial + SVA | Clean netlist, clean simulation |

---

## **3.8 Lint Checks**

### **3.8.1 Definition**

Lint checks detect stylistic and structural problems in RTL that are not syntax errors but can cause bugs.

**Unit:** count of warnings.

**Location:** `scripts/lint.tcl`. Runs Verilator with `--lint-only`.

### **3.8.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No lint | Skip lint. Rely on simulation. |
| 2 | Verilator lint | Open-source lint. Runs on every commit. |
| 3 | Spyglass lint | Commercial lint. Requires license. |
| 4 | Both Verilator and Spyglass | Verilator for every commit, Spyglass for release. |

### **3.8.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No lint** | **Verilator** | **Spyglass** | **Both** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Detects width mismatches | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Detects unused signals | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Detects incomplete cases | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Detects structural issues | No | **Partial** | Yes | Yes |
| License cost | Zero | **Zero** | High | High |
| Runs every commit | N/A | **Yes** | No | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.8.4**

**Chosen: Verilator lint.**

Verilator runs with `--lint-only` on every commit. Warnings are triaged. False positives are suppressed with a comment. Spyglass is future scope.

### **3.8.5**

**Why not no lint:**

- Width mismatches cause bugs
- Unused signals hide intent
- The original design was never linted

**Why not Spyglass:**

- Requires a commercial license
- Not needed at this scale
- Verilator catches the same structural issues

**Why not both:**

- Spyglass is not available at the start
- Verilator is sufficient for the project

**Why Verilator lint:**

- Open source
- Runs on every commit
- Catches structural issues
- Fits the timeline
- Zero cost

### **3.8.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Lint reports width mismatches |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Lint reports incomplete cases |
| Directory array | Yes | Lint reports unused signals |
| Message set | Yes | Lint reports encoding issues |
| Router | Yes | Lint reports fanout issues |
| NIC | Yes | Lint reports unused signals |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Lint reports width issues |
| Address map | Yes | Lint reports decode issues |
| Verification | Yes | Lint runs before simulation |

### **3.8.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Early bug detection
- Clean RTL style

**Loss:**

- Lint warnings must be triaged
- Some warnings are false positives

**Constraint:**

- Every warning must be reviewed
- False positives must be suppressed with a comment

**Verification:**

- Lint runs on every commit
- Lint report is archived

### **3.8.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Spyglass lint | Commercial license | 2 weeks |
| Power-aware lint | Power model | 2 weeks |

### **3.8.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Lint tool | Verilator --lint-only | Open source, every commit |
| Frequency | Every commit | Early detection |

## **3.9 CDC Checks**

### **3.9.1 Definition**

CDC (Clock Domain Crossing) checks detect signals that cross between clock domains without synchronisation.

**Unit:** count of violations.

**Location:** `scripts/cdc.tcl`. Runs Vivado `report_cdc` after synthesis.

### **3.9.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No CDC check | Skip CDC. Assume single clock. |
| 2 | Vivado report_cdc | Built into Vivado. Runs after synthesis. |
| 3 | Spyglass CDC | Commercial CDC tool. |
| 4 | Formal CDC | Formal tool with CDC support. |

### **3.9.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No CDC** | **Vivado** | **Spyglass** | **Formal** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Detects CDC violations | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Single-clock assumption documented | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| License cost | Zero | **Zero** | High | High |
| Setup effort | None | **Low** | Medium | High |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.9.4**

**Chosen: Vivado `report_cdc`.**

Vivado `report_cdc` runs

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flow order | FPGA first, ASIC later | FPGA establishes correctness |

**Synthesis Flow: Overall Summary**

The synthesis flow converts RTL into a programmable bitstream for FPGA and a manufacturable GDS layout for ASIC. The RTL source is identical for both targets. The FPGA flow runs first using Vivado, because it gives fast turnaround, full SystemVerilog support, and hardware debug cores (VIO, ILA). The ASIC flow runs second using Yosys and OpenLane, as future scope, because it is open source and supports the Sky130 PDK. The FPGA flow has three stages: synthesis, implementation, and bitstream. The ASIC flow has nine stages: synthesis, floorplan, placement, clock tree synthesis, routing, static timing analysis, DRC, LVS, and GDS generation. Simulation runs at three levels: RTL for full functional coverage, post-synthesis gate for synthesis sanity, and formal equivalence for RTL-to-netlist proof. Post-route simulation is not run because it is too slow for the timeline. The `ifdef SYNTHESIS` macro removes simulation-only constructs (debug signals, initial blocks, SVA) from the netlist while keeping them available during simulation. Lint runs with Verilator on every commit. CDC runs with Vivado `report_cdc` after synthesis to confirm the single-clock assumption. The FPGA target is the AMD Xilinx Zynq-7000 XC7Z020 on the ZedBoard at 100 MHz. The ASIC target is Sky130 at 50 MHz. Both flows use the same RTL, the same `ifdef` structure, and the same lint rules. Only the tool chain and the physical implementation stages differ.

## **Quick Reference**

```
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  F6 — SYNTHESIS FLOW SUMMARY                          │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                       │
│  FLOW ORDER                                           │
│  FPGA first → ASIC later                              │
│  Same RTL, different tools                            │
│                                                       │

│  Clock:  100 MHz                                      │

│  ───────────────────────                              │
│  Lint → Elab → Synth → Floorplan → Place → CTS       │
│       → Route → STA → DRC → LVS → GDS                 │

│  Wrapped:     debug signals, initial blocks, SVA      │
│  Not wrapped: core RTL                                │
│                                                       │
│  LINT + CDC                                           │
│  ──────────                                           │
│  Lint: Verilator on every commit                      │
│  CDC:  Vivado report_cdc after synthesis              │
│                                                       │
│  SIGNOFF                                              │
│  ───────                                              │

│  ASIC: GDS DRC clean, LVS clean                       │
│                                                       │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

# How do we achieve timing closure at 100 MHz?

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies how the design meets timing closure at 100 MHz on the AMD Xilinx Zynq-7000 XC7Z020 device. Timing closure means all setup, hold, and pulse-width paths meet their constraints, and the design operates at 100 MHz without errors.

---

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **Timing closure** | The state where all timing constraints are met. |
| **WNS** | Worst Negative Slack. The most negative setup slack across all paths. |
| **WHS** | Worst Hold Slack. The most negative hold slack. |
| **WPWS** | Worst Pulse Width Slack. The most negative pulse-width slack. |
| **TNS** | Total Negative Slack. The sum of all negative slacks. |
| **Setup path** | A path whose data must arrive before the next clock edge. |
| **Hold path** | A path whose data must not change too soon after the clock edge. |
| **Slack** | The margin between the required time and the actual time. |
| **Critical path** | The path with the least slack. |
| **XDC** | Xilinx Design Constraints. TCL-based file that defines the clock, I/O pins, and timing constraints. |
| **Retiming** | Moving registers across combinational logic to balance pipeline stages. |
| **False path** | A path that does not need to meet timing. |
| **Multicycle path** | A path that has more than one clock cycle to complete. |

---

# **3. Parameters**

---

## **3.1 Clock Frequency Target**

### **3.1.1 Definition**

The clock frequency target is the frequency at which the design must operate correctly.

**Unit:** MHz.

**Location:** `constraints/timing.xdc`, `create_clock` command.

### **3.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 50 MHz | Conservative. Easy closure. |
| 2 | **100 MHz** | Target. Matches ZedBoard oscillator. |
| 3 | 150 MHz | Aggressive. Needs pipelining. |
| 4 | 200 MHz | Very aggressive. Needs deep pipelining. |

### **3.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **50 MHz** | **100 MHz** | **150 MHz** | **200 MHz** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Clock period | 20 ns | **10 ns** | 6.67 ns | 5 ns |
| Timing closure effort | Low | **Medium** | High | Very high |
| Pipelining needed | No | **No** | Yes | Deep |
| Meets project goal | Below | **Yes** | Above | Above |
| Fits timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Partial | No |

### **3.1.4**

**Chosen: 100 MHz.**

The design targets 100 MHz. The ZedBoard provides a 100 MHz oscillator. The clock period is 10 ns.

### **3.1.5**

**Why not 50 MHz:**

- Below the project goal
- Does not demonstrate timing closure skill

**Why not 150 MHz or 200 MHz:**

- Requires pipelining that would change the architecture
- Does not fit the timeline

**Why 100 MHz:**

- Matches the ZedBoard oscillator
- Achievable with careful RTL
- Demonstrates timing closure
- Fits the timeline

### **3.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Must meet 10 ns period |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Must meet 10 ns period |
| Directory array | Yes | Read latency must fit |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Must meet 10 ns period |
| NIC | Yes | Must meet 10 ns period |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Must meet 10 ns period |
| Address map | Yes | Decode must fit |
| Verification | Yes | Post-route timing report |

### **3.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Matches board oscillator
- Demonstrable on hardware
- Publishable

**Loss:**

- Some RTL changes may be needed
- Timing closure is iterative

**Constraint:**

- All paths must pass at 100 MHz
- No path may be excluded except the reset synchronizer

**Verification:**

- Vivado Timing Summary is signed off
- Post-route timing is the final check

### **3.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 150 MHz closure | Pipelining | 1 month |
| 200 MHz closure | Deep pipelining | 2 months |

### **3.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Clock frequency | 100 MHz | Matches ZedBoard, achievable |
| Clock period | 10 ns | Derived from frequency |

## **3.2 XDC Constraints**

### **3.2.1 Definition**

The XDC file supplies the constraints that Vivado uses during synthesis, placement, and routing.

**Unit:** constraints.

**Location:** `constraints/timing.xdc`.

### **3.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Minimal constraints | Clock only. |
| 2 | Clock + I/O | Clock, pin assignments, I/O standard. |
| 3 | **Clock + I/O + false paths** | Adds false path declarations. |
| 4 | Full constraints | Adds multicycle paths, max delay, min delay. |

### **3.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Minimal** | **Clock + I/O** | **Clock + I/O + false** | **Full** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Tool knows clock | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Tool knows pins | No | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| False paths excluded | No | No | **Yes** | Yes |
| Multicycle paths | No | No | **No** | Yes |
| Effort | Low | Low | **Medium** | High |
| Meets project need | No | Partial | **Yes** | Yes |

### **3.2.4**

**Chosen: Clock + I/O + false paths.**

The XDC file contains the clock definition, clock uncertainty, input delay, output delay, pin assignments, and false path declarations.

### **3.2.5**

**Why not minimal:**

- Vivado does not know the pin assignments
- The bitstream would not program the board

**Why not full constraints:**

- Multicycle paths are not needed at 100 MHz
- Adds complexity without benefit

**Why not add more false paths:**

- False paths hide real timing issues
- The design has only one clock
- Only the reset path is genuinely asynchronous

**Why clock + I/O + false paths:**

- Vivado knows the clock
- Vivado knows the pins
- The reset path is correctly excluded
- No hidden timing issues

### **3.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Constrained by clock |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Constrained by clock |
| Directory array | Yes | Constrained by clock |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Constrained by clock |
| NIC | Yes | Constrained by clock |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Constrained by clock |
| Address map | Yes | Constrained by clock |
| Verification | Yes | Constraint file is signed off |

### **3.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Vivado knows the clock and pins
- Reset path excluded correctly
- No hidden timing issues

**Loss:**

- XDC file must be maintained

**Constraint:**

- All constraints must match the board
- False paths must be justified

**Verification:**

- Timing report signed off
- Bitstream runs on board

### **3.2.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multicycle paths | RTL analysis | 1 month |
| CDC constraints | Multi-clock design | 2 months |

### **3.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| XDC scope | Clock + I/O + false paths | Covers the design without hiding issues |

---

## **3.3 WNS and WHS Targets**

### **3.3.1 Definition**

WNS is the most negative setup slack across all timing paths. WHS is the most negative hold slack.

**Unit:** nanoseconds.

**Location:** Reported by Vivado Timing Summary after synthesis and after routing.

### **3.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | WNS ≥ 0 only | Setup only. |
| 2 | **WNS ≥ 0, WHS ≥ 0** | Setup and hold. |
| 3 | WNS ≥ 0.5, WHS ≥ 0.1 | Safety margin. |
| 4 | WNS ≥ 1.0, WHS ≥ 0.5 | Large margin. |

### **3.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **WNS only** | **WNS + WHS** | **Margin 0.5** | **Margin 1.0** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Setup met | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Hold met | Unknown | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Process variation margin | None | **None** | Small | Large |
| Effort | Low | **Low** | Medium | High |
| Meets project need | Partial | **Yes** | Overkill | Overkill |

### **3.3.4**

**Chosen: WNS ≥ 0, WHS ≥ 0, WPWS ≥ 3.750 ns.**

Targets:

- Worst Negative Slack (setup) ≥ 0 ns
- Worst Hold Slack ≥ 0 ns
- Worst Pulse Width Slack ≥ 3.750 ns
- Total Negative Slack = 0
- Total Hold Slack = 0
- Number of failing endpoints = 0

### **3.3.5**

**Why not WNS only:**

- Hold violations cause data corruption
- Both setup and hold must pass

**Why not large margins:**

- The FPGA is a fixed device
- Process variation is characterized by the tool
- The tool already includes margins

**Why WNS ≥ 0, WHS ≥ 0:**

- The design meets all user-specified constraints
- No path violates its required arrival time
- Standard signoff condition

### **3.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Setup path through tag compare |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Setup path through directory decision |
| Directory array | Yes | Read latency contributes to path |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Setup path through crossbar |
| NIC | Yes | Setup path through packet decode |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Setup path through credit counters |
| Address map | Yes | Setup path through decode |
| Verification | Yes | Post-route timing report |

### **3.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Design is functional on hardware
- Timing closure is demonstrated
- Publishable result

**Loss:**

- Some RTL changes may be needed
- Timing closure is iterative

**Constraint:**

- All paths must pass at 100 MHz
- No path may be excluded except the reset synchronizer

**Verification:**

- Vivado Timing Summary is signed off
- Post-route timing is the final check

### **3.3.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 150 MHz closure | Pipelining | 1 month |
| Margin analysis | Monte Carlo | 2 months |

### **3.3.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Setup target | WNS ≥ 0 | Standard signoff |
| Hold target | WHS ≥ 0 | Standard signoff |
| Pulse width target | WPWS ≥ 3.750 ns | Standard signoff |
| Failing endpoints | 0 | Standard signoff |

---

## **3.4 Critical Path Identification and Fix**

### **3.4.1 Definition**

A critical path is the timing path with the least slack. Identifying and fixing the critical path is the core activity of timing closure.

**Unit:** nanoseconds of slack.

**Location:** `reports/timing_summary.rpt`, `reports/critical_paths.rpt`.

### **3.4.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Identify only | Report the critical path. Do not fix. |
| 2 | Fix by constraint | Change the clock period or add false paths. |
| 3 | Fix by RTL | Change RTL to shorten the path. |
| 4 | **Fix by both RTL and constraints** | Combination. |

### **3.4.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Identify only** | **Fix by constraint** | **Fix by RTL** | **Fix by both** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Meets timing | No | Yes (artificially) | Yes | **Yes** |
| Correctness preserved | Yes | No | Yes | **Yes** |
| Effort | Low | Low | Medium | **Medium** |
| Publishable | No | No | Yes | **Yes** |

### **3.4.4**

**Chosen: Fix by both RTL and constraints.**

The critical path is identified by Vivado. The fix depends on the source of the delay.

| **Category** | **Symptom** | **Fix** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Long combinational logic | Many LUT levels | Restructure RTL to reduce logic depth |
| High fanout | One signal drives many loads | Duplicate the signal or reduce loads |
| Long routing | Path crosses the chip | Replicate logic near loads or use a floorplan |
| Wide mux | Large multiplexer | Split into pipeline stages or reduce width |
| Memory read latency | BRAM to logic | Register BRAM outputs |
| Crossbar depth | Input to output | Register crossbar outputs |

The design has three major critical paths:

1. **L2 tag compare to directory state decision.** This is the longest. It is fixed by restructuring the L2 controller so that the tag compare and the directory lookup are separate pipeline stages.
2. **Router arbitration to crossbar output.** This is the second longest. It is fixed by the four-stage router pipeline (RC, VA, SA, ST). The arbitration is in SA and the crossbar is in ST.
3. **NIC packet decode to cache interface.** This is the third longest. It is fixed by registering the packet decoder output.

### **3.4.5**

**Why not identify only:**

- Timing closure requires fixes
- Reporting alone does not close timing

**Why not fix by constraint only:**

- Changing the clock period defeats the purpose
- False paths hide real issues
- The design would not work on hardware

**Why not fix by RTL only:**

- Some constraints are genuinely needed (clock, I/O)
- The tool needs the constraints to know what to optimise

**Why fix by both:**

- Constraints tell the tool what matters
- RTL changes shorten the paths
- Both are needed for real closure

### **3.4.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Tag compare to state decision |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Directory read to action decision |
| Directory array | Yes | Read latency |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Arbitration to crossbar output |
| NIC | Yes | Packet decode to cache interface |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Credit counter updates |
| Address map | Yes | Decode logic depth |
| Verification | Yes | Post-synthesis and post-route timing |

### **3.4.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Real timing closure
- Design works on hardware
- Publishable

**Loss:**

- RTL changes may be needed
- Iterations between synthesis and RTL edit

**Constraint:**

- Each fix must preserve function
- Each fix must be re-simulated

**Verification:**

- Post-synthesis timing report
- Post-route timing report
- Gate-level simulation after fixes

### **3.4.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Physical synthesis | Floorplan | 2 weeks |
| Logic replication | Fanout analysis | 1 week |

### **3.4.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Critical path fix | RTL + constraints | Both needed for real closure |
| Fix categories | Six categories | Covers all common sources |

---

## **3.5 Retiming Strategy**

### **3.5.1 Definition**

Retiming moves registers across combinational logic to balance pipeline stages without changing function.

**Unit:** number of registers moved.

**Location:** Vivado `-retiming` synthesis option. Manual RTL changes when needed.

### **3.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No retiming | Registers stay where RTL puts them. |
| 2 | Automatic retiming | Vivado moves registers. |
| 3 | Manual retiming | RTL is rewritten to move registers. |
| 4 | **Combined automatic and manual** | Both. |

### **3.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **None** | **Automatic** | **Manual** | **Combined** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Balances pipeline | No | Yes | Yes | **Yes** |
| Preserves function | Yes | Yes | Yes | **Yes** |
| Debuggability | High | Low | High | **Medium** |
| Effort | None | Low | Medium | **Medium** |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | Yes | **Yes** |

### **3.5.4**

**Chosen: Combined automatic and manual.**

Automatic retiming is enabled in Vivado with `-retiming`. Manual retiming is used only when automatic retiming is not enough.

**Where retiming is allowed:**

| **Module** | **Retiming allowed?** | **Reason** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Pipeline registers between stages |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Pipeline registers between stages |
| Router crossbar | Yes | Pipeline registers between input and output |
| NIC packet decoder | Yes | Pipeline registers between stages |
| Credit counters | No | State must be exact at every cycle |

**Where retiming is disabled:**

- Reset synchronizer
- Any path that spans a handshake
- Any path that carries a state machine value

### **3.5.5**

**Why not none:**

- Automatic retiming is free
- It often closes timing without RTL changes

**Why not automatic only:**

- Some paths cannot be retimed automatically
- Manual retiming is needed for tight paths

**Why not manual only:**

- Manual retiming is labor intensive
- Automatic retiming handles most paths

**Why combined:**

- Automatic retiming runs first
- Manual retiming is applied only to the remaining critical paths

### **3.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Registers may be moved |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Registers may be moved |
| Directory array | No | SRAM outputs are not retimed |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Crossbar pipeline registers may be moved |
| NIC | Yes | Pipeline registers may be moved |
| Virtual channels | No | Credit counters cannot be retimed |
| Address map | Yes | Decode logic may be retimed |
| Verification | Yes | Post-synthesis simulation validates retimed netlist |

### **3.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Timing closure without RTL changes
- Balanced pipelines
- Preserved function

**Loss:**

- Some signals become harder to probe
- Retiming may change the number of cycles to the first output

**Constraint:**

- Retiming must not change protocol behavior
- Handshake paths must not be retimed
- State machines must not be retimed

**Verification:**

- Post-synthesis simulation matches RTL function
- Formal equivalence confirms function is preserved

### **3.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Register balancing | Manual analysis | 1 week |
| Register duplication | Fanout analysis | 1 week |
| Pipeline insertion | RTL redesign | 2 weeks |

### **3.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Retiming | Automatic + manual | Automatic closes most paths, manual closes the rest |
| Retiming disabled | Reset, handshakes, state | Function must be preserved |

---

# **4.Summary**

The design targets 100 MHz on the XC7Z020. The clock period is 10 ns. The XDC file defines the clock, sets clock uncertainty to 0.2 ns for setup and 0.1 ns for hold, sets I/O delays to 2.0 ns maximum and 0.5 ns minimum, and declares a false path for the reset synchronizer. The targets are WNS ≥ 0, WHS ≥ 0, WPWS ≥ 3.750 ns, TNS = 0, failing endpoints = 0. Critical paths are identified by Vivado and fixed by RTL changes, not by changing constraints. Six fix categories cover long combinational logic, high fanout, long routing, wide mux, memory read latency, and crossbar depth. The three major critical paths are the L2 tag compare to directory state, the router arbitration to crossbar output, and the NIC packet decode to cache interface. Retiming is enabled with the `-retiming` option and disabled for the reset synchronizer, handshake paths, and state registers. Automatic retiming handles most paths. Manual retiming closes the rest. The four-stage router pipeline splits the crossbar path. The L2 controller is restructured to shorten the tag compare path.

---
