### 📊 Area and Power Budgets

#### FPGA Target: ZedBoard (XC7Z020)

| Metric | Available | 2×2 Config (Guaranteed) | 4×4 Config (Target) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **LUTs** | 53,200 | ≤ 20,000 (38%) | ≤ 40,000 (75%) |
| **Registers (FF)** | 106,400 | ≤ 25,000 (24%) | ≤ 50,000 (47%) |
| **BRAM (36Kb tiles)** | 140 | ≤ 30 (21%) | ≤ 60 (43%) |
| **DSP Slices** | 220 | 0 (unused) | 0 (unused) |
| **Total Power** | ~2.0 W (typical load) | ≤ 1.5 W | ≤ 2.0 W |

> **Note:** The baseline ZedBoard with a moderate design consumes around 2 W total. The 4×4 configuration is a "stretch goal" because routing a full 16-tile mesh may hit congestion limits on this smaller device.
> 

#### ASIC Target: Sky130

| Metric | Budget | Rationale |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Cell Area** | ≤ 1.5 mm² | Leaves margin for clock tree and routing overhead |
| **Standard Cells** | ≤ 50,000 | Ensures place-and-route convergence |
| **Total Power** | ≤ 50 mW | Dominated by clock tree and SRAM leakage at 100 MHz |

---

### 🛠️ How to Measure Them

Measurement is a two-step process: **estimate** during design (for rapid iteration) and **validate** post-implementation (for the final paper).

#### FPGA Measurement (ZedBoard)

1. **Estimation (Pre-Synthesis):** Use **Xilinx Power Estimator (XPE)**. This spreadsheet tool estimates static and dynamic power based on your resource utilization and clock frequency before you even run synthesis.
2. **Post-Implementation (Vivado):** Run the **`report_power`** command in the Vivado Tcl console after implementation. This provides a detailed breakdown (logic, signals, BRAM, clocks) based on actual place-and-route data.
3. **On-Board Physical Measurement (Gold Standard):** Measure the voltage drop across the **shunt resistor** on the ZedBoard. You can use the **PMBus** interface on the board to read real-time voltage and current rail data via I2C, or connect an oscilloscope to the **J21 header** to capture power traces directly.

#### ASIC Measurement (Sky130)

1. **Synthesis (Yosys):** Get the baseline cell area and gate count from the synthesis report.
2. **Post-Layout (OpenLane/OpenSTA):** After place-and-route, use OpenSTA's **`report_power`** command. This is the most accurate tool-based estimate, accounting for actual wire parasitics and clock tree power.
3. **Sign-off (SPEF/VCD):** For the final paper, generate a **VCD (Value Change Dump)** file from post-layout simulation and feed it into OpenSTA along with the **SPEF** (Standard Parasitic Exchange Format) file. This "vector-based" power analysis is the industry-standard method for reporting accurate dynamic power.

---

### 🔗 Connecting Budgets to Your Architecture

These budgets directly support the design decisions we have already made:

- **Virtual Channels & Multi-Flit:** The 4 VCs and 9-flit packets are the primary drivers of **BRAM** and **Register** usage. The budget confirms there is headroom to implement them.
- **Mesh Scalability:** The 2×2 configuration is comfortably within the ZedBoard's resources. The 4×4 configuration sits at the edge of feasibility, which justifies using **simulation as the primary scaling vehicle**.
- **No DSP Usage:** Coherence and routing logic uses zero DSP slices. The budget accounts for this, confirming no multiplier is inferred.
- **Power as a Validation Metric:** Demonstrating a low power profile on the ZedBoard (e.g., < 1.5 W) is a strong result that can be highlighted in the white paper and paper submission.

Would you like me to detail the exact Tcl commands for generating these reports in Vivado or OpenLane, or should we move on to the next section of the white paper?




# How is the design placed and routed?

Placement and routing convert a gate-level netlist into a physical layout. The FPGA flow uses Vivado Place and Route on the XC7Z020. The ASIC flow uses OpenROAD for floorplan, placement, clock tree synthesis, and routing, with Magic for DRC and Netgen for LVS. The uniform tile layout is exploited by placing tiles in a grid that matches the 2×2 mesh. Each tile occupies a rectangular region of equal area. The mesh links are short because adjacent tiles are physically adjacent. The clock tree is balanced because the tiles are symmetric. Floorplanning assigns regions to tiles before placement. This reduces congestion and improves timing predictability. DRC and LVS are ASIC-only signoff steps, marked as future scope. The FPGA flow uses Vivado's built-in DRC. The same RTL drives both flows. Only the physical implementation differs.

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies how the design is placed and routed for two targets:

1. **FPGA** — Vivado Place and Route on the XC7Z020.
2. **ASIC** — OpenROAD for floorplan, placement, CTS, and routing, with Magic for DRC and Netgen for LVS.

The finding also specifies how the uniform tile layout is exploited to reduce congestion and improve timing. DRC and LVS are ASIC-only signoff steps, marked as future scope.

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **Floorplan** | The step that assigns regions of the die to blocks of the design. Decides area, I/O placement, and block positions. |
| **Placement** | The step that assigns each gate to a physical location within its region. |
| **Clock tree synthesis (CTS)** | The step that builds a balanced clock distribution network. |
| **Routing** | The step that connects placed gates with physical wires. |
| **Global routing** | A coarse routing step that plans the path of each net across the die. |
| **Detailed routing** | A fine routing step that assigns exact wire tracks to each net. |
| **Congestion** | The condition where more wires are needed in a region than the region can hold. |
| **DRC** | Design Rule Check. Verifies that the layout obeys foundry rules. |
| **LVS** | Layout Versus Schematic. Verifies that the layout matches the netlist. |
| **Region** | A rectangular area of the die assigned to a specific block. |
| **Tile pitch** | The width of one tile in physical units. Same for every tile. |
| **Clock skew** | The difference in clock arrival time at two flip-flops. |
| **Power grid** | The network of metal wires that distribute power and ground. |

---

# **3. Parameters**

---

## **3.1 Floorplanning**

### **3.1.1 Definition**

Floorplanning assigns regions of the die to blocks of the design before placement begins. It decides the die area, the core area, the I/O ring, and the position of each tile.

**Unit:** area in square micrometers (µm²) for ASIC. Number of clock regions for FPGA.

**Location:** `scripts/floorplan.tcl` for ASIC. FPGA floorplan is implicit and controlled by Vivado Place.

### **3.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Automatic floorplan | Tool decides everything. No manual regions. |
| 2 | Uniform grid floorplan | Tiles placed in an equal-area grid matching the mesh. |
| 3 | Hierarchical floorplan | Each tile is a hierarchy block with its own sub-floorplan. |
| 4 | Row-based floorplan | Tiles placed in horizontal rows. |

### **3.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Automatic** | **Uniform grid** | **Hierarchical** | **Row-based** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Matches mesh topology | No | **Yes** | Yes | Partial |
| Wire length between tiles | Unpredictable | **Short** | Short | Medium |
| Congestion | Unpredictable | **Low** | Low | Medium |
| Timing predictability | Low | **High** | High | Medium |
| Setup effort | Low | **Medium** | High | Medium |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | No | Yes |

### **3.1.4**

**Chosen: Uniform grid floorplan.**

Each tile occupies a rectangular region of equal area. The regions are arranged in a 2×2 grid matching the mesh topology. Adjacent tiles share edges so mesh links are short. Each tile region is subdivided into sub-regions for the L1 cache, L2 bank, directory, NIC, and router.

For the FPGA flow, the same principle applies. Tiles are placed in a uniform grid using Vivado's `create_pblock` and `add_cells_to_pblock` commands.

### **3.1.5**

**Why not automatic:**

- Wire length between tiles becomes unpredictable
- Congestion becomes unpredictable
- Timing closure becomes harder

**Why not hierarchical:**

- Each tile needs its own sub-floorplan
- Setup effort is too high for the 3-month timeline

**Why not row-based:**

- Does not match the 2×2 mesh topology
- Mesh links would cross rows

**Why uniform grid:**

- Matches the 2×2 mesh topology
- Short wires between adjacent tiles
- Low congestion in each tile region
- High timing predictability
- Exploits the identical tile structure
- Fits the timeline

### **3.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Placed in its tile region |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Placed in its tile region |
| Directory array | Yes | Placed near the L2 bank |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Placed at the tile edge facing neighbors |
| NIC | Yes | Placed between the cache and the router |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Placed with the router |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Layout inspected for tile symmetry |

### **3.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Predictable wire length
- Low congestion
- High timing predictability
- Exploits identical tiles

**Loss:**

- Manual region assignment

**Constraint:**

- Every tile must have equal area
- Tile regions must touch at edges for mesh links

**Verification:**

- Layout inspection confirms uniform grid
- Congestion report shows no hotspots

### **3.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4×4 grid floorplan | Tile count increase | 2 weeks |
| Non-uniform tiles (DMA) | DMA design | 1 month |
| Hierarchical tile floorplan | Sub-block partitioning | 1 month |

### **3.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Floorplan | Uniform grid | Matches mesh, short wires, low congestion |
| Tile area | Equal for all tiles | Exploits identical structure |
| Tile arrangement | 2×2 grid | Matches mesh topology |

---

## **3.2 Tile Layout Exploitation**

### **3.2.1 Definition**

Tile layout exploitation means using the identical structure of every tile to simplify placement, routing, and timing closure.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** `scripts/floorplan.tcl`. Tile regions defined once and replicated.

### **3.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No exploitation | Each tile placed independently. |
| 2 | Manual replication | Copy the tile floorplan to each region. |
| 3 | Automatic replication | Use pblock scripts to replicate the tile region. |
| 4 | Fully parameterised floorplan | Generate tile regions from a single parameterised script. |

### **3.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No exploitation** | **Manual** | **Automatic** | **Parameterised** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Setup effort | High | Medium | **Low** | Low |
| Consistency across tiles | Low | Medium | **High** | High |
| Scalability | Low | Low | **Medium** | High |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### **3.2.4**

**Chosen: Automatic replication.**

The floorplan script defines a tile region once and replicates it across the grid. Each region contains the same relative positions for the L1 cache, L2 bank, directory, NIC, and router. The mesh links are short because adjacent regions touch.

For FPGA, pblocks are created with `create_pblock` and cells are assigned with `add_cells_to_pblock`. For ASIC, OpenROAD regions are defined once and copied.

### **3.2.5**

**Why not no exploitation:**

- Each tile placement would differ
- Timing would vary tile to tile
- Debug would be harder

**Why not manual:**

- Error-prone
- Hard to keep consistent

**Why not parameterised:**

- More setup than needed for a 2×2 mesh
- Useful only at larger mesh sizes

**Why automatic:**

- Consistent across tiles
- Low setup effort
- Scalable to 4×4

### **3.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Same relative position in each tile |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Same relative position |
| Directory array | Yes | Same relative position |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Same relative position at the tile edge |
| NIC | Yes | Same relative position |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Same relative position |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Layout inspection confirms symmetry |

### **3.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Consistent timing across tiles
- Easy to debug
- Scalable

**Loss:**

- None significant

**Constraint:**

- All tiles must be identical
- Only the tile ID differs between tiles

**Verification:**

- Layout inspection confirms symmetry
- Timing report shows similar slack across tiles

### **3.2.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Parameterised N×N floorplan | Larger mesh | 2 weeks |
| Region-aware timing | Per-region analysis | 1 month |

### **3.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Tile layout exploitation | Automatic replication | Consistent, low effort, scalable |

---

## **3.3 FPGA Place and Route Flow**

### **3.3.1 Definition**

The FPGA place and route flow is the sequence of Vivado steps that place and route the netlist on the XC7Z020.

**Unit:** stages.

**Location:** `scripts/vivado_synth.tcl`.

### **3.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Default Vivado flow | Let Vivado decide placement and routing. |
| 2 | Flow with pblocks | Assign tiles to pblocks before placement. |
| 3 | Flow with pblocks and directives | Add Vivado directives for congestion or timing. |
| 4 | Manual placement | Place critical cells by hand. |

### **3.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Default** | **Pblocks** | **Pblocks + directives** | **Manual** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Predictability | Low | **High** | High | Very high |
| Setup effort | None | **Low** | Medium | High |
| Timing closure | Tool-dependent | **Good** | Better | Best |
| Scalability | Good | **Good** | Good | Poor |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | No |

### **3.3.4**

**Chosen: Pblocks and directives.**

Vivado Place and Route runs with:

1. **Pblock assignment.** Each tile is assigned to a pblock.
2. **Directives.** `PlaceDirective` set to `AltSpreadLogic_high` to reduce congestion. `RouteDirective` set to `AggressiveExplore` for timing.
3. **Post-route timing analysis.** Vivado Timing Summary checks WNS, WHS, and WPWS.

### **3.3.5**

**Why not default:**

- Predictability is low
- Tiles may not align with the mesh topology

**Why not manual:**

- Too slow for the timeline
- The design has 4 tiles with hundreds of cells each

**Why pblocks and directives:**

- Predictable tile placement
- Better timing closure
- Low setup effort
- Fits the timeline

### **3.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Placed in its pblock |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Placed in its pblock |
| Directory array | Yes | Placed in its pblock |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Placed at pblock edge |
| NIC | Yes | Placed in its pblock |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Placed with the router |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Post-route timing signed off |

### **3.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Predictable placement
- Better timing
- Exploits tile symmetry

**Loss:**

- pblock scripts must be maintained

**Constraint:**

- Pblocks must match the tile grid
- Cells must be assigned to the correct pblock

**Verification:**

- Post-route timing report
- Post-route congestion report

### **3.3.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Incremental place and route | Vivado incremental flow | 1 week |
| Per-tile pblock sizing | Dynamic grid | 2 weeks |

### **3.3.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| FPGA P&R | Pblocks + directives | Predictable, better timing |
| Placement directive | AltSpreadLogic_high | Reduce congestion |
| Route directive | AggressiveExplore | Timing closure |

The ASIC place and route flow is the sequence of OpenROAD steps that place and route the netlist for the Sky130 PDK.

## **3.5 Clock Tree Synthesis**

### **3.5.1 Definition**

Clock tree synthesis (CTS) builds a balanced clock distribution network that delivers the clock to every flip-flop with low skew.

**Unit:** clock skew in picoseconds.

**Location:** TritonCTS in the OpenLane flow. Vivado performs CTS automatically for FPGA.

### **3.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No CTS | Use a single clock buffer. |
| 2 | H-tree | Symmetric tree that balances skew. |
| 3 | Mesh clock | Grid of clock wires with low skew. |
| 4 | Tool-driven CTS | Let TritonCTS decide the tree structure. |

### **3.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No CTS** | **H-tree** | **Mesh** | **TritonCTS** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Skew | High | Low | Very low | **Low** |
| Power | Low | Medium | High | **Medium** |
| Complexity | None | Medium | High | **Automatic** |
| Fits Sky130 | Yes | Yes | Partial | **Yes** |
| Fits timeline | Yes | Yes | No | **Yes** |

### **3.5.4 Chosen Option**

**Chosen: TritonCTS.**

TritonCTS builds a balanced clock tree using the Sky130 clock buffers. Target skew: ≤ 50 ps. Target insertion delay: ≤ 500 ps.

For FPGA, Vivado performs CTS automatically. The clock tree is built into the device fabric.

### **3.5.5**

**Why not no CTS:**

- Skew too high
- Hold violations likely
- Timing closure would fail

**Why not H-tree:**

- Manual layout of the tree
- Does not adapt to placement

**Why not mesh:**

- Too much power
- Overkill for a 2×2 mesh

**Why TritonCTS:**

- Automatic
- Adapts to placement
- Fits Sky130
- Meets skew target

### **3.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Receives balanced clock |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Receives balanced clock |
| Directory array | Yes | Receives balanced clock |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Receives balanced clock |
| NIC | Yes | Receives balanced clock |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Receives balanced clock |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Skew report signed off |

### **3.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Low skew
- Balanced clock
- Hold violations avoided

**Loss:**

- Clock buffers add area
- Insertion delay

**Constraint:**

- Skew target ≤ 50 ps
- Insertion delay ≤ 500 ps

**Verification:**

- Clock skew report
- Hold slack report

### **3.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Clock gating | Clock gating cells | 1 month |
| Multi-clock domains | CDC logic | 2 months |

### **3.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| CTS tool | TritonCTS | Automatic, meets skew target |
| Skew target | ≤ 50 ps | Ensures hold timing |
| Insertion delay | ≤ 500 ps | Reasonable for 2×2 mesh |

## **3.8 Power Grid (ASIC, Future Scope)**

### **3.8.1 Definition**

The power grid is the network of metal wires that distributes power and ground to every cell.

**Unit:** voltage drop in millivolts (mV).

**Location:** OpenROAD power grid script in OpenLane.

### **3.8.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No power grid | Cells connect to power directly. |
| 2 | Single ring | One ring around the core. |
| 3 | Multi-ring + stripes | Rings plus horizontal and vertical stripes. |
| 4 | Mesh grid | Full mesh of horizontal and vertical stripes. |

### **3.8.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No grid** | **Single ring** | **Multi-ring + stripes** | **Mesh** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IR drop | High | Medium | **Low** | Very low |
| Area overhead | None | Low | **Medium** | High |
| Complexity | Low | Low | **Medium** | High |
| Fits Sky130 | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Fits timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | No |

### **3.8.4**

**Chosen: Multi-ring + stripes.**

The power grid has:

1. A power ring around the core.
2. Horizontal stripes on metal 4.
3. Vertical stripes on metal 5.
4. A ring around each tile.

Target IR drop: ≤ 5% of VDD.

### **3.8.5**

**Why not no grid:**

- IR drop too high
- Cells would not receive reliable power

**Why not single ring:**

- IR drop still high in the core

**Why not mesh:**

- Too much area
- Overkill for a 2×2 mesh

**Why multi-ring + stripes:**

- Low IR drop
- Reasonable area
- Fits Sky130
- Fits the timeline

### **3.8.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Powered by the grid |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Powered by the grid |
| Directory array | Yes | Powered by the grid |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Powered by the grid |
| NIC | Yes | Powered by the grid |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Powered by the grid |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | IR drop report signed off |

### **3.8.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Low IR drop
- Reliable power delivery

**Loss:**

- Area overhead
- Routing resources consumed by power

**Constraint:**

- IR drop ≤ 5% of VDD
- EM current density within limits

**Verification:**

- IR drop report
- EM report

### **3.8.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multi-voltage domains | Level shifters | 3 months |
| Power gating | Power switches | 2 months |

### **3.8.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Power grid | Multi-ring + stripes | Low IR drop, reasonable area |
| IR drop target | ≤ 5% of VDD | Reliable power |
