# Debug Infrastructure — Verdict Document

---

## 1. Purpose & Method

This document answers:

> **What debug infrastructure does this project use for FPGA validation, and why?**
> 

It covers the complete debug infrastructure required by the project plan — VIO core, ILA core, bitstream management, observability monitors, and dump tasks — and extends beyond the plan where additional value is available. It presents all genuinely distinct alternatives from published NoC debug research, evaluates each with strengths first, then costs, then constraint check, and selects the option that best fits this project.

The reasoning follows the same structure used in all previous verdict documents:

1. State project constraints.
2. State each alternative's strengths honestly.
3. State each alternative's costs honestly.
4. Check against constraints.
5. Select the option that best fits the project.
6. Provide verifiable estimates and confidence statements.

---

## 2. Project Constraints

| ID | Constraint | Source | Consequence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **D1** | Target FPGA is ZedBoard (XC7Z020) with Vivado 2024.x | FPGA & ASIC Platforms Verdict Document | Xilinx on-chip debug IP available; ~53,200 LUTs, 140 BRAMs |
| **D2** | 2×2 tile count on FPGA | FPGA & ASIC Platforms Verdict Document | Debug logic must fit within remaining headroom (~21K LUTs, ~88 BRAMs) |
| **D3** | 100 MHz PL clock | FPGA & ASIC Platforms Verdict Document | Debug paths must close timing at 10 ns period |
| **D4** | NoC + MESI coherence design | Coherence Protocol Selection Verdict Document; Directory & Message Set Verdict Document | Protocol debug requires temporal sequence visibility, not just current values |
| **D5** | Formal verification in SymbiYosys | Verification Methodology Verdict Document | SVA shared between formal and hardware debug |
| **D6** | No external logic analyzer available | FPGA & ASIC Platforms Verdict Document | Debug must be fully on-chip |
| **D7** | Simulation (Verilator / Vivado xsim) available | Verification Methodology Verdict Document | Functional debug happens in simulation first |
| **D8** | Team of 3–5 people | Project scope | Debug must be reproducible and shareable |
| **D9** | IEEE 1500 / JTAG boundary scan available | FPGA & ASIC Platforms Verdict Document | Standard JTAG access to PL fabric |

---

## 3. Sub-Decision 1 — Primary On-Chip Debug Architecture

**What it means:** What is the main mechanism for observing and controlling the design at runtime on the FPGA?

---

### 3.1 Alternative A — Xilinx Vivado Debug IP Suite

**What it means:** Use the standard Xilinx on-chip debug suite delivered within Vivado, consisting of Integrated Logic Analyzer (ILA), Virtual Input/Output (VIO), and the AXI Debug Hub, connected to the host via JTAG.

| Component | Function |
| --- | --- |
| **ILA** | Waveform capture with programmable triggers, BRAM-buffered |
| **VIO** | Runtime probe of signal values and injection of stimuli without re-synthesis |
| **AXI Debug Hub** | Connects JTAG to multiple ILA/VIO cores through a single hub; supports up to 64 debug cores |
| **System ILA** | AXI-aware ILA for transaction-level protocol analysis |

**Strengths:**

- Native to Vivado; no additional licenses or IP purchase
- ILA provides temporal waveform capture with programmable triggers
- VIO provides runtime probe and injection without re-synthesis
- Debug Hub consolidates multiple debug cores onto a single JTAG chain
- Mature and extensively documented; standard practice in academic NoC projects
- JTAG readout — no external hardware required
- Automatic probe discovery and waveform viewer integration in Vivado
- Cross-triggering between multiple ILA instances supported
- Global timestamping available through the Debug Hub

**Costs:**

- Buffer size limited by BRAM budget — observation window constrained
- Readout of large traces takes seconds (JTAG bandwidth)
- Re-synthesis required to change the probe set
- State-mode only — samples synchronous to a specified clock; cannot directly capture cross-clock-domain timing relationships
- Not NoC-aware — probes raw signals, not protocol transactions

**Constraint check:** D1, D3, D4, D5, D6, D7, D8, D9 — PASS. D2 — PASS (~1,100 LUTs / ~3 BRAMs).

---

### 3.2 Alternative B — Custom NoC Debug Unit

**What it means:** A dedicated RTL debug block inserted between the tile's local interface and its router ports. In normal mode it passes signals through; in test mode it disconnects the tile from the NoC and injects traced transactions. Published in NoC debug research (Tang & Xu; Politecnico di Torino thesis).

| Feature | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Insertion point** | Between tile interface and router ports |
| **Normal mode** | Pass-through; zero impact on NoC operation |
| **Test mode** | NoC disconnected; debug unit drives tile interface with traced instructions |
| **Trace source** | Simulation trace of normal operation; replay preserves ordering |
| **Backpressure handling** | `stop` and `void` signals passed through to prevent message loss |

**Strengths:**

- **NoC-aware** — understands tile protocol, not just raw signals
- **Replay capability** — can reproduce exact NoC traffic from a simulation trace
- **Deeper debugging** — can inject test scenarios and observe tile responses step-by-step
- **Fully on-chip** — no external logic analyzer required
- **Can be replicated per tile** for distributed observation
- **Protocol walkthrough** — allows single-transaction stepping for root-cause analysis

**Costs:**

- **Must be designed and verified as a new RTL block.** The debug unit itself becomes a block under verification. A debug tool with bugs is worse than no debug tool.
- **Consumes LUT/BRAM resources** — approximately 2,000 LUTs per debug tile, in addition to the design itself.
- **Requires trace generation infrastructure** — simulation traces must be formatted for the debug unit. This is an additional toolchain element.
- **Maintenance coupling** — the debug unit must be updated every time the tile interface changes. Any protocol change to the tile-to-router interface requires a corresponding change to the debug unit.
- **No Vivado GUI integration** — trace must be decoded with custom scripts; no waveform viewer.
- **Verification cost is additive** — the debug unit's correctness properties must be proved separately from the main design.

**Constraint check:** D1, D4, D6 — PASS. D2 — borderline (~2,000 LUTs per monitored tile; feasible for 1–2 tiles but consumes headroom). D3 — requires pipelining. D5 — must be verified separately. D7 — compatible. D8 — PASS but adds a verification burden the team must own.

**Honest assessment:** The Custom NoC Debug Unit is a **more capable debug architecture** for NoC-specific debugging than Vivado Debug IP. It provides replay, NoC-aware tracing, and step-by-step protocol walkthrough — capabilities Vivado IP does not have. It is not selected because it adds design effort, verification effort, and maintenance coupling that the project does not need, given that the project's debug requirements are already met by Vivado Debug IP. This is a trade-off of **capability vs. cost**, not a schedule issue.

---

### 3.3 Alternative C — Debug Probe (Between Core and NI)

**What it means:** A probe inserted between the core-under-debug and its network interface, supporting concurrent debug access to multiple cores and the NoC in a unified architecture. Published by Tang & Xu as a multi-core NoC debug platform.

| Feature | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Insertion point** | Between core and network interface |
| **Multi-core support** | Concurrent debug of multiple cores |
| **NoC transaction analysis** | Packet-level tracing |
| **Cross-triggering** | One core's event triggers another's capture |
| **Global timestamping** | Synchronized timestamps across tiles |

**Strengths:**

- Unified core + NoC debug in one architecture
- Cross-triggering — essential for coherence protocol debug
- Global timestamping — correlates events across tiles
- In-depth NoC transaction analysis — packet-level visibility
- Scales to multi-core platforms

**Costs:**

- **Area cost scales with tile count** — one probe per core.
- **NoC traffic overhead** — debug data consumes network bandwidth.
- **Complex design** — requires a multi-core debug controller and network.
- **Verification burden** — additional RTL block to verify.
- **Academic prototype** — not a production tool; documentation and reference designs are limited.

**Constraint check:** D1, D4, D6 — PASS. D2 — area cost proportional to tile count; borderline for 2×2. D5 — must be verified separately. D7 — compatible. D8 — PASS with additional verification burden.

**Honest assessment:** The Debug Probe is a **valid multi-core debug architecture**. It provides unified core + NoC debug with cross-triggering and timestamping. It is not selected because it adds area, verification burden, and complexity that the project does not need. This is a trade-off of **scale vs. cost**, not a schedule issue.

---

### 3.4 Alternative D — NoC Monitoring Service (NoCMS)

**What it means:** Hardware probes placed at NoC routers that observe and filter network traffic. Traces are buffered on-chip or sent off-chip through a dedicated port. Published in NoC monitoring research (TU Eindhoven).

| Feature | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Observation point** | Router input/output ports |
| **Filtering** | Programmable filters on address, transaction type, or source/destination |
| **Buffering** | On-chip trace buffer or off-chip through trace port |
| **Analysis** | Post-processing extracts traffic statistics and event sequences |

**Strengths:**

- Non-intrusive — observes traffic without modifying it
- Scalable to large NoCs
- Supports long-run statistics and short-run event traces
- Programmable filtering reduces trace volume
- Can be distributed across multiple routers

**Costs:**

- Requires dedicated trace buffer or trace port hardware
- Off-chip trace port not available on ZedBoard without additional hardware
- On-chip trace buffer limited by BRAM budget
- Design and verification effort similar to Alternative B
- Adds an RTL block under verification

**Constraint check:** D1 — PASS. D2 — borderline; trace buffer consumes BRAM. D4 — PASS. D5 — additional verification. D8 — PASS with additional verification burden.

**Honest assessment:** NoCMS is a valid **monitoring-focused** architecture. It is not selected because its capabilities overlap with Vivado ILA for this project's scale, and the trace buffer infrastructure is not justified by the debugging needs.

---

### 3.5 Alternative E — Embedded SVA with Trip-Wire Registers

**What it means:** SystemVerilog Assertions embedded in the RTL that monitor protocol invariants (single-writer, multiple-reader, data consistency, directory consistency). On violation, a trip-wire register is set, readable via JTAG.

**Strengths:**

- Fail-fast detection — assertion fires immediately on violation
- Zero BRAM cost — assertions are combinational or small sequential
- Shared with formal verification — same SVA used in SymbiYosys
- Covers protocol invariants directly
- Minimal area overhead

**Costs:**

- No temporal history — knows that a violation occurred, not the sequence leading to it
- Complement, not replacement — must be paired with waveform capture
- Violation-only — does not help with performance debug
- No injection capability

**Constraint check:** All constraints pass as a **complement**. Insufficient as primary debug architecture.

---

### 3.6 Comparison

| Criterion | A (Vivado IP) | B (Custom Tile) | C (Probe) | D (NoCMS) | E (SVA) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| D2 FPGA fit | ✅ ~2% | ⚠ ~4% | ⚠ variable | ⚠ variable | ✅ <1% |
| D3 timing impact | ✅ ~1 ns | ⚠ pipelined | ⚠ | ⚠ | ✅ minimal |
| D4 temporal visibility | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ❌ |
| D5 formal alignment | ⚠ | ❌ | ❌ | ❌ | ✅ |
| D6 fully on-chip | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ⚠ (trace port) | ✅ |
| Replay capability | ❌ | ✅ | ❌ | ❌ | ❌ |
| Cross-triggering | ✅ | ⚠ | ✅ | ⚠ | ❌ |
| NoC-aware | ⚠ | ✅ | ✅ | ✅ | ⚠ |
| Additional RTL to verify | None | ✅ (debug unit) | ✅ (probe) | ✅ (monitor) | Minimal |
| Maintenance coupling | None | Tile interface | Core interface | Router interface | None |
| Production maturity | High | Research | Research | Research | High |

**Selected: Alternative A (Vivado Debug IP Suite) as primary, with Alternative E (SVA) as complement.**

**Reasoning — stated honestly:**

Vivado Debug IP is selected as the primary debug architecture because it satisfies every project constraint at minimal cost. It provides temporal waveform capture (ILA), runtime injection (VIO), cross-triggering (Debug Hub), and integration with the simulation-based verification flow. It has **no additional RTL block to verify**, **no maintenance coupling** to any design interface, and **no design effort** beyond instantiating IP from the catalog.

The Custom NoC Debug Unit (Alternative B) is a **more capable architecture for NoC-specific debugging**. It provides replay, NoC-aware tracing, and step-by-step protocol walkthrough that Vivado IP cannot. It is not selected because:

- **It adds a new RTL block that must be verified.** The debug unit itself becomes part of the verification burden. Any bug in the debug unit would corrupt the very data being used to debug the design.
- **It creates maintenance coupling.** Every change to the tile-to-router interface requires a corresponding change to the debug unit.
- **Its added capabilities are not required by this project.** Vivado ILA with targeted probes already provides the temporal visibility needed for MESI protocol debug. The replay and NoC-aware tracing capabilities are valuable for larger or more complex designs but do not solve a problem this project has.
- **It adds complexity risk.** More RTL means more places for bugs. A debug tool with bugs is worse than no debug tool.

The Debug Probe (Alternative C) and NoCMS (Alternative D) share the same trade-off: more capability, but at the cost of additional RTL to verify, additional maintenance coupling, and complexity risk that the project does not need.

**This is a capability-vs-cost trade-off, not a schedule trade-off.** Even with unlimited design time, Vivado Debug IP remains the right choice for this project because its capabilities are sufficient, and the additional capabilities of the alternatives are not required.

---

## 4. Sub-Decision 2 — Probe Strategy

**What it means:** Which internal signals are connected to the debug infrastructure?

### 4.1 Alternative A — Targeted Probes on Key Interfaces (Selected)

| Probe Target | Width (bits) | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 → Directory request bus | 64 | Observe GETS / GETM / PUTS / PUTM / UNBLOCK |
| Directory → L1 response bus | 64 | Observe DATA / INV / INVACK / FWD_GETS / FWD_GETM / DATAFWD |
| Directory state register | 2 per line | Observe MESI transitions |
| Router input port (one selected) | 64 | Observe flits entering the network |
| Router output port (one selected) | 64 | Observe flits leaving the network |
| NIC TX/RX FIFO status | 16 | Observe backpressure and flow |
| Trip-wire register (from SVA) | 32 | Observe assertion failures |

**Strengths:**

- Focused observation — captures protocol events without capturing every signal
- Low cost — 2 ILA instances and 1 VIO
- Meaningful triggers — can trigger on protocol events directly
- Reproducible — same probe set for every run
- Covers all 14 coherence message types and 4 VC classes

**Costs:**

- Blind spots — signals not on the list are not observable at runtime

**Constraint check:** D2, D4, D8 — PASS.

### 4.2 Alternative B — Broad Probe Coverage

**Strengths:** Maximum visibility.
**Costs:** 10+ ILA instances; does not fit XC7Z020 headroom.
**Constraint check:** D2 — FAIL.

**Selected: A — Targeted probes.** Fits FPGA budget and captures events that matter for coherence debug.

---

## 5. Sub-Decision 3 — Simulation vs FPGA Debug Split

**What it means:** Which bugs are debugged in simulation, which on FPGA?

### 5.1 Alternative A — Simulation-First, FPGA for Bring-Up (Selected)

**What it means:** All functional bugs are debugged in simulation using Verilator / Vivado xsim with the pyuvm verification environment. FPGA is used only for verifying that the design runs on hardware and for observing timing-related behavior.

**Strengths:**

- Simulation iterates 100× faster — no synthesis cycles
- Full visibility — any signal can be observed
- Reproducible — same seed gives same result
- Aligns with D5 — formal verification and functional debug share the same environment
- Waveform dumps and coverage collection in simulation

**Costs:**

- Requires mature simulation environment — pyuvm + scoreboard + assertions
- Some bugs are hardware-only — reset glitches, CDC issues, timing marginality

**Constraint check:** D5, D7 — PASS.

### 5.2 Alternative B — FPGA-First Debug

**Strengths:** Real hardware behavior from day one.
**Costs:** Slow iteration; limited visibility; not reproducible.
**Constraint check:** D7 — PASS with significant iteration cost.

**Selected: A — Simulation-first, FPGA for bring-up.**

---

## 6. Bitstream Management

The project produces two bitstreams for each release.

| Bitstream | Purpose | Debug IP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Debug bitstream** | FPGA bring-up, ILA/VIO capture, hardware-in-the-loop | ILA + VIO + Debug Hub enabled |
| **Production bitstream** | Final timing validation, resource utilization measurement | Debug IP disabled |

### 6.1 Management Rules

- Debug IP is controlled by `ifdef DEBUG` in RTL and TCL scripts
- Two synthesis runs:
    - `vivado -mode batch -source build_debug.tcl` → `design_debug.bit`
    - `vivado -mode batch -source build_prod.tcl` → `design_prod.bit`
- Both bitstreams stored in version control with naming convention `<module>_<mode>_<date>.bit`
- Debug bitstream used for all ILA/VIO sessions
- Production bitstream used for final timing and resource validation
- Bitstream hashes documented in the build log for reproducibility

### 6.2 Programming Flow

- JTAG programming through Vivado Hardware Manager
- PS-side SD card boot possible for standalone operation
- Both bitstreams include the Zynq PS configuration

---

## 7. Observability Monitors

Runtime monitors provide continuous visibility into design behavior. Each monitor is a small counter or status register readable via JTAG.

| Monitor | Function | Implementation | Width |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **L1 miss rate monitor** | Counts L1 misses per tile | Counter per tile | 32 bits |
| **NoC latency monitor** | Measures transaction latency | Timestamp at request; timestamp at response | 32 bits × 2 |
| **Directory occupancy monitor** | Tracks directory line states (I/S/E/M histogram) | 4 counters per bank | 4 × 16 bits |
| **NACK/retry monitor** | Counts NACKs per VC class | 4 counters per tile | 4 × 16 bits |
| **NoC throughput monitor** | Counts flits per VC class per direction | 4 × 4 counters per tile | 16 × 16 bits |
| **MSHR occupancy monitor** | Current MSHR entries in use | Counter per tile | 8 bits |
| **Error monitor** | Sticky trip-wire for SVA violations | Sticky register per assertion | 1 bit × N |
| **Cache hit/miss monitor** | Per-set hit/miss histogram | 4 × 2 counters per tile | 4 × 2 × 16 bits |

**Readout:** All monitors readable via JTAG through a memory-mapped register file connected to the Debug Hub.

**Sampling:** Monitors are sampled on read; do not reset on read unless explicitly cleared.

---

## 8. Dump Tasks

Dump tasks capture detailed data for post-mortem analysis.

| Task | Trigger | Output | Storage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Waveform dump on violation** | SVA trip-wire fires | VCD file (simulation) | Disk |
| **ILA capture on protocol event** | Trigger condition (M-state entry, INV sent, NACK issued) | ILA BRAM buffer | JTAG readout to disk |
| **Transaction trace dump** | Periodic or event-driven | Text log of NoC transactions | Host machine |
| **Directory state snapshot** | Manual (VIO trigger) | Snapshot of all directory entries | JTAG readout |
| **Cache state snapshot** | Manual (VIO trigger) | Snapshot of all L1 and L2 tag/state arrays | JTAG readout |
| **Performance counter dump** | Manual or periodic | CSV of all monitors | Host machine |

### 8.1 Dump Task Configuration

Dump tasks are configured through VIO registers:

- `dump_enable` — 1-bit per dump task
- `dump_trigger_condition` — 8-bit (which SVA, which event, manual)
- `dump_length` — 16-bit (samples to capture for ILA)
- `dump_destination` — 2-bit (VCD / JTAG / host)

Configuration is set at runtime without re-synthesis.

---

## 9. Cross-Triggering and Timestamping

### 9.1 Cross-Triggering

The AXI Debug Hub supports cross-triggering between multiple ILA instances. Configuration:

- One ILA instance (e.g., L1-dir request bus) is designated the **trigger master**
- Other ILA instances (e.g., directory state, router port) are **trigger slaves**
- When the master fires, all slaves capture simultaneously
- Captured data is time-aligned across probes

**Use case:** When a protocol violation is detected at the directory, the request bus and router port captures are triggered at the same cycle, allowing reconstruction of the transaction sequence that led to the violation.

### 9.2 Timestamping

A global 64-bit free-running counter provides timestamps to all monitors and debug captures.

- Counter runs at 100 MHz
- Timestamps embedded in ILA probe set for correlation
- Monitors record first-event and last-event timestamps
- Timestamp overflow at 2^64 cycles ≈ 5,849 years — effectively never

**Use case:** Correlate events across tiles by comparing timestamps. For example, "did tile 2 send GETM before or after tile 5 sent INVACK?"

---

## 10. Estimated Resource Footprint

| Component | LUTs (approx.) | BRAMs (approx.) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ILA × 2 (targeted probes) | ~500 | ~3 |
| VIO × 1 | ~100 | 0 |
| AXI Debug Hub × 1 | ~50 | 0 |
| SVA monitor logic | ~50 | 0 |
| Observability monitors (all) | ~200 | 0 |
| Timestamp counter | ~50 | 0 |
| JTAG register file | ~150 | 0 |
| **Total** | **~1,100** | **~3** |

**Impact on XC7Z020:** ~1,100 LUTs (2.1% of 53,200) and ~3 BRAMs (2.1% of 140). Fits comfortably within the ~21K LUT headroom available after the 2×2 design.

---

## 11. Timing Impact

Debug IP adds approximately 1 ns to probed critical paths. At 100 MHz (10 ns period):

| Scenario | Design Slack | Debug Impact | Result |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Comfortable design | ≥ 3 ns | ~1 ns | ✅ Timing closes |
| Marginal design | 2 ns | ~1 ns | ✅ Timing closes with margin |
| Tight design | 1 ns | ~1 ns | ⚠ Re-timing or debug disabled |

**Mitigation:** If timing is tight, use a separate debug bitstream (debug enabled) and production bitstream (debug disabled). Both are synthesized from the same RTL with `ifdef DEBUG` controlling debug IP instantiation.

---

## 12. Debug Workflow

```
   RTL change
      │
      ▼
   Simulation (Verilator / xsim)
      │
      ├── PASS → Synthesize → FPGA bitstream
      │
      └── FAIL → Waveform dump (VCD) → Fix

   FPGA bring-up:
      │
      ├── Monitor readout (performance counters)
      │
      ├── ILA trigger fires → Capture → Readout
      │
      ├── VIO injects stimuli → Observe response
      │
      └── SVA trip wire → Diagnose via ILA

   Root cause found:
      │
      └── Return to RTL change
```

---

## 13. Honest Reasoning

| Selection | Positive reason | What would change it |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Vivado Debug IP | Native; zero additional RTL; provides temporal capture + injection + cross-triggering | NoC-specific replay capability becomes required for a bug class ILA cannot resolve |
| SVA complement | Shared with formal; fail-fast | None — SVA is standard practice |
| Targeted probes | Fits headroom; meaningful triggers | Larger BRAM budget → broader probes |
| Simulation-first | Fast iteration; reproducible | Hardware-only bug pattern emerges |
| Cross-triggering via Debug Hub | Reconstructs transaction sequences across tiles | None — essential for coherence debug |
| Global timestamping | Correlates events across tiles | None — essential for coherence debug |

---

## 14. What Would Change the Verdict

| If this changes… | Then consider… |
| --- | --- |
| A bug class requires NoC-aware replay that ILA cannot provide | Custom NoC Debug Unit (Alternative B) |
| Tile interface stabilizes and no further protocol changes are expected | Custom NoC Debug Unit becomes lower-maintenance |
| Debug unit can share verification with the main verification effort | Custom NoC Debug Unit's verification burden drops |
| Multi-core cross-triggering becomes essential and ILA cross-triggering is insufficient | Debug Probe (Alternative C) |
| Off-chip trace port added to the board | NoCMS (Alternative D) |

**Note:** These are capability-driven conditions, not schedule-driven conditions. The choice between Vivado Debug IP and the custom alternatives is fundamentally about **whether the additional NoC-specific capability is worth the additional RTL, verification, and maintenance cost** — not about how much time the project has.

---

## 15. Confidence Statement

For this project, the debug infrastructure consists of the Xilinx Vivado Debug IP Suite — ILA for waveform capture, VIO for runtime probe and injection, AXI Debug Hub for multi-core connection and cross-triggering, and System ILA for transaction-level analysis — connected via JTAG. This is complemented by embedded SystemVerilog Assertions with trip-wire registers for fail-fast detection. Bitstream management uses two configurations: debug and production. Observability monitors track miss rates, NoC latency, directory occupancy, NACK/retry counts, and MSHR occupancy. Dump tasks capture waveforms on protocol violations, transaction traces on events, and state snapshots on demand. Global timestamping correlates events across tiles. Total debug footprint is approximately 1,100 LUTs and 3 BRAMs — 2.1% of XC7Z020 resources.

Alternatives exist — Custom NoC Debug Unit, Debug Probe with multi-core support, NoC Monitoring Service — and each is a valid published architecture that provides NoC-specific capabilities beyond what Vivado Debug IP offers. The Custom NoC Debug Unit, in particular, provides **replay, NoC-aware tracing, and step-by-step protocol walkthrough** that Vivado IP does not have. These alternatives are not selected because they add **a new RTL block to verify, maintenance coupling to a design interface, and complexity risk** that the project does not need, given that Vivado ILA with targeted probes already provides the temporal visibility required for MESI protocol debug. This is a **capability-vs-cost trade-off**, not a schedule-driven decision. Even with unlimited design time, Vivado Debug IP remains the right choice for this project because its capabilities are sufficient, and the additional capabilities of the alternatives are not required.

Should the project later encounter a bug class that requires NoC-aware replay or step-by-step protocol walkthrough — capabilities that ILA cannot provide — the Custom NoC Debug Unit should be reconsidered.

---

## 16. Final Decision

| Selection | Value |
| --- | --- |
| **Primary debug** | Xilinx Vivado Debug IP — ILA + VIO + AXI Debug Hub + System ILA |
| **Complement** | Embedded SVA with trip-wire registers |
| **Probe strategy** | Targeted interfaces (L1-dir buses, directory state, one router port, NIC status) |
| **Bitstream management** | Separate debug and production bitstreams via `ifdef DEBUG` |
| **Observability** | Miss rate, NoC latency, directory occupancy, NACK/retry, MSHR, error monitors |
| **Dump tasks** | Waveform on violation, ILA capture on event, transaction traces, state snapshots |
| **Cross-triggering** | Supported via AXI Debug Hub |
| **Timestamping** | Global 64-bit free-running counter |
| **Simulation debug** | Verilator / Vivado xsim |
| **Estimated resource cost** | ~1,100 LUTs, ~3 BRAMs |

---


# L1, L2, and Directory State Machines

## 1. Three Interacting State Machines

The coherence protocol is implemented by three state machines:

| FSM | Where | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 controller FSM | Each L1 cache | Serves core requests, reacts to snoops |
| L2 controller FSM | Each L2 bank | Serves coherence requests, manages directory |
| Directory entry state | Per line in L2 bank | Records who holds the line and in what state |

The directory entry is not a separate FSM in the traditional sense. It is a per-line state variable that the L2 controller reads and writes. The L2 controller's FSM uses the directory state to decide what to do.

## 2. L1 Controller FSM

### 2.1 States

| State | Meaning | Duration |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `IDLE` | No request in progress | Until a request or snoop arrives |
| `COMPARE` | Tag lookup and hit/miss decision | 1 cycle |
| `ALLOCATE` | Victim reserved, waiting for data | Until data arrives |
| `INITIATE_BACK` | Issuing write-back for victim | 1–2 cycles |
| `SNOOP` | Handling a coherence request from L2 | Until snoop completes |
| `DONE` | Transaction complete, respond to core | 1 cycle |

### 2.2 Transitions

```
   IDLE
     │
     ├── core request arrives ──► COMPARE
     │
     └── snoop arrives ──► SNOOP

   COMPARE
     │
     ├── hit ──► DONE
     │
     └── miss ──► ALLOCATE (if victim clean)
                   or
                   INITIATE_BACK (if victim dirty)

   ALLOCATE
     │
     ├── data arrives ──► DONE
     │
     └── snoop arrives ──► SNOOP

   INITIATE_BACK
     │
     └── write-back sent ──► ALLOCATE

   SNOOP
     │
     └── snoop handled ──► IDLE

   DONE
     │
     └── response to core ──► IDLE
```

### 2.3 Hit and Miss Decision Logic

On entering `COMPARE`, the L1 reads the tag array for the set indexed by `addr[L1_SET_LSB +: L1_SET_IDX_WIDTH]`. It compares the tag of all 4 ways against `addr[31:L1_TAG_LSB]`.

```
for (int w = 0; w < WAYS; w++) begin
  hit_way[w] = valid[w] && (tag[w] == req_tag);
end
hit = |hit_way;
```

If `hit` is true, the controller checks the MESI state of the hit way:

| State | Load | Store |
| --- | --- | --- |
| M | Hit, serve | Hit, serve |
| E | Hit, serve | Hit, silent upgrade to M |
| S | Hit, serve | Miss (need upgrade), issue GETM |
| I | Miss, issue GETS | Miss, issue GETM |

If `hit` is false, it is a miss. The controller selects a victim way.

### 2.4 Victim Selection

Victim selection happens in `COMPARE` when a miss is detected.

**True LRU (L1):** The victim is the way with `age == 3`.

```
victim_way = 2'd0;
for (int w = 0; w < 4; w++)
  if (age[set_idx][w] == 2'd3)
    victim_way = w[1:0];
```

**Victim state check:**

| Victim MESI state | Action |
| --- | --- |
| I | No write-back. Go directly to ALLOCATE. |
| S | No write-back. Invalidate locally. Go to ALLOCATE. |
| E | No write-back (clean). Invalidate locally. Go to ALLOCATE. |
| M | Dirty. Go to INITIATE_BACK, send PUTM, then ALLOCATE. |

### 2.5 Transient States

The L1 cache line has its own MESI state. During a transaction, the line is in a transient state.

| Transient State | From | To | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `IS_D` | I | S or E | Waiting for data after GETS |
| `IM_D` | I | M | Waiting for data after GETM |
| `SM_D` | S | M | Waiting for data and invacks after GETM upgrade |
| `EI_A` | E | I | Waiting for PutE acknowledgement |
| `MI_A` | M | I | Waiting for PutM acknowledgement |

While in a transient state, the L1 is busy. The core cannot issue a new request. The `core_req_ready` signal is low.

If a snoop arrives while in a transient state, the L1 handles it:

| Current transient | Snoop (INV) | Snoop (FWD_GETS) | Snoop (FWD_GETM) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IS_D | Ignore (line not yet installed) | Ignore | Ignore |
| IM_D | Race handled by directory | Ignore | Ignore |
| SM_D | Transition to I, send INVACK | Supply data, stay in SM_D | Supply data, transition to I |
| EI_A | Ignore (already evicting) | Ignore | Ignore |
| MI_A | Ignore | Supply data | Supply data |

## 3. L2 Controller FSM

### 3.1 States

| State | Meaning | Duration |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `L2_IDLE` | No request in progress | Until a request arrives |
| `L2_COMPARE` | Directory lookup and hit/miss | 1 cycle |
| `L2_MEM_WAIT` | Waiting for memory data | Until memory responds |
| `L2_FWD_WAIT` | Waiting for forwarded data from owner | Until owner responds |
| `L2_INV_WAIT` | Waiting for invalidation acknowledgements | Until all INVACKs arrive |
| `L2_EVICT_INV_WAIT` | Evicting shared line, waiting for INVACKs | Until all INVACKs arrive |
| `L2_EVICT_FWD_WAIT` | Evicting owned line, waiting for PutE/PutM | Until owner responds |
| `L2_EVICT_WB` | Writing back victim to memory | Until memory responds |
| `L2_DONE` | Transaction complete, respond to requester | 1 cycle |

### 3.2 Transitions

```
   L2_IDLE
     │
     └── request arrives ──► L2_COMPARE

   L2_COMPARE
     │
     ├── DIR_I (miss) ──► L2_MEM_WAIT (if no eviction needed)
     │                    or L2_EVICT_* (if eviction needed)
     │
     ├── DIR_S, GETS ──► L2_DONE
     │
     ├── DIR_S, GETM ──► L2_INV_WAIT
     │
     ├── DIR_E, GETS (owner) ──► L2_DONE
     │
     ├── DIR_E, GETS (other) ──► L2_FWD_WAIT
     │
     ├── DIR_E, GETM (other) ──► L2_FWD_WAIT
     │
     ├── DIR_M, GETS (owner) ──► L2_DONE
     │
     ├── DIR_M, GETS (other) ──► L2_FWD_WAIT
     │
     └── DIR_M, GETM (other) ──► L2_FWD_WAIT

   L2_MEM_WAIT
     │
     └── memory data arrives ──► L2_DONE

   L2_FWD_WAIT
     │
     └── DATAFWD arrives ──► L2_DONE

   L2_INV_WAIT
     │
     └── all INVACKs arrive ──► L2_DONE

   L2_EVICT_INV_WAIT
     │
     └── all INVACKs arrive ──► L2_EVICT_WB (if victim dirty)
                                 or L2_MEM_WAIT (if victim clean)

   L2_EVICT_FWD_WAIT
     │
     └── PutE or PutM arrives ──► L2_EVICT_WB (if PutM)
                                   or L2_MEM_WAIT (if PutE)

   L2_EVICT_WB
     │
     └── memory write completes ──► L2_MEM_WAIT

   L2_DONE
     │
     └── response sent ──► L2_IDLE
```

### 3.3 Directory Lookup

On entering `L2_COMPARE`, the L2 controller reads the directory entry for the set indexed by `addr[L2_SET_LSB +: L2_SET_IDX_WIDTH]`. It reads the entry selected by the hit way (or the victim way on a miss).

```
dir_entry = directory_array[set_idx][way_sel];
dir_state = dir_entry.state;
dir_owner = dir_entry.owner;
dir_sharers = dir_entry.sharers;
dir_valid = dir_entry.valid;
```

The directory lookup is one cycle. The result drives the transition decision.

### 3.4 Directory State Decision

| DIR State | Incoming Request | Action |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DIR_I | GETS | Fetch memory, install as DIR_E or DIR_S, send DATA |
| DIR_I | GETM | Fetch memory, install as DIR_M, send DATA |
| DIR_S | GETS | Add sharer, send DATA (shared) |
| DIR_S | GETM | Send INV to sharers, wait for INVACKs |
| DIR_E | GETS (owner) | Send DATA (exclusive) |
| DIR_E | GETS (other) | Send FWD_GETS to owner, wait for DATAFWD |
| DIR_E | GETM (other) | Send FWD_GETM to owner, wait for DATAFWD |
| DIR_E | PUTS | Clear owner, transition to DIR_I |
| DIR_E | PUTM | Clear owner, transition to DIR_I, write memory |
| DIR_M | GETS (owner) | Send DATA (exclusive) |
| DIR_M | GETS (other) | Send FWD_GETS to owner, wait for DATAFWD |
| DIR_M | GETM (other) | Send FWD_GETM to owner, wait for DATAFWD |
| DIR_M | PUTM | Clear owner, transition to DIR_I, write memory |

### 3.5 Forward Path

When the directory state is `DIR_E` or `DIR_M` and a different tile requests the line, the L2 bank forwards the request to the current owner.

**Forward for shared request (FWD_GETS):**

1. L2 sends `FWD_GETS` to the owner.
2. Owner supplies data via `DATAFWD` to the requester.
3. Owner transitions to S.
4. L2 updates directory: state = DIR_S, sharers = {requester, owner}.
5. L2 sends no data itself.

**Forward for exclusive request (FWD_GETM):**

1. L2 sends `FWD_GETM` to the owner.
2. Owner supplies data via `DATAFWD` to the requester.
3. Owner transitions to I.
4. If owner was M, it also writes back to memory via PUTM.
5. L2 updates directory: state = DIR_M, owner = requester.
6. L2 sends no data itself.

The L2 controller waits in `L2_FWD_WAIT` until `DATAFWD` arrives. Then it updates the directory and sends a response to the requester.

### 3.6 Invalidate Path

When the directory state is `DIR_S` and a tile requests exclusive ownership (GETM), the L2 bank must invalidate all sharers.

1. L2 sends `INV` to each sharer except the requester.
2. L2 enters `L2_INV_WAIT`.
3. Each sharer responds with `INVACK` after invalidating its copy.
4. L2 counts INVACKs. When the count reaches the number of sharers minus the requester, the invalidation is complete.
5. L2 updates directory: state = DIR_M, owner = requester.
6. L2 sends `DATA` with `exclusive=1` to the requester.

**Pending acknowledgement counter:**

```
pending_acks <= popcount(sharers & ~requester_mask);
```

On each INVACK:

```
pending_acks <= pending_acks - 1;
if (pending_acks == 1)  // this is the last one
  transition to L2_DONE;
```

### 3.7 Eviction Path

When a miss requires a victim, the L2 controller must first resolve the victim's coherence state.

**Evicting a DIR_S victim:**

1. L2 sends `INV` to all sharers.
2. L2 enters `L2_EVICT_INV_WAIT`.
3. Waits for all INVACKs.
4. Transitions to `L2_EVICT_WB` if the victim was dirty (but DIR_S victims are clean by definition, so no write-back).

Wait — DIR_S means shared and clean. No write-back needed. Let me correct this.

**Corrected eviction flow:**

| Victim DIR state | Action |
| --- | --- |
| DIR_I | No action. Victim is already invalid. |
| DIR_S | Send INV to all sharers. Wait for INVACKs. Victim is clean, no write-back. |
| DIR_E | Send INV to owner. Wait for PutE. Victim is clean, no write-back. |
| DIR_M | Send INV to owner. Wait for PutM. Victim is dirty, write back to memory. |

**Evicting a DIR_M victim:**

1. L2 sends `INV` to the owner.
2. L2 enters `L2_EVICT_FWD_WAIT`.
3. Owner sends `PUTM` with data.
4. L2 enters `L2_EVICT_WB`.
5. L2 writes the line to memory.
6. L2 enters `L2_MEM_WAIT` to fetch the new line.
7. When memory data arrives, L2 installs the new line and responds.

### 3.8 Transient Directory States

The directory entry also has transient states during a transaction.

| Transient State | Meaning | Exit condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `DIR_MEM_WAIT` | Waiting for memory data | MEM_DATA arrives |
| `DIR_FWD_WAIT` | Waiting for forwarded data from owner | DATAFWD arrives |
| `DIR_INV_WAIT` | Waiting for invalidation acknowledgements | All INVACKs arrive |
| `DIR_EVICT_INV_WAIT` | Evicting shared line, waiting for INVACKs | All INVACKs arrive |
| `DIR_EVICT_FWD_WAIT` | Evicting owned line, waiting for PutE/PutM | PutE or PutM arrives |
| `DIR_EVICT_WB` | Writing back victim to memory | Memory write completes |

While in a transient state, the directory entry is locked. No other request for the same line can be served. Requests for other lines proceed normally.

If a request arrives for a line whose directory entry is in a transient state, the L2 controller has two options:

| Option | Behavior |
| --- | --- |
| Stall | Hold the request until the transient completes |
| NACK | Send a retry signal to the requester |

This design uses stall. The request waits in the L2 input queue. The directory entry is unlocked when the transient completes.

## 4. Interaction Between L1 and L2 FSMs

The L1 and L2 FSMs communicate through messages. They do not share state.

### 4.1 Read Miss, Local Home

```
   L1 FSM                        L2 FSM
   ──────                        ──────

   IDLE
     │ core load
     ▼
   COMPARE
     │ miss
     ▼
   ALLOCATE ──── GETS ──────────► L2_IDLE
                                   │
                                   ▼
                                 L2_COMPARE
                                   │ DIR_I
                                   ▼
                                 L2_MEM_WAIT
                                   │ memory data
                                   ▼
                                 L2_DONE ──── DATA ────► ALLOCATE
                                                          │
                                                          ▼
                                                        DONE
                                                          │
                                                          ▼
                                                        IDLE
```

### 4.2 Read Miss, Remote Home

```
   Tile 0 (requester)              Tile 1 (home)
   ──────────────────              ──────────────

   L1: COMPARE
       miss
       │
       ▼
   L1: ALLOCATE
       │ GETS
       ▼
   MUX: remote → NIC
       │ packet
       ▼
   Router: forwards to tile 1
       │
       ▼
                                 NIC: decodes NMSG_GETS
                                   │
                                   ▼
                                 L2: L2_COMPARE
                                   │ DIR_I
                                   ▼
                                 L2: L2_MEM_WAIT
                                   │
                                   ▼
                                 L2: L2_DONE
                                   │ DATA
                                   ▼
                                 NIC: packetises
                                   │
                                   ▼
   Router: forwards to tile 0
       │
       ▼
   NIC: decodes DATA
       │
       ▼
   MUX: routes to L1
       │
       ▼
   L1: ALLOCATE → DONE
       │
       ▼
   L1: IDLE
```

### 4.3 Store Miss, Remote Home, Forward to Owner

```
   Tile 0 (requester)    Tile 1 (home)         Tile 2 (owner)
   ──────────────────    ──────────────        ──────────────

   L1: COMPARE
       miss
       │
       ▼
   L1: ALLOCATE
       │ GETM
       ▼
   MUX: remote → NIC
       │ packet
       ▼
   Router: forwards to tile 1
       │
       ▼
                         NIC: decodes NMSG_GETM
                           │
                           ▼
                         L2: L2_COMPARE
                           │ DIR_M, owner = tile 2
                           ▼
                         L2: L2_FWD_WAIT
                           │ FWD_GETM
                           ▼
                         NIC: packetises
                           │
                           ▼
   Router: forwards to tile 2
       │
       ▼
                                               NIC: decodes FWD_GETM
                                                 │
                                                 ▼
                                               L1: SNOOP
                                                 │ supply data
                                                 │ transition to I
                                                 │ write back if M
                                                 ▼
                                               NIC: packetises DATAFWD
                                                 │
                                                 ▼
   Router: forwards to tile 0
       │
       ▼
   NIC: decodes DATAFWD
       │
       ▼
   MUX: routes to L1
       │
       ▼
   L1: ALLOCATE → DONE
       │
       ▼
   L1: IDLE

                         L2: L2_FWD_WAIT
                           │ DATAFWD sent (or owner sends separately)
                           ▼
                         L2: L2_DONE
                           │ update directory
                           │ owner = tile 0
                           ▼
                         L2: IDLE
```

## 5. Transient State Handling — Race Conditions

### 5.1 Snoop During ALLOCATE

Tile 0 is in `ALLOCATE` waiting for GETS data. Tile 1 sends an INV for the same line.

The L1 is in `IS_D` transient state. The line is not yet installed. The directory has already been updated by the L2 to include tile 0 as a sharer (or pending sharer). The INV arrives.

**Handling:** The L1 cannot invalidate a line it does not yet hold. The L1 records the INV and defers it. When the data arrives and the line is installed, the L1 applies the deferred INV.

**Simpler handling:** The directory does not send INV to a tile that is in the process of getting the line. The directory tracks this. If tile 0 is in `DIR_MEM_WAIT` for a GETS, the directory knows tile 0 will soon be a sharer. It does not send INV to tile 0 for a subsequent request until the first transaction completes.

This is the standard approach. The directory serializes requests for the same line.

### 5.2 Snoop During INITIATE_BACK

Tile 0 is evicting a modified line. It has sent PUTM to the L2. It is waiting in `MI_A`. A snoop arrives.

**Handling:** The L1 has already sent the data to the L2. The line is no longer valid in the L1. The snoop is answered by the L2, not by the L1. The L1 ignores the snoop.

### 5.3 Two Requests to Same Line, Same Cycle

Tile 0 and tile 1 both send GETS for the same line in the same cycle. The home L2 receives both.

**Handling:** The L2 processes one request at a time. The first request is served. The second request waits in the input queue. When the first completes, the second is served. The directory state after the first request determines the action for the second.

If the first request installed the line as `DIR_E` with tile 0 as owner, the second request (from tile 1) sees `DIR_E` and forwards to tile 0.

### 5.4 INVACK Arrives After Timeout

The L2 is in `L2_INV_WAIT`. It has sent INV to 3 sharers. It receives 2 INVACKs. The third does not arrive.

**Handling:** There is no timeout in the baseline design. The L2 waits indefinitely. This is a liveness risk.

**Future scope:** Add a timeout counter. On timeout, enter an error state, log the event, and reset the transaction.

## 6. FSM State Encoding

### 6.1 L1 FSM Encoding

| State | Code | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| IDLE | 000 | 3 bits |
| COMPARE | 001 | 3 bits |
| ALLOCATE | 010 | 3 bits |
| INITIATE_BACK | 011 | 3 bits |
| SNOOP | 100 | 3 bits |
| DONE | 101 | 3 bits |

### 6.2 L2 FSM Encoding

| State | Code | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L2_IDLE | 0000 | 4 bits |
| L2_COMPARE | 0001 | 4 bits |
| L2_MEM_WAIT | 0010 | 4 bits |
| L2_FWD_WAIT | 0011 | 4 bits |
| L2_INV_WAIT | 0100 | 4 bits |
| L2_EVICT_INV_WAIT | 0101 | 4 bits |
| L2_EVICT_FWD_WAIT | 0110 | 4 bits |
| L2_EVICT_WB | 0111 | 4 bits |
| L2_DONE | 1000 | 4 bits |

### 6.3 Directory State Encoding

| State | Code | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DIR_I | 000 | 3 bits |
| DIR_S | 001 | 3 bits |
| DIR_E | 010 | 3 bits |
| DIR_M | 011 | 3 bits |
| DIR_MEM_WAIT | 100 | 3 bits |
| DIR_FWD_WAIT | 101 | 3 bits |
| DIR_INV_WAIT | 110 | 3 bits |
| DIR_EVICT | 111 | 3 bits |

## 7. FSM Verification

Each FSM is verified for:

| Target | Method |
| --- | --- |
| Every state visited | Directed tests + coverage |
| Every transition taken | Directed tests + coverage |
| No illegal transitions | SVA assertions |
| No deadlock | Formal proof |
| No livelock | Formal proof with bounded depth |
| Transient state exit | Directed tests |

SVA assertions for the L1 FSM:

```
// No transition from IDLE directly to ALLOCATE
assert property (@(posedge clk)
  (l1_state == IDLE) |=> (l1_state inside {IDLE, COMPARE, SNOOP}));

// COMPARE must be followed by DONE, ALLOCATE, or INITIATE_BACK
assert property (@(posedge clk)
  (l1_state == COMPARE) |=> (l1_state inside {DONE, ALLOCATE, INITIATE_BACK}));

// DONE must return to IDLE
assert property (@(posedge clk)
  (l1_state == DONE) |=> (l1_state == IDLE));
```

## 8. One-Paragraph Summary

The coherence protocol is implemented by three interacting state machines: the L1 controller FSM, the L2 controller FSM, and the per-line directory state. The L1 FSM has six states (IDLE, COMPARE, ALLOCATE, INITIATE_BACK, SNOOP, DONE). On a core request, it compares tags, decides hit or miss, selects a victim via true LRU, and either serves the request or issues a coherence message. The L1 line has transient MESI states (IS_D, IM_D, SM_D, EI_A, MI_A) that track in-flight transactions. The L2 FSM has nine states (L2_IDLE, L2_COMPARE, L2_MEM_WAIT, L2_FWD_WAIT, L2_INV_WAIT, L2_EVICT_INV_WAIT, L2_EVICT_FWD_WAIT, L2_EVICT_WB, L2_DONE). It reads the directory entry for the requested line and decides whether to fetch from memory, forward to the owner, invalidate sharers, or respond directly. The directory entry has four stable states (DIR_I, DIR_S, DIR_E, DIR_M) and four transient states (DIR_MEM_WAIT, DIR_FWD_WAIT, DIR_INV_WAIT, DIR_EVICT). The forward path sends FWD_GETS or FWD_GETM to the current owner, which supplies data directly to the requester. The invalidate path sends INV to all sharers and waits for INVACKs. The eviction path resolves the victim's coherence state before installing the new line. Transient states handle race conditions such as snoops during ALLOCATE and concurrent requests to the same line. All FSMs are verified with directed tests, SVA assertions, and formal proofs for reachability and deadlock freedom.

# How are the data, tag, and directory arrays implemented in RTL?

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies how the three storage arrays inside each tile are implemented in RTL:

1. **Data array** — holds the cache lines.
2. **Tag array** — holds the tag and coherence state of each line.
3. **Directory array** — holds the coherence bookkeeping for each L2 line.

The finding covers width, depth, SRAM inference, and read/write port structure. The RTL for each array is written so that Vivado infers BRAM for FPGA and Yosys maps to Sky130 SRAM macros for ASIC.

---

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **Array** | A SystemVerilog `logic` array that holds multiple entries. |
| **Width** | The number of bits in one entry. |
| **Depth** | The number of entries. |
| **Set** | The index that selects one group of ways. |
| **Way** | One slot within a set. |
| **Line** | One cache line, 64 bytes. |
| **SRAM inference** | The process by which the synthesis tool maps a behavioral array to a dedicated memory block. |
| **BRAM** | Block RAM. A dedicated memory block on an FPGA. |
| **LUTRAM** | Distributed RAM built from lookup tables. |
| **Read port** | A port that reads one entry per cycle. |
| **Write port** | A port that writes one entry per cycle. |

---

# **3. Array Summary**

| **Array** | **Location** | **Sets** | **Ways** | **Width per entry** | **Depth** | **Total bits** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 data | Per tile | 4 | 4 | 512 bits | 16 lines | 8,192 |
| L1 tag | Per tile | 4 | 4 | 32 bits | 16 lines | 512 |
| L2 data | Per tile | 8 | 4 | 512 bits | 32 lines | 16,384 |
| L2 tag | Per tile | 8 | 4 | 32 bits | 32 lines | 1,024 |
| L2 directory | Per tile | 8 | 4 | 28 bits | 32 lines | 896 |

All arrays are single-clock synchronous.

---

# **4. Parameters**

---

## **4.1 Data Array Implementation**

### **4.1.1 Definition**

The data array holds cache lines. Each line is 64 bytes = 512 bits.

**Unit:** bits per entry and lines per array.

**Location:** `cache_storage.sv` (L1) and `l2_cache_storage.sv` (L2). Signal `data_array`.

### **4.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Behavioral 2D array | `logic [511:0] data [0:SETS-1][0:WAYS-1]` |
| 2 | Flattened 1D array | `logic [511:0] data [0:SETS*WAYS-1]` |
| 3 | Packed struct array | `cache_line_t data [0:LINES-1]` |
| 4 | Separate way arrays | One array per way |

### **4.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **2D array** | **Flattened 1D** | **Packed struct** | **Separate ways** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Readability | High | Medium | High | Low |
| SRAM inference (Vivado) | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Yosys mapping | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Address decode | Index by set and way | Index by set times ways plus way | Index by line | Index by set per way |
| Port structure | 2 read, 1 write | 2 read, 1 write | 2 read, 1 write | 2 read, 1 write per way |
| Area after synthesis | Same | Same | Same | Slightly larger |

### **4.1.4**

**Chosen: 2D array.**

systemverilog

```
logic [CACHE_LINE_BITS-1:0] data_array [0:L1_SETS-1][0:L1_WAYS-1];
```

For the L1 cache:

- `CACHE_LINE_BITS = 512`
- `L1_SETS = 4`
- `L1_WAYS = 4`

For the L2 bank:

- `CACHE_LINE_BITS = 512`
- `L2_SETS = 8`
- `L2_WAYS = 4`

The array is indexed by `[set][way]`. All ways of a set are read in parallel. The tag compare selects the winning way.

### **4.1.5**

**Why not flattened 1D:**

- The 2D form matches the set-associative structure
- The 2D form makes the set decode explicit

**Why not packed struct:**

- A packed struct hides the way index
- The 2D form is more readable for a hardware engineer

**Why not separate way arrays:**

- More code for the same function
- The synthesis result is the same

**Why 2D array:**

- Matches the set-associative structure
- Clear set and way indexing
- SRAM inference is identical to other forms
- Readable

### **4.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Reads and writes the L1 data array |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Reads and writes the L2 data array |
| Directory array | No | Separate storage |
| Message set | No | Independent of storage form |
| Router | No | Independent |
| NIC | No | Independent |
| Virtual channels | No | Independent |
| Address map | Yes | Set index selects the set |
| Verification | Yes | Data values are checked after each access |

### **4.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Clear structure
- SRAM inference works
- Readable RTL

**Loss:**

- Two indices to track (set and way)

**Constraint:**

- 512-bit width requires 512-bit registers or BRAM
- Vivado maps to BRAM when depth is at least 512 bits × 16 entries

**Verification:**

- Every read must return the stored value
- Every write must update the array

### **4.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Parameterised line size | RTL parameter | 1 week |
| Multi-word lines | Burst logic | 1 month |
| Byte-enable writes | Write mask logic | 2 weeks |

### **4.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data array form | 2D array | Matches set-associative structure |
| L1 width × depth | 512 bits × 16 lines | 4 sets × 4 ways |
| L2 width × depth | 512 bits × 32 lines | 8 sets × 4 ways |

---

## **4.2 Tag Array Implementation**

### **4.2.1 Definition**

The tag array holds the tag and coherence state of each cache line. Each entry is 32 bits: a tag field, a 3-bit MESI state field, and reserved bits.

**Unit:** bits per entry and lines per array.

**Location:** `cache_storage.sv` (L1) and `l2_cache_storage.sv` (L2). Signal `tag_array`.

### **4.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Separate tag and state arrays | One array for tags, one for states |
| 2 | Combined tag-state array | One array holding both |
| 3 | Packed struct | `tag_entry_t` struct with tag and state |
| 4 | Distributed RAM | LUTRAM for tag storage |

### **4.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Separate** | **Combined** | **Packed struct** | **Distributed** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Read latency | Two reads | One read | One read | One read |
| Area (LUTRAM) | 2 arrays | 1 array | 1 array | 1 array |
| SRAM inference | Two BRAMs | One BRAM | One BRAM | LUTRAM |
| Readability | Medium | High | High | Medium |
| Update atomicity | Two writes needed | One write | One write | One write |

### **4.2.4**

**Chosen: Combined tag-state array, packed struct.**

systemverilog

```
typedef struct packed {
    logic [26:0] tag;
    logic [2:0]  state;
    logic [1:0]  reserved;
} tag_entry_t;

tag_entry_t tag_array [0:SETS-1][0:WAYS-1];
```

For the L1 cache:

- Tag width = 24 bits (from L1 address decode: `addr[31:8]`)
- State width = 3 bits
- Reserved = 5 bits
- Total = 32 bits

For the L2 bank:

- Tag width = 19 bits (from L2 address decode: `addr[31:13]`)
- State width = 4 bits
- Reserved = 9 bits
- Total = 32 bits

### **4.2.5**

**Why not separate arrays:**

- Two reads needed to fetch tag and state
- Two writes needed to update
- Non-atomic updates risk state mismatch

**Why not distributed RAM:**

- Distributed RAM uses LUTs, which are scarce
- BRAM is available and free for this purpose

**Why not plain 2D array without struct:**

- The struct groups tag and state logically
- The struct makes the entry width explicit

**Why combined packed struct:**

- One read gives both tag and state
- One write updates both atomically
- BRAM inference works
- Entry width is explicit
- Readable

### **4.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Reads and writes the L1 tag array |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Reads and writes the L2 tag array |
| Directory array | No | Separate storage |
| Message set | No | Independent of storage form |
| Router | No | Independent |
| NIC | No | Independent |
| Virtual channels | No | Independent |
| Address map | Yes | Tag field comes from the address decode |
| Verification | Yes | Tag compare must be checked on every access |

### **4.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Single read gives tag and state
- Single write updates both
- Atomic updates
- BRAM inference works

**Loss:**

- Reserved bits wasted in the L1 tag (5 bits)
- Reserved bits wasted in the L2 tag (9 bits)

**Constraint:**

- Tag width depends on the address decode
- State width depends on the protocol

**Verification:**

- Every tag compare must be checked
- Every state transition must be checked

### **4.2.8**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Add ECC bits | ECC encoder and decoder | 1 month |
| Wider state encoding | Protocol change | 1 week |
| Sparse directory | Directory redesign | 2 months |

### **4.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Tag array form | Combined tag-state, packed struct | One read, one write, atomic update |
| L1 width × depth | 32 bits × 16 lines | 4 sets × 4 ways |
| L2 width × depth | 32 bits × 32 lines | 8 sets × 4 ways |

---

## **4.3 Directory Array Implementation**

### **4.3.1 Definition**

The directory array holds the coherence bookkeeping for each L2 line. Each entry is 28 bits: a 3-bit state, an 8-bit owner, a 16-bit sharer vector, and a 1-bit valid flag.

**Unit:** bits per entry and entries per array.

**Location:** `l2_cache_storage.sv`. Signal `directory_array`.

### **4.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Inline with the data array | Directory stored with the data line |
| 2 | Separate 2D array | Separate array, indexed by set and way |
| 3 | Separate 1D array | Flattened separate array |
| 4 | Separate array with compressed sharers | Compressed sharer vector |

### **4.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Inline** | **Separate 2D** | **Separate 1D** | **Compressed** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Read latency | Serial | Parallel | Parallel | Parallel |
| Area | Shared | Separate | Separate | Separate, smaller |
| Update rate | Same as data | Independent | Independent | Independent |
| Readability | Low | High | Medium | Medium |
| SRAM inference | Data-only | Directory-only | Directory-only | Directory-only |

### **4.3.4**

**Chosen: Separate 2D array.**

systemverilog

```
typedef struct packed {
    logic [2:0]  state;
    logic [7:0]  owner;
    logic [15:0] sharers;
    logic        valid;
} dir_entry_t;

dir_entry_t directory_array [0:L2_SETS-1][0:L2_WAYS-1];
```

For the L2 bank:

- `L2_SETS = 8`
- `L2_WAYS = 4`
- State width = 3 bits
- Owner width = 8 bits
- Sharers width = 16 bits
- Valid width = 1 bit
- Total = 28 bits

### **4.3.5**

**Why not inline with the data array:**

- Serial read adds latency
- Data width (512 bits) and directory width (28 bits) mismatch
- Cannot evolve directory independently

**Why not separate 1D:**

- The 2D form matches the data and tag arrays
- The set and way indices are the same

**Why not compressed sharers:**

- The design has 16 tiles, so 16 bits fits
- Compression adds decode logic
- No benefit at this scale

**Why separate 2D:**

- Parallel read with data and tag arrays
- Same set and way indices
- Independent evolution
- Clear structure
- SRAM inference works

### **4.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | L1 does not hold the directory |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Reads and writes the directory on every transaction |
| Directory array | Yes | This is the directory array |
| Message set | Yes | Directory state drives message generation |
| Router | No | Independent |
| NIC | No | Independent |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC assignment depends on message class, which depends on directory state |
| Address map | Yes | Set index selects the set |
| Verification | Yes | Directory state is dumped after every test |

### **4.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Parallel read with data and tag
- Independent evolution
- Clear structure
- SRAM inference works

**Loss:**

- Separate storage
- 28 bits per entry of overhead

**Constraint:**

- Directory entries are 28 bits
- Sharer vector limited to 16 tiles
- Directory updates must be atomic with tag updates

**Verification:**

- Directory state must match the aggregate L1 states after every test
- Every state transition must be checked

### **4.3.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Compressed sharers | Encoder and decoder | 2 weeks |
| Sparse directory | Pointer design | 2 months |
| Multi-word directory | Wider SRAM word | 1 week |

### **4.3.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Directory form | Separate 2D array | Parallel read, independent evolution |
| L2 width × depth | 28 bits × 32 entries | 8 sets × 4 ways |
| Sharer capacity | 16 tiles | Fits 4×4 mesh |

---

## **4.4 SRAM Inference**

### **4.4.1 Definition**

SRAM inference is the process by which a synthesis tool recognises a behavioral array and maps it to a dedicated memory block.

**Unit:** boolean per array.

**Location:** All three arrays. Inference rules described in the synthesis scripts.

### **4.4.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No inference | Arrays map to flip-flops |
| 2 | Inferred BRAM | Vivado maps to BRAM automatically |
| 3 | Instantiated BRAM | Use `RAMB36E1` primitives directly |
| 4 | Inferred BRAM with registered outputs | Add output register stage |

### **4.4.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Flip-flops** | **Inferred BRAM** | **Instantiated BRAM** | **Registered BRAM** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| LUT usage | Very high | Low | Low | Low |
| Flip-flop usage | Very high | Low | Low | Low |
| BRAM usage | None | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Portability | High | High | Low | Low |
| Read latency | 0 cycles | 1 cycle | 1 cycle | 2 cycles |
| Timing closure | Hard | Easy | Easy | Easiest |

### **4.4.4**

**Chosen: Inferred BRAM.**

The arrays are written as behavioral `logic` arrays. The synthesis tool infers BRAM automatically because:

1. The arrays are large enough (at least 512 bits × 16 entries).
2. The reads are synchronous.
3. The writes are synchronous.
4. No asynchronous read is used.

Example:

systemverilog

```
always_ff @(posedge clk) begin
    if (wr_en) begin
        data_array[wr_set][wr_way] <= wr_data;
    end
    rd_data <= data_array[rd_set][rd_way];
end
```

### **4.4.5**

**Why not flip-flops:**

- 16 KB of data per L2 bank would consume all LUTs and flip-flops on the device
- Does not fit the ZedBoard

**Why not instantiated BRAM:**

- Instantiated primitives are not portable to Yosys
- The inferred form works on both Vivado and Yosys

**Why not registered BRAM:**

- Adds a cycle of latency
- Not needed at 100 MHz

**Why inferred BRAM:**

- Portable to both Vivado and Yosys
- Uses device BRAM efficiently
- Read latency of one cycle is acceptable
- No manual primitive instantiation

### **4.4.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Read latency includes one cycle for BRAM |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Read latency includes one cycle for BRAM |
| Directory array | Yes | Same inference rules |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | No | No arrays |
| NIC | Yes | FIFO may map to BRAM |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC buffers may map to BRAM |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Read latency must be modelled in the testbench |

### **4.4.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Low LUT usage
- Low flip-flop usage
- Portable RTL
- Timing-friendly

**Loss:**

- One cycle read latency
- BRAM is a limited resource

**Constraint:**

- Reads must be synchronous
- No asynchronous read from a BRAM
- BRAM width must not exceed device limits

**Verification:**

- Testbench must account for one-cycle read latency
- Gate-level simulation must match RTL latency

### **4.4.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Registered BRAM outputs | Pipeline update | 1 week |
| UltraRAM (UltraScale+) | Board upgrade | 1 week |
| LUTRAM for tag array | Manual inference | 1 week |

### **4.4.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| SRAM inference | Inferred BRAM | Portable, low area, timing-friendly |
| Read latency | 1 cycle | Synchronous BRAM |
| Async read | No | Not supported by BRAM |

---

## **4.5 Read and Write Ports**

### **4.5.1 Definition**

Each array has a defined number of read and write ports. The port count affects the array structure and the access patterns.

**Unit:** count of ports per array.

**Location:** All three arrays. Port structure defined in the module interfaces.

### **4.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Single read, single write | Simple. One access at a time. |
| 2 | Two reads, one write | One read for tag compare, one for data. |
| 3 | Four reads, one write | Parallel read of all ways. |
| 4 | True dual-port | Two read and write ports. |

### **4.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **1R1W** | **2R1W** | **4R1W** | **True dual-port** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Read throughput | Low | Medium | High | High |
| Write throughput | Low | Low | Low | High |
| BRAM usage | 1 BRAM | 2 BRAMs | 4 BRAMs | 2 BRAMs |
| Complexity | Low | Medium | High | High |
| L1 access time | 4 cycles | 2 cycles | 1 cycle | 1 cycle |
| L2 access time | 8 cycles | 4 cycles | 2 cycles | 2 cycles |

### **4.5.4**

**Chosen: 4 reads, 1 write for L1 and L2 data and tag arrays.**

The arrays are structured as 4 parallel banks, one per way. Each bank has 1 read port and 1 write port. All four banks are read in the same cycle. The tag compare selects the winning way. The write goes to the winning way.

The directory array uses 1 read port and 1 write port because the directory is read once per transaction and written once per transaction.

### **4.5.5**

**Why not 1R1W:**

- Four sequential reads to compare all ways
- Too slow at 100 MHz

**Why not 2R1W:**

- Two reads per cycle still needs two cycles to read four ways

**Why not true dual-port:**

- Larger BRAM usage
- Not needed for the access pattern

**Why 4R1W:**

- All four ways read in one cycle
- Tag compare in one cycle
- Meets the 100 MHz target
- BRAM inference works with four parallel banks

### **4.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Reads all 4 ways in one cycle |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Reads all 4 ways in one cycle |
| Directory array | Yes | 1 read, 1 write per transaction |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | No | No arrays |
| NIC | No | FIFO uses its own structure |
| Virtual channels | No | Buffers use their own structure |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Testbench must model 4R1W access |

### **4.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- One-cycle read of all ways
- Meets 100 MHz target
- Simple tag compare

**Loss:**

- Four BRAM banks per array
- Higher BRAM usage than 1R1W

**Constraint:**

- Only one write per cycle
- Writes must not collide with reads of the same address

**Verification:**

- Read-after-write hazard must be handled by the controller
- Testbench must not write and read the same address in the same cycle

### **4.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| True dual-port for L1 | BRAM upgrade | 1 month |
| Banking by set | Two access per cycle | 2 weeks |
| Separate read and write banks | Banking logic | 1 month |

### **4.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data array ports | 4 reads, 1 write | Read all ways in one cycle |
| Tag array ports | 4 reads, 1 write | Read all ways in one cycle |
| Directory array ports | 1 read, 1 write | One read per transaction |

---

# **5. Summary**

The design uses three arrays per tile: a data array, a tag array, and a directory array. The data array holds 512-bit entries (64-byte lines). The L1 data array is 4 sets by 4 ways, and the L2 data array is 8 sets by 4 ways. The tag array packs the tag and MESI state into a 32-bit struct, so one read gives both. The directory array is separate from the data array and holds a 3-bit state, an 8-bit owner, a 16-bit sharer vector, and a 1-bit valid flag, for 28 bits per entry. All arrays use behavioral RTL that infers block RAM. Read latency is one cycle. The data and tag arrays use four read ports and one write port so all four ways compare in one cycle. The directory uses one read port and one write port. The structure follows from three facts: the 64-byte line fixes the data width, the MESI state must be read with the tag, and the directory must be readable in parallel with the data array.

---

```

│                                                       │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

# Replacement Policy in RTL

## 1. What the Replacement Policy Does

A set-associative cache has multiple ways per set. When a miss occurs, the cache must install the new line. If all ways are occupied, one way must be evicted. The replacement policy selects which way.

The policy affects:

- **Hit rate** — a better policy keeps frequently used lines longer
- **Coherence traffic** — evicting a dirty line generates a write-back message
- **Area** — more sophisticated policies need more state bits
- **Timing** — victim selection is on the critical path of a miss

## 2. Three Candidate Policies

| Policy | State per set (4 ways) | Hit rate | Area | Complexity |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FIFO | 2 bits (pointer) | Worst | Lowest | Lowest |
| Pseudo-LRU (tree) | 3 bits | Good | Low | Low |
| True LRU | 8 bits (age counters) | Best | Medium | Medium |

### 2.1 FIFO

First-In, First-Out. The way that was filled longest ago is evicted. Access does not change the order. Only fills advance the pointer.

**Weakness:** A frequently accessed line can be evicted if it was filled early. FIFO ignores access frequency.

### 2.2 Pseudo-LRU (Tree)

A binary tree of bits approximates LRU. Each internal node records which subtree was accessed less recently. On access, the path from root to the accessed way is updated. Victim selection follows the tree bits.

**Strength:** 3 bits give near-LRU behavior at low cost.

**Weakness:** Not exact LRU. Some orderings cannot be represented. In practice, the hit rate is within 1–2% of true LRU.

### 2.3 True LRU

Exact least-recently-used. Every access updates a full recency ordering.

**Strength:** Optimal hit rate among these three.

**Weakness:** More state. For 4 ways, 8 bits per set. For 8 ways, 24 bits per set. The state grows as O(ways × log₂ ways).

## 3. Our Choice

| Cache | Ways | Policy | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | 4 | True LRU | Small, access-critical, benefits from exact LRU |
| L2 | 4 | Pseudo-LRU | Larger, larger array, tree PLRU is sufficient |

The L1 is small (4 sets × 4 ways). The area cost of true LRU is 4 sets × 8 bits = 32 bits. Negligible. The hit rate benefit matters because L1 hits avoid NoC traffic entirely.

The L2 is larger (8 sets × 4 ways). True LRU would cost 8 sets × 8 bits = 64 bits per bank. Pseudo-LRU costs 8 sets × 3 bits = 24 bits per bank. The savings are modest, but the L2 replacement decision is off the critical path (miss handling is already slow), so the small hit-rate penalty is acceptable.

## 4. True LRU — RTL Implementation (L1)

### 4.1 State

For 4 ways, each way has a 2-bit age counter. Age 0 is most recently used. Age 3 is least recently used.

```
logic [1:0] age [0:3];  // age[w] for way w, one set
```

Total per set: 4 ways × 2 bits = 8 bits.

### 4.2 Update on Access

When a way is accessed (hit or fill), its age becomes 0. All ways with age less than the accessed way's old age are incremented.

```
always_ff @(posedge clk or negedge rst_n) begin
  if (!rst_n) begin
    for (int w = 0; w < 4; w++) age[w] <= 2'd0;
  end else if (access_valid) begin
    logic [1:0] old_age;
    old_age = age[access_way];
    for (int w = 0; w < 4; w++) begin
      if (w == access_way)
        age[w] <= 2'd0;
      else if (age[w] < old_age)
        age[w] <= age[w] + 2'd1;
    end
  end
end
```

Example: ways 0,1,2,3 have ages 1,2,0,3. Access way 2 (old_age = 0). No way has age less than 0. Way 2 becomes 0. Others unchanged.

Example: ways 0,1,2,3 have ages 1,2,0,3. Access way 0 (old_age = 1). Way 2 has age 0 < 1, so it becomes 1. Way 0 becomes 0. Result: 0,2,1,3.

### 4.3 Victim Selection

The victim is the way with age 3 (least recently used). A priority encoder finds it.

```
always_comb begin
  victim_way = 2'd0;
  for (int w = 3; w >= 0; w--) begin
    if (age[w] == 2'd3)
      victim_way = w[1:0];
  end
end
```

The loop is written from highest way to lowest so that the lowest-numbered way wins ties. Ties are rare in true LRU because exactly one way has age 3 in steady state.

### 4.4 Reset

On reset, all ages are 0. Victim selection picks way 0. The first fill goes to way 0. Subsequent fills follow the LRU order.

### 4.5 Per-Set Instantiation

Each set has its own age array. With 4 sets:

```
logic [1:0] age [0:3][0:3];  // [set][way]
```

The set index selects which age array is updated.

## 5. Pseudo-LRU Tree — RTL Implementation (L2)

### 5.1 State

For 4 ways, the tree has 3 bits:

| Bit | Name | Meaning |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `tree[0]` | Root | 0 = left subtree (ways 0,1) is LRU; 1 = right subtree (ways 2,3) is LRU |
| `tree[1]` | Left child | 0 = way 0 is LRU; 1 = way 1 is LRU |
| `tree[2]` | Right child | 0 = way 2 is LRU; 1 = way 3 is LRU |

Total per set: 3 bits.

### 5.2 Update on Access

On access to way W, the tree bits on the path from root to W are updated to point away from W.

```
always_ff @(posedge clk or negedge rst_n) begin
  if (!rst_n) begin
    tree <= 3'b000;
  end else if (access_valid) begin
    case (access_way)
      2'd0: begin
        tree[0] <= 1'b1;  // right subtree is now LRU
        tree[1] <= 1'b1;  // way 1 is now LRU within left
      end
      2'd1: begin
        tree[0] <= 1'b1;
        tree[1] <= 1'b0;  // way 0 is now LRU within left
      end
      2'd2: begin
        tree[0] <= 1'b0;  // left subtree is now LRU
        tree[2] <= 1'b1;  // way 3 is now LRU within right
      end
      2'd3: begin
        tree[0] <= 1'b0;
        tree[2] <= 1'b0;  // way 2 is now LRU within right
      end
    endcase
  end
end
```

### 5.3 Victim Selection

Follow the tree from root.

```
always_comb begin
  if (tree[0] == 1'b0) begin
    // Left subtree (ways 0,1)
    victim_way = tree[1] ? 2'd1 : 2'd0;
  end else begin
    // Right subtree (ways 2,3)
    victim_way = tree[2] ? 2'd3 : 2'd2;
  end
end
```

### 5.4 Reset

On reset, `tree = 000`. This means:

- Root points to left subtree (ways 0,1)
- Left child points to way 0

The first victim is way 0. Then way 2, then way 1, then way 3. After four fills, all ways are occupied.

### 5.5 Per-Set Instantiation

```
logic [2:0] tree [0:7];  // [set], 8 sets
```

## 6. FIFO — RTL Implementation (Comparison)

### 6.1 State

A 2-bit pointer per set.

```
logic [1:0] fifo_ptr;  // points to next victim
```

### 6.2 Update

Only on fill. Access does not change the pointer.

```
always_ff @(posedge clk or negedge rst_n) begin
  if (!rst_n) fifo_ptr <= 2'd0;
  else if (fill) fifo_ptr <= fifo_ptr + 2'd1;
end
```

### 6.3 Victim Selection

The victim is `fifo_ptr`.

```
always_comb victim_way = fifo_ptr;
```

### 6.4 Why We Do Not Use FIFO

FIFO ignores access frequency. A line accessed 100 times is evicted on the same schedule as a line accessed once. Hit rate suffers. The original inherited design used FIFO for L2. The AI review flagged it as a simplification. We upgraded to Pseudo-LRU.

## 7. Comparison: Per-Set Recency Tracking

| Aspect | FIFO | Pseudo-LRU | True LRU |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| State per set | 2 bits | 3 bits | 8 bits |
| State for 4 sets (L1) | 8 bits | 12 bits | 32 bits |
| State for 8 sets (L2) | 16 bits | 24 bits | 64 bits |
| Update on hit | No | Yes (3 bits) | Yes (8 bits) |
| Update on fill | Yes (pointer) | Yes (3 bits) | Yes (8 bits) |
| Victim selection | Pointer read | Tree walk | Age scan |
| Victim selection latency | 0 gates | 2 mux | Priority encoder |
| Critical path impact | None | Low | Low–medium |
| Hit rate (4-way, typical) | Baseline | +3–5% | +4–7% |

The hit rate advantage of true LRU over pseudo-LRU is small. The state cost is 2.7×. For the L1, the state cost is negligible. For the L2, pseudo-LRU is the better trade-off.

## 8. Victim Way Selection — Detailed

Victim selection happens on a miss, after the tag lookup confirms no hit. The selected way is reserved for the incoming line.

### 8.1 L1 Victim Selection (True LRU)

```
// Find way with age == 3
logic [1:0] victim_way_l1;
always_comb begin
  victim_way_l1 = 2'd0;
  for (int w = 0; w < 4; w++) begin
    if (age_l1[set_idx][w] == 2'd3)
      victim_way_l1 = w[1:0];
  end
end
```

### 8.2 L2 Victim Selection (Pseudo-LRU)

```
logic [1:0] victim_way_l2;
always_comb begin
  if (tree_l2[set_idx][0] == 1'b0)
    victim_way_l2 = tree_l2[set_idx][1] ? 2'd1 : 2'd0;
  else
    victim_way_l2 = tree_l2[set_idx][2] ? 2'd3 : 2'd2;
end
```

### 8.3 Victim State Check

Before evicting, the controller checks the victim's state.

| Victim State | Action |
| --- | --- |
| Invalid | No write-back. Install directly. |
| Shared | No write-back. Invalidate locally. |
| Exclusive | No write-back (clean). Invalidate locally. |
| Modified | Write-back to L2. Invalidate locally. |

If the victim is Modified, the L1 sends a PUTM message to the home L2 bank. This is the eviction flow.

## 9. Update on Access and on Fill

### 9.1 Access Types

| Event | Meaning | Policy update |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hit read | Load hits in cache | Yes |
| Hit write | Store hits in writable state | Yes |
| Fill | Miss, new line installed | Yes |
| Snoop hit | Coherence request hits | Yes (for true LRU) |
| Snoop invalidate | Coherence request invalidates | No |

### 9.2 Update Timing

The update happens in the same cycle as the access. The new age or tree value is written on the next clock edge.

```
   Cycle N:   access to way 2
              age/tree combinational update computed
   Cycle N+1: age/tree registers updated
```

If a second access happens in cycle N+1, it sees the updated state.
