### 9.3 Update During Fill

When a miss occurs and a line is installed, the fill counts as an access to the newly filled way. The age or tree is updated to mark the new line as most recently used.

For true LRU:

```
if (fill_valid) begin
  old_age = age[fill_way];
  for (int w = 0; w < 4; w++) begin
    if (w == fill_way) age[w] <= 2'd0;
    else if (age[w] < old_age) age[w] <= age[w] + 2'd1;
  end
end
```

For pseudo-LRU:

```
if (fill_valid) begin
  case (fill_way)
    2'd0: begin tree[0] <= 1'b1; tree[1] <= 1'b1; end
    2'd1: begin tree[0] <= 1'b1; tree[1] <= 1'b0; end
    2'd2: begin tree[0] <= 1'b0; tree[2] <= 1'b1; end
    2'd3: begin tree[0] <= 1'b0; tree[2] <= 1'b0; end
  endcase
end
```

### 9.4 Update Priority

If a hit and a fill happen in the same cycle (impossible in a blocking cache, but possible in a non-blocking cache), the fill has priority. The hit's update is applied first, then the fill's update.

For our blocking L1, this does not occur. For future non-blocking designs, priority logic is needed.

## 10. Reset Behavior

| Policy | Reset value | First victim |
| --- | --- | --- |
| FIFO | pointer = 0 | Way 0 |
| Pseudo-LRU | tree = 000 | Way 0 |
| True LRU | all ages = 0 | Way 0 |

All three pick way 0 first. After the first fill, the policies diverge.

## 11. Area Cost

| Cache | Sets | Ways | Policy | Bits per set | Total bits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | 4 | 4 | True LRU | 8 | 32 |
| L2 | 8 | 4 | Pseudo-LRU | 3 | 24 |

For a 4×4 mesh, total LRU state:

- L1: 16 tiles × 32 bits = 512 bits
- L2: 16 tiles × 24 bits = 384 bits
- Total: 896 bits ≈ 112 bytes

This is negligible compared to the cache arrays and the directory.

## 12. Timing Impact

Victim selection is combinational. It sits between the tag lookup and the fill.

```
   Cycle 0:   address arrives
              tag lookup (combinational)
              hit/miss decision (combinational)

   Cycle 1:   if miss, victim selection (combinational)
              victim state check (combinational)
              send coherence message (registered)

   Cycle 2:   wait for data
```

For true LRU, the victim selection is a priority encoder over 4 ages. Depth: 2 levels of logic.

For pseudo-LRU, the victim selection is 2 muxes. Depth: 1 level.

Neither is on the critical path. The critical path is the tag comparison and the coherence message generation.

## 13. Verification of Replacement Policy

The replacement policy is verified by directed tests.

| Test | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| Fill all 4 ways in order | Verify victim selection matches policy |
| Access way 0, then fill | Verify way 0 is not the victim |
| Access way 0, 1, 2, 3 in order | Verify recency order |
| Access way 0, then way 3, then fill | Verify victim is way 1 or 2 |
| Consecutive fills without access | Verify FIFO-like behavior for pseudo-LRU |

Coverage targets:

- Every way is selected as victim at least once
- Every recency ordering for true LRU is exercised (or a representative subset)
- Every tree state for pseudo-LRU is exercised

## 14. Parameterisation

The replacement policy is parameterised by the way count.

```
parameter int WAYS = 4;

// True LRU
localparam int AGE_WIDTH = $clog2(WAYS);  // 2 for 4 ways
logic [AGE_WIDTH-1:0] age [0:WAYS-1];

// Pseudo-LRU
localparam int TREE_BITS = WAYS - 1;  // 3 for 4 ways
logic [TREE_BITS-1:0] tree;
```

Changing `WAYS` from 4 to 8:

| Policy | 4 ways | 8 ways |
| --- | --- | --- |
| True LRU bits | 8 | 24 |
| Pseudo-LRU bits | 3 | 7 |
| FIFO bits | 2 | 3 |

The RTL uses the parameter everywhere. No manual edits.

## 15. What the Original Design Did

The inherited design used:

- L1: a single toggle bit per set. This is neither true LRU nor pseudo-LRU. It is a 2-way approximation extended to 4 ways, which is incorrect.
- L2: FIFO.

The AI review flagged both:

> "Replacement policies are simple. L1 uses one toggle bit per set (only an approximation of true LRU); L2 uses FIFO."
> 

This white paper replaces both with correct policies.

## 16. One-Paragraph Summary

The replacement policy selects which way to evict on a cache miss. Three candidate policies are FIFO, pseudo-LRU (tree), and true LRU. FIFO uses a 2-bit pointer and ignores access frequency. Pseudo-LRU uses a 3-bit tree that approximates LRU. True LRU uses 8 bits of age counters for 4 ways and gives exact LRU ordering. This design uses true LRU for the L1 (4 sets × 4 ways, 32 bits of state per tile) and pseudo-LRU for the L2 (8 sets × 4 ways, 24 bits per bank). True LRU tracks per-way age: on access, the accessed way's age becomes 0 and all younger ways increment. Pseudo-LRU tracks a tree of bits: on access, the path from root to the accessed way is updated to point away from it. Victim selection for true LRU is a priority encoder over age values. Victim selection for pseudo-LRU is a two-level mux following the tree. Both policies update on hit, on fill, and on snoop hit. Neither updates on snoop invalidate. Reset sets all state to zero, so way 0 is the first victim. The area cost is negligible (896 bits total for a 4×4 mesh). The timing impact is small because victim selection is off the critical path. The inherited design used a faulty toggle bit for L1 and FIFO for L2; this design corrects both. The policy is parameterised by way count so it scales to 8-way or 16-way caches without RTL changes.



# How is the router pipeline organised?

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies how the router pipeline is organised. It defines the pipeline stages, the input FIFO design, the per-VC FIFO depth, the flit decode logic, the route computation, the switch allocator, and the crossbar. The router is the switching element of the 2D mesh NoC.

---

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **Flit** | Flow control unit. One flit is 64 bits. |
| **Packet** | One coherence message. A packet is one or more flits. |
| **VC** | Virtual channel. A logical channel that shares a physical link. |
| **Pipeline stage** | One clock cycle of work in the router. |
| **Wormhole switching** | The header flit reserves the path. Body and tail flits follow without per-hop buffering of the whole packet. |
| **Credit** | A unit of buffer space advertised to the upstream router. |
| **Round-robin** | Arbitration policy that rotates priority among requesters. |
| **Crossbar** | The switch that connects inputs to outputs. |
| **Backpressure** | A signal that tells the upstream router to stop sending. |
| **Head-of-line blocking** | A blocked flit at the head of a FIFO prevents flits behind it from advancing. |

---

# **3. Parameters**

---

## **3.1 Pipeline Stages**

### **3.1.1 Definition**

The pipeline stages are the sequence of operations the router performs on each flit, one stage per clock cycle.

**Unit:** count of stages.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, main `always_ff` block.

### **3.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Single-cycle router | All logic in one cycle. No pipeline. |
| 2 | Two-stage router | Decode and route in stage 1, allocate and switch in stage 2. |
| 3 | Four-stage router | RC, VA, SA, ST as separate stages. |
| 4 | Five-stage router | Adds a FIFO-write stage. |

### **3.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Single-cycle** | **Two-stage** | **Four-stage** | **Five-stage** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Combinational depth | Very high | High | **Medium** | Low |
| Max clock frequency | Low | Medium | **High** | Highest |
| Latency per hop | 1 cycle | 2 cycles | **4 cycles** | 5 cycles |
| Area | Small | Medium | **Medium** | Large |
| Timing closure at 100 MHz | Hard | Doable | **Easy** | Easy |
| Fits 3-month timeline | No | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### **3.1.4**

**Chosen: Four-stage router.**

The router uses four pipeline stages:

| **Stage** | **Name** | **Function** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | RC | Route compute. Read destination, decide output port. |
| 2 | VA | Virtual channel allocation. Select the VC on the output. |
| 3 | SA | Switch allocation. Arbitrate for the crossbar. |
| 4 | ST | Switch traversal. Move the flit through the crossbar. |

Each stage is separated by pipeline registers. The router sustains one flit per cycle per port.

### **3.1.5**

**Why not single-cycle:**

- Combinational depth too high
- Cannot meet 100 MHz with 5 ports and 4 VCs
- Timing closure would fail

**Why not two-stage:**

- Combinational depth still high
- The SA stage would still be in the same cycle as RC

**Why not five-stage:**

- The extra stage adds latency without benefit at 100 MHz
- The area cost is higher

**Why four-stage:**

- Balances latency and timing
- Meets 100 MHz easily
- Matches the standard NoC pipeline in the literature
- Fits the 3-month timeline

### **3.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | Yes | Packet length depends on the number of flits |
| Router | Yes | This is the router |
| NIC | Yes | NIC drives the router input |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VA stage allocates a VC |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Pipeline stages must be exercised |

### **3.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Meets 100 MHz with margin
- Sustains one flit per cycle per port
- Matches standard NoC design

**Loss:**

- 4 cycles of latency per hop
- More pipeline registers

**Constraint:**

- Each stage must fit in one clock cycle
- Pipeline registers must be reset properly

**Verification:**

- Directed test per stage
- Pipeline hazard test (back-to-back flits)

### **3.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Two-stage router | Higher clock | 1 month |
| Speculative VA and SA | Larger area | 2 months |

### **3.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pipeline stages | Four (RC, VA, SA, ST) | Balances latency and timing |

---

## **3.2 Input FIFO Design**

### **3.2.1 Definition**

The input FIFO design defines how flits are buffered at each input port before they enter the pipeline.

**Unit:** FIFO count and depth per port.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, `fifo_in` module instantiated 5 times (one per port).

### **3.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Single FIFO per port | All VCs share one FIFO. |
| 2 | One FIFO per VC per port | 4 FIFOs per input port, one per VC. |
| 3 | Shared FIFO with VC pointers | One FIFO with 4 head pointers. |
| 4 | No FIFO (bufferless) | Deflection routing. |

### **3.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Single FIFO** | **Per-VC FIFO** | **Shared with pointers** | **Bufferless** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Head-of-line blocking | High | **None** | Low | None |
| Area | Small | **Medium** | Medium | Small |
| Control complexity | Low | **Medium** | High | High |
| Deadlock-free with 4 VCs | No | **Yes** | Yes | No |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Marginal | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | No | No |

### **3.2.4**

**Chosen: One FIFO per VC per port.**

Each input port has 4 FIFOs, one for each virtual channel. Each FIFO is 4 flits deep. Total buffering per port is 16 flits. Total buffering per router is 5 ports × 16 flits = 80 flits.

### **3.2.5**

**Why not single FIFO per port:**

- Head-of-line blocking. A blocked request VC would block responses behind it.
- Deadlock. Without VC separation, the coherence protocol can deadlock.

**Why not shared FIFO with VC pointers:**

- More control complexity
- Harder to verify
- Does not fit the timeline

**Why not bufferless:**

- Deflection routing needs a different routing algorithm
- Does not fit the XY deterministic choice

**Why per-VC FIFO:**

- Eliminates head-of-line blocking between VCs
- Enables deadlock-free operation with 4 VCs
- Standard NoC design
- Fits the timeline

### **3.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | Yes | Message class maps to VC |
| Router | Yes | FIFOs are part of the router |
| NIC | Yes | NIC writes into the FIFO |
| Virtual channels | Yes | This is the VC buffering |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | FIFO full and empty cases must be tested |

### **3.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- No head-of-line blocking between VCs
- Deadlock-free with 4 VCs
- Standard design

**Loss:**

- More area than a single FIFO
- More control logic

**Constraint:**

- Each FIFO must be exactly 4 flits deep
- Credit counters must match FIFO depth

**Verification:**

- FIFO full case
- FIFO empty case
- Back-to-back writes across VCs

### **3.2.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Deeper FIFOs | More BRAM | 1 week |
| Shared FIFO with VC pointers | Pointer logic | 1 month |

### **3.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Input FIFO | One per VC per port | No HOL blocking, deadlock-free |

---

## **3.3 Per-VC FIFO Depth**

### **3.3.1 Definition**

The per-VC FIFO depth is the number of flits each virtual channel FIFO can hold.

**Unit:** flits.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, `FIFO_DEPTH` parameter.

### **3.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 2 flits | Minimal buffering. |
| 2 | 4 flits | Moderate buffering. |
| 3 | 8 flits | Deep buffering. |
| 4 | 16 flits | Very deep buffering. |

### **3.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **2 flits** | **4 flits** | **8 flits** | **16 flits** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Buffering per port | 8 flits | **16 flits** | 32 flits | 64 flits |
| Buffering per router | 40 flits | **80 flits** | 160 flits | 320 flits |
| Area (BRAM) | Small | **Medium** | Large | Very large |
| Credit counter width | 1 bit | **2 bits** | 3 bits | 4 bits |
| Throughput at 50% load | Medium | **High** | High | High |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.3.4**

**Chosen: 4 flits per VC.**

Each VC FIFO holds 4 flits. Total buffering per port is 4 VCs × 4 flits = 16 flits. Total per router is 80 flits.

### **3.3.5**

**Why not 2 flits:**

- Throughput drops at moderate load
- Credit round trip needs at least 2 flits of headroom
- Backpressure would trigger frequently

**Why not 8 or 16 flits:**

- BRAM usage grows
- Does not improve throughput at the loads the design targets

**Why 4 flits:**

- Provides 4 flits of headroom for credit round trip
- Meets throughput targets at moderate load
- Fits BRAM budget
- Credit counter is only 2 bits

### **3.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | FIFO depth is a router parameter |
| NIC | Yes | Credit counters must match |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Depth is per VC |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | FIFO full and empty cases |

### **3.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Good throughput
- Low credit counter width
- Fits BRAM budget

**Loss:**

- Higher area than 2 flits

**Constraint:**

- Depth must match credit counter width
- Depth affects credit round-trip time

**Verification:**

- FIFO full and empty
- Backpressure behavior

### **3.3.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Adaptive depth | Runtime tuning | 2 months |
| Per-VC depth tuning | Traffic analysis | 2 weeks |

### **3.3.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Per-VC FIFO depth | 4 flits | Balances throughput and area |

---

## **3.4 Flit Decode Logic**

### **3.4.1 Definition**

The flit decode logic reads the header of each flit and extracts the fields needed for routing and control.

**Unit:** fields decoded.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, `flit_dec` module.

### **3.4.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Decode on demand | Decode only when a flit reaches the head of the FIFO. |
| 2 | Decode at write | Decode when a flit is written into the FIFO. |
| 3 | Pre-decode at the input | Decode all flits as they arrive, store the result. |
| 4 | Full decode of all fields | Decode destination, source, VC, type, head, tail. |

### **3.4.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **On demand** | **At head** | **Pre-decode** | **Full decode** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Latency | 1 cycle | **0 cycles** | 1 cycle | **0 cycles** |
| Area | Small | **Small** | Medium | Medium |
| Field count | Partial | **Full** | Full | Full |
| Pipeline fit | Poor | **Good** | Good | Good |

### **3.4.4**

**Chosen: Decode at head.**

The flit decode reads the 8-bit header from the head of each FIFO. It extracts:

| **Field** | **Bits** | **Purpose** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Destination | 8 | Route computation |
| Source | 8 | Reply path |
| VC ID | 2 | VC allocation |
| Message type | 4 | Control |
| Head flit | 1 | Wormhole control |
| Tail flit | 1 | Wormhole control |

The decode runs in parallel with the RC stage of the pipeline.

### **3.4.5**

**Why not decode on demand:**

- Adds a cycle of latency
- Unnecessary if the decode is parallel with RC

**Why not pre-decode:**

- Adds storage for the decoded fields
- Not needed if decode is fast

**Why not full decode of all fields:**

- Some fields are read only at the destination
- The router does not need the full payload

**Why decode at head:**

- No extra latency
- Only the head flit is decoded for routing
- Standard design

### **3.4.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | Yes | Message type field is decoded |
| Router | Yes | Decode is part of the router |
| NIC | Yes | Source and destination fields come from the NIC |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC ID field is decoded |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Every field must be checked |

### **3.4.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- No latency added
- Simple logic

**Loss:**

- None significant

**Constraint:**

- The header must be at a fixed position
- The fields must fit in the header

**Verification:**

- Every field must be tested with a directed flit

### **3.4.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Variable header length | Flit format change | 2 weeks |
| Decode with error checking | ECC | 1 month |

### **3.4.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flit decode | At head | No latency added |

---

## **3.5 Route Computation**

### **3.5.1 Definition**

Route computation decides which output port a flit must use based on its destination field.

**Unit:** count of output port choices.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, `route_comp` module.

### **3.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | XY deterministic | First move in X, then in Y. |
| 2 | YX deterministic | First move in Y, then in X. |
| 3 | Shortest path | Choose the direction with fewer hops. |
| 4 | Adaptive | Choose based on congestion. |

### **3.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **XY** | **YX** | **Shortest** | **Adaptive** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Deadlock-free (mesh) | Yes | Yes | No | No (needs VCs) |
| Combinational depth | Low | Low | Medium | High |
| Path diversity | Low | Low | Medium | High |
| Adaptive to congestion | No | No | Partial | Yes |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | Yes | Yes | Marginal |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |

### **3.5.4**

**Chosen: XY deterministic routing.**

The route computation compares the X coordinate of the destination with the X coordinate of the current tile. If they differ, the flit goes east or west. If they match, the flit compares the Y coordinate and goes north or south. If both match, the flit ejects to the local port.

The route is computed combinationally from the destination field. No lookup table is needed.

### **3.5.5**

**Why not YX:**

- XY and YX are equivalent in performance on a 2×2 mesh
- XY is the more common convention

**Why not shortest path:**

- The mesh has only 4 tiles, so shortest path equals XY
- On larger meshes, shortest path is not deadlock-free without VCs

**Why not adaptive:**

- Needs virtual channels for deadlock freedom
- Combinational depth is higher
- Does not fit the timeline

**Why XY:**

- Deadlock-free on a mesh
- Simple combinational logic
- Deterministic
- Matches the NoC literature
- Fits the timeline

### **3.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Route computation is part of the router |
| NIC | Yes | The NIC sets the destination field |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC selection is independent of routing |
| Address map | Yes | Destination field comes from the home tile bits |
| Verification | Yes | All source-destination pairs must be tested |

### **3.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Deadlock-free
- Simple logic
- Deterministic

**Loss:**

- No congestion avoidance
- No path diversity

**Constraint:**

- The destination field must be set correctly
- The mesh must be regular

**Verification:**

- All source-destination pairs
- Turn model validation

### **3.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Adaptive routing | Virtual channels | 2 months |
| Source routing | Larger header | 1 month |

### **3.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Routing algorithm | XY deterministic | Deadlock-free, simple |

---

## **3.6 Switch Allocator**

### **3.6.1 Definition**

The switch allocator arbitrates among the input ports that want to use each output port. Only one input may use an output per cycle.

**Unit:** arbitration policy.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, `sw_ctrl` module.

### **3.6.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Fixed priority | Local always wins. Then VC 0, VC 1, VC 2, VC 3. |
| 2 | Round-robin | Rotate priority among requesters. |
| 3 | Age-based | Oldest request wins. |
| 4 | Weighted round-robin | Different weights per VC. |

### **3.6.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Fixed** | **Round-robin** | **Age-based** | **Weighted** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Fairness | Low | **High** | High | Configurable |
| Starvation risk | High | **None** | None | Low |
| Combinational depth | Low | **Medium** | High | Medium |
| Implementation effort | Low | **Medium** | High | Medium |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Marginal | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | No | Yes |

### **3.6.4**

**Chosen: Round-robin.**

The switch allocator uses round-robin arbitration across the 5 input ports for each of the 5 output ports. A winning input holds the output for the duration of the packet (wormhole switching). When the tail flit passes, the grant is released and the next round-robin winner is selected.

Each output port has its own round-robin arbiter. The 5 arbiters run in parallel.

### **3.6.5**

**Why not fixed priority:**

- Starves low-priority VCs
- A blocked high-priority VC can block all others
- The original design used fixed priority and risked starvation

**Why not age-based:**

- Requires a timestamp per request
- Combinational depth too high

**Why not weighted:**

- Weights add complexity
- Round-robin is sufficient for the design

**Why round-robin:**

- Fair across VCs
- No starvation
- Meets 100 MHz
- Standard NoC design

### **3.6.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | Yes | Message class determines VC priority |
| Router | Yes | Switch allocator is part of the router |
| NIC | No | Above the router |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Arbitration is per VC |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | All arbitration cases must be tested |

### **3.6.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Fair arbitration
- No starvation
- Standard design

**Loss:**

- More logic than fixed priority

**Constraint:**

- One winner per output per cycle
- Losers must hold their flits in the FIFO

**Verification:**

- Two inputs request the same output
- All inputs request all outputs
- Back-to-back packets from the same input

### **3.6.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Weighted round-robin | Weight registers | 2 weeks |
| Age-based arbitration | Timestamp logic | 1 month |

### **3.6.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Switch allocator | Round-robin | Fair, no starvation |

---

## **3.7 Crossbar Size and Design**

### **3.7.1 Definition**

The crossbar is the switch that connects the input ports to the output ports. It has one path per input-output pair.

**Unit:** number of input-output paths.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, `xbar` module.

### **3.7.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 5×5 crossbar | 25 paths, one per input-output pair. |
| 2 | 5×5 crossbar with pipeline register | Same plus a register stage. |
| 3 | 5×5 Banyan network | Multi-stage network with fewer paths. |
| 4 | Shared bus | One wire used by all inputs. |

### **3.7.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **5×5 crossbar** | **Crossbar + register** | **Banyan** | **Shared bus** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Paths | 25 | 25 | 15 | 1 |
| Combinational depth | Medium | Low | Low | Low |
| Timing closure at 100 MHz | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Area | Medium | Medium | Small | Small |
| Blocking | Non-blocking | Non-blocking | Blocking | Blocking |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | No | Yes |

### **3.7.4**

**Chosen: 5×5 crossbar.**

The crossbar has 5 inputs and 5 outputs. Each output is driven by a 5-to-1 multiplexer. The multiplexer selects the input chosen by the switch allocator.

The 5 multiplexers run in parallel. Each is 64 bits wide, matching the flit width.

### **3.7.5**

**Why not crossbar with pipeline register:**

- The ST stage already has a pipeline register
- No extra register needed

**Why not Banyan:**

- Blocking network
- Requires more complex control
- Not needed for 5 ports

**Why not shared bus:**

- Blocking
- Serialized access
- Does not match the mesh design

**Why 5×5 crossbar:**

- Non-blocking
- One path per input-output pair
- Simple control from the switch allocator
- Standard design

### **3.7.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Crossbar is part of the router |
| NIC | No | Above the router |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Crossbar carries VC traffic |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | All input-output pairs must be tested |

### **3.7.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Non-blocking
- Simple control
- Meets 100 MHz

**Loss:**

- Area grows as the square of the port count

**Constraint:**

- 5 ports means 25 paths
- Each path is 64 bits

**Verification:**

- All input-output pairs
- Simultaneous traffic on all ports

### **3.7.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 7×7 crossbar | Larger mesh | 2 weeks |
| Multi-stage network | Larger port count | 1 month |

### **3.7.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Crossbar size | 5×5 | Matches router port count |
| Crossbar type | Non-blocking | Standard design |

---

## **3.8 Backpressure and Credit Flow Control**

### **3.8.1 Definition**

Backpressure is the signal that tells the upstream router to stop sending flits when the downstream FIFO is full. Credit-based flow control tracks the available space in each downstream VC FIFO.

**Unit:** credit count per VC.

**Location:** `Router.sv`, credit counters. `NIC.sv`, credit counters.

### **3.8.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No backpressure | Flits are dropped when the FIFO is full. |
| 2 | Valid/ready handshake | The receiver signals when it can accept a flit. |
| 3 | Credit-based | The sender tracks available space. |
| 4 | On/off backpressure | A single bit that stops or resumes the stream. |

### **3.8.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No backpressure** | **Valid/ready** | **Credit-based** | **On/off** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Flit loss | Yes | No | **No** | No |
| Bandwidth efficiency | Low | Medium | **High** | Medium |
| Control wires | 0 | 1 per port | **log2(depth) per VC** | 1 per port |
| Fits VCs | No | Partial | **Yes** | Partial |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | No | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### **3.8.4**

**Chosen: Credit-based.**

Each VC has a credit counter. The counter starts at the FIFO depth (4). When a flit is sent, the counter decrements. When the downstream router forwards a flit out of that FIFO, it sends a credit back, and the counter increments.

The sender transmits only when the credit counter is greater than zero.

### **3.8.5**

**Why not no backpressure:**

- Flits would be dropped
- The coherence protocol would break

**Why not valid/ready:**

- Valid/ready does not work well with VCs
- The ready wire would need to be per VC

**Why not on/off:**

- Cannot track the exact available space
- Would idle the link when the FIFO is partially full

**Why credit-based:**

- Works per VC
- High bandwidth efficiency
- Standard for NoC
- Meets 100 MHz

### **3.8.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the router |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the router |
| Directory array | No | Above the router |
| Message set | No | Independent |
| Router | Yes | Credit counters per VC |
| NIC | Yes | Credit counters per VC |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Credits are per VC |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Credit wrap and starvation cases |

### **3.8.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- No flit loss
- High bandwidth efficiency
- Standard design

**Loss:**

- More control wires
- More counters

**Constraint:**

- Credit counter width must match FIFO depth
- Credits must be returned promptly

**Verification:**

- Credit wrap case
- Backpressure case
- Full FIFO case

### **3.8.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Adaptive credit return | Priority logic | 1 month |
| Speculative credit | Larger buffers | 2 months |

### **3.8.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flow control | Credit-based | No loss, efficient, VC-friendly |

---

# **4. Summary**

The router uses a four-stage pipeline: route compute, virtual channel allocation, switch allocation, and switch traversal. Each stage is separated by pipeline registers, so the router sustains one flit per cycle per port. Each input port has four FIFOs, one per virtual channel, each four flits deep. Total buffering is 16 flits per port and 80 flits per router. Flit decode reads the 8-bit header at the head of each FIFO and extracts the destination, source, VC ID, message type, and head and tail flags. Route computation is XY deterministic: compare X first, then Y. Switch allocation is round-robin across the five input ports for each of the five output ports. A winning input holds the output until the tail flit passes. The crossbar is 5×5, with one 64-bit multiplexer per output. Credit-based flow control on each VC prevents FIFO overflow. The four stages were chosen to meet 100 MHz. A single-cycle or two-stage router would have too much combinational depth. A five-stage router would add latency without benefit. Per-VC FIFOs eliminate head-of-line blocking and enable deadlock-free operation. Round-robin prevents starvation. Credit-based flow control works with VCs and prevents flit loss.

---

# How does the Network Interface Controller work?

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies how the Network Interface Controller works. It defines the TX packet generator, the TX FIFO depth, the TX controller, the RX FIFO depth, the RX controller, the packet decoder, and the cache-NIC backpressure handshake. The NIC is the boundary between the coherence protocol inside a tile and the packet-switched NoC outside it.

---

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **NIC** | Network Interface Controller. The block that connects the tile to the NoC. |
| **TX** | Transmit. The path from the cache to the router. |
| **RX** | Receive. The path from the router to the cache. |
| **Packet** | One coherence message. One or more flits. |
| **Flit** | Flow control unit. 64 bits. |
| **Header flit** | The first flit of a packet. Contains destination, source, message type, VC, and control bits. |
| **Body flit** | A data flit that follows the header. 64 bits of data. |
| **Tail flit** | The last flit of a packet. Marked in the control bits. |
| **Credit** | A unit of buffer space advertised to the upstream block. |
| **Backpressure** | A signal that tells the upstream block to stop sending. |
| **Handshake** | A pair of signals (valid, ready) that transfer one item per cycle. |

---

# **3. Parameters**

---

## **3.1 TX Packet Generator**

### **3.1.1 Definition**

The TX packet generator converts a coherence message from the cache controller into a packet of flits. It fills the header fields and, for data messages, splits the payload into body flits.

**Unit:** fields written per packet.

**Location:** `NIC.sv`, `packet_generator` module.

### **3.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Store-and-forward | Buffer the whole packet before sending. |
| 2 | **Cut-through** | Send the header as soon as it is ready, body follows. |
| 3 | Wormhole | Same as cut-through for the router; NIC just streams. |
| 4 | Single-flit only | No multi-flit packets. |

### **3.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Store-and-forward** | **Cut-through** | **Wormhole** | **Single-flit** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Latency per packet | High | **Low** | Low | Lowest |
| Buffer size | Whole packet | **Header only** | Header only | One flit |
| Complexity | Low | **Low** | Medium | Lowest |
| Multi-flit support | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | No |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.1.4**

**Chosen: Cut-through.**

The generator writes the header flit first. For a coherence message with no data, the packet is one flit. For a data message, the generator writes the header and then streams the 8 body flits from the cache line. The generator does not wait for the whole packet before starting transmission.

The header contains:

| **Field** | **Bits** | **Source** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Destination | 8 | Message's home tile or target tile |
| Source | 8 | This tile's ID |
| VC ID | 2 | Message class |
| Message type | 4 | Coherence message type |
| Head flit | 1 | 1 |
| Tail flit | 1 | 1 for single-flit packets, 0 for multi-flit |
| Write enable | 1 | From the cache controller |
| Exclusive indication | 1 | From the cache controller |
| Address | 32 | From the cache controller |
| Reserved | 6 | 0 |

For body flits, the generator reads 64 bits at a time from the cache line buffer and writes them into the TX FIFO.

### **3.1.5**

**Why not store-and-forward:**

- Adds latency to every packet
- Does not use the router's wormhole capability
- The router already handles wormhole switching

**Why not wormhole as a separate NIC mechanism:**

- Wormhole is a router mechanism, not a NIC mechanism
- The NIC just streams flits

**Why not single-flit only:**

- Cache line transfers need 9 flits
- Single-flit would require splitting cache lines into many messages

**Why cut-through:**

- Low latency
- Simple control
- Matches the router's wormhole switching
- Handles multi-flit packets

### **3.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Supplies the coherence message |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Supplies the coherence message |
| Directory array | No | Above the NIC |
| Message set | Yes | Packet format matches message type |
| Router | Yes | Router receives the stream of flits |
| NIC | Yes | This is the TX generator |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC ID is written by the generator |
| Address map | Yes | Destination is computed from the address |
| Verification | Yes | Every message type must be packetized |

### **3.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Low latency
- Simple control
- Matches router design

**Loss:**

- Header must be correct before body is read

**Constraint:**

- Header fields must be set before the first flit is written
- Body flits must be read in order

**Verification:**

- Every message type
- Every VC
- Single-flit and multi-flit packets

### **3.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Store-and-forward for large packets | Larger FIFO | 2 weeks |
| Compression of body flits | Encoding | 1 month |

### **3.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Packet generator | Cut-through | Low latency, matches router |

---

## **3.2 TX FIFO Depth**

### **3.2.1 Definition**

The TX FIFO depth is the number of packets the TX FIFO can hold before the cache controller must stall.

**Unit:** packets.

**Location:** `NIC.sv`, `TX_FIFO_DEPTH` parameter.

### **3.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 2 packets | Minimal buffering. |
| 2 | **8 packets** | Moderate buffering. |
| 3 | 16 packets | Deep buffering. |
| 4 | 32 packets | Very deep buffering. |

### **3.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **2 pkts** | **8 pkts** | **16 pkts** | **32 pkts** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Flits per packet (data) | 9 | **9** | 9 | 9 |
| Flits per packet (control) | 1 | **1** | 1 | 1 |
| Buffering in flits (max) | 18 | **72** | 144 | 288 |
| Buffering in flits (min) | 2 | **8** | 16 | 32 |
| BRAM usage | Small | **Medium** | Large | Very large |
| Throughput at moderate load | Low | **High** | High | High |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.2.4**

**Chosen: 8 packets.**

The TX FIFO holds 8 packets. For control messages, each packet is one flit, so the FIFO holds 8 flits. For data messages, each packet is 9 flits, so the FIFO holds up to 72 flits. The FIFO is sized in packets, not flits.

### **3.2.5**

**Why not 2 packets:**

- Back-to-back line fills would stall
- The cache controller would not be able to issue a new request while the previous is transmitting

**Why not 16 or 32 packets:**

- BRAM usage grows
- Does not improve throughput at the loads the design targets

**Why 8 packets:**

- Holds one full cache line fill (9 flits) plus 7 control messages
- Allows back-to-back line fills
- Fits BRAM budget

### **3.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Stalls when the TX FIFO is full |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Stalls when the TX FIFO is full |
| Directory array | No | Above the NIC |
| Message set | Yes | Packet size determines FIFO usage |
| Router | Yes | Consumes flits from the FIFO |
| NIC | Yes | This is the TX FIFO |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC traffic shares the FIFO |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Full and empty cases must be tested |

### **3.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Back-to-back line fills allowed
- Good throughput

**Loss:**

- BRAM usage
- Cache controller must handle full FIFO

**Constraint:**

- FIFO depth must be at least one full packet
- Cache controller must stall when the FIFO is full

**Verification:**

- FIFO full
- FIFO empty
- Back-to-back line fills

### **3.2.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Per-VC TX FIFO | VC separation | 2 weeks |
| Adaptive FIFO depth | Runtime tuning | 2 months |

### **3.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| TX FIFO depth | 8 packets | Allows back-to-back line fills |

---

## **3.3 TX Controller**

### **3.3.1 Definition**

The TX controller is a finite state machine that manages the transmission of one packet at a time from the TX FIFO to the router.

**Unit:** states in the FSM.

**Location:** `NIC.sv`, `tx_controller` module.

### **3.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Direct drive | Drive flits to the router whenever ready. |
| 2 | **Four-state FSM** | IDLE, LOAD, WAIT_READY, SEND. |
| 3 | Six-state FSM | Adds RETRY and ERROR. |
| 4 | Credit-only | Drive whenever credit is available. |

### **3.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Direct** | **Four-state** | **Six-state** | **Credit-only** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Control clarity | Low | **High** | High | Medium |
| Handles credit | Partial | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Handles errors | No | **No** | Yes | No |
| Combinational depth | Low | **Low** | Medium | Low |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.3.4 Chosen Option**

**Chosen: Four-state FSM.**

The TX controller has four states:

| **State** | **Meaning** | **Transition** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| IDLE | No packet to send | To LOAD when a message arrives |
| LOAD | Read the next flit from the TX FIFO | To WAIT_READY |
| WAIT_READY | Wait for the router's credit signal | To SEND when credit > 0 |
| SEND | Drive the flit onto the router input | To IDLE when the packet is done; to LOAD otherwise |

### **3.3.5**

**Why not direct drive:**

- Does not handle credit
- Would drop flits when the router is full

**Why not six-state:**

- Errors are handled by the timeout mechanism, not by the NIC
- Adds states without benefit

**Why not credit-only:**

- Does not model the packet boundary
- Does not handle multi-flit packets cleanly

**Why four-state:**

- Clear separation of concerns
- Handles credit
- Handles single-flit and multi-flit packets
- Simple

### **3.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the NIC |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the NIC |
| Directory array | No | Above the NIC |
| Message set | Yes | State transitions per packet |
| Router | Yes | Receives flits from SEND |
| NIC | Yes | This is the TX controller |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Credit is per VC |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Every state must be visited |

### **3.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Clean control
- Handles credit
- Handles multi-flit packets

**Loss:**

- One cycle latency per flit due to LOAD and WAIT_READY

**Constraint:**

- Credit must be checked in WAIT_READY
- Packet boundary tracked by head and tail flits

**Verification:**

- Every state visited
- Back-to-back packets

### **3.3.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pipeline LOAD and SEND | Larger FIFO | 1 month |
| Error states | Error handling logic | 2 weeks |

### **3.3.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| TX controller | Four-state FSM | Clean, handles credit |

---

## **3.4 RX FIFO Depth**

### **3.4.1 Definition**

The RX FIFO depth is the number of packets the RX FIFO can hold before the router must stall.

**Unit:** packets.

**Location:** `NIC.sv`, `RX_FIFO_DEPTH` parameter.

### **3.4.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 2 packets | Minimal buffering. |
| 2 | **8 packets** | Moderate buffering. |
| 3 | 16 packets | Deep buffering. |
| 4 | 32 packets | Very deep buffering. |

### **3.4.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **2 pkts** | **8 pkts** | **16 pkts** | **32 pkts** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Buffering in flits (max) | 18 | **72** | 144 | 288 |
| Buffering in flits (min) | 2 | **8** | 16 | 32 |
| BRAM usage | Small | **Medium** | Large | Very large |
| Absorbs burst traffic | Low | **Medium** | High | High |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.4.4**

**Chosen: 8 packets.**

The RX FIFO holds 8 packets. This matches the TX FIFO depth and gives the cache controller time to process incoming messages.

### **3.4.5**

**Why not 2 packets:**

- Burst traffic would stall the router
- The router would apply backpressure to upstream routers

**Why not 16 or 32 packets:**

- BRAM usage grows
- Does not improve throughput at the loads the design targets

**Why 8 packets:**

- Absorbs burst traffic
- Matches the TX FIFO depth
- Fits BRAM budget

### **3.4.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Consumes messages from the RX FIFO |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Consumes messages from the RX FIFO |
| Directory array | No | Above the NIC |
| Message set | Yes | Packet size determines FIFO usage |
| Router | Yes | Writes flits into the RX FIFO |
| NIC | Yes | This is the RX FIFO |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC traffic shares the FIFO |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Full and empty cases must be tested |

### **3.4.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Absorbs burst traffic
- Matches TX FIFO depth

**Loss:**

- BRAM usage

**Constraint:**

- FIFO depth must be at least one full packet
- Router must apply backpressure when the FIFO is full

**Verification:**

- FIFO full
- FIFO empty
- Burst traffic

### **3.4.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Per-VC RX FIFO | VC separation | 2 weeks |
| Adaptive FIFO depth | Runtime tuning | 2 months |

### **3.4.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RX FIFO depth | 8 packets | Absorbs bursts, matches TX |

---

## **3.5 RX Controller**

### **3.5.1 Definition**

The RX controller is a finite state machine that manages the reception of one packet at a time from the router into the RX FIFO.

**Unit:** states in the FSM.

**Location:** `NIC.sv`, `rx_controller` module.

### **3.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Direct accept | Accept flits whenever the router drives them. |
| 2 | **Four-state FSM** | IDLE, ACCEPT, DECODE, DELIVER. |
| 3 | Six-state FSM | Adds RETRY and ERROR. |
| 4 | Credit-only | Accept whenever the FIFO has space. |

### **3.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Direct** | **Four-state** | **Six-state** | **Credit-only** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Control clarity | Low | **High** | High | Medium |
| Handles backpressure | Partial | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Handles errors | No | **No** | Yes | No |
| Combinational depth | Low | **Low** | Medium | Low |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.5.4**

**Chosen: Four-state FSM.**

The RX controller has four states:

| **State** | **Meaning** | **Transition** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| IDLE | No flit to receive | To ACCEPT when the router has a valid flit |
| ACCEPT | Read the flit from the router input | To DECODE |
| DECODE | Decode the flit header | To DELIVER |
| DELIVER | Write the flit into the RX FIFO | To IDLE when the packet is done; to ACCEPT otherwise |
|  |  |  |

### 3.5.5

**Why not direct accept:**

- Does not handle backpressure
- Would drop flits when the FIFO is full

**Why not six-state:**

- Errors are handled by the timeout mechanism
- Adds states without benefit

**Why not credit-only:**

- Does not model the packet boundary
- Does not handle multi-flit packets cleanly

**Why four-state:**

- Clean separation of concerns
- Handles backpressure
- Handles single-flit and multi-flit packets
- Simple

### **3.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | No | Above the NIC |
| L2 bank controller | No | Above the NIC |
| Directory array | No | Above the NIC |
| Message set | Yes | State transitions per packet |
| Router | Yes | Drives flits into ACCEPT |
| NIC | Yes | This is the RX controller |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Backpressure is per VC |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Every state must be visited |

### **3.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Clean control
- Handles backpressure
- Handles multi-flit packets

**Loss:**

- One cycle latency per flit due to ACCEPT and DECODE

**Constraint:**

- Backpressure must be applied in ACCEPT
- Packet boundary tracked by head and tail flits

**Verification:**

- Every state visited
- Back-to-back packets

### **3.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Pipeline ACCEPT and DELIVER | Larger FIFO | 1 month |
| Error states | Error handling logic | 2 weeks |

### **3.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| RX controller | Four-state FSM | Clean, handles backpressure |

---

## **3.6 Packet Decoder**

### **3.6.1 Definition**

The packet decoder reads a complete packet from the RX FIFO and reconstructs the coherence message for the cache controller.

**Unit:** fields decoded per packet.

**Location:** `NIC.sv`, `packet_decoder` module.

### **3.6.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Decode in the RX controller | Decode as flits arrive. |
| 2 | **Decode in a separate module** | Read the whole packet from the RX FIFO, then decode. |
| 3 | Decode in the cache controller | Pass flits to the cache, let it decode. |
| 4 | No decode | Pass raw flits to the cache. |

### **3.6.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **In RX controller** | **Separate module** | **In cache** | **No decode** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Separation of concerns | Low | **High** | Low | None |
| Debug visibility | Low | **High** | Low | Low |
| Cache controller complexity | Medium | **Low** | High | Very high |
| Combinational depth | Medium | **Low** | High | Low |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | No | No |

### **3.6.4**

**Chosen: Decode in a separate module.**

The packet decoder reads a complete packet from the RX FIFO. It separates the header from the body. It extracts:

| **Field** | **Bits** | **Purpose** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Source | 8 | For the cache controller's reply |
| Message type | 4 | For the cache controller's FSM |
| VC ID | 2 | For the cache controller's priority |
| Write enable | 1 | For write-back messages |
| Exclusive indication | 1 | For read responses |
| Address | 32 | For the cache controller's lookup |
| Data | 512 | For write-back and fill |

The decoder delivers the reconstructed coherence message to the cache controller.

### **3.6.5**

**Why not decode in the RX controller:**

- Mixes concerns
- Harder to debug

**Why not decode in the cache controller:**

- Cache controller would be more complex
- Cache controller would need to know flit boundaries

**Why not no decode:**

- Cache controller would need to know flit layout
- Cache controller would be much more complex

**Why separate module:**

- Clean separation
- Easy to debug
- Cache controller sees only coherence messages

### **3.6.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Receives decoded messages |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Receives decoded messages |
| Directory array | No | Above the NIC |
| Message set | Yes | Decoder extracts message type |
| Router | No | Below the NIC |
| NIC | Yes | This is the packet decoder |
| Virtual channels | No | VC is extracted but not used by the cache |
| Address map | Yes | Decoder extracts the address |
| Verification | Yes | Every message type must be decoded |

### **3.6.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Clean separation
- Easy debug
- Cache sees only coherence messages

**Loss:**

- One extra module
- One cycle latency to read the whole packet

**Constraint:**

- Decoder must wait for the whole packet
- Decoder must know the packet format

**Verification:**

- Every message type
- Single-flit and multi-flit packets

### **3.6.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Streaming decode | Pipeline update | 1 month |
| ECC check in decoder | ECC logic | 2 weeks |

### **3.6.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Packet decoder | Separate module | Clean separation, easy debug |

---

## **3.7 Cache-NIC Backpressure Handshake**

### **3.7.1 Definition**

The cache-NIC backpressure handshake is the protocol by which the cache and the NIC exchange messages without losing any.

**Unit:** signals in the handshake.

**Location:** `NIC.sv` interface, `cache_controller.sv` interface.

### **3.7.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No handshake | Cache writes; NIC reads. |
| 2 | **Valid-ready handshake** | Standard handshake with valid and ready. |
| 3 | Credit-based | Cache tracks NIC buffer space. |
| 4 | Request-acknowledge | Cache requests; NIC acknowledges. |

### **3.7.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No handshake** | **Valid-ready** | **Credit-based** | **Request-ack** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Loss-free | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Simplicity | High | **High** | Medium | Medium |
| Combinational depth | Low | **Low** | Medium | Medium |
| Fit for single message | No | **Yes** | Partial | Partial |
| Fits 100 MHz | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.7.4**

**Chosen: Valid-ready handshake.**

Two handshakes are used:

**Cache to NIC (transmit):**

| **Signal** | **Direction** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| cache_valid | Cache → NIC | Cache has a coherence message to send |
| nic_ready | NIC → Cache | NIC has space in the TX FIFO |

A transfer occurs on a clock edge when both signals are high.

**NIC to Cache (receive):**

| **Signal** | **Direction** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| nic_valid | NIC → Cache | NIC has a coherence message to deliver |
| cache_ready | Cache → NIC | Cache can accept a message |

A transfer occurs on a clock edge when both signals are high.

### **3.7.5**

**Why not no handshake:**

- Messages would be lost
- The protocol would break

**Why not credit-based:**

- Credit is for VC buffers, not for the cache interface
- The cache interface sends one message at a time

**Why not request-acknowledge:**

- Adds a round-trip
- Does not match the ready-valid convention used elsewhere in the design

**Why valid-ready:**

- Standard handshake
- Loss-free
- Simple
- Matches the rest of the design

### **3.7.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Drives cache_valid, cache_ready |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Drives cache_valid, cache_ready |
| Directory array | No | Above the NIC |
| Message set | Yes | Every message uses the handshake |
| Router | No | Below the NIC |
| NIC | Yes | Drives nic_ready, nic_valid |
| Virtual channels | No | Above the handshake |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | Every handshake case must be tested |

### **3.7.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Loss-free
- Simple
- Standard

**Loss:**

- One message at a time

**Constraint:**

- Cache must assert cache_ready when able
- NIC must assert nic_ready when able

**Verification:**

- Cache stalls (cache_ready low)
- NIC stalls (nic_ready low)
- Back-to-back messages

### **3.7.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multiple outstanding messages | Larger FIFOs | 1 month |
| Split transaction interface | Protocol redesign | 2 months |

### **3.7.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cache-NIC handshake | Valid-ready | Loss-free, simple, standard |

---

# 4.Summary

The NIC connects the coherence protocol inside a tile to the NoC outside it. The TX path converts a message into flits. The TX packet generator uses cut-through. The TX FIFO holds 8 packets. The TX controller is a four-state FSM: IDLE, LOAD, WAIT_READY, SEND. The RX path reads flits from the router. The RX FIFO holds 8 packets. The RX controller is a four-state FSM: IDLE, ACCEPT, DECODE, DELIVER. The packet decoder reads a whole packet and delivers the reconstructed message to the cache. The cache-NIC handshake uses valid-ready signals on both directions. Credit-based flow control per VC prevents flit loss. The NIC meets 100 MHz. The TX FIFO holds one full cache line fill plus 7 control messages. Cut-through was chosen over store-and-forward to keep latency low. The four-state FSMs were chosen over six-state because errors are handled by timeout. The valid-ready handshake was chosen over credit-based for the cache interface because credit is for VC buffers.

---

# **5. Quick Reference**

 

```
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  F11 — NETWORK INTERFACE CONTROLLER                   │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                       │
│  TX PATH                                              │
│  packet_generator: cut-through, writes header first   │
│  TX FIFO: 8 packets                                   │
│  tx_controller: IDLE → LOAD → WAIT_READY → SEND       │
│                                                       │
│  RX PATH                                              │
│  RX FIFO: 8 packets                                   │
│  rx_controller: IDLE → ACCEPT → DECODE → DELIVER      │
│  packet_decoder: reads whole packet, sends message    │
│                                                       │
│  CACHE-NIC HANDSHAKE                                  │
│  Transmit: cache_valid, nic_ready                     │
│  Receive:  nic_valid, cache_ready                     │
│  One message per clock edge                           │
│                                                       │
│  CREDIT FLOW CONTROL                                  │
│  Per VC                                                │
│  Credit counter matches router FIFO depth (4)         │
│  Send only when credit > 0                            │
│                                                       │
│  WHY THIS DESIGN                                      │
│  Coherence produces one message at a time             │
│  Router consumes one flit per cycle per port          │
│  Cache line fills need 9 flits, so TX FIFO ≥ 8 packets │
│                                                       │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

# Intra-Tile Multiplexer Routing

## 1. What the Multiplexer Is

The multiplexer sits between the L1 cache and the rest of the tile. It is not a data multiplexer in the classical sense. It is a **request router**.

The L1 cache produces two kinds of requests:

1. **Local requests** — the home L2 bank is in this tile.
2. **Remote requests** — the home L2 bank is in another tile.

The multiplexer examines the address of each request and decides where the request goes. If the home bank is local, the request goes to the local L2 bank. If the home bank is remote, the request goes to the NIC for transmission over the NoC.

The multiplexer also handles incoming traffic. Packets arriving from the NoC may be destined for the L1 cache (data replies) or for the L2 bank (forwarded requests from other tiles). The multiplexer routes them to the correct destination.

## 2. Why a Multiplexer Is Needed

Without the multiplexer, the L1 would need two separate ports: one to the local L2 and one to the NIC. This would double the L1's interface complexity.

With the multiplexer, the L1 has one port. The multiplexer decides which path the request takes. The L1 does not need to know whether the home bank is local or remote.

This is a clean separation of concerns. The L1 handles cache behavior. The multiplexer handles routing. The NIC handles packetization.

## 3. Where the Multiplexer Sits

```
   ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │                          TILE                              │
   │                                                            │
   │  ┌──────────┐                                              │
   │  │ L1 CACHE │                                              │
   │  └────┬─────┘                                              │
   │       │                                                    │
   │       │  (one port)                                        │
   │       ▼                                                    │
   │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────┐        │
   │  │              MULTIPLEXER                       │        │
   │  │                                                │        │
   │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐      │        │
   │  │  │  Address decode                       │      │        │
   │  │  │  addr[12:9] = home tile?             │      │        │
   │  │  └──────────────┬───────────────────────┘      │        │
   │  │                 │                              │        │
   │  │       ┌─────────┴─────────┐                    │        │
   │  │       │                   │                    │        │
   │  │       ▼                   ▼                    │        │
   │  │  ┌─────────┐         ┌─────────┐               │        │
   │  │  │ LOCAL   │         │ REMOTE  │               │        │
   │  │  │ path    │         │ path    │               │        │
   │  │  └────┬────┘         └────┬────┘               │        │
   │  │       │                   │                    │        │
   │  └───────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────┘        │
   │          │                   │                             │
   │          ▼                   ▼                             │
   │     ┌─────────┐         ┌─────────┐                        │
   │     │ L2 BANK │         │   NIC   │                        │
   │     └─────────┘         └─────────┘                        │
   │                                                            │
   └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

The multiplexer has three interfaces:

| Interface | Direction | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 side | Bidirectional | Requests from L1, responses to L1 |
| Local side | Bidirectional | Requests to L2, responses from L2 |
| Remote side | Bidirectional | Requests to NIC, responses from NIC |

## 4. Address Decode

The multiplexer decodes the home tile from the address. For a 4×4 mesh with 32-bit addresses:

| Bits | Field | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [31:13] | L2 tag | 19 |
| [12:9] | Home tile (X,Y) | 4 |
| [8:6] | L2 set | 3 |
| [5:0] | Byte offset | 6 |

The home tile field is 4 bits. It encodes both X and Y coordinates:

```
   home_tile[3:2] = X coordinate
   home_tile[1:0] = Y coordinate
```

The multiplexer compares `home_tile` with the tile's own ID. If they match, the request goes to the local L2 bank. If not, the request goes to the NIC.

### 4.1 Example

Tile at (1, 2) receives a request for address 0x0000_3040.

```
   addr = 0x0000_3040 = 0000_0000_0000_0000_0011_0000_0100_0000
   bits [12:9] = 1001 = tile (2, 1)
   my tile ID = (1, 2)

   1001 ≠ 0110  →  REMOTE
```

The multiplexer routes the request to the NIC. The NIC constructs a packet with `dest = (2,1)` and sends it over the mesh.

## 5. Request Path (L1 → Destination)

When the L1 issues a request, the multiplexer routes it.

### 5.1 Local Request

1. L1 issues a request with address `A`.
2. The multiplexer extracts `home_tile` from `A`.
3. `home_tile == my_tile_id` → local path.
4. The request goes to the local L2 bank.
5. The L2 bank processes the request and returns a response.
6. The response goes back through the multiplexer to the L1.

### 5.2 Remote Request

1. L1 issues a request with address `A`.
2. The multiplexer extracts `home_tile` from `A`.
3. `home_tile != my_tile_id` → remote path.
4. The request goes to the NIC.
5. The NIC constructs a packet with `dest = home_tile` and `src = my_tile_id`.
6. The NIC sends the packet to the router.
7. The router forwards the packet over the mesh.
8. The destination tile's router delivers the packet to its NIC.
9. The destination NIC decodes the packet and delivers it to its multiplexer.
10. The destination multiplexer routes the request to its local L2 bank.
11. The L2 bank processes the request and sends a response.
12. The response travels back through the NoC in reverse.
13. The source NIC receives the response and delivers it to the source multiplexer.
14. The source multiplexer routes the response to the L1.

## 6. Response Path (Destination → L1)

Incoming packets arrive at the NIC. The NIC decodes them. The multiplexer routes them based on the message type.

### 6.1 Response to L1

If the packet is a **data reply** (DATA or DATAFWD), it is destined for the L1 cache.

1. NIC decodes the packet. It identifies `msg = DATA`.
2. The decoded message goes to the multiplexer.
3. The multiplexer recognizes the message type. It routes it to the L1 cache.
4. The L1 cache installs the data.

### 6.2 Forwarded Request to L2

If the packet is a **forwarded request** (FWD_GETS or FWD_GETM), it is destined for the local L1 cache as a snoop.

Wait — that is wrong. Let me correct it.

A forwarded request is sent from a home L2 bank to the **owner** of a cache line. If the owner is in this tile, the forwarded request must go to the local L1 cache. The L1 then supplies the data to the requester.

If the packet is a **coherence request from another tile** (GETS, GETM, PUTS, PUTM, INVACK), it is destined for the local L2 bank.

Wait — that is also imprecise. Let me correct it again.

Incoming coherence messages fall into two categories:

**Category 1: Messages for the local L2 bank**

These are requests from other tiles for addresses whose home bank is in this tile:

- `NMSG_GETS` — another tile wants a shared copy
- `NMSG_GETM` — another tile wants an exclusive copy
- `NMSG_PUTS` — another tile is writing back a clean line
- `NMSG_PUTM` — another tile is writing back a dirty line
- `NMSG_INVACK` — another tile is acknowledging an invalidation

These go to the local L2 bank. The multiplexer routes them there.

**Category 2: Messages for the local L1 cache**

These are coherence messages from the local L2 bank or from other tiles that the L1 cache must respond to:

- `DATA` — data reply from the home bank
- `DATAFWD` — forwarded data from an owner
- `FWD_GETS` — the local L1 holds the line, and another tile wants a shared copy
- `FWD_GETM` — the local L1 holds the line, and another tile wants exclusive ownership
- `INV` — the local L1 must invalidate its copy
- `UNBLOCK` — an acknowledgment of installation

These go to the local L1 cache. The multiplexer routes them there.

### 6.3 Multiplexer Routing Table for Incoming Packets

| Message | Destination |
| --- | --- |
| NMSG_GETS | Local L2 bank |
| NMSG_GETM | Local L2 bank |
| NMSG_PUTS | Local L2 bank |
| NMSG_PUTM | Local L2 bank |
| NMSG_INVACK | Local L2 bank |
| DATA | Local L1 cache |
| DATAFWD | Local L1 cache |
| FWD_GETS | Local L1 cache (snoop) |
| FWD_GETM | Local L1 cache (snoop) |
| INV | Local L1 cache |
| UNBLOCK | Local L1 cache |
| MEM_REQ | Local mem_tap |
| MEM_DATA | Local mem_tap |

The multiplexer reads the `msg` field from the packet and routes accordingly.

## 7. The Multiplexer as a Crossbar

The multiplexer has two inputs and two outputs on each side. It is a small crossbar.

```
   L1 side:
     L1 → MUX (request)
     MUX → L1 (response)

   Local side:
     MUX → L2 (request)
     L2 → MUX (response)

   Remote side:
     MUX → NIC (request)
     NIC → MUX (response)
```

The multiplexer selects the path based on:

- The direction (L1 → L2, L1 → NIC, L2 → L1, NIC → L1, etc.)
- The address (for outbound requests)
- The message type (for inbound packets)

### 7.1 Outbound Path Selection

For outbound requests from the L1:

```
   if home_tile == my_tile_id:
       route L1 → L2
   else:
       route L1 → NIC
```

### 7.2 Inbound Path Selection

For inbound packets:

```
   if msg in {NMSG_GETS, NMSG_GETM, NMSG_PUTS, NMSG_PUTM, NMSG_INVACK}:
       route NIC → L2
   if msg in {DATA, DATAFWD, FWD_GETS, FWD_GETM, INV, UNBLOCK}:
       route NIC → L1
   if msg in {MEM_REQ, MEM_DATA}:
       route MUX → mem_tap
```

## 8. Arbitration

The multiplexer may receive requests from multiple sources at once:

- L1 issues a request
- NIC delivers an incoming packet
- L2 returns a response

Only one path can use a shared resource at a time. The multiplexer arbitrates.

### 8.1 Priority

| Source | Priority |
| --- | --- |
| Local L1 request | Highest |
| Incoming forwarded request (FWD_*) | High |
| Incoming invalidation (INV) | High |
| Incoming data (DATA, DATAFWD) | Medium |
| Incoming request from another tile (NMSG_*) | Low |

The priority order matters. Local requests are from the tile's own core. They should not be delayed by remote traffic. Forwarded requests and invalidations are protocol-critical. They should not wait behind routine requests.

### 8.2 Blocking Behavior

If the local L2 bank is busy, an incoming request from another tile waits. The multiplexer holds it. When the L2 is free, the request is delivered.

If the L1 is busy, an incoming data reply waits. When the L1 is free, the data is delivered.

If the NIC's TX FIFO is full, a remote request waits. When the FIFO drains, the request is delivered.

### 8.3 Deadlock Avoidance

The multiplexer's arbitration is designed so that no request can wait indefinitely. The priority order ensures that protocol-critical messages are always serviced. The blocking behavior is bounded by the upstream FIFO depths.

## 9. Multiplexer State Machine

The multiplexer has a small state machine.

| State | Meaning | Duration |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `IDLE` | No request in progress | Until a request arrives |
| `DECODE` | Reading address or message type | 1 cycle |
| `ROUTE` | Forwarding the request to the selected path | 1 cycle |
| `WAIT` | Waiting for the destination to accept | Variable |
| `RESPOND` | Forwarding the response back | 1 cycle |

The state machine is simple. Most cycles are in `IDLE`.

## 10. What the Multiplexer Does Not Do

| Feature | Status |
| --- | --- |
| Cache coherence | No — handled by L1 and L2 controllers |
| Packetization | No — handled by the NIC |
| Routing over the NoC | No — handled by the router |
| Address translation | No — the address is used as-is |
| Data buffering | Minimal — one flit of buffering for pipeline registers |
| Error detection | No — handled by higher layers |

The multiplexer is a pure router. It does not make coherence decisions. It does not modify messages. It does not buffer large amounts of data.

## 11. Multiplexer Interface Summary

### 11.1 L1 Side

| Signal | Width | Direction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `l1_req_valid` | 1 | L1 → MUX |
| `l1_req_op` | 2 | L1 → MUX |
| `l1_req_addr` | 32 | L1 → MUX |
| `l1_req_wdata` | 64 | L1 → MUX |
| `l1_req_ready` | 1 | MUX → L1 |
| `l1_resp_valid` | 1 | MUX → L1 |
| `l1_resp_rdata` | 64 | MUX → L1 |
| `l1_resp_hit` | 1 | MUX → L1 |
| `l1_resp_ack` | 1 | MUX → L1 |

### 11.2 L2 Side

| Signal | Width | Direction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `l2_req_valid` | 1 | MUX → L2 |
| `l2_req_msg` | 4 | MUX → L2 |
| `l2_req_addr` | 32 | MUX → L2 |
| `l2_req_data` | 64 | MUX → L2 |
| `l2_req_ready` | 1 | L2 → MUX |
| `l2_resp_valid` | 1 | L2 → MUX |
| `l2_resp_msg` | 4 | L2 → MUX |
| `l2_resp_data` | 64 | L2 → MUX |
| `l2_resp_ack` | 1 | L2 → MUX |

### 11.3 NIC Side

| Signal | Width | Direction |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `nic_tx_valid` | 1 | MUX → NIC |
| `nic_tx_msg` | 4 | MUX → NIC |
| `nic_tx_dest` | 8 | MUX → NIC |
| `nic_tx_addr` | 32 | MUX → NIC |
| `nic_tx_data` | 64 | MUX → NIC |
| `nic_tx_ready` | 1 | NIC → MUX |
| `nic_rx_valid` | 1 | NIC → MUX |
| `nic_rx_msg` | 4 | NIC → MUX |
| `nic_rx_src` | 8 | NIC → MUX |
| `nic_rx_addr` | 32 | NIC → MUX |
| `nic_rx_data` | 64 | NIC → MUX |
| `nic_rx_ack` | 1 | MUX → NIC |

## 12. Timing Example: Remote Load Miss

Tile 0 (source), address 0x80 (home tile 2).

```
   Cycle 0:   L1 issues load, addr = 0x80
              MUX receives request

   Cycle 1:   MUX decodes addr[12:9] = tile (2,0)
              home ≠ my_tile (0,0)
              MUX routes to NIC

   Cycle 2:   NIC receives request
              NIC builds header flit
              NIC writes to TX FIFO

   Cycle 3:   NIC sends flit to router
              Router forwards east (toward tile 2)

   Cycle 4–8: Flit travels over mesh

   Cycle 9:   Tile 2 router receives flit
              Router ejects to local NIC
              NIC decodes NMSG_GETS
              NIC delivers to tile 2's MUX

   Cycle 10:  Tile 2 MUX routes to local L2
              L2 processes request
              L2 returns DATA

   Cycle 11:  Tile 2 MUX routes DATA to NIC
              NIC builds header flit + 8 body flits
              NIC writes to TX FIFO

   Cycle 12–20: Packet travels over mesh back to tile 0

   Cycle 21:  Tile 0 router receives packet
              Router ejects to local NIC
              NIC decodes DATA
              NIC delivers to tile 0's MUX

   Cycle 22:  MUX routes DATA to L1
              L1 installs line
              L1 signals core

   Cycle 23:  Core sees response
              Transaction complete
```

Total latency: 23 cycles for a remote load miss.

## 13. Why the Multiplexer Is Separate From the NIC

The multiplexer and the NIC are two separate blocks. They could be combined into one, but keeping them separate has advantages.

| Aspect | Separate | Combined |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Verification | Each block verified independently | Larger block, harder to verify |
| Debug | Waveforms show clear boundaries | Signals mixed |
| Reuse | MUX can be reused without NIC | Coupled |
| Timing | Short combinational paths | Longer path |
| Synthesis | Better partitioning | Slightly better area |

The separate design matches the layered architecture of the report (Figure 7.1). Each layer presents a simpler interface to the layer above. The multiplexer is a routing block. The NIC is a protocol translation block.

## 14. Multiplexer Summary

| Aspect | Decision |
| --- | --- |
| Function | Route requests based on home tile |
| Inputs | L1, L2, NIC |
| Outputs | L1, L2, NIC |
| Address decode | `addr[12:9]` selects home tile |
| Routing | Local (L2) or remote (NIC) |
| Incoming routing | By message type |
| Arbitration | Fixed priority (local L1 > protocol-critical > incoming requests) |
| State machine | 5 states (IDLE, DECODE, ROUTE, WAIT, RESPOND) |
| Buffering | Minimal (1 flit) |
| Coherence logic | None |

## Summary

the intra-tile multiplexer (MUX) acts like a traffic controller inside one tile. It selects which request is allowed to travel to a shared resource, such as an L2 cache bank or a memory interface.

Suppose the instruction cache and data cache both want to access a shared interface.

1. L1I requests an instruction.
2. L1D requests data.
3. The multiplexer receives both requests.
4. An arbiter selects one request to proceed.
5. The selected request's address, operation and data are forwarded to the destination.
6. The response is routed back to the correct requester.

The intra-tile MUX helps share hardware efficiently, reduces conflicting accesses, and connects local components to the next level of the memory hierarchy.

# Clock and Reset Distribution

**White Paper Section — Design Parameter Justification**

---

## 1. Definition

### 1.1 What Clock and Reset Distribution Are

The clock distribution network delivers the system clock to every sequential element in every tile. The reset distribution network delivers the reset signal to every FSM, FIFO, and register.

**Location:**

- Clock and reset ports in `top.sv`
- Reset synchronizer in `reset_sync.sv`
- Per-tile reset fanout in `tile.sv`
- Boot FSM in `boot_ctrl.sv`

### 1.2 Scope of This Section

This section covers four related decisions:

1. Single vs multiple clock domains
2. Synchronous vs asynchronous reset
3. Reset distribution topology
4. Boot and initialization sequence

### 1.3 Why It Matters

Every FSM in the design depends on a clean clock and a clean reset. A metastable reset or a clock skew between tiles can leave the coherence protocol in an unreachable state. The boot sequence determines whether the directory and caches start in a known state. This section justifies every choice.

---

## 2. Alternatives

### 2.1 Clock Domain Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Single clock domain | One clock drives every tile |
| 2 | Two clock domains | Core clock and NoC clock run at different frequencies |
| 3 | Per-tile clock gating | Single clock domain, gated per tile |
| 4 | Fully asynchronous | No global clock; each tile runs on its own clock |

### 2.2 Reset Type Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Synchronous reset | Reset sampled on the clock edge |
| 2 | Asynchronous reset | Reset takes effect immediately |
| 3 | Async assert, sync deassert | Reset asserts asynchronously, releases synchronously |
| 4 | Software reset | Reset issued by a control register |

### 2.3 Reset Distribution Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Global reset | One reset drives every tile |
| 2 | Per-tile reset | Each tile has its own reset |
| 3 | Hierarchical reset | Global reset plus per-tile sub-resets |
| 4 | Reset tree with buffers | Buffered tree from a root |

### 2.4 Boot Sequence Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Parallel boot | All tiles initialise simultaneously |
| 2 | Sequential boot | Tiles initialise one at a time |
| 3 | Boot FSM | Central FSM sequences the initialisation |
| 4 | Host-driven boot | Host loads configuration after power-on |

---

## 3. Comparison Matrices

### 3.1 Clock Domain Comparison

| Criterion | Single | Two | Gated | Async |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CDC logic required | **No** | Yes | No | Yes |
| Timing closure complexity | **Low** | High | Low | Very high |
| Area cost | **Lowest** | Medium | Low | High |
| Verification cost | **Lowest** | High | Low | Very high |
| FPGA fit (XC7Z020) | **Yes** | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| Matches original design | **Yes** | No | No | No |
