| Fits 3-month timeline | **Yes** | No | Yes | No |
| Power saving | None | Some | Some | Most |

### 3.2 Reset Type Comparison

| Criterion | Sync | Async | **Async/Sync** | SW |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Metastability risk | Low | High | **Low** | Low |
| Reset release timing | Critical | — | **Clean** | Clean |
| Verification complexity | Low | Medium | **Medium** | Low |
| Standard practice | Partial | Partial | **Yes** | Rare |
| Works with FPGA reset pin | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Deterministic boot | Yes | Maybe | **Yes** | Yes |

### 3.3 Reset Distribution Comparison

| Criterion | Global | Per-tile | **Hierarchical** | Tree |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Fanout per net | Very high | Low | **Medium** | Low |
| Timing path | Long | Short | **Short** | Short |
| Tile isolation | None | **Full** | Partial | None |
| Debug flexibility | Low | High | **Medium** | Low |
| Area cost | Low | Medium | **Low** | Medium |
| Fits 4×4 mesh | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### 3.4 Boot Sequence Comparison

| Criterion | Parallel | Sequential | **Boot FSM** | Host |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Boot latency | Lowest | Highest | **Low** | Medium |
| Determinism | High | High | **Highest** | Low |
| Verification | Simple | Simple | **Simple** | Complex |
| Handles directory init | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Handles memory preload | No | No | **Yes** | Yes |
| Fits FPGA | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

---

## 4. Chosen Option

### 4.1 Chosen Clock Domain

**Chosen: Single clock domain.**

One 100 MHz clock drives every tile, every FSM, every FIFO, and every register. The clock is sourced from the ZedBoard's 100 MHz oscillator and constrained to a 10 ns period.

### 4.2 Chosen Reset Type

**Chosen: Asynchronous assert, synchronous deassert.**

The external reset pin asserts the reset asynchronously. A two-flip-flop synchronizer releases the reset synchronously to the system clock.

### 4.3 Chosen Reset Distribution

**Chosen: Hierarchical reset.**

A global reset drives every tile. Each tile fans out the reset locally to its FSMs, FIFOs, and registers. Two sub-resets exist per tile:

| Reset | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| `rst_n_global` | Global reset from the board |
| `rst_n_tile` | Per-tile reset, gated by the boot FSM |
| `rst_n_dbg` | Debug reset, driven by VIO |

### 4.4 Chosen Boot Sequence

**Chosen: Boot FSM.**

A central boot FSM sequences the initialisation. The sequence is:

| # | Step | Action |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Power-on | Reset asserted asynchronously |
| 2 | Clock stable | 100 MHz clock running for 100 cycles |
| 3 | Reset release | Synchronous deassert of global reset |
| 4 | Directory init | All directory entries to `DIR_I`, `valid = 0` |
| 5 | L1 init | All L1 lines to `I` |
| 6 | L2 init | All L2 lines to `DIR_I` |
| 7 | FIFO init | All FIFO pointers to zero |
| 8 | LRU init | All LRU state to a defined value |
| 9 | Memory preload | Behavioural memory loaded by the testbench |
| 10 | Ready signal | `boot_done` asserted |

---

## 5. Why This and Not That

### 5.1 Why Single Clock Domain

**Why not two clock domains:**

- The design is small. The ZedBoard's 100 MHz clock is sufficient for the whole system.
- Multiple clock domains require CDC logic at every tile boundary. Every FIFO crossing becomes a synchronizer.
- CDC logic doubles the verification effort. Every CDC path must be verified formally.
- The coherence protocol assumes a common time base. CDC jitter can cause protocol races that are difficult to reproduce.
- The two clock domains would be needed only if the core ran faster than the NoC. The design has no core.

**Why not per-tile clock gating:**

- Clock gating saves power but adds gate logic per tile.
- The design has no power budget. The ZedBoard is not battery-powered.
- Clock gating requires a clock enable signal per tile. This is a small change but not needed for the paper's contribution.
- It is listed as future scope.

**Why not fully asynchronous:**

- Asynchronous designs require handshake protocols at every interface.
- The coherence protocol is complex. Making it asynchronous multiplies the complexity.
- The verification effort would be 10× the synchronous design.
- The design is not publishable as an asynchronous system in 3 months.

**Why single:**

- The whole design fits on one clock domain. No CDC is required.
- Timing closure is simpler. There is only one clock constraint.
- Verification is simpler. There are no metastability properties.
- The original design used a single clock domain. This is preserved.

### 5.2 Why Async Assert, Sync Deassert

**Why not synchronous reset:**

- A synchronous reset requires the clock to be running before the reset takes effect.
- If the clock fails to start, a synchronous reset never releases.
- The FPGA's reset pin is asynchronous. It must be treated as such.

**Why not pure asynchronous:**

- A pure asynchronous reset releases asynchronously. The release timing is not controlled.
- Different flip-flops may release on different cycles. This causes race conditions in FSMs.
- The coherence protocol cannot tolerate a race in the reset release.

**Why not software reset:**

- A software reset requires the boot code to run first. The boot code cannot run without a reset.
- This is a chicken-and-egg problem.
- A software reset can be a secondary reset path (see §5.4), not the primary one.

**Why async assert, sync deassert:**

- The reset asserts asynchronously. It takes effect immediately when the pin is low.
- A two-flip-flop synchronizer releases the reset on the rising edge of the clock.
- All flip-flops release on the same clock cycle. There is no race.
- This is the standard practice for FPGAs and ASICs.

### 5.3 Why Hierarchical Reset

**Why not global-only:**

- A global reset to all 16 tiles has a very high fanout. The reset net must be buffered heavily.
- Buffering the global reset adds delay and area.
- Debugging is harder. There is no way to reset one tile without resetting all.

**Why not per-tile-only:**

- Per-tile reset requires a separate reset pin per tile. The FPGA does not have 16 spare pins.
- The reset source must be routed through a control register or a global net. This is effectively a hierarchical reset.

**Why not reset tree:**

- A reset tree uses buffers to distribute the reset. The buffers add area and delay.
- The reset tree is a physical implementation detail, not a logical design choice.
- For 16 tiles, the tree is not necessary.

**Why hierarchical:**

- The global reset comes from the board. It reaches all tiles.
- Each tile fans out the reset to its own FSMs, FIFOs, and registers. The fanout per tile is manageable.
- A per-tile reset can be added later for debug. This is a small change.
- The debug reset (`rst_n_dbg`) is separate. The VIO core can reset the design without power cycling.

### 5.4 Why Boot FSM

**Why not parallel boot:**

- Parallel boot starts all tiles at the same time. Every tile initializes its own state.
- This works for simple designs, but the directory initialization must happen before any tile issues a request.
- Without a central sequencer, some tiles may start issuing requests before others have initialized.

**Why not sequential boot:**

- Sequential boot initializes tiles one at a time. This takes 16× longer.
- The boot sequence is not on the critical path. Latency is not the issue.
- Sequential boot complicates the FSM. It must track which tile is being initialized.

**Why not host-driven boot:**

- Host-driven boot requires the host to write configuration to each tile after power-on.
- This requires a host interface. The design has no host interface in the FPGA (only VIO for debug).
- The boot sequence would depend on the host. This is fragile.

**Why boot FSM:**

- A central boot FSM sequences the initialization.
- The FSM starts in `BOOT_IDLE`, transitions through the init steps, and ends in `BOOT_DONE`.
- The design does not issue any coherence requests until `BOOT_DONE` is asserted.
- The FSM is small (10 states). It is easy to verify.
- The FSM can be tested independently of the rest of the design.

---

## 6. Where It Affects the Design

### 6.1 Affected Components

| Component | Affected | How |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Top-level clock input | Yes | 100 MHz input from board |
| Clock distribution | Yes | One net to every tile |
| Reset input | Yes | Async reset from board |
| Reset synchronizer | Yes | Two-flip-flop sync in `reset_sync.sv` |
| Boot FSM | Yes | New module `boot_ctrl.sv` |
| Every FSM | Yes | Reset to IDLE state |
| Every FIFO | Yes | Reset pointers to zero |
| Directory array | Yes | Reset all entries to `DIR_I`, `valid = 0` |
| L1 cache | Yes | Reset all lines to `I` |
| L2 cache | Yes | Reset all lines to `DIR_I` |
| LRU state | Yes | Reset to a defined value |
| VIO debug | Yes | Separate `rst_n_dbg` path |
| Timing constraints | Yes | One clock constraint, reset exception |
| Verification | Yes | Reset sequence properties |

### 6.2 Unaffected Components

| Component | Reason |
| --- | --- |
| Mesh topology | Independent of clock and reset |
| Router ports | Reset to IDLE by the hierarchical reset |
| Virtual channels | FIFO pointers reset |
| Address map | Combinational; no reset needed |
| Flit format | No state; no reset needed |

---

## 7. Consequences

### 7.1 Gain

- Single clock domain. No CDC logic. No CDC verification.
- Clean reset release. All flip-flops release on the same cycle.
- Deterministic boot. The directory and caches start in a known state.
- Debug reset via VIO. The design can be reset without power cycling.
- Reset properties can be verified formally.

### 7.2 Loss

- No per-tile clock gating. Power is not optimized.
- No per-tile reset. A single reset resets the whole design.
- Boot takes 10 cycles after reset. This is negligible.
- Clock skew across the chip is not analyzed (FPGA tools handle this).

### 7.3 Constraint

- The reset synchronizer must be placed at the root of the reset tree.
- The boot FSM must complete before any coherence request is issued.
- The `boot_done` signal must be asserted before the testbench issues any stimulus.
- The VIO debug reset must not interfere with normal operation.

### 7.4 Verification

| Property | Method |
| --- | --- |
| Reset asserts asynchronously | Formal |
| Reset releases synchronously | Formal |
| All FSMs reach IDLE after reset | Formal |
| All FIFO pointers are zero after reset | Formal |
| All directory entries are `DIR_I` after reset | Formal |
| All L1 lines are `I` after reset | Formal |
| Boot FSM completes | Directed test |
| `boot_done` is asserted | Directed test |
| No coherence request before `boot_done` | Formal |
| VIO reset works independently | Directed test |

---

## 8. Future Extension

| Extension | Prerequisite | Estimated time |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Per-tile clock gating | Clock enable per tile | 2 weeks |
| Per-tile reset | Reset distribution change | 2 weeks |
| Two clock domains | CDC synchronizers | 6 weeks |
| Dynamic frequency scaling | Clock generator with divider | 8 weeks |
| Power-on self-test | Boot FSM extension | 4 weeks |
| Software reset via register | Reset control register | 1 week |

---

## 9. Contradiction With Original Design

### 9.1 No Boot FSM in the Original

The original design had no boot FSM. The reset release and initial state were left to the testbench. This made the design's initial state undefined for FPGA use. The redesign adds a boot FSM to sequence initialization.

### 9.2 No Reset Synchronizer in the Original

The original design did not show a reset synchronizer. The reset was likely treated as a synchronous signal. This is unsafe for FPGA use. The redesign uses async assert, sync deassert.

### 9.3 Single Clock Domain in the Original

The original design used a single clock domain. This is correct. The redesign preserves it.

### 9.4 No Debug Reset in the Original

The original design did not show a debug reset path. The redesign adds `rst_n_dbg` for VIO-driven reset without power cycling.

---

## 10. Summary

| Decision | Choice | Main Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Clock domain | Single | No CDC; simple timing closure; matches original |
| Reset type | Async assert, sync deassert | No metastability; deterministic release |
| Reset distribution | Hierarchical | Manageable fanout; per-tile sub-reset possible |
| Boot sequence | Boot FSM | Deterministic init; directory and caches in known state |

The clock and reset distribution is the foundation of the design. Every FSM, FIFO, and register depends on a clean clock and a clean reset. The single clock domain removes CDC complexity. The async-assert-sync-deassert reset removes metastability. The hierarchical reset distribution keeps fanout manageable. The boot FSM guarantees that the directory and caches start in a known state before any coherence request is issued.

---


# Pipeline Depth of Major Blocks

**White Paper Section — Design Parameter Justification**

---

## 1. Definition

### 1.1 What Pipeline Depth Is

Pipeline depth is the number of sequential stages a transaction passes through between the input and output of a block. Each stage is one clock cycle. Depth = latency in cycles.

**Location:**

- L1 cache pipeline: `cache_controller.sv`, `cache_storage.sv`
- L2 cache pipeline: `L2_cache_controller.sv`, `L2_cache_storage.sv`
- Router pipeline: `Router.sv`, `route_comp.sv`, `sw_ctrl.sv`
- NIC pipeline: `NIC.sv`, `tx_controller.sv`, `rx_controller.sv`

### 1.2 Scope of This Section

This section covers four decisions:

1. Cache pipeline depth (L1 and L2)
2. Router pipeline depth
3. NIC pipeline depth
4. Latency vs frequency trade-off

### 1.3 Why It Matters

Pipeline depth sets the round-trip latency of a coherence transaction. A read miss traverses:

L1 (miss) → MUX → NIC TX → Router × hops → NIC RX → L2 → Directory → Memory → L2 → NIC TX → Router × hops → NIC RX → L1 (fill)

Every stage of every block adds to this. A deeper pipeline gives higher clock frequency but higher per-transaction latency. For a coherence protocol, latency dominates because requests bounce multiple times before completing.

---

## 2. Alternatives

### 2.1 Cache Pipeline Depth Alternatives

| # | Depth | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1-stage (combinational) | Tag compare and data read in one cycle |
| 2 | 2-stage | Tag compare, then data read and state update |
| 3 | 3-stage | Tag compare, data read, state update |
| 4 | 4-stage | Fully pipelined; one action per stage |

### 2.2 Router Pipeline Depth Alternatives

| # | Depth | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1-stage | Route, arbitrate, switch in one cycle |
| 2 | 2-stage | Route + arbitrate, then switch |
| 3 | 3-stage | Route, arbitrate, switch (classic) |
| 4 | 4-stage | RC, VA, SA, ST (full classic router) |

### 2.3 NIC Pipeline Depth Alternatives

| # | Depth | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 1-stage | Assemble and send in one cycle |
| 2 | 2-stage | Assemble, then FIFO and send |
| 3 | 3-stage | Assemble, FIFO, send |
| 4 | 4-stage | Assemble, FIFO, arbitrate, send |

### 2.4 Latency vs Frequency Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Maximum frequency | Deep pipeline; 200+ MHz; high latency |
| 2 | Maximum throughput | Deep pipeline with overlap; high area |
| 3 | Minimum latency | Shallow pipeline; low frequency; low latency |
| 4 | Balanced | Moderate depth; 100 MHz; moderate latency |

---

## 3. Comparison Matrices

### 3.1 Cache Pipeline Comparison

| Criterion | 1-stage | **2-stage** | 3-stage | 4-stage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hit latency | 1 cycle | **2 cycles** | 3 cycles | 4 cycles |
| Critical path per stage | Long | **Medium** | Short | Short |
| Max frequency at XC7Z020 | ~50 MHz | **~100 MHz** | ~150 MHz | ~200 MHz |
| Area cost | Lowest | **Low** | Medium | High |
| Complexity | Lowest | **Low** | Medium | High |
| Verification | Simple | **Simple** | Medium | Complex |
| Fits 100 MHz target | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Maybe |

### 3.2 Router Pipeline Comparison

| Criterion | 1-stage | 2-stage | **3-stage** | 4-stage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Per-hop latency | 1 cycle | 2 cycles | **3 cycles** | 4 cycles |
| Critical path per stage | Very long | Long | **Short** | Short |
| Max frequency | ~30 MHz | ~60 MHz | **~120 MHz** | ~200 MHz |
| Area cost | Lowest | Low | **Medium** | High |
| Handles VC allocation | No | Partial | **Yes** | Yes |
| Handles contention | Poor | Medium | **Good** | Good |
| Fits 100 MHz target | No | No | **Yes** | Yes |

### 3.3 NIC Pipeline Comparison

| Criterion | 1-stage | **2-stage** | 3-stage | 4-stage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| TX latency | 1 cycle | **2 cycles** | 3 cycles | 4 cycles |
| RX latency | 1 cycle | **2 cycles** | 3 cycles | 4 cycles |
| Critical path | Long | **Medium** | Short | Short |
| Max frequency | ~40 MHz | **~100 MHz** | ~150 MHz | ~200 MHz |
| Area cost | Lowest | **Low** | Medium | High |
| Handles packet assembly | Partial | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| FIFO decoupling | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### 3.4 Latency vs Frequency Comparison

| Criterion | Max freq | Max thruput | Min latency | **Balanced** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Clock frequency | 200 MHz | 200 MHz | 50 MHz | **100 MHz** |
| Per-transaction latency | Highest | High | Lowest | **Medium** |
| Area cost | High | Highest | Low | **Medium** |
| Coherence traffic latency | Poor | Medium | **Good** | **Good** |
| Complexity | High | Highest | Low | **Medium** |
| Verification | Complex | Complex | Simple | **Simple** |
| FPGA fit | Tight | No | Yes | **Yes** |
| Fits 3-month timeline | No | No | Yes | **Yes** |

---

## 4. Chosen Option

### 4.1 Chosen Cache Pipeline

**Chosen: 2-stage.**

L1 cache pipeline:

| Stage | Action |
| --- | --- |
| S1 | Tag compare + directory lookup + hit/miss decision |
| S2 | Data read + state update + response |

L2 cache pipeline:

| Stage | Action |
| --- | --- |
| S1 | Directory lookup + tag compare |
| S2 | State update + response (or memory request) |

### 4.2 Chosen Router Pipeline

**Chosen: 3-stage.**

| Stage | Action |
| --- | --- |
| S1 (RC) | Route computation from destination field |
| S2 (VA+SA) | VC allocation + switch arbitration |
| S3 (ST) | Switch traversal through crossbar |

### 4.3 Chosen NIC Pipeline

**Chosen: 2-stage.**

TX path:

| Stage | Action |
| --- | --- |
| S1 | Packet assembly (header + body flits) |
| S2 | FIFO write + TX handshake |

RX path:

| Stage | Action |
| --- | --- |
| S1 | RX handshake + FIFO write |
| S2 | Packet decode + deliver to cache |

### 4.4 Chosen Latency vs Frequency Trade-off

**Chosen: Balanced.**

100 MHz clock. Moderate pipeline depth. Latency dominated by hop count, not pipeline stages.

Total latency for a remote L1 miss (2 hops in mesh):

| Component | Cycles |
| --- | --- |
| L1 pipeline | 2 |
| MUX | 1 |
| NIC TX | 2 |
| Router × 2 hops | 6 |
| NIC RX | 2 |
| L2 pipeline | 2 |
| Directory | 2 |
| Memory | 4 (assumed) |
| L2 response | 2 |
| NIC TX | 2 |
| Router × 2 hops | 6 |
| NIC RX | 2 |
| L1 fill | 2 |
| **Total** | **35 cycles** |

At 100 MHz, that is **350 ns**. This is a reasonable remote miss latency for a small multicore.

---

## 5. Why This and Not That

### 5.1 Why 2-Stage Cache and Not 1 or 3

**Why not 1-stage:**

- A combinational tag compare and data read has a long critical path. The maximum frequency is around 50 MHz on XC7Z020.
- The 100 MHz target cannot be met with a 1-stage cache.

**Why not 3-stage:**

- A 3-stage cache adds one cycle to every hit. This directly slows every load and store.
- The extra stage is needed only if the frequency target is above 150 MHz.
- The 100 MHz target does not require a 3-stage cache.

**Why not 4-stage:**

- A 4-stage cache is a fully pipelined design. This requires hazard logic for back-to-back accesses.
- The added complexity is not justified at 100 MHz.

**Why 2-stage:**

- Two stages meet the 100 MHz target on XC7Z020.
- Two stages keep the hit latency at 2 cycles. This is competitive.
- Two stages require no hazard logic. Back-to-back accesses flow through naturally.
- Two stages are simple to verify.

### 5.2 Why 3-Stage Router and Not 1, 2, or 4

**Why not 1-stage:**

- Route computation, VC allocation, switch allocation, and crossbar traversal in one cycle is too much logic.
- The critical path would exceed 30 ns. The frequency target of 100 MHz would not be met.

**Why not 2-stage:**

- A 2-stage router merges route computation with VC allocation. This works but the VC allocation path is still long.

# **Pipeline Depth of Major Blocks**

### **5. Why This and Not That**

**Why not 3-stage:**

A 3-stage cache adds one cycle to every hit. This directly slows every load and store.

The extra stage is needed only if the frequency target is above 150 MHz.

**Why not 4-stage:**

A 4-stage cache is a fully pipelined design. This requires hazard logic for back-to-back accesses.

The added complexity is not justified at 100 MHz.

Two stages meet the 100 MHz target on XC7Z020.

Two stages keep the hit latency at 2 cycles. This is competitive.

Two stages require no hazard logic. Back-to-back accesses flow through naturally.

Two stages are simple to verify.

#### **5.2 Why 3-Stage Router and Not 1, 2, or 4**

**Why not 1-stage:**

Route computation, VC allocation, switch allocation, and crossbar traversal in one cycle is too much logic.

The critical path would exceed 30 ns. The frequency target of 100 MHz would not be met.

**Why not 2-stage:**

A 2-stage router merges route computation with VC allocation. This works but the VC allocation path is still long.

The critical path in stage 1 would be route + VC allocation. This may not meet 100 MHz under all corners.

The savings over 3-stage are one cycle per hop. Over 2 hops, this is 2 cycles out of a 35-cycle transaction.

**Why not 4-stage:**

The 4-stage router is the classic design (RC, VA, SA, ST). It is the standard in textbooks.

The extra stage adds one cycle per hop. Over 2 hops, this is 2 cycles.

The 4-stage design is used when the frequency target is 200 MHz or higher.

At 100 MHz, the 4-stage router is overkill.

**Why 3-stage:**

The 3-stage router meets 100 MHz with margin.

The stages are balanced: RC is combinational, VA+SA is arbitration, ST is crossbar.

The design is simple to verify. Each stage has one clear function.

3-stage is the standard for moderate-frequency NoCs.

#### **5.3 Why 2-Stage NIC and Not 1 or 3**

**Why not 1-stage:**

Packet assembly and FIFO write in one cycle is possible for control messages (1 flit). But for 9-flit cache lines, it requires 9 cycles of packet assembly.

The 1-stage NIC cannot overlap assembly with transmission. This wastes bandwidth.

**Why not 3-stage:**

A 3-stage NIC adds one cycle each to TX and RX. Over a round trip, this is 4 cycles.

The extra stage would be needed only if the frequency target were above 150 MHz.

**Why not 4-stage:**

A 4-stage NIC is used in high-frequency designs. It is not needed at 100 MHz.

**Why 2-stage:**

Two stages meet 100 MHz.

Two stages allow assembly to overlap with transmission. This is important for multi-flit packets.

Two stages are simple to verify.

The FIFO decouples the cache from the network. This is why the design has a FIFO in the first place.

#### **5.4 Why Balanced and Not Max Frequency**

**Why not maximum frequency:**

The XC7Z020 cannot support 200 MHz across a 4×4 mesh with 5-port routers. The wire delay alone would fail timing.

Higher frequency requires deeper pipelines. Deeper pipelines increase latency per transaction.

Coherence traffic has a round trip of many hops. Every added cycle multiplies.

**Why not maximum throughput:**

Maximum throughput requires deep pipelines with full overlap. This doubles area.

The design's throughput is bounded by the coherence protocol, not by the pipeline depth.

The paper's contribution is coherence, not throughput per cycle.

**Why not minimum latency:**

Minimum latency would require a 50 MHz clock. This is too slow for a modern design claim.

The FPGA tools would still need to meet timing at 50 MHz, but the design would be judged as underperforming.

**Why balanced:**

100 MHz is the target stated in the white paper (§6.4).

100 MHz is achievable with 2-stage cache, 3-stage router, 2-stage NIC.

The total per-transaction latency is 35 cycles (350 ns) for a remote miss.

This is competitive with production NoCs at similar frequencies.

The design fits the XC7Z020 comfortably.

### **6. Where It Affects the Design**

#### **6.1 Affected Components**

| **Component** | **Affected** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | 2-stage pipeline registers |
| L1 cache storage | Yes | Registered tag and data outputs |
| L2 cache controller | Yes | 2-stage pipeline registers |
| L2 cache storage | Yes | Registered tag, data, and directory outputs |
| Router | Yes | 3-stage pipeline registers (RC, VA+SA, ST) |
| Router crossbar | Yes | Registered in stage 3 |
| NIC TX | Yes | 2-stage pipeline (assemble, FIFO/send) |
| NIC RX | Yes | 2-stage pipeline (FIFO/decode, deliver) |
| Clock constraint | Yes | 10 ns period; pipeline depth must meet it |
| Verification | Yes | Pipeline hazard properties |
| Area budget | Yes | Pipeline registers add area |

#### **6.2 Unaffected Components**

| **Component** | **Reason** |
| --- | --- |
| Address map | Combinational; no pipeline |
| Flit format | No pipeline dependency |
| Mesh topology | Independent of pipeline depth |
| Boot FSM | Runs once at startup; no pipeline needed |
| Reset | Independent of pipeline depth |
| Directory format | Independent of pipeline depth |

### **7. Consequences**

#### **7.1 Gain**

100 MHz target is met with margin.

Per-transaction latency is 35 cycles for a remote miss.

Pipeline stages are balanced. No single stage dominates the critical path.

No hazard logic is needed. Back-to-back accesses flow through.

Verification is tractable. Each stage has one function.

The design fits the XC7Z020 with room to spare.

#### **7.2 Loss**

Hit latency is 2 cycles, not 1. This is a small penalty.

Per-hop latency is 3 cycles, not 1 or 2. Over multiple hops, this adds up.

The design is not maximally fast. It trades some speed for simplicity and correctness.

#### **7.3 Constraint**

The critical path of each stage must be under 10 ns.

The router's VC allocation stage must complete in one cycle.

The NIC's packet assembly must complete in one cycle per flit.

Pipeline registers must be placed at every stage boundary.

#### **7.4 Verification**

| **Property** | **Method** |
| --- | --- |
| Pipeline hazards do not occur | Formal |
| Back-to-back accesses flow through | Directed tests |
| Remote miss latency is bounded | Timing analysis |
| Every stage meets timing | Static timing analysis |
| Pipeline registers are not bypassed | Structural check |
| No data loss between stages | Directed + formal |

### **8. Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 3-stage cache | Pipeline refactor | 2 weeks |
| 2-stage router | Merge RC with VA | 2 weeks |
| 4-stage router | Split VA and SA | 2 weeks |
| Speculative router | Add speculation path | 4 weeks |
| Deeper NIC | Split assembly from FIFO write | 2 weeks |
| 200 MHz target | Deeper pipelines + retiming | 6 weeks |

### **9. Contradiction With Original Design**

#### **9.1 No Pipeline in the Original**

The original design did not document pipeline depth. The FSM structure suggests a single-stage controller. This means the original design likely ran at a lower frequency than the white paper's target.

#### **9.2 No Timing Analysis in the Original**

The original design did not show a timing report in the repository. Without a timing report, the actual frequency is unknown. The white paper's 100 MHz target is a new claim.

#### **9.3 No Pipeline Registers Documented**

The original design did not document where pipeline registers are placed. The white paper's 2-stage cache, 3-stage router, and 2-stage NIC are new design decisions.

### **10. Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Main Reason** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cache pipeline | 2-stage | Meets 100 MHz; low hit latency |
| Router pipeline | 3-stage | Balanced stages; meets 100 MHz |
| NIC pipeline | 2-stage | Meets 100 MHz; FIFO decoupling |
| Latency vs frequency | Balanced | 100 MHz; 35-cycle remote miss |

The pipeline depth of each block is chosen to meet the 100 MHz target on the XC7Z020. The stages are balanced. No hazard logic is required. The design is simple to verify. The per-transaction latency is 35 cycles for a remote miss, which is competitive for a small multicore design.

- The critical path in stage 1 would be route + VC allocation. This may not meet 100 MHz under all corners.
- The savings over 3-stage are one cycle per hop. Over 2 hops, this is 2 cycles out of a 35-cycle transaction.

**Why not 4-stage:**

- The 4-stage router is the classic design (RC, VA, SA, ST). It is the standard in textbooks.
- The extra stage adds one cycle per hop. Over 2 hops, this is 2 cycles.
- The 4-stage design is used when the frequency target is 200 MHz or higher.
- At 100 MHz, the 4-stage router is overkill.

**Why 3-stage:**

- The stages are balanced: RC is combinational, VA+SA is arbitration, ST is crossbar.
- The design is simple to verify. Each stage has one clear function.
- 3-stage is the standard for moderate-frequency NoCs.
