# Design Parameterisation for N×N Mesh and Variable Widths

## 1. Why Parameterise

The design must support multiple configurations from the same RTL:

| Configuration | Purpose |
| --- | --- |
| N=2 (2×2) | FPGA implementation (fits on XC7Z020) |
| N=4 (4×4) | Simulation and emulation |
| N=8 (8×8) | Scaling study (simulation only) |
| N=16 (16×16) | Future scope |

The same RTL must instantiate all of these without modification. Parameterisation makes this possible.

## 2. Parameter Taxonomy

Parameters are grouped into five categories.

| Category | Examples | Where Defined |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mesh dimension | N | Top-level package |
| Tile identity | TILE_ID, X_COORD, Y_COORD | Per-tile parameter |
| Data width | ADDR_WIDTH, DATA_WIDTH, FLIT_WIDTH | Top-level package |
| Cache geometry | L1_SETS, L1_WAYS, L2_SETS, L2_WAYS, LINE_BYTES | Top-level package |
| Network geometry | NUM_VC, FIFO_DEPTH | Top-level package |

## 3. The Parameter Package

All global parameters are defined in one SystemVerilog package.

```
package noc_pkg;

  // ============================================================
  // Mesh dimension
  // ============================================================
  parameter int N = 4;                       // 4x4 mesh default
  parameter int NUM_TILES = N * N;           // 16 tiles
  parameter int TILE_ID_WIDTH = $clog2(NUM_TILES);   // 4 bits
  parameter int X_WIDTH = $clog2(N);         // 2 bits
  parameter int Y_WIDTH = $clog2(N);         // 2 bits

  // ============================================================
  // Data widths
  // ============================================================
  parameter int ADDR_WIDTH = 32;             // 4 GB address space
  parameter int DATA_WIDTH = 64;             // 8 bytes per flit
  parameter int FLIT_WIDTH = 64;             // Same as DATA_WIDTH
  parameter int LINE_BYTES = 64;             // Cache line size
  parameter int LINE_WIDTH = LINE_BYTES * 8; // 512 bits
  parameter int BODY_FLITS = LINE_WIDTH / FLIT_WIDTH;   // 8
  parameter int PKT_FLITS = BODY_FLITS + 1;  // 9 (header + body)

  // ============================================================
  // Cache geometry
  // ============================================================
  parameter int L1_SETS = 4;
  parameter int L1_WAYS = 4;
  parameter int L2_SETS = 8;
  parameter int L2_WAYS = 4;
  parameter int L1_SET_IDX_WIDTH = $clog2(L1_SETS);   // 2
  parameter int L2_SET_IDX_WIDTH = $clog2(L2_SETS);   // 3
  parameter int OFFSET_WIDTH = $clog2(LINE_BYTES);     // 6

  // ============================================================
  // Directory
  // ============================================================
  parameter int DIR_STATE_WIDTH = 3;
  parameter int OWNER_WIDTH = TILE_ID_WIDTH;  // 4
  parameter int SHARER_WIDTH = NUM_TILES;     // 16
  parameter int DIR_ENTRY_WIDTH =
      DIR_STATE_WIDTH + OWNER_WIDTH + SHARER_WIDTH + 1;  // 24

  // ============================================================
  // Network
  // ============================================================
  parameter int NUM_VC = 4;
  parameter int FIFO_DEPTH = 4;
  parameter int CREDIT_WIDTH = $clog2(FIFO_DEPTH + 1);   // 3
  parameter int NUM_PORTS = 5;               // LOCAL, N, S, E, W

  // ============================================================
  // Derived address field positions
  // ============================================================
  // L2 decode: [31:13] tag | [12:9] home | [8:6] L2 set | [5:0] offset
  parameter int L2_TAG_LSB = L2_SET_IDX_WIDTH + OFFSET_WIDTH
                             + TILE_ID_WIDTH;             // 13
  parameter int HOME_TILE_LSB = L2_SET_IDX_WIDTH + OFFSET_WIDTH;  // 9
  parameter int L2_SET_LSB = OFFSET_WIDTH;                   // 6

  // L1 decode: [31:8] tag | [7:6] L1 set | [5:0] offset
  parameter int L1_TAG_LSB = L1_SET_IDX_WIDTH + OFFSET_WIDTH;     // 8
  parameter int L1_SET_LSB = OFFSET_WIDTH;                   // 6

endpackage
```

## 4. How N Affects Everything

Changing N cascades through the design.

### 4.1 Parameter Cascade for N

| Derived Parameter | Formula | N=2 | N=4 | N=8 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NUM_TILES | N² | 4 | 16 | 64 |
| TILE_ID_WIDTH | ⌈log₂(N²)⌉ | 2 | 4 | 6 |
| X_WIDTH | ⌈log₂(N)⌉ | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| Y_WIDTH | ⌈log₂(N)⌉ | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| SHARER_WIDTH | N² | 4 | 16 | 64 |
| OWNER_WIDTH | TILE_ID_WIDTH | 2 | 4 | 6 |
| DIR_ENTRY_WIDTH | 3+owner+N²+1 | 10 | 24 | 74 |
| HOME_TILE_LSB | set+offset | 8 | 9 | 10 |
| L2_TAG_LSB | home+set+offset | 11 | 13 | 17 |

The RTL does not need to be edited. Only the value of N changes.

### 4.2 Address Decode for N=2, 4, 8

**N=2 (4 tiles):**

| Bits | Field | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [31:11] | L2 tag | 21 |
| [10:9] | Home tile | 2 |
| [8:6] | L2 set | 3 |
| [5:0] | Offset | 6 |

**N=4 (16 tiles):**

| Bits | Field | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [31:13] | L2 tag | 19 |
| [12:9] | Home tile | 4 |
| [8:6] | L2 set | 3 |
| [5:0] | Offset | 6 |

**N=8 (64 tiles):**

| Bits | Field | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [31:17] | L2 tag | 15 |
| [16:11] | Home tile | 6 |
| [10:8] | L2 set | 3 |
| [7:0] | Offset | 8 |

Wait — the offset width changed. That is because LINE_BYTES is a separate parameter. For this design, LINE_BYTES = 64 regardless of N. So OFFSET_WIDTH = 6 always.

**Corrected N=8:**

| Bits | Field | Width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [31:15] | L2 tag | 17 |
| [14:9] | Home tile | 6 |
| [8:6] | L2 set | 3 |
| [5:0] | Offset | 6 |

The address decode is computed from parameters. The RTL uses the package constants. No manual edits.

### 4.3 Sharer Vector

The sharer vector is a bit vector with one bit per tile. Its width is `SHARER_WIDTH = N²`.

| N | SHARER_WIDTH | Directory entry width |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2 | 4 | 10 |
| 4 | 16 | 24 |
| 8 | 64 | 74 |
| 16 | 256 | 266 |

The directory entry grows with N². At N=16, the directory entry is 266 bits. This is still manageable in BRAM.

## 5. Parameter Propagation Through the Hierarchy

Parameters flow from the top module down to every leaf module.

```
   top
    │
    ├── noc_pkg (imported everywhere)
    │
    ├── tile[N][N]   (generate loop)
    │    │
    │    ├── tile_id = x*N + y
    │    │
    │    ├── l1_cache
    │    │    └── imports noc_pkg
    │    │
    │    ├── mux
    │    │    └── imports noc_pkg
    │    │
    │    ├── l2_cache
    │    │    └── imports noc_pkg
    │    │
    │    ├── mem_tap
    │    │    └── imports noc_pkg
    │    │
    │    ├── nic
    │    │    └── imports noc_pkg
    │    │
    │    └── router
    │         └── imports noc_pkg
    │
    └── mesh_interconnect
         └── wires between routers
```

Each module `import noc_pkg::*;` at the top. It then uses `N`, `FLIT_WIDTH`, `TILE_ID_WIDTH`, etc. directly.

### 5.1 Module Parameter Declarations

Some modules need per-instance parameters, not just package constants. These use the module parameter list.

```
module tile #(
  parameter int TILE_ID = 0,
  parameter int X_COORD = 0,
  parameter int Y_COORD = 0
) (
  input  logic clk,
  input  logic rst_n,
  // Core interface
  input  logic                          core_req_valid,
  input  logic [1:0]                    core_req_op,
  input  logic [ADDR_WIDTH-1:0]         core_req_addr,
  input  logic [DATA_WIDTH-1:0]         core_req_wdata,
  output logic                          core_req_ready,
  // ... more ports
);
  import noc_pkg::*;

  // Inside, use TILE_ID, X_COORD, Y_COORD for routing decisions
  // Use package constants for widths and geometry
endmodule
```

### 5.2 Top-Level Instantiation

```
module top #(
  parameter int N = 4
) (
  input logic clk,
  input logic rst_n
);
  import noc_pkg::*;

  // Tile instances
  tile #(
    .TILE_ID (0),
    .X_COORD (0),
    .Y_COORD (0)
  ) u_tile_0_0 (
    .clk (clk),
    .rst_n (rst_n),
    // ...
  );

  // ... N² instances
endmodule
```

### 5.3 Generate Loop for N² Tiles

Manual instantiation for 16 tiles is verbose. A generate loop is cleaner.

```
module top #(
  parameter int N = 4
) (
  input logic clk,
  input logic rst_n
);
  import noc_pkg::*;

  // Tile array
  tile #(.N (N)) u_tile [N][N] ();

  // Or, with explicit parameters per tile:
  generate
    for (genvar x = 0; x < N; x++) begin : gen_x
      for (genvar y = 0; y < N; y++) begin : gen_y
        localparam int TILE_ID = x * N + y;

        tile #(
          .TILE_ID (TILE_ID),
          .X_COORD (x),
          .Y_COORD (y)
        ) u_tile (
          .clk (clk),
          .rst_n (rst_n),
          // ...
        );
      end
    end
  endgenerate
endmodule
```

The generate loop instantiates N² tiles. Each gets its own `TILE_ID`, `X_COORD`, and `Y_COORD`.

### 5.4 Mesh Interconnect

The mesh wiring is also generated.

```
  // East-west links
  generate
    for (genvar x = 0; x < N-1; x++) begin : gen_ew
      for (genvar y = 0; y < N; y++) begin : gen_y
        assign u_tile[x][y].east_out = u_tile[x+1][y].west_in;
        assign u_tile[x+1][y].west_out = u_tile[x][y].east_in;
      end
    end
  endgenerate

  // North-south links
  generate
    for (genvar x = 0; x < N; x++) begin : gen_ns
      for (genvar y = 0; y < N-1; y++) begin : gen_y
        assign u_tile[x][y].north_out = u_tile[x][y+1].south_in;
        assign u_tile[x][y+1].south_out = u_tile[x][y].north_in;
      end
    end
  endgenerate
```

At the edges, the unconnected ports are tied off.

```
  // Tie off west port of column 0
  generate
    for (genvar y = 0; y < N; y++) begin : gen_west_tie
      assign u_tile[0][y].west_in = '0;
      // west_out is unused
    end
  endgenerate

  // Similar for east, north, south
```

## 6. Synthesis-Time Specialisation

The synthesis tool does not support runtime parameter changes. Parameters are fixed at elaboration time. This means:

1. To build for N=2, the top-level parameter is set to 2.
2. The design is elaborated.
3. The synthesis tool specialises all modules for N=2.
4. Tied-off ports and unused logic are optimised away.

### 6.1 Synthesis Constraints

Synthesis constraints are parameter-dependent. The XDC file uses parameter-derived values.

```
# XDC constraints (parameterised)
set N 4
set NUM_TILES [expr {$N * $N}]

# Clock constraint
create_clock -period 10.0 [get_ports clk]

# Timing exceptions (if any)
# ...
```

### 6.2 Build Targets

The build system supports multiple configurations.

```makefile
# Makefile
N ?= 4

SIM_TOP = top

all: sim_N$(N)

sim_N4:
	verilator --binary --timing \
	  -GN=4 \
	  -I. \
	  --top-module $(SIM_TOP) \
	  $(SRCS)

synth_N2:
	vivado -mode batch -source synth.tcl -tclargs N=2

synth_N4:
	vivado -mode batch -source synth.tcl -tclargs N=4
```

The `-G` flag (Verilator) or `-generic` flag (Vivado) overrides the top-level parameter.

### 6.3 FPGA Specialisation

The FPGA build uses N=2 because the XC7Z020 has limited resources.

| Resource | N=2 estimate | N=4 estimate | XC7Z020 available |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| LUTs | ~15,000 | ~60,000 | 53,200 |
| BRAMs | ~40 | ~240 | 140 |
| Registers | ~20,000 | ~80,000 | 106,400 |

N=2 fits. N=4 does not. This is why the FPGA build uses N=2.

### 6.4 Simulation Specialisation

Simulation is not resource-constrained. The default is N=4. N=8 can be built for scaling studies, but simulation time grows.

| N | Tiles | Simulation time (relative) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2 | 4 | 1× |
| 4 | 16 | 4× |
| 8 | 64 | 16× |
| 16 | 256 | 64× |

The scaling is roughly N² for memory and N² for time. N=16 is possible but slow.

## 7. Configuration Table

All supported configurations and their parameters.

| Parameter | N=2 | N=4 | N=8 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NUM_TILES | 4 | 16 | 64 |
| TILE_ID_WIDTH | 2 | 4 | 6 |
| X_WIDTH | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| Y_WIDTH | 1 | 2 | 3 |
| SHARER_WIDTH | 4 | 16 | 64 |
| OWNER_WIDTH | 2 | 4 | 6 |
| DIR_ENTRY_WIDTH | 10 | 24 | 74 |
| HOME_TILE_LSB | 8 | 9 | 10 |
| L2_TAG_LSB | 11 | 13 | 17 |
| ADDR_WIDTH | 32 | 32 | 32 |
| DATA_WIDTH | 64 | 64 | 64 |
| FLIT_WIDTH | 64 | 64 | 64 |
| LINE_BYTES | 64 | 64 | 64 |
| L1_SETS | 4 | 4 | 4 |
| L1_WAYS | 4 | 4 | 4 |
| L2_SETS | 8 | 8 | 8 |
| L2_WAYS | 4 | 4 | 4 |
| NUM_VC | 4 | 4 | 4 |
| FIFO_DEPTH | 4 | 4 | 4 |
| NUM_PORTS | 5 | 5 | 5 |

## 8. Variable Width Parameters

Beyond N, the design also parameterises data widths.

### 8.1 Address Width

`ADDR_WIDTH` is 32 by default. It can be reduced to save area.

| ADDR_WIDTH | Address space | Use case |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 8 | 256 bytes | Original CDAC design |
| 16 | 64 KB | Small testbed |
| 32 | 4 GB | Full design |
| 64 | 16 EB | Future extension |

Changing `ADDR_WIDTH` affects:

- The address field in the flit
- The tag width in L1 and L2
- The address decode

The RTL uses `ADDR_WIDTH` everywhere. No manual edits.

### 8.2 Data Width

`DATA_WIDTH` is 64 by default. It can be reduced.

| DATA_WIDTH | Bytes per flit | Flits per 64B line |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 32 | 4 | 16 |
| 64 | 8 | 8 |
| 128 | 16 | 4 |

Changing `DATA_WIDTH` changes the flit width. The router crossbar, the NIC FIFOs, and the L2 data array all scale.

### 8.3 Cache Line Size

`LINE_BYTES` is 64 by default. It can be changed.

| LINE_BYTES | BODY_FLITS (64-bit flit) | Spatial locality |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 16 | 2 | Low |
| 32 | 4 | Medium |
| 64 | 8 | High |
| 128 | 16 | Very high |

Changing `LINE_BYTES` changes:

- The offset width
- The number of body flits per data packet
- The L1 and L2 line width

## 9. Parameter Validation

Not all parameter combinations are legal. The package includes assertions.

```
package noc_pkg;

  // ... parameter definitions ...

  // Validation
  initial begin
    assert (N >= 2) else $error("N must be >= 2");
    assert (N <= 16) else $error("N must be <= 16");
    assert (FLIT_WIDTH == 64) else $error("Only 64-bit flits supported");
    assert (LINE_BYTES % 8 == 0) else $error("Line size must be multiple of 8");
    assert (NUM_VC == 4) else $error("Only 4 VCs supported");
    assert (FIFO_DEPTH >= 2) else $error("FIFO depth must be >= 2");
  end

endpackage
```

These checks run at elaboration. If a parameter is out of range, the build fails.

## 10. What Is Not Parameterised

Some things are fixed by design choice:

| Element | Value | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Protocol | MESI | Chosen protocol |
| Routing | XY | Chosen routing |
| VC count | 4 | Chosen VC assignment |
| Topology | 2D mesh | Chosen topology |
| Directory location | Per-bank | Chosen design |
| Flit width | 64 | Chosen flit layout |

Changing these would require RTL changes, not parameter overrides.

## 11. Parameter Propagation Summary Diagram

```
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  noc_pkg.sv                                          │
   │  ─────────────                                       │
   │  parameter int N = 4;                                │
   │  parameter int ADDR_WIDTH = 32;                      │
   │  parameter int DATA_WIDTH = 64;                      │
   │  parameter int FLIT_WIDTH = 64;                      │
   │  parameter int L1_SETS = 4;                          │
   │  parameter int L2_SETS = 8;                          │
   │  parameter int NUM_VC = 4;                           │
   │  ...                                                 │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │
                          │ import noc_pkg::*
                          │
                          ▼
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  top.sv                                              │
   │  ───────                                             │
   │  module top #(parameter int N = 4);                  │
   │    import noc_pkg::*;                                │
   │                                                      │
   │    generate                                          │
   │      for (x=0; x<N; x++)                             │
   │        for (y=0; y<N; y++)                           │
   │          tile #(.TILE_ID(x*N+y), ...) u_tile();      │
   │    endgenerate                                       │
   │  endmodule                                           │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │
                          │ instantiate N² tiles
                          │
                          ▼
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  tile.sv                                             │
   │  ───────                                             │
   │  module tile #(TILE_ID, X_COORD, Y_COORD);           │
   │    import noc_pkg::*;                                │
   │    l1_cache  u_l1();                                 │
   │    mux       u_mux();                                │
   │    l2_cache  u_l2();                                 │
   │    nic       u_nic();                                │
   │    router    u_router();                             │
   │  endmodule                                           │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                          │
                          │ instantiate leaf modules
                          │
                          ▼
   ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │  router.sv                                           │
   │  ─────────                                           │
   │  module router;                                      │
   │    import noc_pkg::*;                                │
   │    // Uses N, FLIT_WIDTH, NUM_VC, FIFO_DEPTH         │
   │    // All from package, no module parameters         │
   │  endmodule                                           │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

## 12. One-Paragraph Summary

The design is parameterised through a single SystemVerilog package (`noc_pkg`) that defines N, address width, data width, flit width, cache geometry, directory geometry, and network geometry. Derived parameters (tile ID width, sharer width, address field positions) are computed from the primary parameters. Every module imports the package and uses the constants directly. The top module instantiates N² tiles via generate loops. Each tile receives its own `TILE_ID`, `X_COORD`, and `Y_COORD` as module parameters. The mesh wiring is generated by nested generate loops. Edge ports are tied off. Synthesis-time specialisation is achieved by overriding the top-level parameter (e.g., `-GN=2` for Verilator, `-generic N=2` for Vivado). The same RTL supports N=2, N=4, N=8, and N=16 without edits. Variable widths (address, data, line size) are also parameterised. Parameter validation assertions catch illegal configurations at elaboration. Elements not parameterised (protocol, routing, VC count, topology) are fixed design choices that would require RTL changes if altered.

**White Paper Section — Design Parameter Justification**

---

## 1. Definition

### 1.1 What Parameterisation Is

Parameterisation is the use of SystemVerilog `parameter` and `localparam` declarations to make the RTL compile-time configurable. One source file produces different hardware for different parameter values.

**Location:**

- Mesh dimension: `MESH_N` in `mesh_pkg.sv`
- Data width: `DATA_WIDTH` in `cache_pkg.sv`
- Address width: `ADDR_WIDTH` in `cache_pkg.sv`
- Flit width: `FLIT_WIDTH` in `noc_pkg.sv`
- VC count: `VC_COUNT` in `noc_pkg.sv`
- Cache geometry: `L1_SETS`, `L1_WAYS`, `L2_SETS`, `L2_WAYS` in `cache_pkg.sv`

### 1.2 Scope of This Section

This section covers four decisions:

1. Mesh dimension parameter (`MESH_N`)
2. Data and address width parameters
3. Parameter propagation through the module hierarchy
4. Synthesis-time specialisation

### 1.3 Why It Matters

The white paper claims a design that scales from 4×4 in simulation to parameterised N×N. This claim is only true if the RTL is parameterised. A hard-coded 4×4 design cannot be reused at 8×8. The parameterisation determines:

1. What configurations the design supports.
2. How much RTL must change for a new configuration.
3. How verification can be reused across configurations.
4. How the synthesis tool optimises for the target.

---

## 2. Alternatives

### 2.1 Mesh Dimension Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Hard-coded 4×4 | `MESH_N = 4` written directly in RTL |
| 2 | Preprocessor macro | `define MESH_N 4` |
| 3 | Parameter with default | `parameter MESH_N = 4` in each module |
| 4 | Package parameter | `parameter MESH_N = 4` in `mesh_pkg.sv` |

### 2.2 Width Parameter Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Hard-coded widths | `[31:0]` everywhere |
| 2 | Preprocessor macro | `define ADDR_WIDTH 32` |
| 3 | Parameter with default | `parameter ADDR_WIDTH = 32` per module |
| 4 | Package parameter | `parameter ADDR_WIDTH = 32` in `cache_pkg.sv` |

### 2.3 Parameter Propagation Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Global macros | Every module reads `define` values |
| 2 | Explicit propagation | Every module passes parameters to its children |
| 3 | Package import | Every module imports from a shared package |
| 4 | Interface-based | Parameters passed through SV interfaces |

### 2.4 Synthesis Specialisation Alternatives

| # | Alternative | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Elaboration-time only | Parameters resolved at elaboration; no further specialisation |
| 2 | Generate blocks | `generate` for loops unrolled for the specific N |
| 3 | Conditional compilation | `ifdef` selects RTL variants |
| 4 | Multiple top modules | One top per configuration |

---

## 3. Comparison Matrices

### 3.1 Mesh Dimension Comparison

| Criterion | Hard-coded | Macro | **Package Parameter** | Per-module |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Single source of truth | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | No |
| Change requires edit | RTL | Header | **Package** | Many files |
| Synthesis specialisation | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Verification reuse | Low | Low | **High** | Low |
| Type safety | N/A | Low | **High** | High |
| Debug visibility | High | Low | **High** | High |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### 3.2 Width Parameter Comparison

| Criterion | Hard-coded | Macro | **Package Parameter** | Per-module |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Single source of truth | No | Yes | **Yes** | No |
| Change requires edit | Every module | Header | **Package** | Many files |
| Supports 32/64-bit | No | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Type safety | Low | Low | **High** | High |
| Verification reuse | Low | Low | **High** | Low |
| Arithmetic correctness | Manual | Manual | **Automatic** | Manual |

### 3.3 Parameter Propagation Comparison

| Criterion | Macros | **Explicit** | Package | Interfaces |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Single source of truth | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Module reuse | Low | **High** | High | Medium |
| Hierarchy visibility | Low | **High** | Medium | Medium |
| Compile-time checks | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Tool support | Universal | **Universal** | Good | Partial |
| Fits SystemVerilog LRM | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### 3.4 Synthesis Specialisation Comparison

| Criterion | Elaboration | **Generate** | `ifdef` | Multiple tops |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dead logic removed | Yes | **Yes** | No | Yes |
| Timing closure | Good | **Good** | Poor | Good |
| Code readability | High | **High** | Low | Medium |
| Verification scope | One config | **All configs** | Many configs | One per top |
| Synthesis time | Fast | **Medium** | Fast | Slow |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | No |

---

## 4. Chosen Option

### 4.1 Chosen Mesh Dimension

**Chosen: Package parameter.**

`MESH_N` is defined in `mesh_pkg.sv`. The default is 4. Every module that needs the mesh dimension imports this package.

Derived parameters:

| Parameter | Formula | Example at N=4 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MESH_N` | Direct | 4 |
| `TILE_COUNT` | `MESH_N * MESH_N` | 16 |
| `TILE_ID_WIDTH` | `$clog2(TILE_COUNT)` | 4 |
| `SHARER_WIDTH` | `TILE_COUNT` | 16 |
| `OWNER_WIDTH` | `$clog2(TILE_COUNT)` | 4 |

### 4.2 Chosen Width Parameters

**Chosen: Package parameters.**

| Parameter | Default | Package |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ADDR_WIDTH` | 32 | `cache_pkg.sv` |
| `DATA_WIDTH` | 8 | `cache_pkg.sv` |
| `LINE_BYTES` | 64 | `cache_pkg.sv` |
| `FLIT_WIDTH` | 64 | `noc_pkg.sv` |
| `VC_COUNT` | 4 | `noc_pkg.sv` |

### 4.3 Chosen Parameter Propagation

**Chosen: Explicit propagation at every module boundary.**

Every module declares parameters with defaults. The parent passes parameters to the child. The package provides the global defaults.

Example:

```
module router #(
    parameter int MESH_N     = mesh_pkg::MESH_N,
    parameter int FLIT_WIDTH = noc_pkg::FLIT_WIDTH,
    parameter int VC_COUNT   = noc_pkg::VC_COUNT
) ( ... );
```

### 4.4 Chosen Synthesis Specialisation

**Chosen: Generate blocks.**

`generate` blocks instantiate the correct number of tiles, VCs, and FIFO entries for the chosen configuration. The synthesis tool removes unused logic at elaboration time.

Example:

```
generate
    for (genvar x = 0; x < MESH_N; x++) begin : gen_tile_x
        for (genvar y = 0; y < MESH_N; y++) begin : gen_tile_y
            tile #(
                .MESH_N(MESH_N),
                .TILE_X(x),
                .TILE_Y(y)
            ) u_tile ( ... );
        end
    end
endgenerate
```

---

## 5. Why This and Not That

### 5.1 Why Package Parameters and Not Macros or Hard-Coding

**Why not hard-coded:**

- A hard-coded design cannot be reused at a different mesh size. The white paper's parameterisation claim would be false.
- Changing 4×4 to 8×8 would require editing every file.
- Verification at multiple configurations would require multiple RTL copies.

**Why not macros:**

- Macros have no type. `define MESH_N 4` has no width. It cannot be used in `$clog2`.
- Macros are global to the compilation unit. Name clashes are common.
- Macros do not show up in the hierarchy. Debugging is harder.
- Macros cannot be overridden per instance. Every instance of a module uses the same value.

**Why not per-module parameters only:**

- Each module would have its own default. There would be no single source of truth.
- Changing the mesh dimension would require editing every module's default.
- The design would drift out of sync.

**Why package parameters:**

- One source of truth. `mesh_pkg.sv` defines `MESH_N` once.
- Type safety. Parameters have widths and types.
- Support for derived parameters. `TILE_ID_WIDTH = $clog2(MESH_N * MESH_N)`.
- Compile-time checking. The simulator and synthesis tool verify parameter consistency.
- Visibility in the hierarchy. Every module shows its effective parameter values.

### 5.2 Why Explicit Propagation and Not Global Import Only

**Why not global import only:**

- If every module reads directly from the package, the module cannot be reused with different parameters.
- A module that hard-codes `mesh_pkg::MESH_N` cannot be instantiated with a different value.
- Testing a module in isolation requires the package to exist.

**Why explicit propagation:**

- Each module declares its parameters with package defaults. The parent overrides as needed.
- A module can be instantiated with any value the parent chooses.
- Unit testing is possible. A testbench can drive the module with any parameters.
- The hierarchy is visible. The effective parameters are shown at each level.

### 5.3 Why Generate Blocks and Not `ifdef`

**Why not `ifdef`:**

- `ifdef` selects RTL variants at compile time. The unused code is not elaborated.
- The synthesis tool cannot analyse the unused code. Timing closure is done on the selected variant only.
- Verification must cover each `ifdef` combination separately.
- `ifdef` is not part of the SystemVerilog LRM. It is a preprocessor directive.

**Why not multiple top modules:**

- One top per configuration multiplies the RTL. Every change must be applied to every top.
- Verification must cover each top separately.
- The repository would have 4×4, 8×8, and 16×16 tops. This is not maintainable.

**Why generate blocks:**

- `generate` is part of the SystemVerilog LRM. It is standard.
- The synthesis tool elaborates the generate block for the specific parameters. It removes dead logic.
- One top module handles every configuration. The top takes `MESH_N` as a parameter.
- Verification can run against multiple configurations using the same testbench.

### 5.4 Why Elaboration-Time and Not Runtime

**Why not runtime:**

- The mesh dimension must be fixed at synthesis time. The FPGA has a fixed number of tiles.
- A runtime-configurable mesh would require a much larger FPGA. The unused tiles would still occupy area.
- The coherence protocol assumes a fixed topology.

**Why elaboration-time:**

- The synthesis tool specialises the RTL for the chosen parameters.
- The resulting netlist contains only the logic for the target configuration.
- The area, timing, and power reports reflect the actual configuration.

---

## 6. Where It Affects the Design

### 6.1 Affected Components

| Component | Affected | How |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `mesh_pkg.sv` | Yes | Defines `MESH_N`, derived parameters |
| `cache_pkg.sv` | Yes | Defines `ADDR_WIDTH`, `DATA_WIDTH`, `LINE_BYTES` |
| `noc_pkg.sv` | Yes | Defines `FLIT_WIDTH`, `VC_COUNT` |
| `tile.sv` | Yes | Parameterised tile instantiation |
| `tile_array.sv` | Yes | Generate block for N×N tiles |
| `Router.sv` | Yes | Parameterised port count, FIFO depth |
| `NIC.sv` | Yes | Parameterised flit width, FIFO depth |
| `cache_controller.sv` | Yes | Parameterised sets, ways, tag width |
| `L2_cache_controller.sv` | Yes | Parameterised sets, ways, sharer width |
| `directory.sv` | Yes | Parameterised sharer vector width |
| `top.sv` | Yes | Top-level parameter override |
| Testbenches | Yes | Must be parameterised to match |
| Synthesis scripts | Yes | Must pass parameter values |

### 6.2 Unaffected Components

| Component | Reason |
| --- | --- |
| Coherence message set | Same messages for every N |
| MESI state encodings | Same states for every N |
| Boot FSM | Same sequence for every N |
| Reset logic | Same reset for every N |
| Clock distribution | Same clock for every N |

---

## 7. Consequences

### 7.1 Gain

- One RTL source produces any N×N mesh.
- One testbench runs against multiple configurations.
- Synthesis tool removes dead logic at elaboration.
- Derived parameters prevent arithmetic errors.
- Type safety catches parameter mismatches at compile time.
- The design supports 2×2, 4×4, and 8×8 without RTL changes.

### 7.2 Loss

- Parameterisation adds complexity to the RTL.
- Every module must declare its parameters explicitly.
- Debugging is harder when the effective parameters are not obvious.
- Verification must cover the parameter space, not just one configuration.

### 7.3 Constraint

- Every module must declare its parameters with package defaults.
- Every parent must pass parameters explicitly to children.
- Every testbench must override the top-level parameters.
- Every synthesis run must pass the target parameters.

### 7.4 Verification

| Property | Method |
| --- | --- |
| All configurations elaborate | Elaboration check in CI |
| All configurations pass directed tests | Regression at 2×2, 4×4 |
| 4×4 passes formal properties | SymbiYosys |
| Parameter arithmetic is correct | Unit tests on `mesh_pkg` |
| Derived parameters match | Assertions in `mesh_pkg` |
| No hard-coded magic numbers | Lint rule |

---

## 8. Future Extension

| Extension | Prerequisite | Estimated time |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 8×8 mesh | Parameter change | 1 week |
| 16×16 mesh | Parameter change + timing analysis | 2 weeks |
| Variable data width | RTL refactor for wider data | 2 weeks |
| Variable cache geometry | RTL refactor for non-square sets | 2 weeks |
| Hierarchical mesh | New topology parameter | 4 weeks |
| Runtime reconfiguration | New control interface | 8 weeks |

---

## 9. Contradiction With Original Design

### 9.1 No Parameterisation in the Original

The original design was hard-coded for 2 tiles and 8-bit addresses. It could not be reused at a different tile count or address width. The redesign parameterises every dimension.

### 9.2 Package Location in the Original

The original design defined packages inside `Tile_2tb.sv`. This forced a specific compile order. The redesign places packages in their own files (`mesh_pkg.sv`, `cache_pkg.sv`, `noc_pkg.sv`) with no compile-order dependency.

### 9.3 Hard-Coded Widths in the Original

The original design used hard-coded widths in many places. The redesign uses package parameters everywhere. Derived parameters prevent arithmetic errors.

---

## 10. Summary

| Decision | Choice | Main Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Mesh dimension | Package parameter (`mesh_pkg::MESH_N`) | Single source of truth; derived parameters |
| Width parameters | Package parameters (`cache_pkg`, `noc_pkg`) | Type safety; one place to change |
| Parameter propagation | Explicit at every module boundary | Reusability; unit testing; hierarchy visibility |
| Synthesis specialisation | Generate blocks | Standard; tool removes dead logic |

The design is parameterised at three levels: package parameters for global values, module parameters for overrides, and generate blocks for structural repetition. The synthesis tool specialises the RTL for the chosen configuration. The design supports 2×2, 4×4, and 8×8 with no RTL changes. Only the parameter values passed to the top module change.

---

The term **"overall verification methodology"** is broad and depends heavily on the context—semiconductor chip design, software engineering, systems engineering, scientific computing, AI/ML models, etc. Since you didn't specify a domain, I'll cover the most common interpretations and can drill down once you clarify.

---

## 1. In Semiconductor / Hardware Verification (most common usage)

This is the most likely context if you're asking about "verification methodology" as a formal term. The overall methodology typically combines several layers:

**A. Verification planning**

- Extract requirements from the specification
- Build a verification plan mapping each requirement to test cases
- Define coverage metrics (functional, code, assertion)

**B. Verification approaches (usually combined)**

| Approach | Description |
| --- | --- |
| **Dynamic simulation** | Testbench drives stimulus into DUT, checks outputs (UVM is the dominant standard) |
| **Formal verification** | Mathematical proof of properties; no stimulus needed |
| **Static checks** | Lint, CDC (clock domain crossing), RDC (reset domain crossing) |
| **Emulation/FPGA prototyping** | Hardware-accelerated, for software bring-up and long tests |
| **Post-silicon validation** | Real silicon testing after fabrication |

**C. Standard methodologies**

- **UVM** (Universal Verification Methodology) — SystemVerilog-based, industry standard
- **OVM** (predecessor to UVM)
- **VMM** (Synopsys, largely superseded)
- **PSS** (Portable Stimulus Standard) for reuse across platforms

**D. Coverage-driven verification (CDV)**

- Functional coverage + code coverage + assertion coverage
- Coverage closure is the exit criterion

**E. Metric-driven sign-off**

- Regression suites, bug rates, coverage targets, formal proof completion

## 3. In Systems Engineering (e.g., NASA, INCOSE)

- Verification: meets specifications (analysis, test, demonstration, inspection)
- Validation: meets user needs
- V-model lifecycle, requirements traceability matrix

## Summary

the overall verification methodology is the process of checking that every component works correctly on its own and that the complete system works correctly when all components are connected.

Think of it like testing a car: first test the engine, brakes and steering separately, then test the complete car on the road.

Suppose Core 0 writes the value `100` to a memory address, and Core 1 subsequently reads that address.

1. The testbench instructs Core 0 to write `100`.
2. The cache and directory controllers process the write.
3. The testbench asks Core 1 to read the same address.
4. The verification environment checks whether Core 1 observes the correct value under the specified memory-ordering rules.
5. If the result is incorrect, the simulator's waveform helps identify where the problem occurred.

The design should demonstrate that:

- Data is routed to the correct destination.
- Cache coherence is maintained between cores.
- Simultaneous requests are handled correctly.
- No requests or responses are lost.
- The system makes progress without deadlock under its stated assumptions.
- The design passes the required tests and achieves its coverage goals.

Directed tests are hand-crafted test cases that drive specific, known input sequences into a design and check for exact expected outputs. Unlike randomized tests, they are deterministic and target particular features, edge cases, or protocol behaviors.

### 🎯 Typical Directed Tests and Their Coverage

**Protocol & Interface Compliance**
Directed tests validate that the design correctly implements bus protocols and interface timing. For example, verifying basic AHB transactions like single transactions and WRAP4 bursts, or checking that a UART correctly transmits and receives specific bytes (e.g., `0x55` and `0xAA`). These tests ensure point-to-point connectivity and that signals toggle as expected.

**Reset and Initialization**
Tests verify that reset signals correctly initialize registers, state machines, and memory to their default values. This includes ensuring resets are asserted and de-asserted with respect to the correct clock domains. For processors, this often involves checking that the core branches to the reset vector and begins executing code correctly after reset.

**Register Access & Control**
Directed tests perform read/write operations to all registers, often using specific data patterns (like `1-0-1` walking patterns) to increase toggle coverage and verify that status bits can be set and cleared according to the specification. This covers register blocks, control logic, and configuration settings.

**Functional Block Verification**
For subsystems like caches or accelerators, tests are written to exercise specific functionality. For instance, in a processor verification environment, directed tests may enable L1/L2 caches and check data flow, or test the integration of timer, SPI, and I2C subcomponents by accessing their registers. In analog/mixed-signal designs, directed tests measure specific parameters like line/load regulation, PSRR, and quiescent current against design specs.

**Processor Instruction Execution**
Directed tests often use assembly or C test programs preloaded into memory. These programs execute specific instructions (add, xor, jump, etc.) and use a halt opcode or status flags to signal completion. The testbench then checks that the final processor state or memory contents match expectations.

**Clock, Power, and Tie-off Checks**
Simple directed checks verify clock frequencies, clock enables, and that unused signals are tied to appropriate constant values to prevent unwanted interrupts or behaviors.

### 🔄 Directed vs. Constrained-Random

It's worth noting that directed tests are often used alongside constrained-random verification. Directed tests provide clear, debuggable coverage of known scenarios and corner cases, while random tests can find unexpected bugs. Modern methodologies often integrate both approaches within a unified testbench to leverage the strengths of each.

If you can tell me the specific design you're verifying (e.g., a CPU, a bus protocol, an analog block), I can give you a more targeted list of directed tests.

## Summmary

private L1 caches, shared L2 banks, directory-based cache coherence, and a NoC, directed tests are specific tests designed to check one feature or one situation at a time.

Each directed test should have an expected result and explicit checks.

For example, in the cache-coherence test:

- Data check: Core 1 reads the expected value.
- Protocol check: Directory state and sharer information are legal.
- Handshake check: Requests and responses obey the interface protocol.
- Completion check: All expected transactions finish.
- Coverage check: The intended scenario was actually exercised.

Start with basic single-core memory operations, then test L1/L2 hits and misses, followed by directory and coherence scenarios. Next, test router routing, arbitration and backpressure. Finally, run multicore contention and forward-progress tests.

# What is the constrained-random verification environment?

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies the constrained-random verification environment for the tiled multicore design. It defines the use of pyuvm and cocotb, the per-tile agents, the cycle-accurate scoreboard, the constrained-random sequences, and the coverage-driven closure. The environment is the primary verification method for the coherence protocol and the NoC.

---

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **pyuvm** | A Python implementation of the Universal Verification Methodology. Provides agents, sequencers, drivers, monitors, and scoreboards. |
| **cocotb** | A Python cosimulation library. Drives and observes an RTL design in a simulator. |
| **Agent** | A UVM component that bundles a driver, monitor, and sequencer for one interface. |
| **Driver** | The component that converts a sequence item into pin-level stimulus. |
| **Monitor** | The component that observes pin-level activity and publishes transactions. |
| **Sequencer** | The component that supplies sequence items to the driver. |
| **Sequence** | A list of sequence items. Can be directed or constrained-random. |
| **Scoreboard** | A reference model that checks that observed behavior matches expected behavior. |
| **Coverage** | A measure of how much of the design's state space has been exercised. |
| **Constrained-random** | Stimulus that is random but respects constraints. |
| **Covergroup** | A collection of coverage points. |
| **Cross coverage** | Coverage of the combination of two or more values. |
| **SVA** | SystemVerilog Assertion. A property that must hold. |

---

# **3. Parameters**

---

## **3.1 pyuvm and cocotb Integration**

### **3.1.1 Definition**

pyuvm provides the UVM structure in Python. cocotb provides the interface between Python and the RTL simulator.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** `verif/` directory. `verif/env.py`, `verif/agents/`, `verif/scoreboard.py`, `verif/coverage.py`.

### **3.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | SystemVerilog UVM | Standard UVM in SystemVerilog. |
| 2 | pyuvm + cocotb | UVM in Python with cocotb. |
| 3 | cocotb only | Python testbench without UVM structure. |
| 4 | Directed tests only | No UVM, no constrained-random. |

### **3.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **SV UVM** | **pyuvm + cocotb** | **cocotb only** | **Directed only** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| License cost | High | **Zero** | Zero | Zero |
| Simulator support | Commercial only | **Verilator, Icarus** | Verilator, Icarus | Any |
| UVM structure | Full | **Full** | None | None |
| Reference model language | SystemVerilog | **Python** | Python | SystemVerilog |
| Coverage support | Full | **Full** | Partial | None |
| Learning curve | High | **Medium** | Low | Low |
| Fits 3-month timeline | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### **3.1.4**

**Chosen: pyuvm + cocotb.**

The environment uses pyuvm for the UVM structure and cocotb for simulation control. The reference model and testbench are written in Python. The environment runs on Verilator and Icarus Verilog, both open source.

## 3.1.5

**Why not SystemVerilog UVM:**

- Requires a commercial simulator (Questa, VCS, Xcelium)
- License cost
- Does not fit the 3-month timeline
- The team does not have access to a commercial simulator

**Why not cocotb only:**

- No UVM structure
- No sequencer, no agent, no standard coverage
- Harder to scale to 16 tiles

**Why not directed tests only:**

- Cannot reach the corner cases
- Cannot prove coverage closure
- The protocol has too many state combinations

**Why pyuvm + cocotb:**

- Open source
- Runs on Verilator and Icarus
- Full UVM structure
- Reference model in Python
- Shares code with the scoreboard
- Fits the timeline

### **3.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Driven and observed by agents |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Observed by scoreboard |
| Directory array | Yes | Modeled in scoreboard |
| Message set | Yes | Sequence items use message types |
| Router | Yes | Observed by monitor |
| NIC | Yes | Driven and observed |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Coverage tracks VC usage |
| Address map | Yes | Coverage tracks address classes |
| Verification | Yes | This is the environment |

### **3.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Open source
- No license cost
- Full UVM structure
- Python reference model
- Code sharing between testbench and scoreboard

**Loss:**

- Python simulator support is limited
- Some SystemVerilog features are not available in cocotb

**Constraint:**

- The RTL must be compatible with Verilator
- The RTL must use `ifdef SYNTHESIS` to remove SVA when running under Verilator

**Verification:**

- Environment runs on every commit
- Coverage reports are archived
- Scoreboard mismatches are treated as failures

### **3.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| SystemVerilog UVM | Commercial license | 2 months |
| Multi-simulator CI | More CI runners | 2 weeks |

### **3.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| UVM framework | pyuvm | Open source, Python |
| Simulation control | cocotb | Open source, multi-simulator |
| Simulators | Verilator, Icarus | Open source |

---

## **3.2 Per-Tile UVM Agents**

### **3.2.1 Definition**

A per-tile UVM agent drives stimulus into one tile and observes its behavior.

**Unit:** number of agents.

**Location:** `verif/agents/tile_agent.py`. One agent per tile. 16 agents in a 4×4 mesh.

### **3.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Single agent for the whole design | One driver, one monitor. |
| 2 | Per-tile agents | One agent per tile. |
| 3 | Per-interface agents | One agent per interface (core, NIC, router). |
| 4 | Hierarchical agents | Top-level agent with sub-agents. |

### **3.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Single** | **Per-tile** | **Per-interface** | **Hierarchical** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Scale to 16 tiles | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Reusability | Low | **High** | Medium | High |
| Configuration complexity | Low | **Medium** | High | High |
| Coverage per tile | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | No | No |

### **3.2.4**

**Chosen: Per-tile agents.**

Each tile has one UVM agent. The agent contains a driver, a monitor, and a sequencer. The driver issues load, store, and flush instructions to the tile's core interface. The monitor observes the cache controllers, the NIC, and the router, and publishes transactions to the scoreboard.

The 16 agents run in parallel. Each agent is configured with its tile ID.

### **3.2.5**

**Why not a single agent:**

- Cannot drive multiple tiles in parallel
- Cannot observe per-tile behavior
- Does not scale

**Why not per-interface agents:**

- Too many agents
- Complex configuration
- Does not fit the timeline

**Why not hierarchical agents:**

- More setup than needed
- Does not fit the timeline

**Why per-tile agents:**

- One agent per tile matches the tiled architecture
- Scales to any mesh size
- Simple configuration
- Reusable across tiles

### **3.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Driven by the tile agent |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Observed by the tile agent |
| Directory array | Yes | Observed by the tile agent |
| Message set | Yes | Sequence items use message types |
| Router | Yes | Observed by the tile agent |
| NIC | Yes | Observed by the tile agent |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Observed for coverage |
| Address map | Yes | Sequence items use addresses |
| Verification | Yes | This is the environment |

### **3.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Per-tile stimulus
- Per-tile observation
- Scales to any mesh size
- Reusable

**Loss:**

- 16 agents to configure

**Constraint:**

- Each agent must be configured with its tile ID
- Agents must coordinate to create cross-tile traffic

**Verification:**

- Each agent's coverage is tracked separately
- Cross-tile coverage is tracked at the environment level

### **3.2.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hierarchical agents | Agent hierarchy | 2 weeks |
| Randomized agent count | Configurable mesh size | 1 week |

### **3.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Agent structure | Per-tile | Matches tiled architecture, scales |

---

## **3.3 Cycle-Accurate Scoreboard**

### **3.3.1 Definition**

The scoreboard is a cycle-accurate Python model of the MESI directory protocol. It mirrors the expected state of every L1 cache, every L2 directory entry, and the memory. It compares the expected state to the observed state on every clock.

**Unit:** state variables tracked.

**Location:** `verif/scoreboard.py`.

### **3.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No scoreboard | Directed tests only. |
| 2 | Transaction-level scoreboard | Compares transactions, not cycle-by-cycle. |
| 3 | Cycle-accurate scoreboard | Compares state every cycle. |
| 4 | Formal-only scoreboard | Formal proves properties; no scoreboard. |

### **3.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **None** | **Transaction-level** | **Cycle-accurate** | **Formal-only** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Detects protocol bugs | No | Partial | **Yes** | Yes |
| Detects timing bugs | No | No | **Yes** | Partial |
| Debug visibility | Low | Medium | **High** | Low |
| Effort | None | Medium | **High** | High |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### **3.3.4**

**Chosen: Cycle-accurate scoreboard.**

The scoreboard models:

1. Every L1 cache line: set, way, tag, state.
2. Every L2 directory entry: set, way, state, owner, sharers, valid.
3. Every memory location: address, data.
4. Every transient state in the controllers.

On every clock, the scoreboard updates its model based on observed transactions and compares to the DUT state.

### **3.3.5**

**Why not no scoreboard:**

- Directed tests miss corner cases
- No automated checking
- Bugs would hide

**Why not transaction-level:**

- Cannot detect timing bugs
- Cannot check transient states
- The protocol has timing-dependent behavior

**Why not formal-only:**

- Formal is for invariants, not full state comparison
- Formal cannot cover the full address space

**Why cycle-accurate:**

- Detects protocol bugs
- Detects timing bugs
- High debug visibility
- Compares to the RTL state directly

### **3.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Modeled in the scoreboard |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Modeled in the scoreboard |
| Directory array | Yes | Modeled in the scoreboard |
| Message set | Yes | Messages drive scoreboard updates |
| Router | Yes | Observed to check message delivery |
| NIC | Yes | Observed to check packetization |
| Virtual channels | No | Not modeled |
| Address map | Yes | Modeled in the scoreboard |
| Verification | Yes | This is the scoreboard |

### **3.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Detects protocol bugs
- Detects timing bugs
- High debug visibility

**Loss:**

- High setup effort
- Python model must match RTL behavior

**Constraint:**

- The model must be updated when the RTL changes
- Any mismatch is a failure

**Verification:**

- Scoreboard runs on every transaction
- Mismatches stop the test
- Waveform is dumped on mismatch

### **3.3.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multi-threaded scoreboard | Faster model | 2 weeks |
| Formal equivalence with scoreboard | Formal model | 1 month |

### **3.3.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Scoreboard type | Cycle-accurate | Detects protocol and timing bugs |
| Model language | Python | Shares code with testbench |

---

## **3.4 Constrained-Random Sequences**

### **3.4.1 Definition**

Constrained-random sequences generate stimulus that is random but respects constraints. Constraints define legal transactions, addresses, and message classes.

**Unit:** number of sequences.

**Location:** `verif/sequences/`.

### **3.4.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Directed tests only | Hand-written stimulus. |
| 2 | Pure random | No constraints. |
| 3 | Constrained-random | Random with constraints. |
| 4 | Coverage-driven random | Random guided by coverage holes. |

### **3.4.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **Directed** | **Pure random** | **Constrained-random** | **Coverage-driven** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Reaches corner cases | Partial | No | **Yes** | Yes |
| Illegal stimulus | No | Yes | **No** | No |
| Coverage closure | Manual | No | **Automatic** | Automatic |
| Effort | High | Low | **Medium** | High |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | No |

### **3.4.4**

**Chosen: Constrained-random.**

The environment has these sequences:

| **Sequence** | **Constraints** |
| --- | --- |
| `load_seq` | Address in valid range, tile ID in range |
| `store_seq` | Address in valid range, data in range |
| `flush_seq` | Address in valid range |
| `mixed_seq` | Random choice of load, store, flush |
| `cross_tile_seq` | Two tiles access the same address |
| `hot_line_seq` | All tiles access the same address |
| `cold_line_seq` | All tiles access distinct addresses |

Each sequence respects the address map and the message classes.

### **3.4.5**

**Why not directed only:**

- Cannot reach all corner cases
- Coverage closure would be manual
- Does not scale

**Why not pure random:**

- Generates illegal stimulus
- Illegal stimulus is meaningless
- Would waste simulation time

**Why not coverage-driven:**

- Requires an iterative loop
- Too complex for the timeline

**Why constrained-random:**

- Random within legal bounds
- Automatic coverage closure
- Finds corner cases
- Fits the timeline

### **3.4.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Driven with random loads, stores, flushes |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Exercised by the sequences |
| Directory array | Yes | Exercised by the sequences |
| Message set | Yes | All message types exercised |
| Router | Yes | Cross-tile traffic exercises the router |
| NIC | Yes | Cross-tile traffic exercises the NIC |
| Virtual channels | Yes | All VCs exercised |
| Address map | Yes | All address classes exercised |
| Verification | Yes | These are the sequences |

### **3.4.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Automatic coverage closure
- Finds corner cases
- Legal stimulus only

**Loss:**

- Constraints must be written
- Constraints must be maintained

**Constraint:**

- Constraints must match the address map
- Constraints must match the message classes

**Verification:**

- Coverage reports show holes
- Sequences are tuned to close holes
- Regression runs all sequences

### **3.4.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Coverage-driven random | Iterative loop | 2 months |
| Directed test seeds | Test analysis | 2 weeks |

### **3.4.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Stimulus type | Constrained-random | Finds corner cases, legal only |
| Sequences | 7 sequences | Cover load, store, flush, cross-tile |

---

## **3.5 Coverage-Driven Closure**

### **3.5.1 Definition**

Coverage-driven closure is the process of defining coverage points, running the environment, checking coverage, adding stimulus to fill holes, and repeating until the coverage target is met.

**Unit:** coverage percentage.

**Location:** `verif/coverage.py`.

### **3.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No coverage | No measurement. |
| 2 | Code coverage only | Measures RTL lines, toggles, FSM states. |
| 3 | Functional coverage only | Measures design intent. |
| 4 | Both code and functional | Full coverage measurement. |

### **3.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **None** | **Code only** | **Functional only** | **Both** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Detects untested logic | No | Yes | Partial | **Yes** |
| Detects untested behavior | No | No | Yes | **Yes** |
| Guides stimulus | No | Partial | Yes | **Yes** |
| Effort | None | Low | Medium | **Medium** |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | Yes | **Yes** |

### **3.5.4**

**Chosen: Both code and functional coverage.**

**Code coverage (Verilator):**

- Line coverage
- Toggle coverage
- FSM state coverage
- Branch coverage

**Functional coverage (pyuvm covergroups):**

| **Covergroup** | **Coverage Points** |
| --- | --- |
| State × message | Every state × every message type |
| Transient states | Every transient state visited |
| VC usage | Every VC used |
| Address map | Every address class |
| Tile pairs | Every source-destination tile pair |
| Packet sizes | Single-flit and multi-flit packets |
| Cross-tile | Cross-tile transactions |

Target: 100% functional coverage on all covergroups, 100% code coverage on the RTL.

### **3.5.5**

**Why not no coverage:**

- No way to measure completeness
- Holes would go undetected

**Why not code only:**

- Code coverage does not measure protocol behavior
- The protocol could be wrong even with high code coverage

**Why not functional only:**

- Does not measure RTL implementation
- Dead code would go undetected

**Why both:**

- Code coverage measures RTL exercise
- Functional coverage measures protocol exercise
- Together they measure completeness

### **3.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Code and functional coverage |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Code and functional coverage |
| Directory array | Yes | State coverage |
| Message set | Yes | Message type coverage |
| Router | Yes | VC and packet size coverage |
| NIC | Yes | VC and packet size coverage |
| Virtual channels | Yes | Per-VC usage coverage |
| Address map | Yes | Address class coverage |
| Verification | Yes | This is the coverage model |

### **3.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Measurable completeness
- Guides stimulus development
- Detects untested logic
- Detects untested behavior

**Loss:**

- Coverage model must be written
- Closure iterations take time

**Constraint:**

- Coverage target must be 100% for functional
- Code coverage target must be 100% for RTL
- Holes must be closed or justified

**Verification:**

- Coverage reports on every commit
- Holes block the release
- Justified holes are documented

### **3.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Coverage-driven stimulus | Iterative loop | 2 months |
| Coverage merging across tests | Coverage tool | 2 weeks |

### **3.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Coverage type | Code + functional | Measures RTL and protocol |
| Target | 100% functional, 100% code | Completeness |
| Closure method | Iterative hole filling | Fits the timeline |

---

# **4. Summary**

The verification environment uses pyuvm for UVM structure and cocotb for simulation control. Each tile has its own agent with a driver, monitor, and sequencer. The driver issues load, store, and flush instructions. The monitor observes the cache controllers, NIC, and router, and publishes transactions to the scoreboard. The scoreboard is a cycle-accurate Python model of the MESI directory protocol. It mirrors every L1 cache line, every L2 directory entry, and memory, and compares to the DUT on every clock. Seven constrained-random sequences generate load, store, flush, mixed, cross-tile, hot-line, and cold-line traffic. Coverage closure uses code coverage from Verilator and functional coverage from pyuvm covergroups. Target: 100% functional and 100% code coverage. The environment runs on Verilator and Icarus, avoiding commercial simulator cost. SystemVerilog UVM was rejected for license cost, cocotb-only for lack of UVM structure, and directed-only for incomplete coverage. The design has 16 tiles, so a single-tile testbench would miss cross-tile paths. The protocol is complex enough to need a reference model. Coverage-driven closure needs constrained-random stimulus.

---

# What properties do we formally prove, and with what tool?

# **1. Purpose**

This finding specifies the formal verification properties and the tool used to prove them. Six properties are proven on a 2-tile configuration using SymbiYosys. The properties cover single-writer safety, multiple-reader safety, data consistency, directory consistency, no message loss, and deadlock freedom.

---

# **2. Definitions**

| **Term** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| **Formal verification** | Proving that a design satisfies a property for all possible inputs and states. |
| **SVA** | SystemVerilog Assertion. A property that must hold. |
| **BMC** | Bounded Model Checking. Proving a property up to a bounded number of cycles. |
| **k-induction** | A proof technique that generalizes BMC to unbounded time. |
| **Safety property** | A property that must never be violated. |
| **Liveness property** | A property that must eventually be satisfied. |
| **Invariant** | A property that must hold in every reachable state. |
| **SymbiYosys** | An open-source formal verification front end for Yosys. |
| **SMT solver** | A tool that solves satisfiability modulo theories. Used as the back end for SymbiYosys. |
| **2-tile configuration** | A reduced version of the design with two tiles, used for formal analysis. |
| **Depth** | The number of cycles the proof covers. |

---

# **3. Parameters**

---

## **3.1 Formal Tool: SymbiYosys**

### **3.1.1 Definition**

SymbiYosys is the formal verification front end. It drives Yosys as the elaborator and an SMT solver as the back end.

**Unit:** tool selection.

**Location:** `formal/` directory. `formal/*.sby` scripts.

### **3.1.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | **SymbiYosys** | Open-source front end for Yosys. |
| 2 | JasperGold | Commercial formal tool from Cadence. |
| 3 | VC Formal | Commercial formal tool from Synopsys. |
| 4 | Questa Formal | Commercial formal tool from Siemens. |

### **3.1.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **SymbiYosys** | **JasperGold** | **VC Formal** | **Questa Formal** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| License cost | **Zero** | High | High | High |
| Open source | **Yes** | No | No | No |
| SVA support | **Yes** | Yes | Yes | Yes |
| SMT solver choice | **Yices, Boolector, Z3** | Built-in | Built-in | Built-in |
| CI integration | **Easy** | Hard | Hard | Hard |
| Learning curve | **Medium** | High | High | High |
| Fits 3-month timeline | **Yes** | No | No | No |

### **3.1.4**

**Chosen: SymbiYosys.**

SymbiYosys drives Yosys to elaborate the RTL, then calls an SMT solver to prove or refute the properties. The SVA properties are written in the RTL under `ifdef FORMAL`. The proofs run in CI on every commit.

Supported solvers: Yices, Boolector, Z3.

### **3.1.5**

**Why not JasperGold or VC Formal:**

- License cost
- Not available to the team
- Does not fit the timeline

**Why not Questa Formal:**

- Same reasons

**Why SymbiYosys:**

- Open source
- No license cost
- SVA support
- Runs in CI
- Fits the timeline

### **3.1.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Contains SVA properties |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Contains SVA properties |
| Directory array | Yes | Modeled in the proofs |
| Message set | Yes | Message behavior proven |
| Router | Yes | Deadlock freedom proven |
| NIC | Yes | Message loss proven |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC separation proven |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | This is the formal tool |

### **3.1.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Open source
- Zero cost
- Runs in CI
- SVA support

**Loss:**

- Does not support all SystemVerilog features
- Some RTL restructuring may be needed

**Constraint:**

- RTL must be compatible with Yosys
- Proof scripts must be maintained

**Verification:**

- Every property has a `.sby` script
- Proofs run on every commit
- Failures block the release

### **3.1.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multi-solver proof | Yices, Boolector, Z3 | 2 weeks |
| Proof coverage measurement | Coverage tool | 1 month |

### **3.1.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Formal tool | SymbiYosys | Open source, runs in CI |
| Back-end solvers | Yices, Boolector, Z3 | Multiple options |

---

## **3.2 Single-Writer Invariant**

### **3.2.1 Definition**

The single-writer invariant says that at most one cache is in the Modified or Exclusive state for any block.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** `formal/single_writer.sby`, property defined in `l1_cache_controller.sv` under `ifdef FORMAL`.

### **3.2.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No proof | Assume single writer. |
| 2 | **Prove single writer** | SVA property proved by SymbiYosys. |
| 3 | Test single writer | Directed test. |
| 4 | Coverage-only | Check that the state is reached. |

### **3.2.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No proof** | **Prove** | **Test** | **Coverage** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Covers all cases | No | **Yes** | No | No |
| Covers all addresses | No | **Yes** | Partial | No |
| Runs in CI | N/A | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Effort | None | **High** | Low | Low |
| Confidence | Low | **High** | Medium | Low |

### **3.2.4 Chosen Option**

**Chosen: Prove single writer.**

SVA property:

text

```
property single_writer;
    int unsigned m_count;
    int unsigned e_count;
    m_count = 0;
    e_count = 0;
    foreach (tiles[i]) begin
        if (tiles[i].l1.state == M) m_count++;
        if (tiles[i].l1.state == E) e_count++;
    end
    (m_count + e_count) <= 1;
endproperty
```

The property is proved for every reachable state.

### **3.2.5**

**Why not no proof:**

- Single writer is the core safety property
- Violation would break coherence

**Why not test:**

- A test covers only the specific stimulus
- Single writer must hold for all stimulus

**Why not coverage-only:**

- Coverage checks that the state is reached
- It does not check that the invariant holds

**Why prove single writer:**

- Covers all cases
- Covers all addresses
- Runs in CI
- High confidence

### **3.2.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Contains the state variable |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Enforces the invariant |
| Directory array | Yes | Tracks the state |
| Message set | Yes | Messages drive state transitions |
| Router | No | Below the protocol |
| NIC | No | Below the protocol |
| Virtual channels | No | Below the protocol |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | This is the proof |

### **3.2.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Core safety property proven
- Covers all cases
- Publishable

**Loss:**

- Proof time grows with design size

**Constraint:**

- Proof must complete in the time budget
- The 2-tile config keeps the proof tractable

**Verification:**

- Proof runs in CI
- Failure blocks the release

### **3.2.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 4-tile proof | Faster solver | 2 months |
| Proof with DMA | DMA design | 2 months |

### **3.2.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Single writer | Prove | Core safety property |

---

## **3.3 Multiple-Reader Invariant**

### **3.3.1 Definition**

The multiple-reader invariant says that any number of caches may be in the Shared state for a block, but no cache may be in Modified or Exclusive at the same time.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** `formal/multiple_reader.sby`.

### **3.3.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No proof | Assume multiple readers allowed. |
| 2 | **Prove multiple reader** | SVA property proved by SymbiYosys. |
| 3 | Test multiple reader | Directed test with two readers. |
| 4 | Coverage-only | Check that the state is reached. |

### **3.3.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No proof** | **Prove** | **Test** | **Coverage** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Covers all cases | No | **Yes** | No | No |
| Covers all addresses | No | **Yes** | Partial | No |
| Runs in CI | N/A | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Effort | None | **High** | Low | Low |
| Confidence | Low | **High** | Medium | Low |

### **3.3.4 Chosen Option**

**Chosen: Prove multiple reader.**

SVA property:

text

```
property multiple_reader;
    (state[i] == S) -> (state[j] != M) && (state[j] != E)
    for all tiles i, j
endproperty
```

### **3.3.5 Why this**

Same reasoning as single writer.

### **3.3.6 Where It Affects the Design**

Same as single writer.

### **3.3.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Multiple-reader property proven
- Covers all cases

**Loss:**

- Proof time

**Constraint:**

- Proof must complete

**Verification:**

- Proof runs in CI

### **3.3.8 Future Extension**

## **3.4 Data Consistency Invariant**

### **3.4.1 Definition**

The data consistency invariant says that if a cache is in Modified, memory is stale; if in Shared or Exclusive, memory is current.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** `formal/data_consistency.sby`.

### **3.4.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No proof | Assume data consistency. |
| 2 | **Prove data consistency** | SVA property proved by SymbiYosys. |
| 3 | Test with data values | Directed test with data checks. |
| 4 | Coverage-only | Check that the state is reached. |

### **3.4.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No proof** | **Prove** | **Test** | **Coverage** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Covers all cases | No | **Yes** | No | No |
| Covers all addresses | No | **Yes** | Partial | No |
| Covers data values | No | **Yes** | Yes | No |
| Runs in CI | N/A | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Effort | None | **High** | Low | Low |
| Confidence | Low | **High** | Medium | Low |

### **3.4.4**

**Chosen: Prove data consistency.**

SVA property:

```
property data_consistency;
    (l1.state == M) -> (l1.data != mem[addr])
    (l1.state inside {S, E}) -> (l1.data == mem[addr])
endproperty
```

### **3.4.5**

**Why not no proof:**

- Data consistency is the core correctness property
- A violation would return wrong data

**Why not test:**

- A test covers only the specific data values
- The proof covers all data values

**Why not coverage-only:**

- Coverage checks that the state is reached
- It does not check the data

**Why prove data consistency:**

- Covers all cases
- Covers all data values
- Runs in CI

### **3.4.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Contains the data and state |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Enforces the invariant |
| Directory array | Yes | Tracks the state |
| Memory model | Yes | Holds the reference data |
| Message set | Yes | Messages drive state changes |
| Router | No | Below the protocol |
| NIC | No | Below the protocol |
| Virtual channels | No | Below the protocol |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | This is the proof |

### **3.4.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Data consistency proven
- Covers all data values

**Loss:**

- Proof time
- Memory model must be formal

**Constraint:**

- The proof must model memory
- The 2-tile config keeps the proof tractable

**Verification:**

- Proof runs in CI

### **3.4.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Multi-word data | Larger model | 1 month |
| ECC data | ECC model | 1 month |

### **3.4.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Data consistency | Prove | Core correctness property |

## **3.5 Directory Consistency Invariant**

### **3.5.1 Definition**

The directory consistency invariant says that the directory state matches the aggregate state of the L1 caches.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** `formal/dir_consistency.sby`.

### **3.5.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No proof | Assume directory consistency. |
| 2 | **Prove directory consistency** | SVA property proved by SymbiYosys. |
| 3 | Test directory state | Directed test with directory dumps. |
| 4 | Coverage-only | Check that the state is reached. |

### **3.5.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No proof** | **Prove** | **Test** | **Coverage** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Covers all cases | No | **Yes** | No | No |
| Covers all addresses | No | **Yes** | Partial | No |
| Covers all sharers | No | **Yes** | Partial | No |
| Runs in CI | N/A | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Effort | None | **High** | Low | Low |
| Confidence | Low | **High** | Medium | Low |

### **3.5.4**

**Chosen: Prove directory consistency.**

SVA property:

```
property dir_consistency;
    // Directory says DIR_I: no L1 holds a copy
    (dir.state == DIR_I) -> (no_l1_holds(addr))

    // Directory says DIR_S: sharers match
    (dir.state == DIR_S) ->
        (l1_holds(addr, i) == dir.sharers[i]) for all i

    // Directory says DIR_E: one owner, no sharers
    (dir.state == DIR_E) ->
        (one_l1_holds(addr)) && (l1.state == E)

    // Directory says DIR_M: one owner, no sharers
    (dir.state == DIR_M) ->
        (one_l1_holds(addr)) && (l1.state == M)
endproperty
```

### **3.5.5**

**Why not no proof:**

- Directory consistency is essential for correctness
- A violation would cause stale data

**Why not test:**

- A test covers only the specific stimulus
- The proof covers all cases

**Why not coverage-only:**

- Coverage checks the state
- It does not check the invariant

**Why prove directory consistency:**

- Covers all cases
- Covers all sharers
- Runs in CI

### **3.5.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Holds the L1 state |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Holds the directory state |
| Directory array | Yes | Stores the state |
| Message set | Yes | Messages drive state changes |
| Router | No | Below the protocol |
| NIC | No | Below the protocol |
| Virtual channels | No | Below the protocol |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | This is the proof |

### **3.5.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- Directory consistency proven
- Covers all cases

**Loss:**

- Proof time
- Directory model must be formal

**Constraint:**

- The proof must model the directory
- The 2-tile config keeps the proof tractable

**Verification:**

- Proof runs in CI

### **3.5.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Compressed sharers | New model | 1 month |
| Sparse directory | New model | 2 months |

### **3.5.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Directory consistency | Prove | Essential for correctness |

## **3.6 No Message Loss Property**

### **3.6.1 Definition**

The no message loss property says that every request receives a response within a bounded number of cycles.

**Unit:** boolean.

**Location:** `formal/no_message_loss.sby`.

### **3.6.2 Alternatives**

| **#** | **Alternative** | **Description** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No proof | Assume no loss. |
| 2 | **Prove no loss** | SVA property proved by SymbiYosys. |
| 3 | Test no loss | Directed test with timeout. |
| 4 | Coverage-only | Check that responses arrive. |

### **3.6.3 Comparison Matrix**

| **Criterion** | **No proof** | **Prove** | **Test** | **Coverage** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Covers all cases | No | **Yes** | No | No |
| Covers all messages | No | **Yes** | Partial | No |
| Runs in CI | N/A | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Effort | None | **High** | Low | Low |
| Confidence | Low | **High** | Medium | Low |

### **3.6.4**

**Chosen: Prove no loss.**

SVA property:

text

```
property no_message_loss;
    // Every request gets a response within K cycles
    (request_valid) |-> ##[1:K] (response_valid);
endproperty
```

K is set to the BMC depth (32).

### **3.6.5**

**Why not no proof:**

- Message loss would break the protocol
- The original design had no timeout and no proof

**Why not test:**

- A test covers only the specific stimulus
- The proof covers all cases

**Why not coverage-only:**

- Coverage checks that responses arrive in the tested case
- It does not check that they always arrive

**Why prove no loss:**

- Covers all cases
- Runs in CI

### **3.6.6 Where It Affects the Design**

| **Component** | **Affected?** | **How** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Issues requests |
| L2 bank controller | Yes | Issues responses |
| Directory array | Yes | Tracks outstanding requests |
| Message set | Yes | Every message type |
| Router | Yes | Delivers messages |
| NIC | Yes | Delivers messages |
| Virtual channels | Yes | VC separation |
| Address map | No | Independent |
| Verification | Yes | This is the proof |

### **3.6.7 Consequences**

**Gain:**

- No message loss proven
- Covers all cases

**Loss:**

- Proof time
- The proof must model the NoC

**Constraint:**

- The proof must model the network
- The BMC depth must be large enough

**Verification:**

- Proof runs in CI

### **3.6.8 Future Extension**

| **Extension** | **Prerequisite** | **Estimated time** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Longer BMC depth | Faster solver | 2 months |
| Full k-induction | k-induction setup | 1 month |

### **3.6.9 Summary**

| **Decision** | **Choice** | **Why** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No message loss | Prove | Core liveness property |
