# summary


@Joshna Priya 

## What is a Cache Bank

A cache stores copies of memory. A processor reads from cache instead of memory because cache is faster.

In a multicore chip, the last-level cache is often **split into banks**. Each bank holds a slice of the address space. Banks are placed near the tiles that use them.

In this project:

- Every tile holds **one L2 bank**.
- The bank holds data for the addresses that map to it.
- The bank also holds **directory information** for those addresses.

The bank is called the **home** for those addresses. Any tile that wants a block must ask the home bank.

---

## **Why a Shared L2**

Three options exist for the last-level cache:

| **Option** | **Description** | **Problem** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Private L2 per tile | Every tile has its own L2 | Data replicated. Coherence gets harder. |
| Central L2 | One big L2 for the whole chip | Single bottleneck. Wire delay grows. |
| Distributed shared L2 | Split into banks, one per tile | This is what we use. |

The distributed shared L2 gives the capacity of a shared cache but the locality of a private cache. Each bank is close to its tile. No central bottleneck.

---

## **Bank Configuration**

We chose these numbers:

| **Parameter** | **Value** |
| --- | --- |
| Sets per bank | 8 |
| Ways per set | 4 |
| Bytes per line | 64 |
| Lines per bank | 32 |
| Capacity per bank | 2 KB |
| Total L2 (16 banks) | 32 KB |

The next chapters explain each choice.

---

## **Why 64-Byte Lines**

### **The Line Is the Unit of Transfer**

A cache does not transfer one byte at a time. It transfers a **line**. A line is a block of adjacent bytes.

Common line sizes: 16, 32, 64, 128 bytes.

### **Why 64 Is the Best Choice**

**Reason 1 — Spatial locality.**

A program that reads `array[i]` usually reads `array[i+1]` next. Loading 64 bytes at once means the next 63 reads are already in cache. Smaller lines miss more often.

**Reason 2 — False sharing.**

Two cores write to different variables that happen to sit in the same line. If the line is small, this causes unnecessary invalidations. 64-byte lines reduce this problem compared to 16-byte lines.

**Reason 3 — Flit packing.**

Our flit is 64 bits = 8 bytes. A 64-byte line = 8 body flits + 1 header flit = 9 flits. A clean match. A 32-byte line would waste half a flit per transfer.

**Reason 4 — Industry standard.**

Intel, AMD, and ARM all use 64-byte lines. Using the same size lets us compare against published work.

### **What Happens Without It**

| **If line = 32 bytes** | **If line = 128 bytes** |
| --- | --- |
| Flit packing wastes space | More data transferred than needed |
| Not comparable to published work | Doubles miss penalty for random access |
| More tag storage per byte | Wastes bandwidth |

---

## **Why 4 Ways**

### **What "Ways" Means**

A set has several slots. Each slot is called a **way**. If a set has 4 ways, it can hold 4 different lines that map to the same set index.

### **Why Not 1 Way (Direct-Mapped)**

A 1-way cache is direct-mapped. Each address maps to exactly one slot. If two hot addresses map to the same slot, they keep evicting each other. This is called a **conflict miss**.

1-way caches have many conflict misses. They are fast but wasteful.

### **Why Not 8 or 16 Ways**

More ways reduce conflict misses. But:

- The tag comparator grows. 8-way needs 8 tag comparators. 16-way needs 16.
- The LRU logic grows. 4-way needs 6 bits. 8-way needs 28 bits. 16-way needs 120 bits.
- The access latency grows. All ways are read in parallel. A wider mux is slower.

### **Why 4 Is the Sweet Spot**

| **Ways** | **Conflict Misses** | **LRU Bits** | **Tag Comparators** | **Verdict** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | High | 0 | 1 | Too many misses |
| 2 | Medium | 1 | 2 | Acceptable |
| **4** | **Low** | **6** | **4** | **Best** |
| 8 | Very low | 28 | 8 | Too costly |
| 16 | Rare | 120 | 16 | Too large |

4 ways is the industry standard for L1 and L2 caches.

---

## **Why 8 Sets**

### **What a Set Is**

The set index selects one of the sets. All addresses with the same set index map to the same set. Within the set, they compete for the 4 ways.

### **Why 8 Is Enough**

**Reason 1 — Directory size.**

8 sets × 4 ways = 32 directory entries per bank. 16 banks = 512 entries. This is enough to demonstrate real coherence traffic.

**Reason 2 — Simulation speed.**

Evictions happen in hundreds of cycles. Tests run fast. If we used 256 sets, tests would need millions of cycles to trigger eviction.

**Reason 3 — Address coverage.**

3 bits of set index covers 8 × 64 = 512 bytes before tag comparison starts. Enough to demonstrate set-indexed behavior.

**Reason 4 — Hardware fit.**

8 sets × 4 ways × 64 bytes = 2 KB per bank. 16 banks = 32 KB. This fits the ZedBoard's BRAM budget.

### **What Happens Without It**

| **If sets = 2** | **If sets = 64** |
| --- | --- |
| Too few directory entries | Too many entries. Simulation slow. |
| Thrashing. No realistic behavior. | Doesn't fit in LUTRAM cleanly. |

---

## **Why a Separate Directory**

 **What the Directory Is**

The directory stores **bookkeeping** for each line. It does not hold the data itself. It holds:

- The coherence state of the line.
- The tile that owns the line (if any).
- The tiles that share the line (if any).
- A valid bit.

The directory answers: *"Who has a copy of this line, and in what state?"*

### **Why Not Mix Directory With Data**

The data array and the directory have different properties. Mixing them causes problems:

| **Property** | **Data Array** | **Directory** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Width per entry | 512 bits (64 bytes) | 28 bits |
| Update rate | On fill or write-back | Every coherence event |
| Access pattern | Every read | Every coherence decision |
| Size | Large | Small |

# **Purpose**

This finding specifies the **complete coherence message set** and the **transient states** used by the L1 caches and the L2 directory. It defines what messages exist, when each message is sent, what transient state each agent enters while a message is in flight, and why the set is both complete and minimal.

This finding is a **specification**. It does not describe the RTL implementation. It describes the protocol contract that the RTL must satisfy.

# **Complete Coherence Message Set**

The protocol uses 14 messages, grouped into three classes.

## **Request Messages (L1 → Directory)**

These messages originate at a private L1 cache and are addressed to the home bank of the block.

| **Message** | **Full Name** | **Purpose** | **Size** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| GETS | Get Shared | Request a read-only copy of a block | 1 flit |
| GETM | Get Modified | Request an exclusive copy of a block | 1 flit |
| PUTS | Put Shared | Write back a clean copy on eviction | 1 flit |
| PUTM | Put Modified | Write back a dirty copy on eviction | 9 flits |
| UNBLOCK | Unblock | Acknowledge data receipt and release the directory | 1 flit |

## **Response Messages (Directory ↔ L1)**

These messages originate at the home bank or at the current owner.

| **Message** | **Full Name** | **Purpose** | **Size** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| INV | Invalidate | Ask a sharer to invalidate its copy | 1 flit |
| INVACK | Invalidate Acknowledge | Confirm that invalidation is complete | 1 flit |
| DATA | Data | Carry read data to the requester, with optional exclusive indication | 9 flits |
| FWD_GETS | Forward Get Shared | Ask the current owner to send a shared copy to the requester | 1 flit |
| FWD_GETM | Forward Get Modified | Ask the current owner to send a writable copy to the requester | 1 flit |
| DATAFWD | Data Forwarded | Carry forwarded data from owner to requester | 9 flits |

## **DMA Messages (Under Evaluation)**

These messages support DMA transfers. The DMA design is under evaluation.

| **Message** | **Full Name** | **Purpose** | **Size** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DMA_READ | DMA Read | Ask the directory for a block on behalf of a DMA engine | 1 flit |
| DMA_WRITE | DMA Write | Send a block from the DMA engine to the directory | 9 flits |
| DMA_ACK | DMA Acknowledge | Confirm that the DMA transfer is complete | 1 flit |

## **Message Set Summary**

| **Class** | **Messages** | **Count** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Requests | GETS, GETM, PUTS, PUTM, UNBLOCK | 5 |
| Responses | INV, INVACK, DATA, FWD_GETS, FWD_GETM, DATAFWD | 6 |
| DMA | DMA_READ, DMA_WRITE, DMA_ACK | 3 |
| **Total** |  | **14** |

---

# **L1 Transient States**

An L1 cache line is in one of four **stable states** at rest: I, S, E, M. When a transaction is in flight, the line is in a **transient state**. The transient state records what the line is waiting for.

## **L1 Transient State Table**

| **State** | **Meaning** | **Entry Condition** | **Exit Condition** | **Next State** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| IS_D | Invalid → Shared, waiting data | Load miss on a line in I | DATA (shared) arrives | S |
| IM_D | Invalid → Modified, waiting data | Store miss on a line in I | DATA (exclusive) arrives | M |
| SM_D | Shared → Modified, waiting data + acks | Store hit on a line in S | DATA (exclusive) + all INVACKs | M |
| EI_A | Exclusive → Invalid, waiting ack | Eviction of a line in E | PUTS acknowledge arrives | I |
| MI_A | Modified → Invalid, waiting ack | Eviction of a line in M | PUTM acknowledge arrives | I |

## **Why These States Are Needed**

A cache cannot change state instantly. The data or the acknowledgement must travel over the NoC. During that time, the line must be marked so that:

1. **No other request to the same line is issued.** The line is busy.
2. **The eventual response can be matched.** The transient state tells the controller what to do when the response arrives.
3. **The core sees a consistent view.** A read or write to a line in a transient state stalls until the transaction completes.

## **L1 State Transition Rules**

| **From** | **Event** | **To** | **Actions** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| I | Load miss | IS_D | Send GETS |
| IS_D | DATA (shared) | S | Install line, send UNBLOCK |
| IS_D | DATA (exclusive) | E | Install line, send UNBLOCK |
| I | Store miss | IM_D | Send GETM |
| IM_D | DATA (exclusive) | M | Install line, write data, send UNBLOCK |
| S | Store hit | SM_D | Send GETM |
| SM_D | DATA (exclusive) + all INVACKs | M | Write data |
| E | Store hit | M | Silent upgrade, no message |
| E | Eviction | EI_A | Send PUTS |
| EI_A | PUTS acknowledge | I | Release way |
| M | Eviction | MI_A | Send PUTM |
| MI_A | PUTM acknowledge | I | Release way |
| S | Eviction | I | Silent drop |

# **Directory Transient States**

The directory entry is in one of four **stable states** at rest: DIR_I, DIR_S, DIR_E, DIR_M. When a coherence transaction is in flight, the entry is in a **transient state**.

## **Directory Transient State Table**

| **State** | **Meaning** | **Entry Condition** | **Exit Condition** | **Next State** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DIR_MEM_WAIT | Waiting for memory data | Read or write miss on a line in DIR_I | MEM_DATA arrives | DIR_E, DIR_S, or DIR_M |
| DIR_FWD_WAIT | Waiting for owner's data | Request on a line owned by another tile | DATAFWD arrives | DIR_S or DIR_M |
| DIR_INV_WAIT | Waiting for all INVACKs | Write request on a line in DIR_S | All INVACKs received | DIR_M |
| DIR_EVICT_INV | Evicting a shared line | A new fill needs a victim way in DIR_S | All sharers invalidated | DIR_MEM_WAIT for the new line |
| DIR_EVICT_FWD | Evicting an owned line | A new fill needs a victim way in DIR_E or DIR_M | PUTS or PUTM arrives | DIR_EVICT_WB or DIR_MEM_WAIT |
| DIR_EVICT_WB | Writing victim back to memory | A dirty victim needs write-back | Memory write completes | DIR_ME |

## **Why These States Are Needed**

The directory is the ordering point for its blocks. When a request arrives, the directory must:

1. **Block new requests to the same line.** While a transaction is in flight, no second request may be accepted.
2. **Track outstanding messages.** The directory must know which INVACKs, DATAFWDs, or memory replies it is waiting for.
3. **Resolve the line before filling a new one.** If the requested block needs a victim way, the victim must be handled first. This is why eviction uses its own transient states.

## **Directory State Transition Rules**

| **From** | **Event** | **To** | **Actions** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| DIR_I | GETS | DIR_MEM_WAIT | Fetch from memory |
| DIR_I | GETM | DIR_MEM_WAIT | Fetch from memory |
| DIR_MEM_WAIT | MEM_DATA | DIR_E or DIR_S | Install, reply |
| DIR_S | GETS | DIR_S | Reply, add sharer (no transient) |
| DIR_S | GETM | DIR_INV_WAIT | Send INV to all sharers |
| DIR_INV_WAIT | All INVACKs | DIR_M | Set owner, reply |
| DIR_E | GETS (owner) | DIR_E | Local serve |
| DIR_E | GETS (other) | DIR_FWD_WAIT | Send FWD_GETS to owner |
| DIR_FWD_WAIT | DATAFWD | DIR_S | Add sharer |
| DIR_E |  GETM (other) | DIR_FWD_WAIT | Send FWD_GETM to owner |
| DIR_FWD_WAIT | DATAFWD | DIR_M | Set owner |
| DIR_M | GETS (owner) | DIR_M | Local serve |
| DIR_M | GETS (other) | DIR_FWD_WAIT | Send FWD_GETS, expect write-back  |
| DIR_FWD_WAIT | DATAFWD | DIR_S | Add sharer |
| Any | Eviction needed |  DIR_EVICT_* | Resolve victim first |

The message set is **complete** if every state transition in the MESI protocol is driven by a message. The table below shows this.

| **Transition** | **Message That Drives It** |
| --- | --- |
| I → S (remote fill) | GETS, DATA |
| I → E (remote fill, exclusive) | GETS, DATA (exclusive bit set) |
| I → M (write miss) | GETM, DATA |
| S → M (upgrade) | GETM, INV, INVACK, DATA |
| E → M (silent upgrade) | None (local only) |
| M → I (eviction) | PUTM |
| E → I (eviction) | PUTS |
| S → I (eviction) | None (silent drop) |
| S → I (remote invalidation) | INV, INVACK |
| E → S (sharing) | FWD_GETS, DATAFWD |
| M → S (sharing) | FWD_GETS, DATAFWD, write-back |
| M → M (ownership transfer) | FWD_GETM, DATAFWD |

**Result:** Every transition is covered. The message set is complete.

---

# **Minimality**

A message set is **minimal** if no message can be removed without breaking the protocol.

| **Message** | **Can it be removed?** | **Consequence** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| GETS | No | Read misses would have no request |
| GETM | No | Write misses and upgrades would have no request |
| PUTS | No | Clean evictions could not update the directory |
| PUTM | No | Dirty evictions would lose data |
| UNBLOCK | No | Directory would not know when to release the transient state |
| INV | No | Sharers would keep stale copies |
| INVACK | No | Directory would not know when invalidation is complete |
| DATA | No | Requester would never receive data |
| FWD_GETS | No | Owner would not supply shared data |
| FWD_GETM | No | Owner would not supply modified data |
| DATAFWD | No | Forwarded data would have no carrier |
| DMA_READ | Under evaluation | DMA read path would not exist |
| DMA_WRITE | Under evaluation | DMA write path would not exist |
| DMA_ACK | Under evaluation | DMA completion would not be signalled |

**Result:** The core MESI message set (11 messages) is minimal. The DMA messages are additive and are marked as under evaluation.

---

# **Protocol Invariants**

The message set and the transient states must preserve the following invariants. These invariants become SVA assertions in the verification plan.

| **Invariant** | **Meaning** |
| --- | --- |
| Single Writer | At most one cache is in M or E state for any block |
| Multiple Reader | Any number of caches may be in S state for a block |
| Data Consistency | If a cache is in M, memory is stale. If S or E, memory is current. |
| Directory Consistency | Directory state matches the aggregate L1 states |
| No Lost Messages | Every request receives a response |
| No Orphan Transient | Every transient state is exited by a known message or a timeout |
| Ordering | Two requests to the same block are ordered by the directory |

---

# **Open Items**

| **Item** | **Status** |
| --- | --- |
| DMA message set | Under evaluation |
| Timeout mechanism for transient states | To be specified |
| Formal properties for completeness | To be written |
| Coverage model for state × message cross | To be defined |
| Interaction with DMA engine | To be specified |

---

# **Dependencies**

| **Finding** | **Relationship** |
| --- | --- |
| F1 — L1 cache spec | Defines the L1 stable and transient states |
| F3 — Directory spec | Defines the directory stable and transient states |
| F7 — Flit format | Defines how each message is encoded on the NoC |
| F5 — Protocol flow | Uses this message set in transaction sequences |
| F8 — Verification plan | Uses these invariants as formal properties |

---

# **Summary**

The coherence protocol uses 14 messages. The core MESI set has 11 messages: 5 requests and 6 responses. Three additional messages support DMA and are under evaluation. When a transaction is in flight, both the L1 cache and the directory enter transient states. The L1 uses 5 transient states: IS_D, IM_D, SM_D, EI_A, MI_A. The directory uses 6 transient states: DIR_MEM_WAIT, DIR_FWD_WAIT, DIR_INV_WAIT, DIR_EVICT_INV, DIR_EVICT_FWD, DIR_EVICT_WB. Each transient state records what the agent is waiting for. Each message moves an agent out of a transient state and into a new stable state. The message set is complete because every MESI transition is driven by a message or by a local upgrade. The message set is minimal because no core MESI message can be removed without breaking the protocol.

---

# **Quick Reference Card**

```
┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  COHERENCE MESSAGE SET AND TRANSIENT STATES           │
├───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                       │
│  MESSAGES (14)                                        │
│  ─────────────                                        │
│  Requests:   GETS, GETM, PUTS, PUTM, UNBLOCK          │
│  Responses:  INV, INVACK, DATA,                       │
│              FWD_GETS, FWD_GETM, DATAFWD              │
│  DMA:        DMA_READ, DMA_WRITE, DMA_ACK             │
│                                                       │
│  L1 TRANSIENT STATES (5)                              │
│  ───────────────────────                              │
│  IS_D   Invalid → Shared, waiting data                │
│  IM_D   Invalid → Modified, waiting data              │
│  SM_D   Shared → Modified, waiting data + acks        │
│  EI_A   Exclusive → Invalid, waiting ack              │
│  MI_A   Modified → Invalid, waiting ack               │
│                                                       │
│  DIRECTORY TRANSIENT STATES (6)                       │
│  ──────────────────────────────                       │
│  DIR_MEM_WAIT    Waiting for memory data              │
│  DIR_FWD_WAIT    Waiting for owner's DATAFWD          │
│  DIR_INV_WAIT    Waiting for all INVACKs              │
│  DIR_EVICT_INV   Evicting shared line                 │
│  DIR_EVICT_FWD   Evicting owned line                  │
│  DIR_EVICT_WB    Writing victim back                  │
│                                                       │
│  PROPERTIES                                           │
│  ──────────                                           │
│  Complete: every MESI transition is covered           │
│  Minimal:  no core message can be removed             │
│                                                       │
└───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

##

**White Paper Section — Design Parameter Justification**

---

## 1. Definition

### 1.1 What the State Machines Are

The cache and directory state machines control every coherence decision in the design. Three separate finite state machines (FSMs) cooperate:

| FSM | Location | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 FSM | `cache_controller.sv` | Handle core requests and coherence messages at the L1 cache |
| L2 FSM | `L2_cache_controller.sv` | Handle requests at the home bank; drive directory lookups |
| Directory FSM | `L2_cache_controller.sv` | Track in-flight transactions at the directory |

### 1.2 Scope of This Section

This section covers seven related decisions:

1. L1 FSM design
2. Hit and miss decision logic
3. Victim selection
4. L2 FSM design
5. Directory lookup
6. Forward and invalidate paths
7. Transient state handling

### 1.3 Location

All three FSMs live in the L1 cache controller and L2 cache controller. State encodings and transitions are in `cache_controller.sv` and `L2_cache_controller.sv`.

---

## 2. Alternatives

### 2.1 L1 FSM Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Monolithic FSM | Single FSM handles hits, misses, and coherence |
| 2 | Split FSM | Separate FSMs for core path and coherence path |
| 3 | Microcoded FSM | Instruction ROM drives state transitions |
| 4 | Hierarchical FSM | Top-level FSM dispatches to sub-FSMs per operation type |

### 2.2 Hit/Miss Decision Logic Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Combinational compare | Tag compare in one cycle; hit/miss in the same cycle |
| 2 | Registered compare | Tag compare registered; hit/miss one cycle later |
| 3 | Parallel compare | All ways compared in parallel; result select |
| 4 | Serial compare | Ways compared one at a time; slower but smaller |

### 2.3 Victim Selection Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | LRU | Track least-recently-used way |
| 2 | Pseudo-LRU | Approximate LRU with tree bits |
| 3 | FIFO | Replace oldest way |
| 4 | Random | Replace a random way |

### 2.4 L2 FSM Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Monolithic FSM | Single FSM handles all L2 activity |
| 2 | Split by role | Separate FSMs for memory fetch and directory update |
| 3 | Split by port | Separate FSMs for local L1 path and network path |
| 4 | Hierarchical | Top-level FSM with sub-FSMs per operation |

### 2.5 Directory Lookup Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Combinational lookup | Directory read and decode in one cycle |
| 2 | Registered lookup | Directory read registered; decode one cycle later |
| 3 | Pipelined lookup | Directory read and decode in separate stages |
| 4 | Speculative lookup | Lookup starts before request is confirmed |

### 2.6 Forward and Invalidate Path Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Shared path | Forwards and invalidations share the same FSM |
| 2 | Separate paths | Dedicated FSM states per message type |
| 3 | Unified path | One state covers all outbound messages |
| 4 | Priority-ordered | Path chosen by message priority |

### 2.7 Transient State Handling Alternatives

| # | Approach | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | No transient states | Blocking; one request at a time |
| 2 | Minimal transient states | Only IS_D and IM_D |
| 3 | Per-transition states | One transient state per transition type |
| 4 | Categorized states | Transient states grouped by wait type |

---

## 3. Comparison Matrices

### 3.1 L1 FSM Comparison

| Criterion | Monolithic | **Split** | Microcoded | Hierarchical |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| States | Fewer | **More** | Many | Medium |
| Transition clarity | Low | **High** | Medium | High |
| Verification per state | Complex | **Simple** | Complex | Medium |
| Area cost | Low | **Low** | Medium | Medium |
| Debug visibility | Low | **High** | Low | Medium |
| Handles concurrent core + net | No | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | **Yes** | No | Maybe |

### 3.2 Hit/Miss Decision Comparison

| Criterion | Comb. | Reg. | **Parallel** | Serial |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hit latency | 1 cycle | 2 cycles | **1 cycle** | N cycles |
| Area cost | Low | Low | **Medium** | Low |
| Timing path length | Long | Short | **Long** | Short |
| Verification | Simple | Simple | **Simple** | Complex |
| Fits 4-way L1 | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Fits 4-way L2 | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |

### 3.3 Victim Selection Comparison

| Criterion | **True LRU** | Pseudo-LRU | FIFO | Random |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hit rate (L1) | Highest | High | Medium | Low |
| Hit rate (L2) | Highest | High | Medium | Low |
| Storage per set | N × log2(N) | N−1 bits | N bits | 0 |
| Logic cost | High | **Low** | Low | Lowest |
| Verification | Medium | **Low** | Low | Low |
| Deterministic | Yes | Yes | Yes | No |
| Fits 4-way | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### 3.4 L2 FSM Comparison

| Criterion | Mono. | Split role | **Split port** | Hier. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Handles local + remote | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Directory interaction | Mixed | **Clear** | Clear | Medium |
| Verification | Complex | Medium | **Simple** | Medium |
| Area cost | Low | Low | **Low** | Medium |
| Handles concurrent requests | Partial | Yes | **Yes** | Yes |
| Fits 3-month timeline | Yes | Yes | **Yes** | Maybe |

### 3.5 Directory Lookup Comparison

| Criterion | **Comb.** | Reg. | Pipe. | Spec. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Lookup latency | **1 cycle** | 2 cycles | 2 cycles | 1 cycle |
| Timing path | **Long** | Short | Short | Long |
| Complexity | **Low** | Low | Medium | High |
| Verification | **Simple** | Simple | Medium | Complex |
| Handles hit + state decode | Yes | Yes | Yes | Maybe |
| Fits 8-set L2 | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Maybe |

### 3.6 Forward and Invalidate Path Comparison

| Criterion | Shared | **Separate** | Unified | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FSM states | Fewer | **More** | Fewer | Medium |
| Protocol clarity | Low | **High** | Low | Medium |
| Deadlock risk | High | **Low** | High | Medium |
| Verification | Complex | **Simple** | Complex | Medium |
| Handles both paths | Yes | **Yes** | Yes | Yes |

### 3.7 Transient State Comparison

| Criterion | None | Minimal | **Per-transition** | Categorized |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Blocks on request | Yes | Partial | No | **No** |
| Handles concurrency | No | Partial | Yes | **Yes** |
| Race coverage | None | Partial | Full | **Full** |
| Verification | Simple | Medium | Complex | **Complex** |
| Area cost | Lowest | Low | **Medium** | Medium |

---

## 4. Chosen Option

### 4.1 Chosen L1 FSM

**Chosen: Split FSM.**

The L1 FSM has two independent but coupled state registers:

- **Core FSM:** handles core requests (load, store, flush)
- **Coherence FSM:** handles incoming coherence messages (DATA, INV, FWD_GETS, FWD_GETM)

L1 core FSM states:

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| IDLE | Waiting for a core request |
| COMPARE | Comparing tag and reading directory |
| ALLOCATE | Reserving a victim way |
| INITIATE | Sending a request (GETS, GETM) |
| SNOOP | Handling an incoming coherence message |
| DONE | Completing the core request |

L1 coherence FSM states:

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| COH_IDLE | Waiting for a coherence message |
| COH_RECEIVE | Receiving and decoding |
| COH_APPLY | Applying the state change |
| COH_RESPOND | Sending the response |

### 4.2 Chosen Hit/Miss Decision Logic

**Chosen: Parallel combinational compare.**

All ways in the set are compared in parallel. The match signal is computed combinationally from the tag array output. The state of the matched way is read in the same cycle.

### 4.3 Chosen Victim Selection

**Chosen: True LRU for L1, pseudo-LRU for L2.**

| Cache | Policy | Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 | True LRU | 4-way set is small; LRU fits in a few bits |
| L2 | Pseudo-LRU | 4-way set; tree bits are cheaper |

### 4.4 Chosen L2 FSM

**Chosen: Split by port.**

The L2 FSM has two coupled state registers:

- **Local FSM:** handles requests from the local L1
- **Network FSM:** handles requests from remote tiles

Both FSMs interact with the same directory array. A small arbiter resolves conflicts.

L2 FSM states (per port):

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| L2_IDLE | Waiting for a request |
| L2_COMPARE | Directory lookup and tag compare |
| L2_ALLOCATE | Reserving a victim way |
| L2_MEM_WAIT | Waiting for memory response |
| L2_FWD_WAIT | Waiting for forwarded data from an owner |
| L2_INV_WAIT | Waiting for INVACKs from sharers |
| L2_EVICT_WAIT | Evicting a victim |
| L2_RESPOND | Sending the response |

### 4.5 Chosen Directory Lookup

**Chosen: Combinational lookup.**

The directory array is read combinationally. The state decode is done in the same cycle. The FSM decision is made on the same edge.

### 4.6 Chosen Forward and Invalidate Paths

**Chosen: Separate FSM states per message type.**

The L2 FSM has distinct states for `FWD_GETS`/`FWD_GETM` forwarding and `INV` broadcast. The paths share the message queue but have separate state tracking.

### 4.7 Chosen Transient State Handling

**Chosen: Per-transition transient states.**

Every transition that involves an in-flight transaction has a distinct transient state.

L1 transient states:

| State | From | To |
| --- | --- | --- |
| IS_D | I | S or E |
| IM_D | I | M |
| SM_D | S | M |
| EI_A | E | I |
| MI_A | M | I |

Directory transient states:

| State | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| DIR_MEM_WAIT | Waiting for memory response |
| DIR_FWD_WAIT | Waiting for owner to supply data |
| DIR_INV_WAIT | Waiting for INVACKs |
| DIR_EVICT_INV_WAIT | Evicting a shared line |
| DIR_EVICT_FWD_WAIT | Evicting an owned line |
| DIR_EVICT_WB | Writing back a dirty victim |

---

## 5. Why This and Not That

### 5.1 Why Split L1 FSM and Not Monolithic

**Why not monolithic:**

- A monolithic FSM mixes core request handling with coherence message handling. The states become ambiguous.
- Verification must reason about every combination of core and network activity.
- Debugging requires tracing states that serve two unrelated purposes.

**Why not microcoded:**

- A microcoded FSM adds an instruction ROM and a sequencer. This is overkill for the L1's simple state space.
- Microcoding is useful when the state space is large and regular. The L1 FSM is small and irregular.

**Why not hierarchical:**

- Hierarchical FSMs help when sub-operations share common steps. The L1's operations (load, store, flush) have little in common beyond the tag compare.
- The added dispatch layer is not worth the savings.

**Why split:**

- The core FSM and coherence FSM have distinct concerns. Separating them makes each state machine's purpose clear.
- Verification can prove the core FSM's correctness independently of the coherence FSM.
- Debug traces show which FSM is active. This speeds bring-up.
- The two FSMs interact only at well-defined points (e.g., when a snoop interrupts an outstanding core request).

### 5.2 Why Parallel Combinational Compare and Not Registered

**Why not registered:**

- A registered compare adds a cycle to every hit. This slows the critical path.
- The L1 cache is on the core's critical path. Every cycle counts.

**Why not serial:**

- A serial compare is slower. It compares ways one at a time. This is only useful when area is critical.
- The 4-way set is small. Parallel compare is cheap.

**Why parallel combinational:**

- One-cycle hit latency. This is the standard for cache access.
- The tag array output is stable in the same cycle as the request.
- The synthesis tool can meet timing if the set size is small.
- For 4 ways, the compare logic is a handful of XOR and OR gates.

### 5.3 Why True LRU for L1 and Pseudo-LRU for L2

**Why true LRU for L1:**

- The L1 has 4 ways per set. True LRU needs log2(4!) ≈ 4.6 bits per set. This is affordable.
- True LRU gives the best hit rate for the L1's small set count.
- The L1 is on the critical path. A better hit rate means fewer misses. Fewer misses mean fewer network transactions.

**Why not true LRU for L2:**

- The L2 is larger. True LRU for 4 ways is still affordable, but the marginal hit rate over pseudo-LRU is small.
- Pseudo-LRU uses fewer bits per set. The savings add up across 8 sets × 16 banks.
- The L2's misses are less frequent than the L1's. The hit rate difference has less impact.

**Why not FIFO for either:**

- FIFO does not capture recency. It performs worse than LRU under most workloads.

**Why not random:**

- Random is non-deterministic. This complicates verification.
- Random performs worse than LRU on average.

### 5.4 Why Split L2 FSM by Port and Not Monolithic

**Why not monolithic:**

- The L2 serves both the local L1 and remote tiles. A monolithic FSM would have to track which source a request came from.
- The two request sources have different priorities and different paths. Mixing them causes confusion.

**Why not split by role:**

- Splitting by role (memory fetch vs directory update) requires coordination between the two FSMs. This is a chicken-and-egg problem.
- The port split is cleaner because it matches the physical structure.

**Why not hierarchical:**

- Hierarchical FSMs add a dispatch layer. For only two ports, the dispatch layer costs more than it saves.

**Why split by port:**

- Each port has its own FSM. The FSMs share the directory array through an arbiter.
- The local port has higher priority. The arbiter enforces this.
- Each FSM's state is easy to reason about. It handles only requests from one source.
- Verification can prove each FSM's correctness independently.

### 5.5 Why Combinational Directory Lookup and Not Registered

**Why not registered:**

- A registered lookup adds a cycle to every directory decision.
- The directory is not on the core critical path. But it is on the coherence path. Every cycle counts.

**Why not pipelined:**

- A pipelined lookup requires pipeline registers and hazard logic. The directory is small. The extra stages are not worth the complexity.

**Why not speculative:**

- Speculative lookup requires a rollback path. The complexity is high. The benefit is small.

**Why combinational:**

- The directory array is small (8 sets × 4 ways = 32 entries). The read is fast.
- The state decode is a small combinational function.
- The FSM decision is made in the same cycle as the request. No latency penalty.

### 5.6 Why Separate Forward and Invalidate Paths

**Why not shared:**

- Forwards and invalidations have different targets. A forward goes to one owner. An invalidation goes to all sharers.
- Sharing the path requires a multi-cycle broadcast. This complicates the FSM.

**Why not unified:**

- A unified state cannot express both "wait for one response" and "wait for N responses" cleanly.
- Verification of a unified state is harder.

**Why not priority-ordered:**

- Priority ordering means the FSM must know all priorities. This adds combinational logic to the critical path.

**Why separate:**

- The forward path is a point-to-point transaction. It waits for one response.
- The invalidate path is a broadcast transaction. It waits for N responses.
- The two paths have distinct state machines. This makes each path's correctness provable.
- The design is publishable as a clean separation.

### 5.7 Why Per-Transition Transient States

**Why not no transient states:**

- The design would block on every request. It would not handle concurrent requests.
- The original design's lack of transient states was the source of many bugs.

**Why not minimal:**

- Only IS_D and IM_D do not cover upgrades or evictions.
- The design would fail on S → M upgrades and on evictions with outstanding messages.

**Why not categorized:**

- Grouping transient states by wait type (memory, forward, invalidation) works for the directory but not for the L1.
- The L1 needs distinct states per transition because each transition has its own final state.

**Why per-transition:**

- Every transition that involves an in-flight transaction has a distinct state.
- The state encodes exactly what the design is waiting for.
- Verification can prove each transient state's exit condition.
- The design is compatible with the standard MESI vocabulary in the Primer.

---

## 6. Where It Affects the Design

### 6.1 Affected Components

| Component | Affected | How |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 cache controller | Yes | Core FSM and coherence FSM |
| L2 cache controller | Yes | Local FSM and network FSM |
| Directory array | Yes | Read by L2 FSM |
| Tag array | Yes | Read by hit/miss logic |
| Victim way | Yes | Selected by LRU module |
| Message set | Yes | Messages trigger FSM transitions |
| NIC | Yes | Packetizes FSM outputs |
| Router | No | Routing is independent of FSM |
| Virtual channels | Yes | FSM assigns messages to VC classes |
| Verification | Yes | Properties per FSM state |

### 6.2 Unaffected Components

| Component | Reason |
| --- | --- |
| Mesh topology | FSM design is independent of topology |
| Flit format | FSM outputs are encoded into flits |
| Address map | FSM uses address bits for set and tag |
| Clock frequency | FSM timing depends on the clock constraint |

---

## 7. Consequences

### 7.1 Gain

- Clear separation of concerns. Each FSM has one job.
- Verification can prove each FSM's correctness independently.
- Debug traces show which FSM is active. Bring-up is faster.
- Transient states cover every race. No silent hangs.
- LRU policies balance hit rate and area.
- Combinational decisions keep the critical path short.

### 7.2 Loss

- More FSM states than a monolithic design. More verification properties.
- Split FSMs require coordination at well-defined points.
- True LRU costs more bits than pseudo-LRU. This is acceptable at 4 ways.
- The L2's split FSMs need an arbiter for the directory array.

### 7.3 Constraint

- Every transient state must have a defined exit condition.
- Every FSM transition must be triggered by a core request, a message, or a timer.
- Every state must be reachable and every state must return to IDLE.
- The L1 FSM must not block on a coherence message while a core request is in flight.

### 7.4 Verification

| Property | Method |
| --- | --- |
| Every FSM state is reachable | Formal reachability |
| Every FSM state returns to IDLE | Formal liveness |
| No FSM deadlock | Formal + stress |
| Every transient state exits | Formal liveness |
| LRU updates correctly | Directed tests |
| Hit/miss decision is correct | Directed + formal |
| Forward path reaches owner | Directed + formal |
| Invalidate path reaches all sharers | Directed + formal |
| Directory lookup matches state | Formal |

---

## 8. Future Extension

| Extension | Prerequisite | Estimated time |
| --- | --- | --- |
| MSHR (multiple outstanding requests) | L1 FSM extension | 4 weeks |
| Non-blocking L1 | MSHR + scoreboard | 4 weeks |
| Adaptive routing interaction | L2 FSM extension | 4 weeks |
| Speculative directory lookup | Rollback path | 6 weeks |
| Tree-based pseudo-LRU for L1 | Replace true LRU | 2 weeks |
| 8-way caches | Wider tag compare | 2 weeks |

---

## 9. Contradiction With Original Design

### 9.1 Monolithic FSM in the Original

The original design used a monolithic FSM in the L1 cache controller. This made the state hard to debug and hard to verify. The redesign splits it into a core FSM and a coherence FSM.

### 9.2 No Transient States in the Original

The original design had few transient states. This was the source of the `DIR_I` valid-bit bug and the `PROXY_WAIT` dead state. The redesign introduces per-transition transient states and asserts their exit conditions.

### 9.3 Fixed Replacement in the Original

The original design used a fixed replacement policy. This is fine for a prototype but not for a publishable design. The redesign uses true LRU for the L1 and pseudo-LRU for the L2.

### 9.4 Combinational Compare in the Original

The original design used combinational compare. This is correct. The redesign keeps it.

---

## 10. Summary

| Decision | Choice | Main Reason |
| --- | --- | --- |
| L1 FSM | Split (core + coherence) | Clear separation of concerns |
| Hit/miss | Parallel combinational compare | One-cycle hit latency |
| Victim selection | True LRU (L1), pseudo-LRU (L2) | Balance hit rate and area |
| L2 FSM | Split by port (local + network) | Matches physical structure |
| Directory lookup | Combinational | Small array; no latency penalty |
| Forward and invalidate | Separate FSM paths | Different targets; different state machines |
| Transient states | Per-transition | Every race covered; every exit asserted |

The cache and directory FSMs form the control backbone of the coherence protocol. Each FSM has a single job. Each FSM's correctness can be proven independently. Transient states cover every in-flight race. The design is publishable as a clean separation of concerns.

---
